But the *content* of the network is (a) a designer-written rewrite grammar and (b) an LLM's vibes about what catalyses what. The grammar is a small authored object whose shape determines everything; the oracle is the same pretrained-prior-as-tech-tree problem as ideas 1/5/6, just confined to one boolean relation. The doc's own framing — 'assigned by an LLM it inherits arbitrariness; assigned by typed rules it inherits their finiteness; there is no third option' — is an accurate statement that the design has no answer to the central question.

Compute honesty

HONEST on the arithmetic, DISHONEST about laziness. The RAF fixpoint is trivially cheap at these sizes, the memory claims are fine, and 'you could run this on a Pi' is true. But there is a correctness bug in the coupling of lazy catalysis with RAF closure: maxRAF is computed over a network whose *edges are mostly unqueried*. Whether reaction r is in the RAF depends on whether some producible stuff catalyses it — a fact you have deferred. So either you compute maxRAF over a network you know to be incomplete (and 'exactly computable' becomes false — your headline event is an artifact of query order), or you must eagerly adjudicate the entire frontier every tick, and the token cost is nowhere near 'a few thousand calls a night'. The doc never confronts this and it is the design's most concrete technical hole.

Nonsense risk

HIGH. 'Model ritual and story as reactions too: a practice that consumes surplus and catalyses cohesion' — that is relabeling, not mechanism, and it is the tell. When every kind of thing is a 'stuff' and every process is a 'reaction', the type system stops doing work and the graph stops meaning anything. Combined with string-concatenation stuffs, you get a large, precisely-computed, beautifully-rendered graph of nothing. The maths will remain exactly correct about a network that has ceased to be about anything.

Claims to re-check

The complexity framing is garbled and inverted. Deciding whether a RAF exists and computing the maxRAF is polynomial in the *general* case (that is Hordijk & Steel's central result); what is NP-hard is finding *minimal/irreducible* RAFs and several constrained variants. The doc says the reverse — 'the general RAF setting is NP-hard but graph-theoretic restrictions give efficient polynomial algorithms' — which misdescribes the one paper the whole design rests on. Second: transferring Kauffman's phase-transition result to this world is unsound. That result concerns *random* catalysis at fixed probability in a binary-polymer model. Here catalysis is assigned by an LLM's semantic prior, which is strongly non-random and correlated with the rewrite grammar's own structure; the threshold theory simply does not carry over, and 'every closure raises local density, seeding the next transition' is asserted, not derived. Third, minor: chemlambda's rewrites are local but not reversible in general. Fourth: the Sayama Hash Chemistry arXiv IDs look mismatched to the described works, and the 2025 ALIFE AlChemy-steering paper is beyond what I can verify. Hordijk & Steel 2004 JTB and the 2018 Interface review are real and correctly attributed otherwise.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Kill the LLM catalysis oracle and make catalysis a deterministic function of the rewrite grammar's own type structure — i.e. give stuffs real internal structure (a term, not a string) and derive catalysis from structural matching. Then catalysis density becomes a tunable, principled parameter, the phase-transition theory you are citing actually applies, the network is self-consistent so maxRAF is genuinely exact, the lazy-adjudication hole disappears, and the grammar — which the doc correctly identifies as 'the game' — becomes something you can deepen with structure rather than with more string operators.

▸

Sandwright

A falling-sand material world where the physics itself is the oracle — no LLM decides whether anything works — and technologies are spatial machine assemblies discovered by a MAP-Elites archive, with recurring sub-assemblies frozen into named modules that become the alphabet for the next generation of search. Arthur's "technology is combination" implemented literally in space, on the GPU, at thousands of blueprint evaluations per second.

Ceilinged7.6
The world

A 2D cellular material world in the Powder-Toy / Noita lineage: 512×512 per region, ~30 material types, local rules for gravity, heat conduction, ignition, phase change, fluid flow, pressure, structural load, corrosion, and charge diffusion. Tribes of agents live on top of it and are relatively simple — this is the one design where the agents are not the interesting part; the MACHINES are. Every material behaviour is a rule you wrote once, and every consequence of those rules is something you did not write. Wood chars in low oxygen. Water boils and the steam has pressure. Ore reduces above a temperature you never labelled as a milestone.

How invention actually happens

A technology is a BLUEPRINT: a small spatial arrangement of materials (say 24×24 cells) plus a control program (strike here, open that, wait) and declared PORTS (input cell region, output cell region). Three parts: (1) NEEDS AS MEASURABLE OBJECTIVES. Agents pose objectives the physics can score: 'raise 1 kg water 3 m', 'hold 900 K for 200 ticks', 'cut a 5-cell notch in oak', 'keep this cell between 600 and 700 K unattended'. (2) MAP-ELITES OVER BLUEPRINT SPACE. Archive keyed by behavioural descriptors — materials used, footprint, energy in, port topology, module count. Fitness = objective achievement per unit input. Mutation operators: spatial (grow, erode, mirror, translate), material substitution, control-program mutation, and crucially MODULE INSERTION — dropping a previously discovered blueprint in as a black box. (3) MODULE ABSTRACTION — the compounding step. Any sub-assembly appearing in ≥K elite blueprints across ≥3 distinct objectives is frozen into a named MODULE with an interface: its ports plus a MEASURED TRANSFER FUNCTION (what comes out as a function of what goes in, fitted from simulation runs). Modules become primitives for future search. A bellows becomes a part. A furnace becomes a part. Furnace+bellows becomes 'hot furnace' — one part. Modules are what tribes teach, trade, steal, and lose.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The space of spatial arrangements over 30 materials in a 24×24 blueprint is astronomically large, and unlike a token space it is GROUNDED: the physics adjudicates, so the nonsense-soup failure mode that haunts every LLM-crafting design simply does not exist here. Nothing can be invented that does not work, because working is defined by simulation. Compounding comes from modules-as-primitives — search operates over an alphabet that grows with every discovery, so the effective depth of what search can reach in a fixed budget increases monotonically. The real bound is the physics's expressive ceiling: you can only invent what the CA rules can express. That makes 'add one material rule' a legitimate, high-leverage god-tier nudge rather than a chore — each new rule measurably reopens whole regions of the archive, and you can watch archive coverage jump the next morning.

The ladder

WEEK 1: sharp edges, a fire pit that stays lit, a windbreak that actually changes local heat loss. WEEK 2–4: a water lift; a charcoal pile that requires oxygen starvation (a real emergent discovery in falling-sand chemistry that you will not have anticipated); a kiln that holds temperature. MONTH 2: bellows + furnace composite — the first two-module machine — then smelting, then the first machine that manufactures a part for another machine. MONTH 3–6: heat engines, because steam pressure is already in the CA; mechanical linkages; water-powered trip hammers; electricity if you put charge diffusion in from day one, which you should. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE: a self-regulating machine. MAP-Elites finds an arrangement that holds a kiln at temperature with no agent tending it — a thermostat discovered by search, not designed — which means the tribe has invented CONTROL, and you can watch it work pixel by pixel on your phone.

What kills you

Thermodynamics, all the way down. Fuel is finite and local, and the CA tracks the actual trees, so deforestation is a literal pixel event that forces the charcoal transition. Machines wear, corrode and fail; a tribe that cannot rebuild a module loses it, and rebuilding requires the modules that built it. Tribes compete for ore and fuel gradients. Machines that fail kill people — the physics does the killing, which is far more convincing than a damage formula. Population is search throughput: more people means more blueprint evaluations per day, so demography and invention rate are coupled in both directions.

How the world changes

Regions with different geology and climate: a wet region makes fire tech expensive and water tech cheap, and the two regions develop visibly different engineering traditions from identical rules. Eras cycle — a volcanic era adds obsidian and sulfur, an ice age changes fluid behaviour and makes stored heat precious. And the honest non-stationarity lever: adding ONE new material rule every few weeks is your only maintenance ritual, it takes twenty minutes, and its effect on archive coverage is immediately measurable, which makes it satisfying rather than a chore.

How it knows something is new

Archive-native. Push when (a) a previously empty MAP-Elites cell fills and its elite beats the global best on a new objective, (b) a new MODULE is frozen — module births are rare and always interesting, (c) an elite uses ≥3 modules (a compositional-depth record), (d) an objective unsolved for ≥14 days is solved. Then the best signal in this entire document: PHYSICS SURPRISE — a machine whose measured transfer function deviates >3σ from the linear composition of its constituent modules' transfer functions. That is, mechanically, 'the machine does something its parts do not predict', which is the operational definition of an emergent invention, and it is computable from data you are already collecting.

On your phone

A three-second looping WebP of the new machine actually running, autoplaying on the card, with its name, the objective it serves, and its module ancestry as three small icons. Nothing on earth explains a machine faster than watching it. Generation is cheap: render 90 CA frames to a canvas and encode. Below it, one number — ARCHIVE COVERAGE (% of descriptor cells filled) — and one counter, MODULES. Tap for the machine catalogue, which is the binge view: scrolling a catalogue of little looping machines your laptop invented is exactly the artifact worth building.

Your hardware

This is the only idea that uses the RTX 5070 for what it is actually good at, and it is a much better use of 8 GB than holding an LLM. The material CA is embarrassingly parallel: write it as a WebGPU/WGSL compute shader (or CUDA) and run hundreds of 256×256 test chambers concurrently in VRAM — 8 GB holds on the order of hundreds of chambers with double-buffered state, giving thousands of blueprint evaluations per second, which is what makes MAP-Elites actually converge on a laptop. Write the kernel in WGSL specifically so the same code runs in the browser for PWA replay. The local 4B is relegated to naming things and writing the chronicle, which is the correct job for it. BENCHMARK HYGIENE: fitness in CA ticks and material counts, never wall-clock — under thermal throttling a timing-based fitness would quietly select for machines that happen to be evaluated when the laptop is cool.

What you have to do

Near zero. The optional weekly ritual is 'add a material rule', which is genuinely fun and takes twenty minutes.

First weekend

SATURDAY: falling-sand CA with 8 materials and heat, on the CPU first at 128×128 — do NOT start on the GPU, you want to debug the rules in a language you can print from. Add one fixed objective ('get this cell above 800 K'). SUNDAY: random blueprint mutation, a MAP-Elites archive with three descriptors, and a GIF of the best elite. The delight moment: search finds an oxygen-starved charcoal pile that your own rules permitted and you did not know about.

Six months in

A catalogue of 50–200 modules with measured transfer functions; regional engineering traditions that diverged from identical rules; a phone feed of little machines that actually run; and the specific pleasure of scrolling a machine catalogue nobody designed.

Where it stops (self-reported)

It plateaus at your CA's physics, and that ceiling is closer than it feels: without pressure, electricity and some chemistry you top out at roughly 'medieval workshop', maybe 20–40 qualitatively distinct machine classes. A 2D CA also makes rotation and gears genuinely hard — either accept a world with no wheels-as-machines, or add a rigid-body layer, which is a serious project in itself. And MAP-Elites descriptor design is the real craft here; bad descriptors give you an archive full of near-duplicates that looks productive and is not. Budget for redesigning your descriptors at least twice, and for one weekend lost to a CA rule that is subtly non-conservative and quietly breaks every fitness measurement downstream.

Stack

WebGPU compute via wgpu (Rust) or a node/Deno WebGPU binding, so the CA kernel is literally shared between the headless runner and the PWA. MAP-Elites archive in SQLite + numpy; blueprint = a small integer tensor plus a control program. WebP/GIF encoding for the loops. llama.cpp for names and chronicle prose. ntfy.sh; PWA over Tailscale plus nightly static export to GitHub Pages. SHARED SUBSTRATE with idea 2: same archive/lineage DB, same chronicle PWA, same push layer. This idea also has the best physical-artifact story: a receipt printer (python-escpos over USB) printing one machine's blueprint as ASCII plus its name and date, every morning.

Verified

Verified: MAP-Elites is the standard illumination algorithm — a multi-dimensional archive of phenotypic elites over a user-defined feature/behaviour-descriptor space, producing the best solution of each type rather than one global optimum (Mouret & Clune 2015; overview https://www.emergentmind.com/topics/map-elites-algorithm). Extensions worth knowing before you design descriptors: Multi-Emitter MAP-Elites for convergence speed (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2007.05352), Multi-Objective MAP-Elites/MOME where each cell holds a local Pareto front (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.03715), and Multi-Objective QD in unbounded spaces — relevant because a growing module alphabet means your descriptor space is not fixed. Directly analogous precedent for QD over generative game systems: 'Quality Diversity in the Amorphous Fortress' evolving for complexity in 0-player games (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.02231) and micro RollerCoaster Tycoon open-ended gameplay-feature search (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2105.04342). For the interestingness/task-generation half, OMNI-EPIC (ICLR 2025) is the reference architecture — a growing task archive of learned and failed tasks, a task generator conditioned on the archive, and an environment generator emitting environment code (https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.15568, code at https://github.com/maxencefaldor/omni-epic); its explicit warning about agents optimising a metric rather than capturing interestingness is why my novelty detector here is grounded in physics deviation rather than a learned score. Theory: W. Brian Arthur, *The Nature of Technology* — technologies are combinations of prior technologies, encapsulated as modules with interfaces, which is exactly the module-freezing step. Physical artifact: python-escpos / python-printer-escpos drives USB ESC/POS thermal printers directly (https://github.com/shantanubhadoria/python-printer-escpos, https://pypi.org/project/escpos-thermal/).

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The CA's expressive ceiling, and it is much nearer than the depth ladder pretends. Falling-sand cellular automata are structurally incapable of rigid bodies, rotation, persistent multi-cell object identity, and load transfer through a mechanism. The doc flags this honestly in `honest_ceiling` ('rotation and gears genuinely hard') and then contradicts itself by listing heat engines, mechanical linkages and water-powered trip hammers in the month 3–6 ladder. A heat engine needs a piston: a rigid moving body with a seal, i.e. exactly the thing the substrate cannot represent. What this world can actually reach is the thermal/fluid/chemical family — fire pit, windbreak, charcoal pile, kiln, smelter, siphon/water-lift, still, maybe a self-regulating thermal loop if your rules happen to permit hysteresis. That is roughly 15–30 distinct machine classes, not 20–40, reached in 6–10 weeks. After that MAP-Elites keeps filling cells with near-duplicates and archive coverage keeps rising, which will *look* like progress on the phone and will not be.

Is the discovery real?

LOWEST OF THE SIX ALONGSIDE ASHCAIRN, and for the cleanest reason: no LLM is in the adjudication loop at all. The oxygen-starved charcoal pile really is a consequence of rules you wrote that you did not anticipate — that is authentic emergence, and the delight moment described for Sunday is real. The correct reading is that the designer authored a *generator* (the rule set) rather than a *catalogue*, which is the distinction the builder is actually asking for. The remaining authored content is objective specification: 'raise 1kg water 3m', 'hold 900K for 200 ticks' are hand-written goals, and the machine catalogue is therefore shaped by which objectives you thought to pose. That is a real but much milder version of the problem, and it is fixable with auto-generated objectives.

Compute honesty

OPTIMISTIC BY ~10×, AND ONE CLAIM IS SIMPLY WRONG. 'The material CA is embarrassingly parallel' is false. Falling-sand movement has write conflicts — two source cells wanting the same destination — plus update-order dependence that produces the characteristic sand behaviour in the first place. Correct GPU implementations need Margolus/block partitioning or atomic conflict resolution, both of which change the dynamics relative to a sequential CPU implementation. This directly breaks the recommended plan ('debug on CPU at 128×128, then port to GPU'): your fitness landscape will shift under you at the port, and every archive elite found on CPU may be invalid on GPU. Throughput: memory is fine (a 256² chamber double-buffered is ~2MB, so hundreds fit in 8GB), but a branchy divergent falling-sand kernel gets nowhere near the arithmetic throughput of a clean kernel. At 500 chambers × 65k cells × ~500–1000 ticks per evaluation you are looking at ~10^10 cell-updates per batch; realistic is on the order of 10²–10³ evaluations/sec, not 'thousands'. Still enough for MAP-Elites — the design survives — but budget accordingly, and pin determinism early or your fitness measurements are unreproducible.

Nonsense risk

ESSENTIALLY ZERO, and this is the design's great virtue. Nothing can be invented that does not physically work, because working is defined by simulation. There is no drivel failure mode. The residual risk is the inverse: things that work but are not *interesting* — MAP-Elites archives fill with mechanically valid near-duplicates, and bad behavioural descriptors will make that look like productivity. The doc's warning to budget for redesigning descriptors twice is correct and probably still an underestimate.

Claims to re-check

THE 'BEST SIGNAL IN THIS DOCUMENT' IS PROBABLY NOISE. 'PHYSICS SURPRISE — a machine whose measured transfer function deviates >3σ from the linear composition of its constituent modules' transfer functions.' In a CA with heat conduction, pressure and fluid flow, sub-assemblies are coupled non-locally through shared fields; non-additive composition is the DEFAULT, not the exception. Every multi-module machine will deviate from linear composition, so this detector fires constantly and carries no information. Worse, the same fact undermines the compounding mechanism itself: 'modules as black boxes with measured transfer functions' presumes interfaces that compose, and CA modules do not have them — a furnace's measured behaviour in isolation will not hold when a bellows is bolted to it, so MODULE INSERTION as a mutation operator is far weaker than advertised. This is the design's most serious hidden problem, and it sits precisely where the depth is supposed to come from. Also: 'electricity if you put charge diffusion in from day one' — charge diffusion is not circuits; you need conserved charge and a potential field solve, a different and much heavier component. Citation-level: MOME is Pierrot et al. (arXiv 2202.03057), not the ID given; Mouret & Clune 2015 and OMNI-EPIC (2405.15568) are correctly attributed; Arthur's modules-with-interfaces framing is used correctly and is the right theory for this design.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Add a rigid-body/mechanism layer above the CA — blueprints become jointed part-graphs (links, pivots, constraints) coupled to the cell field, rather than pure cell arrangements. This is the single edit that separates 'medieval kitchen chemistry' from 'machines', unlocks the entire month 3–6 ladder the doc already promises but cannot currently deliver, and gives module composition real interfaces (kinematic ports) instead of statistically-fitted transfer functions that don't compose. It is also a genuinely hard project — but it is the difference between a partial and a yes here, and no other change in this idea moves the ceiling at all.

▸

Ninety-Nine Anvils

Dozens of small worlds running at once, each with its own LAW VECTOR that conditions both the mechanical referee and the LLM oracle, so the same combination is genuinely adjudicated differently under different physics — and the object of study is the difference between worlds. A shared ledger with nearest-law inheritance makes the token cost scale with consequence rather than with attempts, and anonymous web visitors act as gods, one omen per day.

Not real depth
The world

12–48 concurrent worlds, each a coarse 64×64 grid with a few hundred agents, sharing the typed-substance substrate from The Slow Kiln. Each world has a LAW VECTOR: 16 named continuous constants (gravity, oxygen fraction, water boiling point, decay rate, ore abundance, day length…) plus 6 boolean MYTHS that are injected into the oracle's system prompt and into the referee's parameters ('metals rust within a day', 'fire needs no air', 'the dead answer questions'). A myth is a physics constant and a cultural fact at the same time, which is the conceit that makes the whole thing sing. Worlds are born, run, and die; a dead world becomes an ASH WORLD you can still browse — the graveyard is a first-class feature.

How invention actually happens

Infinite-Craft-style unbounded recipe space, made lawful and made cheap: (a) TYPED SCHEMA + CONSERVATION REFEREE, shared with The Slow Kiln — the LLM fills typed property slots and mechanical laws audit it. (b) LEDGER KEY = (canonical combo, LAW BUCKET), where the law bucket is a coarse quantisation of the 16 constants + 6 myths into ~64 buckets. Same combo, different bucket, different legitimate answer. (c) NEAREST-LAW INHERITANCE — the cost trick. On a ledger miss, find the k nearest law buckets that HAVE adjudicated this combo; if ≥2 agree, adopt the result with confidence 0.8 and mark it PROVISIONAL. Provisional results are only re-adjudicated for real when a tribe's survival comes to depend on them. LLM calls therefore scale with CONSEQUENCE, not with attempts, which is what makes 40 worlds cost about the same as one. (d) LAW DIVERGENCE OBJECTS. When the same combo yields materially different outcomes across two adjudicated buckets, that difference is promoted to a first-class browsable record with both results side by side. Comparative physics as a UI object; over months it becomes an atlas of how laws shape technology.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two axes multiply. The recipe space is the usual unbounded hypergraph (each combination can mint a new node, tamed but not bounded by the property schema). The LAW space is continuous and mutable, and worlds are cheap to spawn, so the system explores rule-sets as well as inventions — a civilization in a low-oxygen world builds a different tech tree from identical primordials. Formally the object being explored is a law-conditioned recipe manifold, and every new law bucket is a fresh slice of it. Honest bound: the LLM's cultural attractors (Infinite Craft's graph collapses toward meme-y sinks, and yours will too), plus the fact that law vectors only reach as far as the 16 constants you chose — the 17th constant is the god-tier nudge.

The ladder

WEEK 1: three worlds, identical 20 primordials, visibly different first tools by day 3 — that divergence alone is the proof-of-concept and it arrives fast. WEEK 2–4: twelve worlds plus a migration mechanic; the first recipe that is essential in one world and lethal in another. MONTH 2: a traveller caste; a queryable meta-ledger ('show me inventions that exist ONLY in high-gravity worlds' — a question no hand-authored tech tree can even express). MONTH 3–6: worlds FORKED from a snapshot with a single mutated constant, giving you true A/B physics on a running civilization; long-lived worlds developing distinct materials cultures; visitor-gods shaping worlds they have adopted. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE: a comparative-science page showing the same technology re-invented along three disjoint ancestral paths in three differently-lawed worlds — convergent technological evolution, discovered rather than authored, with the divergence points highlighted.

What kills you

Per-world survival as usual (calories, warmth, winter, raiding). But the distinctive layer is WORLD-LEVEL SELECTION: worlds are scored nightly on invention rate, divergence from siblings, and population stability, and the bottom 10% are retired and replaced by mutated forks of the top worlds. That is MAP-Elites applied to worlds, and it means your compute is continuously reallocated to whichever worlds are still surprising. It is the most direct structural answer to 'boring by week 2' in this whole document — boredom is measured and then killed.

How the world changes

It is the premise, not a feature. Plus the perturbation layer: anonymous visitors to the public GitHub Pages site can spend one OMEN per day on any world — a weather event, a gift substance, a plague, or a line added to that world's SCRIPTURE, which is a text blob genuinely included in that world's oracle prompt. A guestbook that literally becomes law. This is cheap non-stationarity, a real reason for the artifact to be public, and the thing that makes a friend check in twice.

How it knows something is new

(a) Cross-world CONTRADICTION with high consequence — the same combination, adjudicated for real in two worlds, giving outcomes that matter differently. (b) A world's first divergence from every sibling on a common recipe. (c) CONVERGENT RE-INVENTION: the same result reached by disjoint ancestral paths in ≥2 worlds (compare ancestor sets, Jaccard below threshold). (d) A world crossing a civilizational threshold no world has crossed. Score all four nightly, push the top 1–3. The comparative framing is what keeps this feed interesting: a single world's fiftieth invention is boring, but 'world 7 got there a completely different way' never is.

On your phone

A 4×3 grid of world tiles, each a 40×40 glyph encoding population, tech depth and mood as shape and colour — a dashboard of civilizations, readable in three seconds, with one tile pulsing for tonight's headline. This is the single best 30-second check-in view of the six ideas, because it shows you a whole portfolio at a glance. Tap a tile to fall into that world's chronicle; tap the headline for the divergence record with both outcomes side by side.

Your hardware

The sim is tiny and CPU-bound; the cost centre is adjudication. Local Qwen3.5-4B Q4_K_M under GBNF handles routine adjudication, and nearest-law inheritance means most misses never reach any model at all. Cloud usage is one nightly Haiku 4.5 Batch job for the ~300 most consequential adjudications plus the chronicle: at $1/$5 per MTok, 50% Batch discount, and a cached ~2k-token system prompt read at ~0.1×, that is roughly $0.0005 per call → $0.05–0.20 a night, call it $5/month. 8 GB VRAM is ample and you never need a bigger model, because the typed schema is doing the work the model would otherwise have to do. 64 GB RAM comfortably holds 48 world states plus the shared ledger in memory.

What you have to do

Zero. The only thing you may find yourself doing by hand is retiring a world you have grown tired of, which the selection layer would do for you anyway.

First weekend

SATURDAY: one world — property schema, referee, local oracle, ledger. (If you build The Slow Kiln first, this Saturday is free.) SUNDAY: clone to three worlds with different law vectors, implement nearest-law inheritance, and render the three-tile dashboard. Delight moment: the same combination returning three different but internally coherent results, and the divergence record writing itself.

Six months in

Dozens of ash worlds with epitaphs, a browsable comparative-physics atlas, a handful of long-lived worlds with genuinely distinct materials cultures, and a scripture page where a friend's joke from March has become three worlds' religion.

Where it stops (self-reported)

LLM cultural attractors are real and they erode divergence: the same model writes every world, so names and vibes converge over time no matter how you condition the prompt. Typed schemas and law-conditioned prompts slow this measurably; they do not stop it, and by month four you will see it. Breadth also trades against depth — forty shallow worlds are less moving than one deep one, and I expect you will cull to eight or twelve within two months and be happier for it. The visitor-god layer only matters if anyone actually visits; build it, but do not let the design depend on strangers showing up. And nearest-law inheritance is a lie you are choosing to tell: provisional results that never get verified are load-bearing fiction, so keep the PROVISIONAL flag visible in the UI or you will eventually confuse yourself.

Stack

node + SQLite: one DB per world plus one shared ledger DB (this separation matters — you will want to delete worlds). llama.cpp GBNF; Anthropic Batch API. A static PWA build published to GitHub Pages nightly so visitors need no Tailscale, plus the live build over Tailscale for you. ntfy.sh for push. The omen intake with no inbound ports: a Cloudflare Worker (or a GitHub Issues form) writes to a gist that your laptop polls every 10 minutes — laptop stays firewalled, world stays public. SHARED SUBSTRATE with ideas 1 and 6.

Verified

Verified: Infinite Craft's caching architecture is exactly the pattern I am extending — check the pair database first, only novel pairings hit the LLM, then cache forever so results are globally consistent (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infinite_Craft; implementation walkthrough https://www.conjecture.dev/research/building-an-infinite-craft-clone-with-tactics; hacking notes https://debarghyadas.com/writes/infinite-craft/). Scale reality check for what an unbounded recipe space actually becomes: >1M unique items and >10M recipes recorded by community databases, and the formal structure is a directed hypergraph (https://quuxplusone.github.io/blog/2024/03/03/infinite-craft-theory/). The degeneracy risk is documented informally in the community wikis' 'step depth' vs 'element depth' distinction (https://infinite-craft-nealfun.fandom.com/wiki/Step_Depth) — most new elements are shallow re-skins, which is precisely why I gate on consequence and divergence rather than on count. Relevant caution on LLM-driven search converging: 'Mutation Without Variation: Convergence Dynamics in LLM-Driven Program Evolution' (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.05408) — the same attractor problem in a different setting, and a good reason to keep a mechanical referee in the loop. Many-agent civilization precedent: Project Sid ran up to 1,000 autonomous agents in Minecraft for hours-to-days unattended and got a gem-based market economy, religious transmission, and constitutional voting — emergent, not scripted (https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00114, https://github.com/altera-al/project-sid). Pricing verified against the current Anthropic model table (claude-haiku-4-5 $1/$5 per MTok, 200K context; Batch API 50% off; prompt-cache reads ~0.1×, writes ~1.25×). Push/PWA: ntfy.sh is installable as a PWA with Web Push and app-badge unread counts, self-hostable if you'd rather (https://docs.ntfy.sh/releases/, https://ntfy.sh/).

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

There is no new depth mechanism here. This is The Slow Kiln instantiated 40 times with a comparison UI, and breadth across shallow worlds is not depth in any of them — the doc concedes this ('forty shallow worlds are less moving than one deep one; I expect you will cull to eight or twelve'). Each world inherits Kiln's ceiling and hits it *sooner*, because per-world compute is divided by 40 and the nearest-law inheritance path means most worlds are not even independently adjudicated. Realistic: divergence visible day 3 (true, and it will feel like proof), the comparative atlas is genuinely fun for 3–4 weeks, and by week 6 you are looking at 40 instances of the same shallow tree with different adjectives.

Is the discovery real?

THE HIGHEST OF THE SIX, WITH A COMPOUNDING FACTOR. Idea 1's problem (the tech tree is the pretrained model's compressed memory of human material culture) is inherited whole. Then the distinctive mechanism makes it worse in two specific ways. (1) The LLM has no coherent counterfactual physics for 'oxygen fraction 0.14' or 'metals rust within a day' — it has adjectival associations. So law-conditioned adjudication is prompt sensitivity, and the comparative-physics atlas — the entire point of the design — is an atlas of a 4B model's response to adjectives, presented as a finding about how laws shape technology. (2) NEAREST-LAW INHERITANCE FABRICATES THE DATA THE PROJECT EXISTS TO STUDY. Most cross-world outcomes are copied from k-nearest buckets, so the observed pattern of similarity and divergence between worlds is largely a readout of your own interpolation scheme and bucket geometry. 'Convergent re-invention across disjoint ancestral paths' will fire constantly for the trivial reason that inheritance copied the result. The doc calls this 'a lie you are choosing to tell' and proposes to manage it with a PROVISIONAL flag in the UI — but a flag does not stop the fabricated majority from being what the atlas is made of.

Compute honesty

HONEST, and the cheapest of the six — but honest for a bad reason. Cost scales with consequence only because most adjudications are never performed; the compute saving and the epistemic problem are the same fact. The Haiku arithmetic is right (~$0.0006/call cached-batch, ~$0.20/night, $5/mo is if anything conservative). 48 world states plus ledger in 64GB is fine. The Cloudflare-Worker-to-gist polling design for inbound omens without open ports is genuinely good engineering. THERE IS ALSO AN ARITHMETIC BUG IN THE CORE MECHANISM: 16 continuous constants plus 6 booleans quantised into '~64 buckets' — but the 6 booleans alone generate exactly 64 combinations. At that resolution the law bucket is effectively just the myth bitmask, and the sixteen continuous constants, which are the entire 'law-conditioned recipe manifold' premise, get zero discriminating power. Either the bucket count is wrong by orders of magnitude or the continuous laws are decorative.

Nonsense risk

HIGH and self-amplifying. The myths ('the dead answer questions') go into the oracle's system prompt, so the model is being asked to reason materially inside a fictional physics it has no model of. Output will be internally fluent and externally arbitrary. The referee catches conservation violations only, and law-conditioned referee parameters do not constrain semantics. Add LLM cultural attractors — same model writing all 40 worlds — and by month two the worlds converge in vibe while the divergence detector, reading fabricated inheritance data, keeps reporting that they haven't.

Claims to re-check

'Mutation Without Variation: Convergence Dynamics in LLM-Driven Program Evolution', arXiv 2606.05408 — I cannot verify this; the identifier places it at the edge of what I can check and it should be treated as unconfirmed rather than as the supporting citation for the design's key risk. Project Sid (2411.00114) is real, but 'a gem-based market economy, religious transmission and constitutional voting — emergent, not scripted' repeats a company preprint's own framing; those behaviours were produced under substantial architectural scaffolding and the paper is not independent evidence that many-agent LLM civilisations self-organise. The Infinite Craft caching account and the step-depth/element-depth degeneracy note are correct — and the latter is the strongest thing in these notes, because it describes exactly what will happen here. Pricing verified correct. Separately, the world-selection rule is Goodhart bait: retiring the bottom 10% on 'invention rate and divergence' selects precisely for worlds that churn out shallow re-skins, which is the degeneracy the same research notes warn about.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Cut to 4–8 worlds and route the law vector through the MECHANICAL REFEREE ONLY — never into the prompt — while deleting nearest-law inheritance entirely and adjudicating each world independently. Then divergence between worlds is causally produced by rules rather than by adjectives and interpolation, the comparative atlas becomes a real finding instead of a prompt-sensitivity study, and the per-world compute budget rises 6–10× so each world can actually get deep. This costs the design its headline scale and is the only version of it worth building.

▸

The Telling

Techniques are not stored as recipes but as UTTERANCES in each band's evolving language, learned by children through a transmission bottleneck that forces the language to become compositional — and once it is compositional, a child can SAY a technique nobody ever taught, and go try it. Invention as a side effect of grammar, with mis-hearing as the mutation operator and the Tasmania effect as the constant threat.

Ceilinged
The world

A river valley, ~200 agents in 6–10 bands, sitting on the typed-substance substrate from The Slow Kiln (substances with property vectors, process verbs, a conservation referee). But the knowledge layer is the protagonist. A MEANING is a structured frame: `(verb, patient, instrument, condition)` — HEAT(clay, in:pit, until:red). A band's LANGUAGE is a mapping from meanings to strings over an evolving phoneme inventory. An agent that wants to do something must be able to SAY it; an agent that cannot say a technique cannot reliably do it, and cannot teach it at all. Knowledge is linguistic, therefore lossy, therefore precious.

How invention actually happens

Three coupled loops, only one of which is conventional. (1) DOING. An agent produces the utterance for a meaning and attempts it; the typed referee and (only for genuinely novel material/process pairs) the local LLM oracle adjudicate against the world. Standard. (2) TEACHING — Kirby/Smith iterated learning. Children observe only a SUBSET of adult utterance-meaning pairs (the bottleneck) and induce a grammar by minimum description length over a small context-free grammar — this is a well-trodden ~300-line model and it reliably works. The bottleneck forces generalisation, and generalisation is compositionality: shared morphemes emerge for shared meaning components. (3) THE INVENTION VALVE — the part that is genuinely different from every other idea here. Once the grammar is compositional, a child can PRODUCE a well-formed utterance for a meaning that was never taught, because the grammar generates it. Most such utterances denote techniques nobody has ever tried. The agent tries one. The world says yes or no. Invention arrives as a side effect of language becoming systematic — the tribe can literally say things before it can do them, and then goes to find out. Plus NOISE: transmission is phoneme-level noisy, so techniques drift; a drifted utterance sometimes parses to a different, workable meaning. Selection acts on comprehensibility within the band and on results in the world.

Why it doesn't bottom out

A compositional grammar over a lexicon of size L with a handful of productive rules generates infinitely many well-formed utterances, and the meaning space is the product of a growing substance set × a growing process set × a growing condition set. Each new material added to the lexicon multiplies the set of EXPRESSIBLE techniques, almost none of which have been tried. The frontier of this system is precisely 'sentences we can say but have never said' — which is the cleanest statement of an adjacent possible I can give you, and it is directly computable: enumerate the grammar, subtract the attempted set. Honest bound: the meaning-space schema. Four-slot frames give a big but finite-dimensional space; it only grows as the underlying substance/process sets grow, which means this engine's long-run depth is inherited from the chemistry engine beneath it.

The ladder

WEEK 1: holistic language — random strings per meaning, terrible transmission, techniques constantly lost. The world feels tragic and that is correct; you are watching pre-cumulative culture. WEEK 2–4: the bottleneck starts producing regularity. The first shared morpheme appears, and the night your log prints 'morpheme /ka/ now appears in 83% of HEAT meanings' is a genuine thrill, because you did not assign /ka/ to anything. MONTH 2: full compositionality in the leading band. Knowledge stops leaking, technique count inflects sharply upward — you can literally see the cultural ratchet engage as a kink in a chart. MONTH 3–6: dialects diverge between bands; a trade pidgin forms at the confluence; translation errors produce inventions; one band invents a MARK SYSTEM (a second bottleneck-relaxing technology, reachable through the same substance substrate) and pulls decisively ahead. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE: a band develops a productive morphological rule covering a CLASS of processes it has not yet discovered, and then systematically goes looking — the language predicting the technology. If you see that once, the project has paid for itself.

What kills you

Survival on technique quality. Band size drives transmission fidelity directly (Henrich's demographic model, implemented rather than gestured at), so a band that splits loses its hardest-to-learn techniques first — the fishhook result, live. Comprehensibility drives communicative success within a band, creating pressure toward regularity that competes with pressure toward expressivity: that tension IS the engine. Mergers, splits and contact events change effective population and therefore change what can be held. Small isolated bands genuinely lose technologies you watched them invent, with names and dates, which is the most emotionally powerful mechanic in any of these six designs.

How the world changes

Valley geography changes: floods, a landslide isolating a band, a new pass opening contact between two dialects (a language-contact event with real technique transfer). Across worlds: each world seeds a different phoneme inventory and a different meaning schema, so languages are mutually alien, and the PWA gets a translation page comparing how two worlds carved up the same physics — which is a strange and wonderful thing to browse.

How it knows something is new

(a) PRODUCTIVE INVENTION — a technique whose utterance was generated by the grammar, never taught, and worked. Push these above everything; they are direct evidence the engine is running, and they are rare enough to stay special. (b) COMPOSITIONALITY SCORE crossing a threshold, measured with the standard iterated-learning metrics: expressivity plus stability under transmission, or topographic similarity between distances in meaning space and distances in string space. Computable nightly, one number, and a beautiful sparkline. (c) LOSS EVENTS — a technique whose speaker count hits zero; these get a different, quieter notification sound, and you will care about them. (d) CONTACT EVENTS transferring ≥5 techniques.

On your phone

The card is a sentence. Last night's first-ever-spoken utterance, rendered in the band's orthography, with its gloss underneath, whether it worked, and who said it. Below: two numbers — COMPOSITIONALITY (0–1, with a sparkline) and TECHNIQUES HELD, with a small red LOST counter. A sentence in a language your laptop invented, which turned out to describe a real thing, is a superb ten-second check-in and a genuinely uncanny thing to read at 7am. The binge view is the DICTIONARY: an explorable lexicon with etymologies, which you will browse for an hour without meaning to.

Your hardware

Very light. MDL grammar induction is CPU and small; the whole valley's grammars fit in a few hundred MB, and 64 GB RAM lets you keep every generation's grammar for diffing (which is where the good analysis lives). The local 4B has exactly two jobs: adjudicating genuinely novel technique semantics against the substance schema, and GLOSSING utterances into English for you. That second job is the most appropriate use of an LLM anywhere in this document — the model is the translator, not the physics. 8 GB VRAM is generous for this.

What you have to do

None. Optional joy: carve a word onto a stone and see whether it spreads. That is a two-minute intervention with a genuinely unpredictable outcome, which is the best kind.

First weekend

SATURDAY: the iterated-learning core alone. Meanings as 3-slot frames, holistic initial language, a transmission bottleneck, MDL grammar induction, ten generations. Watch compositionality emerge in a toy run in minutes — this is a well-trodden model and it WILL work on Saturday, which makes it an unusually safe first weekend. SUNDAY: wire it to 20 substances and 6 process verbs, and let grammar-generable-but-unspoken utterances be tried against the referee. Delight moment: the first working technique that nobody taught anyone.

Six months in

Several mutually unintelligible dialects with a trade pidgin between them, a dictionary with etymologies you browse for pleasure, technologies whose names encode their ancestry, and a graveyard page of lost techniques with the name of the last person who knew each one.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The classic iterated-learning result is convergence to a STABLE compositional grammar — and stability is exactly where the drift half of the engine dies. Once the language is fully compositional and the bottleneck is comfortable, the language stops changing and the surprises come only from the meaning space growing underneath it. Which means this is really The Slow Kiln plus a language layer, and you should build it that way rather than pretending it stands alone. Two more honest costs: glossing from a 4B is mediocre and the chronicle will lean on the nightly Haiku pass to be readable; and this is the most conceptually demanding of the six — expect a month before it FEELS like a world rather than a linguistics demo. Build it second, after you have a substrate.

Stack

Python for the grammar induction and MDL search (this is the mathematically fiddly part and Python is right for it); the sim in node or Python on the shared typed-substance substrate; SQLite for lexicons, utterances, techniques and loss records; llama.cpp for glossing and novel-technique adjudication; a nightly Haiku Batch pass for chronicle prose, because glossing quality matters here more than anywhere else. PWA with a dictionary browser; ntfy.sh push. This idea has the best receipt-printer story of the six: python-escpos prints one utterance and its gloss every morning — a daily scrap of an invented language on your desk.

Verified

Verified: iterated learning models show that expressive, compositional languages arise spontaneously from a TRANSMISSION BOTTLENECK — learners see only a subset of the previous generation's language and must generalise, and only compositional languages remain stable under that constraint (Kirby, 'Spontaneous evolution of linguistic structure: an iterated learning model of the emergence of regularity and irregularity' — https://www.ling.ed.ac.uk/~simon/Papers/Kirby/Spontaneous%20evolution%20of%20linguistic%20structure%20an%20iterated.pdf; Smith/Kirby/Brighton overview https://www.lel.ed.ac.uk/~kenny/publications/smith_03_iterated.pdf; Griffiths & Kalish's Bayesian treatment https://cocosci.princeton.edu/tom/papers/IteratedLearningEvolutionLanguage.pdf). Note the honest caveat from the literature: 'Compositional structure can emerge without generational transmission' (Cognition, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0010027718302464) — the bottleneck is sufficient, not strictly necessary, so do not over-attribute. Modern ML framing of the same compression/expressivity tension: 'A Compressive-Expressive Communication Framework for Compositional Representations' (https://arxiv.org/html/2501.19182) — also the source of usable compositionality metrics like topographic similarity. Demographic knowledge loss: Henrich 2004 on Tasmania — inaccuracies of cultural transmission produce technological LOSS below a critical population threshold, and the hardest-to-learn technologies go first (https://www2.psych.ubc.ca/~henrich/Website/Papers/HenrichTasmania.pdf); a runnable R version of the gain/loss model exists at https://bookdown.org/amesoudi/ABMtutorial_bookdown/model9.html. Cultural evolution among LLM agent populations, if you later want model-based agents rather than symbolic ones: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.10270 and the Cultural Evolution in LLM Populations line of work — but note the standing critique that validation is the central unsolved problem for generative social simulation (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10462-025-11412-6), which is a good reason to keep your agents symbolic and your LLM confined to adjudication and glossing. Printer: python-escpos over USB (https://github.com/shantanubhadoria/python-printer-escpos).

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two ceilings, and the technological one is not this idea's at all. (a) TECHNOLOGICAL DEPTH IS ENTIRELY INHERITED. The invention valve produces utterances; whether an utterance denotes a workable technique is adjudicated by the same 4B oracle over the same typed-substance substrate as The Slow Kiln. The language layer therefore changes the *order and sociology* of attempts and adds nothing whatsoever to the reachable set. It is an enumeration policy over Idea 1's space, so it inherits Idea 1's week-8 ceiling and reaches it *later* because transmission loss slows the ratchet. The doc concedes exactly this ('this is really The Slow Kiln plus a language layer') and it is the correct reading. (b) THE LINGUISTIC ENGINE STOPS ON SCHEDULE. The classic iterated-learning result is convergence to a stable compositional grammar — stability is the *finding*. Once the leading band is compositional and the bottleneck is comfortable, the language freezes; drift stops being generative and becomes noise. Expect compositionality to saturate around week 6–10 in the leading band, after which the sparkline is flat forever and the remaining interest is dialect divergence and loss events. Genuinely moving, but it is a two-month arc, not a six-month one.

Is the discovery real?

SPLIT VERDICT, AND THE SPLIT MATTERS. The linguistic half has the lowest authored-content risk in the document: nobody assigned /ka/ to HEAT, compositionality genuinely emerges from MDL induction under a bottleneck, and the loss records are real computed facts about who knew what and when. The technological half has the same fatal problem as ideas 1 and 5 — the tech tree is the pretrained model's memory of human material culture, revealed in an order now determined by grammar rather than by inventory. And there is a specific rhetorical sleight to call out: 'the frontier is sentences we can say but have never said, which is the cleanest statement of an adjacent possible I can give you.' A compositional grammar over (verb × patient × instrument × condition) generates the CROSS-JOIN of those slots. Enumerating unattempted cells of a cross-join is trivially available in Idea 1 too — it is just 'combinations nobody has tried'. Productive morphology dressed over a Cartesian product is not an adjacent possible; it is a for-loop with an accent. The 'wildest plausible' outcome — a morphological rule covering a class of undiscovered processes, and the band systematically going looking — is that for-loop, described as prophecy.

Compute honesty

HONEST FOR THE TOY, UNDERESTIMATED AT SCALE. The claim that this 'WILL work on Saturday' is true and is a real virtue — Kirby-style iterated learning at toy scale is genuinely ~300 lines and reliably produces the effect. But the toy scale is load-bearing: the canonical models use meaning spaces of a few slots with 5–10 values each and fixed vocabulary. Here the meaning space grows with the substance set toward hundreds or thousands of values, and per-child MDL grammar induction over CFGs is a search whose cost grows badly in lexicon size and corpus size. With 200 agents each inducing a grammar over a growing lexicon, grammar induction — not the LLM, not the sim — becomes the compute bottleneck within weeks, and you will be forced into approximations that may destroy the very effect you are simulating. The doc's 'very light, a few hundred MB, 8GB is generous' budget accounts for none of this. The LLM's role (glossing plus novel-technique adjudication) is correctly scoped and is the most appropriate LLM use in the document.

Nonsense risk

MEDIUM-HIGH, concentrated in two places. First, glossing: a 4B model producing English glosses for an invented morphology will generate fluent, confident, wrong readings, and glosses are the entire phone experience — you will be reading the model's imagination about your simulation. The design correctly identifies this and correctly plans a nightly Haiku pass, which makes the chronicle a paid dependency rather than a nicety. Second, technique semantics: 'does this well-formed utterance denote a workable technique' is adjudicated by the same thin referee as Idea 1, so a grammatically productive utterance can denote physical nonsense that the referee waves through, and the band then 'discovers' it. The prettiest failure mode in the document: a perfectly compositional language for a materials science that isn't real.

Claims to re-check

Griffiths & Kalish is cited as supporting evidence when it is substantially a caution: the Bayesian analysis shows iterated learning converges to the *learners' prior*, meaning what you will be watching emerge is largely your MDL prior's shape rather than a discovered fact about language. Given that your prior is a hand-written MDL score over a small CFG class, that is a direct authored-content leak into the one part of the design with the least authored content — worth confronting head-on. The doc also cites the Cognition result that compositional structure can emerge without generational transmission, notes the caveat honestly, and then builds the entire engine on the bottleneck anyway. Henrich 2004 is again treated as settled when the population-size→complexity effect is contested (Vaesen et al. 2016 PNAS). Topographic similarity is a real and standard compositionality metric but is noisy and highly sensitive to the choice of distance metrics in both spaces — 'one number and a beautiful sparkline' oversells its stability. Kirby's and Smith/Kirby/Brighton's results are correctly described.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make the meaning schema itself grammatically extensible: let productive morphology introduce new SLOT TYPES — an applicative that adds an argument position, an aspectual that adds a temporal condition dimension — rather than only tiling a fixed four-slot frame. Then the language can grow the meaning space instead of enumerating it, 'sentences we can say but have never said' stops being a cross-join and becomes a genuinely expanding frontier, and the technological depth stops being purely inherited from the substrate underneath. Also follow the doc's own advice and build this second, on a working substrate — as a standalone it is a linguistics demo with a materials simulator attached.

Library learning

Agents survive by writing programs; compression turns repeated fragments into named technologies

▸

Emberlisp

A tribe in an ash-choked valley survives only by executing programs written in a tiny survival lisp, and every night a compressor (Stitch) finds the repeated fragments in the programs that kept them alive and promotes them into named primitives — those named primitives ARE the tech tree, and you never authored a single node of it. Fire is not a recipe you wrote; fire is the abstraction `(repeat n (strike flint pyrite tinder))` that the MDL objective decided was worth a name.

Ceilinged8.1
The world

The Ashfall: a 64x64 hex valley, 6 ticks/day, 96-day seasonal cycle, a volcano that periodically buries strata and exposes new ones. Sites hold resource stacks. Crucially there are NO nouns like 'fire' or 'axe' in the substrate — there are ~14 materials, each a property record (hardness, ignition_temp, melt_temp, mass, moisture, toughness, edge, conductivity), and ~12 deterministic physical rules over those records (percussion transfers energy and can spark if hardness and pyrite content clear thresholds; heat crosses ignition/melt thresholds and REWRITES the property record; drying lowers moisture; binding creates composite objects whose property record is a function of parts). Agents are bodies: calories, core temperature, hydration, injury, two hands, a position, and a pointer to the tribe's shared library. Death is real and permanent. The DSL (~28 base primitives) is typed lambda calculus in s-expressions: `move`, `gather`, `hold`, `strike`, `heat`, `bind`, `grind`, `soak`, `drop`, `nearest`, `has?`, `prop>`, `if`, `repeat`, `map`, `let`, plus arithmetic. Everything else in the civilization's vocabulary must be discovered.

How invention actually happens

Literal DreamCoder wake-sleep, three phases per simulated night. (1) WAKE: the world emits Tasks — a Task is a goal predicate over world state plus a node budget, e.g. `(and (core-temp-above 36) (night? t))`. Bottom-up enumerative synthesis over the CURRENT library with observational-equivalence dedup: maintain a bank of programs keyed by their evaluation vector on a set of sampled world-states; discard any new program whose vector already exists. Search order is best-first by a learned cost (Probe-style just-in-time bigram reweighting on the library, arXiv 2010.08663). Solutions are recorded as s-expressions in SQLite. (2) SLEEP-G (abstraction): dump the entire corpus of surviving programs as a JSON list of s-expression strings and hand it to `stitch` (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, Rust, or `stitch_core.compress(programs, iterations=5, max_arity=3)` via the Python bindings). Stitch returns candidate abstractions ranked by corpus compression; accept any whose MDL gain clears a threshold. Each accepted abstraction becomes a first-class library primitive with an id, arity, body, birth-tick, and a `parents` edge list to the abstractions its body references — that edge list is the tech tree, materialized. (3) SLEEP-R (dreaming): sample programs from the library, run them from random world-states, record (resulting state-delta -> program) as a fantasy task, and train a tiny recognition model (a 2-layer MLP over hand-features of the goal predicate + bigram counts over library ids, ~200k params, trains in seconds on CPU) to bias tomorrow's search. Naming/documentation is LILO-style: a local Qwen3.5-4B Q4_K_M, GBNF-constrained to emit exactly `(short-kebab-name . one-line-doc)`, sees the abstraction body plus three call-sites and names it. `fn_31` becomes `bank-the-coals`. That naming step costs nothing and is 80% of the delight.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Programs of depth d over a library of size L number roughly L^d. Every accepted abstraction does two things at once: it increments L, and it COLLAPSES the depth needed for anything that used that fragment. So the set of programs reachable within a fixed node budget grows super-exponentially in the number of accepted abstractions — this is W. Brian Arthur's 'technology is combination' made mechanical, and it is the reason the invention space is not a list. There is no authored enumeration of what can be built; there is only a search budget and a compressor. Honest limit on the argument: the *physics* is finite in dimension. The library keeps growing forever, but the number of qualitatively distinct WORLD EFFECTS is bounded by the property vector's dimensionality (8 fields x 14 materials). Growth in library size is unbounded; growth in kinds-of-thing-that-can-happen is not. The mitigation baked in from day one is that property records are rewritten by heat/binding/grinding, so composite materials have property vectors no raw material has — the material space itself is combinatorial, which is what keeps feeding new effects.

The ladder

Week 1 (crude but already not-yours): 20 base primitives, one need (cold nights), enumerative search finds `(strike flint pyrite)` produces a spark, Stitch names it, and you get a push notification containing a word you did not write. Rung 2 (~night 30): `keep-warm` abstraction composes spark + tinder + repeat; night mortality drops from 40% to 5%; population grows; population is the search budget (each living agent contributes node-expansions), so survival literally buys more invention. Rung 3 (~night 80): fire lets heat rewrite property records — `heat-treat` raises hardness, which makes `grind` produce an edge, which makes hunting programs feasible, which is calorie surplus. Nobody authored 'stone tools'; it fell out of a property-vector threshold. Rung 4 (~night 200): clay + sustained heat = a composite with high toughness and zero moisture permeability, i.e. pottery; storage programs appear; winter becomes survivable; SEASONAL tasks (which were previously unsolvable at any budget) enter the task distribution for the first time. Rung 5: add higher-order primitives to the base DSL (`(delegate agent prog)`, `(while pred prog)`) and abstractions start compressing SOCIAL patterns — a named abstraction that is a division-of-labour protocol. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: with `(teach agent fn)` in the base DSL, the compressor finds an abstraction whose body is a teaching schedule — the tribe invents pedagogy as a technology. The genuinely wild endpoint, and it is plausible rather than fantasy because the ingredients are all present: an abstraction whose body is itself a bounded search loop over the library. The tribe invents planning, and after that its inventions are one meta-level up.

What kills you

Calories and core temperature, both hard-coded and both lethal. An agent that ends a night below 30C dies; a tribe that cannot solve the winter-storage task loses 60% of its members. Because search budget is proportional to living agents, a failed invention cycle compounds into a slower invention cycle — the civilization can enter a death spiral, and watching one is compelling. Library entries are garbage-collected by a use-it-or-lose-it rule: an abstraction not referenced in any accepted solution for 200 nights is demoted (its call sites re-inline), which prunes the MDL objective's junk finds. Selection is at the level of programs (does this solve the task within budget) and the level of abstractions (does this compress the corpus and keep earning call-sites).

How the world changes

The volcano is a slow physics-parameter drift, not scenery. Ashfall events bury a stratum and expose another, so material availability is a non-stationary vector; a tribe whose whole edge-making stack routes through flint faces an existential re-derivation problem when flint goes under two metres of tephra, and you get to watch it either rediscover an edge from obsidian or die. Seasons rotate the task distribution on a 96-day cycle. Every ~5000 nights the valley resets with a new terrain seed and a new material-abundance draw, but the library is inherited — so you see which technologies were LOCAL TRICKS (they break) and which were LAWS (they transfer). That transfer test is the cheapest possible measure of invention depth and it costs one boolean per abstraction.

How it knows something is new

Five signals, combined into one nightly surprise score, only the top-3 get pushed. (1) First-time-ever event: hash the tuple of world-state predicates that fired this tick against a bloom filter of all previously-seen tuples — a miss is a literally unprecedented event. (2) MDL shock: keep a running distribution of nightly compression gains; flag a Stitch step whose gain exceeds the 95th percentile of the last 100 nights. (3) Depth increase: an accepted abstraction whose body references an abstraction that references an abstraction that references an abstraction — depth-4 entries are rare and always mean something composed. (4) Long-standing unsolved solved: a task that failed for N consecutive nights and just succeeded; report N, because 'the tribe has been failing to cross the river for 84 nights and tonight they didn't' is a story. (5) Regime change: Bayesian online changepoint detection on population, mean calories, and library size — a detected changepoint means the civilization's dynamics just switched. Everything else goes into the DB unpushed.

On your phone

PWA, static JSON regenerated each morning and pushed to GitHub Pages (plus Tailscale Serve for the live view). Top of screen, three lines: `NIGHT 214 · POP 23 (+2) · LIB 87 entries, depth 6`. Then the headline card: the new abstractions with their generated names and one-line docs, each showing its parent chain as a breadcrumb (`strike -> spark -> catch-tinder -> BANK-THE-COALS`). Then one sparkline of population over the last 90 nights with death events as ticks. Then one sentence of chronicle from the local model. That is the 30-second read. The hour-long binge is one tap down: the full abstraction DAG rendered as an interactive graph (canvas, force layout, colour by birth-epoch), where tapping a node shows its body, its call sites, the first task it solved, and a scrubber that replays the night it was born as a hex-map animation. ntfy.sh push only when the surprise score clears threshold.

Your hardware

This is a CPU and RAM project wearing a GPU hat. The sim, the enumerative search, and Stitch are all CPU; 64GB DDR5 is the actual enabling resource because the observational-equivalence bank for a deep search holds millions of programs keyed by evaluation vectors, and you want it resident. Run the search across all cores with a shared bank per tribe. Stitch itself runs in milliseconds-to-seconds on one core for corpora of thousands of programs (the README's own example compresses ~1.9M cost units to ~317k in 120ms). The 8GB of the 5070 holds exactly one thing: Qwen3.5-4B or Qwen3-4B at Q4_K_M, ~3.4-4.6GB resident, ~40 tok/s, used for naming and documentation with a GBNF grammar. That leaves headroom for a long context (the library dump) and for the tiny recognition MLP, which is small enough it can honestly stay on CPU. Do NOT plan on a 9B+ model here — Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M is ~6.6GB on disk and will fight your context window at 8GB. Do NOT plan on finetuning anything in this idea. Thermal hygiene, and this matters because the laptop runs 24/7: never put wall-clock time in any fitness or budget. Budget search in NODE EXPANSIONS and cost programs in INTERPRETER STEPS. Both are deterministic, so a hot laptop and a cool one produce identical history and last month's run is reproducible.

What you have to do

Zero-touch nominally. You read the morning chronicle with coffee. Real interventions available but never required: (a) add a material or a physical rule to the substrate — a ten-line edit that visibly expands what the compressor can find, worth doing maybe monthly; (b) hand-inject a Task ('cross the river') to see if the library is ready for it; (c) bump the node budget. One genuine chore exists and you should know about it: about once a month you will want to eyeball the abstraction list for MDL junk (syntactic coincidences that compress but mean nothing) and tune the acceptance threshold. Fifteen minutes.

First weekend

Saturday: the substrate. Materials as property records, ~12 physical rules, a hex map, agents with calories and core temperature, and a tree-walking interpreter for a 20-primitive DSL that charges interpreter steps. Get one agent to die of cold. Saturday night: bottom-up enumerative search with observational-equivalence dedup against a single task predicate `core-temp-above 36 at night`. Let it run to a few million nodes. Sunday: pipe every surviving program into Stitch, print the top abstraction with its compression gain, and add it to the library as a new primitive. Re-run the search and confirm the same task now solves in a fraction of the nodes. Sunday afternoon: wire llama.cpp with a GBNF grammar to name it, and wire ntfy.sh. The delight moment is a phone notification that says something like 'The Ash clan invented SPARK-ON-DRY-MOSS (fn_3, arity 2) — compresses 14 programs, MDL -22%.' You wrote no such word.

Six months in

The library is a few hundred entries deep with a DAG you have to zoom out to read, and its lower layers are stable — the equivalent of a settled physics. New abstractions arriving in month six are compositions at depth 6-8, and the interesting ones are no longer material technologies but organisational ones: scheduling, caching, delegation, teaching. You will have added two or three new material classes and a new physical rule by hand over that time (each addition visibly detonates a new burst of invention over the following week, which is its own pleasure). The transfer test across valley resets has given you a genuine, computed classification of every technology into local trick versus law. The thing that stays surprising is not the tech tree filling in — it's watching a re-derivation after a catastrophe take a completely different route than the original.

Where it stops (self-reported)

This plateaus, and the plateau has a known cause: DreamCoder-family systems saturate when the TASK DISTRIBUTION stops growing, not when the library stops growing. Expect the rate of qualitatively-new abstractions to fall off somewhere between week 4 and week 10 of continuous running, because the reachable region of an 8-dimensions-x-14-materials physics gets covered. After that you are getting recombinations, which are pretty and which compress well, but which stop feeling like discoveries. There are two honest responses and both require you: expand the physics (cheap, ten lines, monthly) or move to one of the architectures below that generates its own task growth structurally. Second ceiling, more painful: the enumerative search is the hard engineering. As L grows the branching factor grows, and naive best-first search stops keeping up around library size 50-150 depending on typing discipline. You will spend more of your build time on the enumerator (type-directed pruning, observational equivalence, just-in-time bigram reweighting, budget allocation across tasks) than on the world, and if the enumerator stalls the whole civilization freezes. Third: MDL is not meaning. Stitch will happily hand you abstractions that are syntactic coincidences with high compressivity and no interpretation; the use-it-or-lose-it demotion rule handles most of it but not all, and some fraction of your 'technologies' will be noise wearing a name the language model gave it.

Stack

Python 3.12 for the sim, the DSL interpreter, and the loop (fast enough at 6 ticks/day; port the interpreter to Rust or use numpy-vectorised state only if profiling demands it). Stitch via `stitch_core` Python bindings, or shell out to the Rust `compress` binary with `--max-arity 3 --iterations 5` — either way the input is just a JSON list of s-expression strings, and Stitch treats any unreserved token as a DSL primitive, so your survival lisp needs no declaration step at all. SQLite as the single source of truth: `library`, `programs`, `tasks`, `events`, `agents`, `nights`, with the abstraction DAG as an edge table. llama-cpp-python + Qwen3.5-4B-Q4_K_M with a GBNF grammar for naming. Frontend: vanilla JS + canvas PWA reading a generated `world.json`, deployed to GitHub Pages by a morning cron and served live over Tailscale Serve. ntfy.sh for push. No frameworks, no torch required for v1.

Verified

Verified: DreamCoder (Ellis et al., PLDI 2021, dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454080; original code github.com/ellisk42/ec is OCaml+Python and is a genuine pain to run — do not try to run it, reimplement the loop, it is a few hundred lines). Stitch (Bowers et al., POPL 2023, arxiv.org/abs/2211.16605; repo github.com/mlb2251/stitch) is the load-bearing dependency: 1000-10000x faster and ~100x more memory-efficient than DreamCoder's own compressor, runs in seconds on one CPU core, README's own graphics-DSL example compresses 1,919,558 -> 316,890 in 120ms with `--max-arity 2 --iterations 3`. Confirmed the input format is 'a JSON file containing a list of input programs, each a string in lisp-like lambda calculus syntax' and that any space-separated non-reserved token is treated as a DSL primitive — meaning a hand-rolled survival lisp pipes straight in with zero adapter. Python bindings exist as `stitch_core` (github.com/mlb2251/stitch_bindings, docs at stitch-bindings.readthedocs.io) exposing `compress(programs, iterations, max_arity=2, threads=1)` -> CompressionResult; PyPI listing exists for stitch-core but was not loadable at time of writing, so budget an hour for a `cargo build --release` fallback. LILO (Grand/Wong et al., ICLR 2024, arxiv.org/abs/2310.19791) is the precedent for Stitch + LLM auto-documentation producing interpretable libraries, and reports solving more complex tasks than DreamCoder — that is exactly the naming step here. Probe / just-in-time learning for bottom-up enumerative synthesis (Barke et al., arxiv.org/pdf/2010.08663) for the search reweighting; 'Program Synthesis with Best-First Bottom-Up Search' (arxiv.org/pdf/2310.04327) for search ordering. Hardware verified: Qwen3-4B / Qwen3.5-4B at Q4_K_M is ~3.4-4.6GB and comfortable on 8GB with 40+ tok/s; Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M is ~6.6GB and tight. llama.cpp GBNF grammars (github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/grammars/README.md) constrain output to a custom DSL — confirmed as a standard technique, with a documented caveat that runtime token checking adds overhead.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two ceilings, and the engineering one hits first. (a) Enumerator wall at library size ~50-150 with a stateful, side-effecting DSL — realistically week 2-4 of continuous running, at which point nightly wake produces nothing new and the civilization freezes with no error message. (b) Task-distribution wall: the whole thing is driven by a handful of authored survival predicates (core-temp, calories, hydration, injury). Once each is solved cheaply — library depth ~4-6, roughly 60-120 accepted abstractions — Stitch keeps returning compressive refactors of a solved corpus forever. Their own estimate of 4-10 weeks is right for 'new kinds' but assumes the enumerator survives, which is the optimistic half. The physics bound they concede (8 fields x 14 materials) is not actually the binding constraint; the predicate vocabulary is, and it is much smaller.

Is the discovery real?

Not a hand-authored tech tree — genuinely credit them for that, the node LIST is not written down. But the authored surface has just moved one level up and shrunk: 12 rules, 14 property records, ~28 primitives, and (fatally) ~5 goal predicates. 'Fire' is fully pre-figured — you wrote an ignition_temp threshold, a percussion-sparks-if-pyrite rule, and a heat-rewrites-properties rule, so the only thing discovered is the s-expression that trips a switch you installed. 'Stone tools fell out of a property-vector threshold' — you wrote that threshold and you chose hardness and edge as fields precisely because you knew where it led. Verdict: the ORDER, the PHRASING, and the refactoring are genuinely emergent; the SET of reachable qualitative effects is small enough that you could enumerate it by hand in an afternoon, which means after ~a month you are watching your own afternoon get re-derived in a random order with prettier names.

Compute honesty

GPU side: honest and then some — 8GB holds a 4B Q4 namer with room to spare, and nothing trains. The dishonesty is on CPU and it is load-bearing. DreamCoder's observational-equivalence trick is cheap because its tasks are PURE functions on tiny inputs; here evaluating a candidate means ROLLING OUT a stateful world with agent bodies across 64 probe states. A 30-node program is ~2k interpreter steps per probe set; a Python tree-walker gives maybe 0.3-1M steps/sec/core, so ~300-2000 candidates/sec/core, i.e. low millions of nodes per core-night at best. The design needs 10^6-10^7 evaluations per night per tribe to keep a growing library fed. 'Port to Rust only if profiling demands it' is wrong: profiling will demand it in week one. Memory is the least of the problems — 1M banked programs at ~300B is 0.3GB; it is throughput, not RAM, that 64GB does not buy you.

Nonsense risk

High, and structurally so. Stitch optimises SYNTACTIC compression and cannot see semantics; with only ~28 primitives, high-frequency n-grams are everywhere and a large fraction of accepted abstractions will be positional coincidences. Then a 4B model, GBNF-forced to emit a plausible kebab-case name, will confidently christen every one of them — the naming step manufactures the appearance of meaning at exactly the point where you have the least. 'That naming step is 80% of the delight' is the honest and alarming sentence in the pitch: the delight is generated by the component with zero epistemic content. Use-it-or-lose-it demotion is the right antidote but it is a 200-night lag, so the chronicle is wrong for six months at a time.

Claims to re-check

(1) BIGGEST: 'Stitch treats any unreserved token as a DSL primitive, so your survival lisp pipes straight in with zero adapter' — overstated to the point of being a schedule risk. Stitch consumes lambda-calculus with `(lam ...)` and de Bruijn `$0` indices; a typed lisp with `let`, `if`, `repeat`, `map` and named binders does NOT pipe straight in. Desugar every binding form to de Bruijn lambdas first, or Stitch will treat bound variable names as opaque primitives and hand you abstractions that are literally nonsense. Budget a day, not an hour. (2) Probe (2010.08663) learns a UNIGRAM PCFG over productions from partial solutions; calling it 'bigram reweighting on the library' conflates it with DreamCoder's bigram recognition model. Harmless conceptually, but do not expect Probe's paper numbers from a bigram implementation. (3) The Stitch README figure (1,919,558 -> 316,890 in 120ms) is on a corpus of pure graphics programs; do not extrapolate the throughput to corpora containing your stateful lisp.
