(4) Qwen3.5-4B/9B and their sizes: checked, real (Qwen3.5 small models, Mar 2026); ~3.4-4.6GB for the 4B Q4_K_M is on the conservative side if you run the text-only path, so you have MORE headroom than claimed, not less.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make the task distribution endogenous instead of authoring ~5 survival predicates. Replace them with an auto-generated curriculum over the MATERIAL PROPERTY SPACE: maintain a MAP-Elites grid over composite property vectors (hardness x toughness x ignition x conductivity...), and every night emit as tasks 'produce an object landing in an empty cell'. The goal set then grows exactly as fast as the material space the abstractions open up, which converts the flat 4-10 week plateau into a frontier that recedes as the tribe advances — and it costs about 80 lines. Survival predicates stay as the lethal floor; novelty predicates become the ceiling.

▸

The Long Teaching

Nine tribes, nine private DSL libraries, one map — and an abstraction only exists as long as a living agent still knows it. Technology here is not a permanent unlock: it is carried in heads with limited transmission bandwidth, so libraries are taught, traded, stolen, and above all FORGOTTEN, and the invention of writing is an emergent event rather than a tech-tree node.

Ceilinged6.6
The world

An archipelago-and-isthmus map, ~200 sites, nine founding tribes seeded in different biomes with genuinely different material abundance vectors (a volcanic island has obsidian and no wood; a delta has reed and clay and no hard stone). Same substrate as Emberlisp — materials as property records, ~12 physical rules, a small typed survival lisp — but with three additions that change everything. First, KNOWLEDGE IS EMBODIED: the table is `knows(agent_id, abstraction_id, fidelity)`. A tribe's effective library is the union over its living members. Second, TEACHING IS A PROGRAM: `(teach student fn)` is a base primitive costing ticks, with success probability falling in the abstraction's DAG depth — deep abstractions are hard to transmit, which is the iterated-learning bandwidth bottleneck made mechanical. Third, DEPENDENCIES ARE REAL: if an abstraction's transitive dependencies are not all known by the executing agent, the call re-inlines to base primitives and the interpreter-step cost explodes past the budget, so the program simply fails. Losing one link in the chain breaks everything above it.

How invention actually happens

Nine parallel DreamCoder loops with crossover. Each tribe runs its own wake (enumerative search over ITS library), its own Sleep-G (Stitch over ITS solution corpus — nine separate `compress` calls, each still milliseconds), and its own Sleep-R. What makes this a different engine rather than nine copies of one is the transmission layer. (a) FORGETTING: when the last carrier of abstraction A dies, A is deleted from the library and every abstraction whose body references A is recursively invalidated. The tribe's programs that relied on it start failing tonight. This is a dark age with a stack trace. (b) TRADE: when agents of two tribes co-locate peacefully, a `(trade fn fn)` program can execute, transplanting abstraction bodies — but only successfully if the receiving tribe already knows the full dependency closure, otherwise the gift is inert and the receiving tribe must first derive the missing layers. Technology transfer requires the whole stack, which is both realistic and dramatic. (c) RAID: a raid steals carriers (captives) rather than goods, which is a horrible and correct model of how technology actually moved. (d) WRITING: `(inscribe fn surface)` is NOT in the base DSL. But `(mark site material)` is, and site-marks persist, and `(read site)` returns marks. Whether a tribe ever composes those into a durable, decodable encoding of a library entry is an open search problem for the system. If it does — if Stitch compresses a recurring mark-then-read pattern into a named abstraction that reconstitutes a lost library entry — that tribe has invented writing, its libraries stop being mortal, and the whole simulation changes character. That single event is the reason to build this.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Same L^d combinatorics as Emberlisp, times nine, PLUS crossover between lineages. Nine libraries searching different regions (because their biomes make different primitives cheap) and periodically recombining is a genetic algorithm whose genome is a DSL. Hybrid abstractions — an obsidian-edge technique composed with a delta binding technique — are reachable by neither parent lineage alone. That is combinatorial invention at the level of whole traditions rather than single programs. The honest strengthening: forgetting means the system does not monotonically fill in a space; it can lose regions and re-derive them by a different route, so the same 'invention' can be discovered three times with three different bodies, and the compressor will treat them as three different technologies because they are.

The ladder

Week 1: two tribes, forgetting turned on, and you watch a tribe lose fire because the two agents who knew it were the two who starved. That is a genuinely upsetting week-1 moment and it is trivially achievable. Rung 2: teaching programs get invented — the compressor finds that surviving tribes have a recurring pattern of `(teach x fn)` calls before winter, and names it. Pedagogy as technology, discovered. Rung 3: divergence. Nine tribes' libraries stop resembling each other; the volcanic tribe's whole edge stack routes through obsidian fracture, the delta tribe's through fired clay. Now trade has actual value. Rung 4: first successful cross-biome transplant, where the receiving tribe has to derive three missing dependency layers before a gifted abstraction becomes executable. You will see a tribe spend forty nights 'catching up'. Rung 5: writing, or a durable proxy for it — a mark-and-read composition that survives death. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: after writing, libraries stop being mortal and depth stops being bandwidth-limited, so the abstraction DAG's depth ceiling lifts and the tribes that have it out-invent the tribes that don't by a widening margin. A literacy divide, emergent, with a timestamp you can point at.

What kills you

Three layered pressures. Individual: calories and temperature, same as Emberlisp. Tribal: winter and predation cull tribes whose library lacks storage and defence; a tribe that drops below 3 members merges into a neighbour or dies out and its library dies with it. Inter-tribal: contact events resolve by comparing the executing programs' outcomes — a tribe with better tools wins raids, takes carriers, and grows. The selection unit that actually matters is the LIBRARY, and libraries compete by being learnable (shallow, teachable, robust to death) as much as by being powerful. That is a real and interesting tension: the deepest abstraction is the most powerful and the least transmissible, so there is genuine evolutionary pressure toward flattening and re-factoring the library for teachability. Watching a tribe refactor its own tech tree for pedagogy is the kind of thing you cannot author.

How the world changes

The map moves: sea level shifts on a long cycle, connecting and severing islands, which switches tribes between isolation (divergence, novel libraries) and contact (convergence, transplant). Storms and blights hit specific biomes. And there is a slow one worth building: abandoned settlements leave marks and works behind, so a migrating tribe can encounter another tribe's ruins and attempt to decode them — archaeology as a search problem, where the goal predicate is 'reconstruct an abstraction whose behaviour matches these marks'. Human perturbation slots in naturally as a tenth actor: web visitors to the public page are 'the Sea People', and each unique visitor deposits a small resource cache or a rumour (a partial abstraction body) at a random coastal site. Strangers become a genuine, cheap non-stationarity, and the visit log becomes a historical record.

How it knows something is new

Tuned for this idea's specific drama. (1) FORGETTING EVENTS: the last carrier of an abstraction dies — always push, with the abstraction's name, its age in nights, and the count of dependent entries it just invalidated. 'The Reed clan has forgotten TWIST-CORD (age 611 nights, 9 dependents orphaned)' is a devastating notification. (2) REDISCOVERY: a new abstraction is accepted whose behaviour (evaluation vector on the standard probe set) matches a previously-deleted one but whose body differs — convergent re-invention, and reporting the body diff is fascinating. (3) TRANSPLANT SUCCESS: first time an abstraction executes in a tribe that did not invent it. (4) DIVERGENCE INDEX: pairwise Jaccard over tribes' abstraction behaviour-signatures, tracked nightly; push on a changepoint (the moment the archipelago's traditions split or merge). (5) The Emberlisp signals (first-ever event bloom filter, MDL shock, depth increase) all still apply, scored per-tribe. Sonification is the passive companion: a generative radio where each tribe is a voice in a nine-voice drone, pitch mapped to library depth, timbre to biome, volume to population — and when a tribe forgets something, a voice drops a note. You can leave it playing and hear a dark age happen from the next room.

On your phone

Nine tiles in a 3x3 grid, one per tribe, each showing name, population, library size, and a single colour bar for library health (fraction of entries with more than one carrier — the redundancy of their knowledge, which is the real vital sign). Under the grid: tonight's headline events in one line each, colour-coded — green for invented, blue for taught/traded, red for FORGOTTEN. That is the 30-second read and it is genuinely readable at arm's length. Tap a tribe for its abstraction DAG with carrier counts on each node (you can see at a glance which technologies are one funeral away from extinction). Tap an event for the replay. A second tab is the comparative view: all nine DAGs overlaid, shared abstractions in the centre, divergent ones on the rim, which after a few months is a beautiful and legitimately informative picture.

Your hardware

Nine tribes is nine independent searches, which is the perfect shape for the 64GB machine: run each tribe's enumerative search in its own process pinned to a core or two, each with its own observational-equivalence bank (a few GB each at depth, which is exactly why the 64GB matters and why this would not fit on a 16GB machine). Stitch is called nine times per night and is still a rounding error. The GPU holds one Qwen3.5-4B Q4 instance shared by all nine tribes for naming and chronicle — batch the nine naming requests into one nightly pass. Sonification is WebAudio in the browser, zero cost. Nothing here needs training. Determinism discipline is doubly important because inter-tribal outcomes must not depend on which process finished first: give every tribe an identical node budget per night, resolve contact events in a fixed tribe-id order, and seed each tribe's RNG from (world_seed, tribe_id, night) so the entire nine-way history replays bit-identically on a cold laptop.

What you have to do

Read the morning grid. The one thing worth doing occasionally, maybe fortnightly, is seeding a tenth tribe from a saved library snapshot to see whether an old tradition can survive in the current world — thirty seconds of typing, and it produces a week of interesting outcomes. The public page needs no maintenance once the visitor-as-Sea-People hook is in. Genuine chore, and it is small: when a tribe goes extinct you may want to decide whether to reseed the slot or let the map depopulate; automate it with a rule and forget it.

First weekend

If you have Emberlisp's substrate, this is one weekend on top. Saturday: `knows` table, teaching primitive, and the deletion cascade when the last carrier dies. Two tribes only. Sunday: run it overnight Saturday and spend Sunday building the nine-tile phone view and the red FORGOTTEN event line. If you are starting from scratch, budget two weekends and cut teaching fidelity to a constant for v1. The delight moment is very reliable here and it arrives fast: the first time you get a red line saying a tribe lost a technology it had for hundreds of nights, the sim stops feeling like a program.

Six months in

Nine traditions with visibly different shapes, a documented history of at least a few dark ages and re-derivations, and — if the mark/read composition ever lands — a literate tribe pulling away from illiterate neighbours. The comparative DAG view becomes the artifact you show people. What keeps it alive at six months is that forgetting makes the history non-monotonic: unlike a tech tree, this world can go backwards, so there is never a state where everything is unlocked and nothing is at stake.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Convergence is the enemy. Nine tribes under identical physics tend to find the same abstractions, and after a couple of months the diversity can become cosmetic — the same technology with nine different generated names. The biome-differentiated material abundance is the main defence and you should push it harder than feels natural (make biomes genuinely incompatible, not merely differently weighted). Second, the writing event may simply never happen. `(mark)` + `(read)` composing into a durable, decodable, dependency-preserving encoding is a hard synthesis problem and there is a real chance the search never finds it in six months; if you decide it must happen you will be tempted to author it, and the moment you author it you have a tech tree again. My honest recommendation is to leave it undiscovered and let it be a genuine open question about your own world. Third, nine parallel searches means one-ninth the node budget per tribe versus Emberlisp, so each individual tribe's library grows noticeably slower — this idea buys drama with depth. Fourth, the SQLite contention and determinism-under-parallelism problems are the kind of bug that eats a whole weekend and produces no visible progress.

Stack

Everything from Emberlisp plus: multiprocessing with one process per tribe and a single SQLite writer process (WAL mode, or hand writes to one coordinator to avoid lock contention — nine writers on SQLite will bite you, plan for it on day one). Tables gain `knows(agent, abstraction, fidelity)`, `tribes`, `contacts`, `transplants`, `forgettings`. The behaviour-signature for rediscovery detection is just the evaluation vector on a fixed probe set of 64 world-states, stored as a blob and indexed. Sonification via a small WebAudio synth in the PWA driven by the same nightly JSON — no audio generated server-side, which keeps it free.

Verified

Verified: iterated learning as the standard formalism for cultural transmission, with recent work reporting a per-generation communication bandwidth constraint on cultural accumulation and a threshold effect around 8-10 bits per channel below which cumulative learning fails to beat singleton copying (summarised at emergentmind.com/topics/iterated-learning-model) — that result is the direct justification for making teaching fidelity fall in abstraction depth, and it predicts your tribes will face real pressure to refactor deep libraries into shallow teachable ones. 'Artificial Generational Intelligence: Cultural Accumulation in Reinforcement Learning' (2024) establishes that performance reliably increases across generations of agents that pass advice forward. 'Mutual benefits of social learning and algorithmic mediation for cumulative culture' (arxiv.org/pdf/2410.00780, PMC11978438) models per-timestep acquisition of a cultural trait by either copying or innovation — close to the copy-or-search choice each agent makes here. 'High Selection Pressure Promotes Increase in Cumulative Adaptive Culture' (PMC3906051) supports tying invention rate to survival pressure rather than to slack. Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch) is cheap enough to call nine times a night without thinking about it. On the parallel-SQLite point: WAL mode plus a single writer is the well-trodden answer and worth setting up before you have nine processes.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Shallower than Emberlisp by construction and they half-admit it: nine tribes on one machine is ~1/9 the node budget each, on top of an enumerator that was already the bottleneck, and forgetting actively destroys accumulated depth. Expect per-tribe library depth 3-4 and 20-40 live abstractions, plateauing at week 3-6, with the aggregate looking busier than any individual tribe is deep. Convergence, which they name as the enemy, is worse than stated: contact plus trade plus raid are all homogenising forces, and nine tribes under one shared rule set will hold maybe 6-10 genuinely distinct abstractions between them by month two, wearing nine different generated names. The headline rung (writing) does not have a ceiling — it has a floor it never reaches; see below.

Is the discovery real?

Low for the tech itself, and the forgetting/rediscovery machinery is the most genuinely unauthored dynamic in the whole document — a non-monotonic history really cannot be faked with a tech tree, and 'rediscovered with a different body, same behaviour signature' is a real, computable, unspoofable result. But the risk is concentrated and acute at exactly the rung they are selling: WRITING. `(mark site material)` + `(read site)` composing into a durable, decodable, dependency-preserving encoding of a library entry is not a hard search problem, it is an out-of-horizon one. There is no task predicate that rewards it: the payoff of writing accrues to a DIFFERENT AGENT AFTER THE INVENTOR IS DEAD, and a per-night node budget searching against survival predicates has no credit-assignment path to a multi-generational return. On top of that the program itself — an encoder over a 14-symbol alphabet plus a parser that reconstitutes an s-expression — is deeper than anything else in the ladder by a wide margin. It will not happen in six months, or six years. Their own honest_ceiling says 'if you author it you have a tech tree again', which is correct and is precisely the trap this design walks toward. Score the idea on the rungs below it, because rung 5 is fiction.

Compute honesty

GPU: honest (one shared 4B, nine naming requests batched nightly — genuinely cheap). CPU/RAM: nine processes each with 'a few GB' of bank is the claim, and the memory arithmetic is fine on 64GB, but again the constraint is evaluation throughput, not residency, and it is now divided nine ways. Also under-costed: the deletion cascade. When the last carrier of A dies, every abstraction transitively referencing A is invalidated and every stored program re-inlines — that is a corpus-wide rewrite plus a forced re-run of Stitch, and on a bad night several tribes do it at once. Budget for it. The SQLite multi-writer warning is correct and they should be believed; WAL plus a single writer process, day one.

Nonsense risk

Moderate — lower than Emberlisp on abstractions that survive, because teachability and carrier count act as a second filter that MDL junk tends to fail (nobody bothers teaching a coincidence), and higher on the chronicle, because a red FORGOTTEN line for an abstraction that never meant anything reads exactly like a tragedy. The rediscovery detector (same behaviour signature, different body) is the one signal in this document that cannot be fooled by a naming model, and it should be the centrepiece rather than a bullet.

Claims to re-check

(1) The '8-10 bits per channel threshold below which cumulative learning fails to beat singleton copying' is the weakest citation in the set. It traces to arXiv 2511.18220 ('Lossy communication constrains iterated learning', Nov 2025) via an AI-generated emergentmind topic page, and the summary states the inequality in the OPPOSITE direction from the pitch ('below ~8-10 bits/channel, cumulative learning exceeds singleton copying'). Read the paper, not the topic page. Either way it is a rate-distortion result about BITS on a Bayesian chain, and it licenses nothing about making teaching fidelity fall in DAG DEPTH — depth is not bits, and the substitution is a design choice dressed as a finding. (2) 'Artificial Generational Intelligence' (Cook et al., NeurIPS 2024) is real and correctly characterised. (3) RAF/iterated-learning framing aside, the claim that biome-differentiated abundance defends against convergence is asserted, not supported; nine tribes sharing 12 physics rules converge in behaviour signature even when their materials differ, because the abstractions that matter are about leverage, containment and sequencing, not about which rock. (4) 'Stitch nine times a night is a rounding error' — true for compression, false once you count the re-inline cascades it triggers.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Give marks an INTRAGENERATIONAL payoff so the search can actually find them. Make external marks readable as cheap working memory within a single agent's lifetime — e.g. an agent executing a deep abstraction whose dependencies it only partially knows can pay ticks to `read` a site-mark and skip part of the re-derivation cost, right now, in this program, scored by this night's budget. Writing then arrives by exaptation (a within-lifetime cost-saver that happens to outlive its author) instead of requiring the search to solve a multi-generational credit-assignment problem it structurally cannot see. This is also how writing actually arose — as tallies and receipts, not as archives — so it is the historically honest fix as well as the computationally possible one.

▸

Glyphworks

Discovered abstractions do not just enter the library — they can be BUILT INTO THE LAND, becoming place-bound primitives that are cheap only where they stand. The tribe's own past inventions physically reshape which programs are affordable, so the task distribution is generated by the civilization itself rather than by you, and the map slowly becomes a readable technosphere of kilns, weirs, and ruins.

Ceilinged8.4
The world

A river valley of ~400 sites with elevation, flow, and a substrate identical in spirit to Emberlisp (materials as property records, physical rules over them, a typed survival lisp). The one structural difference is that SITES CAN HOLD WORKS. A works is a built object that carries a program: it exposes a primitive `(use-works site args...)` whose semantics are a stored abstraction body and whose interpreter-step cost is a fraction (say 1/8) of executing that body by hand. Works have material requirements, maintenance costs, a decay rate, and destructibility (flood, fire, raid). Works can reach physical regimes bare hands cannot: a hearth works can hold a temperature the `heat` primitive alone never sustains long enough, which means a works genuinely extends the reachable state space of the physics rather than merely making things cheaper. Adjacent works compose: a program at a site can reference the works there AND at neighbouring sites in one call, which is how a workshop, then a village, then something like a factory falls out without any of those words existing in the code.

How invention actually happens

Wake-sleep as in Emberlisp, with a fourth phase, SLEEP-B (building), inserted after abstraction. After Stitch returns accepted abstractions, each is tested for STATIONARITY — does its body operate on a fixed site, and is its interpreter-step cost above a threshold? Stationary expensive abstractions become WORKS BLUEPRINTS: a new task automatically enters the distribution, 'construct works W at site S', with material costs derived from the abstraction's body (an abstraction that references heating a lot needs refractory material; one that references water needs an excavation). Once built, the works installs `(use-works S)` into the DSL as a place-indexed primitive, and — this is the crux — the enumerator's cost model changes at that site. Programs that were over budget become in-budget. New solutions appear. Those new solutions go into the next Stitch pass. Those abstractions become new works. The loop is closed and the world is now the thing that grows the search space. Two extra mechanisms make it rich: DECOMPILATION (a ruined works can be read by a program, recovering a partial abstraction body — archaeology as synthesis-from-partial-evidence), and WORKS COMPOSITION (an abstraction whose body calls two adjacent works can itself become a works, giving you multi-stage installations emergently).

Why it doesn't bottom out

This is the strongest unboundedness claim of the six, and the reason is that it does not rest only on program-space combinatorics. In Emberlisp, abstractions make things CHEAPER; the reachable state space is fixed by the physics. Here, works make things POSSIBLE — a sustained-temperature works reaches a region of the material property space that no hand-executed program can reach at any budget, and the composites produced there have property vectors that then unlock further physics. That is Kauffman's adjacent possible with a mechanism: each realised technology expands the boundary of what is realisable next, and the boundary is computed, not enumerated by a designer. Add place-indexing and the DSL is no longer one grammar but a family of grammars indexed by location, so the same tribe has different affordances in different parts of its own valley and geography becomes a variable in invention. Honest bound: works can only compose the physics rules you wrote, so the reachable regime is still ultimately capped by the rule set — but because works can stack (a works built out of materials only another works could make), the depth of that stacking is what carries you, and stacking depth is limited by material property dynamic range rather than by an authored list.

The ladder

Week 1: hand-executed programs only, plus one hardcoded works type to prove the cost-model rewiring works. You watch a search that failed at 10M nodes succeed at 200k nodes because a works exists. Rung 2 (~night 50): the first EMERGENT works — an abstraction the compressor found, judged stationary, built by the tribe, and now standing on the map with a generated name. Your map has a building on it that you did not design. Rung 3: works that only work together; the enumerator starts preferring sites with two adjacent works, and agents cluster. A settlement forms for a computational reason. Rung 4 (~night 200): a works built from a material that only another works can produce — second-order infrastructure, which is the real threshold between tools and industry. Rung 5: catastrophe and archaeology. A flood destroys the lower works; the tribe's programs break; a later generation decompiles the ruins and rebuilds along a different route, and the DAG now has a visible scar. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: a works whose stored abstraction is itself a search procedure over the library — a place where thinking is cheap. The tribe builds a library, in both senses, and the sites near it out-invent everywhere else. That is not a stretch given higher-order primitives; it is what the cost model rewards.

What kills you

Survival as elsewhere, but with a distinctive pressure: MAINTENANCE. Works decay and cost upkeep in materials and agent-ticks, so a civilization can over-build and starve. That is a real and rarely-modelled selection pressure and it produces genuinely interesting behaviour — the pruning of infrastructure, the abandonment of a periphery, retreat to a defensible core. Works are also the thing rivals want, so they are what raids target. And because works are place-bound, geography selects: a tribe that built its whole stack in a floodplain is playing a different game than one on a terrace. Abstractions are still garbage-collected by use-it-or-lose-it; works are garbage-collected by the world.

How the world changes

The river is the clock: it meanders on a long cycle, floods on a stochastic one, and both rewrite which sites are viable, so the tribe's own infrastructure is periodically invalidated by geology. This is non-stationarity that is neither random nor scripted — it interacts with what was built. Longer term, the valley's mineral strata deplete: a works that consumes ore will exhaust its site, and the map's economic centre migrates. And because works persist after their builders are gone, a fresh tribe seeded a thousand nights later inherits a landscape of ruins it must decode — which is the most evocative version of 'the world changes' available, because the change is legible as history.

How it knows something is new

Spatially-aware signals. (1) FIRST WORKS OF A KIND: any works type built for the first time — always push, with its name, its blueprint's parent abstractions, and a small rendered map thumbnail of where it stands. (2) SECOND-ORDER CONSTRUCTION: a works whose material requirements can only be met by the output of another works — this is the industry threshold and it should be a loud, distinct notification. (3) REGIME BREACH: the sim tracks the maximum value achieved on each physics dimension (temperature, pressure, sustained-duration, composite complexity); pushing past a previous all-time maximum means the civilization has reached a physical regime it never has, which is a clean, computable definition of a technological first. (4) SETTLEMENT FORMATION: changepoint detection on the spatial entropy of agent positions — when agents stop being uniformly distributed, a place has become a place. (5) DECOMPILATION SUCCESS: a ruin read and rebuilt, with a diff between the original abstraction body and the reconstruction. An e-ink frame on the wall showing today's technosphere map — works as glyphs, ruins in grey — is the right physical artifact for this one; refresh once a day at dawn.

On your phone

The map IS the check-in. A single rendered valley, works drawn as small distinct glyphs (the local model can even pick a unicode/emoji glyph per works type at naming time), ruins in grey, agents as dots, the river in blue. Above it, one line: `NIGHT 340 · POP 41 · WORKS 19 (+1) · LIB 112`. Below it, at most three event lines. You can read the state of a civilization from a map in about four seconds, which beats any dashboard. Tap a works to see its stored abstraction, its parents, its build date, its total lifetime call count, and how much the local cost model changed when it went up. Tap-and-hold for a time-lapse scrubber of the whole map history, which is the hour-long binge and is genuinely mesmerising.

Your hardware

CPU-bound as elsewhere, with one extra memory cost: the enumerator now needs a per-site cost model, so the observational-equivalence bank is effectively partitioned by location context. Do not materialise a bank per site — key programs by (evaluation vector, works-context signature) where the context signature is a small bitset of which works are in reach, and only branch the bank when contexts genuinely differ. This is the single most important performance decision in the design and getting it wrong will cost you 64GB fast. Naming and glyph-picking is one nightly batched call to Qwen3.5-4B Q4 on the GPU. Map rendering is client-side canvas; the e-ink frame is a Raspberry Pi Zero or an old tablet pulling the same PNG the PWA renders. Budget everything in node expansions and interpreter steps, never seconds, because works are a cost-model change and if your cost model has any wall-clock component the whole economy becomes thermally dependent.

What you have to do

Watch the map with coffee. Optional monthly nudge: add a physical rule that opens a new regime (a pressure dimension, a fermentation rule) and watch the works stack grow a storey. One real chore: the stationarity heuristic that decides which abstractions become works blueprints will need tuning a few times in the first month — too loose and the map fills with junk buildings, too tight and nothing gets built. Once tuned it is set-and-forget.

First weekend

Weekend one is honestly Emberlisp's weekend; this idea's distinctive part needs that substrate underneath. Given it: Saturday, the works table, the cost-model override at a site, and one hardcoded works to prove the loop; get a task that was unsolvable to become solvable purely because a building exists. Sunday, the stationarity heuristic and automatic blueprint tasks, so the tribe builds something YOU did not pick, plus the map view. The delight moment is specific and strong: a building appears on your map with a name from the language model and a body from the compressor, and the search behaviour visibly changes around it.

Six months in

A valley with a hundred-odd works in visible clusters, a couple of abandoned districts, a river that has moved, and a technosphere map that reads like a real historical landscape. The time-lapse of six months of map history is the single most shareable artifact of any of these six ideas. Substantively, the second-order and third-order works stacks are what keep arriving — the invention rate does not fall off as fast as Emberlisp's because the world keeps expanding its own affordance surface.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The engineering is the hardest of the six and I want to be blunt about it: place-indexed enumeration with context-partitioned observational equivalence is a genuinely tricky piece of systems work, and if you get the context signature wrong you either blow memory or silently share banks across incompatible contexts and get wrong results that are very hard to debug. Expect the first month to be the enumerator, not the watching. Second, works can trap the civilization in a local optimum — cheap infrastructure makes the enumerator lazy, it stops exploring expensive-but-better routes, and the tribe ossifies. This actually happens in real economies and it is interesting the first time and tedious the fifth; the flood cycle is your designed antidote and you will need to tune its frequency. Third, the underlying physics rule set still caps the works stack; if your rules only support three regimes you get three storeys and then it is over. Realistically the interesting-new-kind rate holds up for two to three months here versus four to ten weeks for Emberlisp — better, but not forever, and the fix is still 'add physics'.

Stack

Emberlisp's stack plus: a `works(id, site, abstraction_id, built_tick, decay, upkeep)` table and a `site_context(site, works_bitset)` index; the enumerator takes a context parameter and consults a per-context cost table. Rendering is canvas 2D with a fixed tile atlas plus generated glyphs; the daily PNG for the e-ink frame comes from the same code path via headless Chromium or a small Pillow renderer. Everything else — Stitch, SQLite, llama.cpp with GBNF, ntfy, Tailscale Serve, GitHub Pages — is unchanged.

Verified

Verified conceptual grounding: niche construction and the adjacent possible are the right frames, and the specific formal link is 'Emergence of autocatalytic sets in a simple model of technological evolution' (arxiv.org/pdf/2204.01059, published in J Evol Econ 2023, link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00191-023-00838-2) which combines Kauffman-style TAP (theory of the adjacent possible) combinatorial technology models with RAF autocatalytic-set detection and finds that product transformation networks from combinatorial models have a high probability of containing self-sustaining sets — that is exactly the 'works that make the materials for other works' threshold this design detects. W. Brian Arthur's 'The Nature of Technology' (technology as combination) is the informal backbone. On the engine: Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, POPL 2023) is unchanged and still the compressor; the place-indexed cost model is a modification to the search, not to Stitch, since Stitch only ever sees s-expression strings. For the equality-modulo-rewrites variant of abstraction discovery — useful if you want works to be recognised as equivalent despite different bodies — babble (e-graphs + anti-unification, par.nsf.gov/biblio/10401739) and egglog-python (github.com/egraphs-good/egglog-python, equality saturation with a typed Python API) are the live options; egglog is actively maintained in 2026 and has real Python bindings via PyO3.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The best real ceiling of the six, and for the right reason: works change what is POSSIBLE, not just what is cheap, so the reachable region of the physics genuinely grows with the built environment. But it grows in STOREYS, and you author how many storeys exist. Each 'new regime' (sustained temperature, then pressure, then whatever) is one authored rule-guard relaxation; with ~12 rules you get roughly 3-5 qualitative storeys, and each storey takes a few weeks to climb. Expect genuinely new KINDS for 6-10 weeks (better than Emberlisp's 3-6, worse than the pitch's 2-3 months), after which the map keeps filling with more works of already-known types — quantitative sprawl that photographs beautifully and means nothing. Second ceiling, arriving earlier: the context-partitioned observational-equivalence bank. Works are built and decay, so the works-context signature at a site MUTATES over time, which invalidates bank entries keyed to it. They present this as a static partitioning problem; it is actually a cache-coherence problem, and the first month is the enumerator, exactly as they say, possibly the first two.

Is the discovery real?

Lower than Emberlisp on the tech itself, because works must be built, maintained, and pay for themselves — an economic filter MDL alone does not have. But there are two authored seams and they are load-bearing. (1) The STATIONARITY HEURISTIC that decides which abstractions become blueprints is a hand-written classifier standing exactly where the designer's taste re-enters, and 'you will need to tune it a few times in the first month' means 'you will tune it until it promotes the buildings you were hoping for'. (2) Worse: 'material costs derived from the abstraction's body (references heating -> needs refractory material; references water -> needs excavation)' is a hand-authored mapping from program syntax to build costs. That table IS a recipe book. It is small and it is not a tech tree, but it is the point where you get to decide what a kiln costs, and therefore which stacks are affordable.

Compute honesty

GPU: honest (nightly batched 4B for names and glyphs, ~4GB). CPU/RAM: the most likely of the six to actually blow 64GB, and they say so. Their own warning — key by (eval vector, works-context bitset) and branch only when contexts differ — is correct and insufficient, because it does not address invalidation on build/decay. Concretely: adopt an epoch counter per site and treat bank entries as valid only within an epoch, or accept silent cross-context contamination that produces wrong search results which are, as they note, extremely hard to debug. Also note the per-site cost model multiplies enumerator work by the number of distinct contexts in reach, so the throughput problem from Emberlisp is not merely inherited, it is multiplied.

Nonsense risk

Lowest of the library-learning trio. A works has to be built out of real materials, maintained, and called enough to justify itself, so an MDL coincidence that gets promoted to a blueprint dies visibly rather than sitting in a list wearing a name. The map view also makes junk legible in a way a DAG does not — a field of never-used buildings is self-evidently a bug, whereas a list of 90 named abstractions looks like success. That is a real design virtue and worth stating: THIS IDEA'S UI IS ITS BEST BULLSHIT DETECTOR.

Claims to re-check

(1) 'The strongest unboundedness claim of the six' — true relative to 1, 2 and 4, but the Kauffman adjacent-possible framing oversells it. TAP-style unboundedness requires that combination generate new PRIMITIVES; here combination generates new CACHED COMPOSITES over a fixed rule set, and the rule set is the boundary. The design's own honest_bound says this; the pitch language does not. (2) arXiv 2204.01059 / J Evol Econ 2023 is real and the RAF-in-technology-networks framing is legitimate, but that paper's high-probability result comes from combinatorial models with randomly assigned transformation networks; it says nothing about whether YOUR 12 hand-written rules contain a self-sustaining set, and it should not be cited as evidence that second-order works will appear. (3) babble (POPL 2023) and egglog-python are real and correctly described; e-graph equality-modulo-rewrites is genuinely the right tool for recognising works equivalence, but it is a second systems project bolted onto the hardest systems project in the set — do not attempt it in year one. (4) 'The interesting-new-kind rate holds up for two to three months versus four to ten weeks for Emberlisp' is directionally right but both numbers are optimistic by roughly a factor of two given the enumerator throughput problem inherited from Emberlisp.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Let a works instantiate a PARAMETERIZED RULE SCHEMA, not just cache an abstraction body. Rewrite the 12 physics rules as ~6 schemas with open constants (a threshold, a duration, a coupling coefficient, a participating material class); a works then declares a schema instance with constants it can physically sustain, and those instances are locally active. Now the built environment extends the RULE SET rather than reaching further along a fixed one, the storey count stops being something you authored, and stacking depth becomes limited by material dynamic range — which is the thing the pitch claims and the current mechanism does not deliver. Bonus: it deletes the authored syntax-to-build-cost table, because a schema instance's cost falls out of the constants it has to hold.

▸

The Slow Kiln

Instead of authoring materials, you author a dozen reaction RULES over a combinatorial substance space — so nobody, including you, has a list of what can be made. Programs are process recipes, the compressor names the recurring fragments (that is how 'smelting' and 'quenching' get invented), and a RAF detector watches for the night a set of processes becomes self-sustaining, which is the moment a civilization crosses from craft into industry.

Ceilinged
The world

A settlement on a mineral-rich slope. The substrate is an ARTIFICIAL CHEMISTRY, not a list of nouns. A substance is a record: a composition multiset over 12 elemental tokens (up to size ~8), a structure tag from a small set (crystalline, amorphous, fibrous, colloidal, gaseous, molten), and derived properties computed by fixed functions of composition and structure — hardness, melting point, toughness, density, reactivity, opacity, calorific value. You author roughly 15 REACTION RULES, not recipes: oxidation, carbon reduction, eutectic melting of mixtures, precipitation from solution, polymerisation, fermentation by a biotic agent, calcination, fluxing, quench-vs-anneal structural divergence, and so on. Each rule is a guarded transformation with conditions on temperature, duration, and the presence of a catalyst substance. The environment supplies a handful of raw feedstocks (ores, clays, plant matter, water, air) at sites. Agents have calories and temperature and die; a civilization needs substances with particular property thresholds to survive winter and to eat.

How invention actually happens

The DSL is a process language: `(hold X temp duration)`, `(mix A B)`, `(quench X medium)`, `(grind X)`, `(filter X)`, `(seal X vessel)`, `(wait X n)`, plus control flow. A program is a recipe; running it against the rule engine deterministically yields a substance (often a useless one). Wake: enumerative search for programs producing substances that satisfy a survival predicate ('a substance with hardness above H and toughness above T', 'a substance with calorific value above C that is not toxic'), the predicates coming from world needs, not from a materials wishlist. Sleep-G: Stitch over the corpus of successful recipes finds the fragments that recur across many products — and those fragments are exactly the named unit processes of real metallurgy and chemistry: a reduce step, a flux step, a temper step. The compressor discovers process engineering. The second engine, and the reason to build this one, is RAF DETECTION. Maintain the reaction network as a directed hypergraph (substances as nodes, executed process-abstractions as hyperedges from input multisets to output multisets) and run a RAF (Reflexively Autocatalytic and Food-generated) algorithm over it nightly: find the maximal subset of processes such that every process's inputs are either environmental feedstock or produced by another process in the subset, AND every process's required catalysts/tools are produced within the subset. When a non-trivial RAF first appears, an industry has bootstrapped itself — the settlement can now make the things it needs to make the things it needs, from dirt. That is a computable, non-arbitrary, genuinely surprising threshold event, and it is the best push notification in this entire document.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The substance space is combinatorial and enormous: multisets of size up to 8 over 12 elements crossed with 6 structure tags is on the order of 10^7-10^8 distinct substances before you count process history effects. The REACHABLE subset is gated by achievable temperature, duration, and catalyst availability — all of which are themselves products of earlier discoveries, so the reachable frontier expands as a consequence of what has been made. You did not write the product list; you wrote the rules, and the rules generate a space neither of you has seen. On top of that sits the usual L^d program-space growth over the process DSL. The compression argument that matters here: because properties are computed functions of composition, the system can produce substances with property combinations you never anticipated, and the survival predicates will select the useful ones without you having to judge. This is the idea where you are most likely to be genuinely surprised by an OBJECT rather than by a program.

The ladder

Week 1: the rule engine plus enumerative search over a 6-primitive process DSL against one predicate ('produce something with calorific value above C'). The tribe discovers charcoal-analogues. Rung 2: Stitch names its first unit process — a hold-at-temperature fragment that appears in forty recipes. You now have a word for 'roasting' that you did not write. Rung 3 (~night 60): reduction. A recipe that mixes an oxide feedstock with the tribe's own charcoal-analogue at temperature yields a metal-analogue with hardness nothing else has. Tools follow immediately because the survival predicates were already asking for hardness. Rung 4: quench-vs-anneal divergence — the same composition with two structure tags and wildly different toughness. The compressor finds both branches and names them separately, and the tribe has heat treatment. Rung 5 (~night 150-300): the first RAF. Some set like {char, flux, reduce, cast} closes on itself. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: a second, disjoint RAF using different feedstocks appears, and the two compete for site resources — two industries, endogenously defined, in economic conflict. Beyond that, catalysis by a produced substance means a process can make itself cheaper, and you get the runaway that the RAF literature predicts.

What kills you

Survival predicates are the fitness function and they are property thresholds, never named goods: winter needs a substance with insulation above X in quantity; hunting needs hardness and toughness above thresholds simultaneously (which is the whole difficulty of metallurgy, honestly modelled); storage needs impermeability and non-toxicity; illness pressure needs an antimicrobial property. Recipes compete on interpreter-step cost, feedstock scarcity, and yield. Processes compete on how many products they appear in — which is precisely the MDL objective, so the compressor and the economy are optimising the same thing, which is elegant. Failure mode with teeth: a settlement that specialises on a feedstock and then exhausts the deposit must re-derive its entire stack on a different chemistry.

How the world changes

Deposits deplete and are non-uniform, so the accessible feedstock vector drifts and the settlement must migrate or re-derive. A cheap and very effective second axis: run several settlements on slopes with DIFFERENT elemental abundance, so their chemistries diverge — a tin-poor valley never finds bronze-analogues and goes somewhere else entirely. Slower still, and worth building: perturb one rule's threshold constants per world (a world where carbon reduction needs 200 degrees more) and you get chemically distinct histories from identical code. Weather affects achievable temperature, which affects which regimes are open in winter versus summer, which makes the seasonal cycle chemically meaningful.

How it knows something is new

This idea has the best natural signals of the six because chemistry gives you objective firsts. (1) FIRST RAF, and every subsequent RAF expansion — the closure event, reported with the member processes and the feedstocks it eats. (2) PROPERTY RECORD: any substance that sets an all-time maximum on a property dimension, or that occupies a previously-empty cell in a coarse 2D property grid (hardness x toughness, say) — this is MAP-Elites-style illumination of substance space used as a novelty measure, and it directly answers 'is this genuinely new or just another sludge'. (3) MDL shock on the process library, as elsewhere. (4) CATALYTIC CLOSURE: a substance that catalyses a process that produces it. (5) A boredom guard specific to this idea: track the rate of new substances that are USED (appear in an accepted survival solution) versus merely produced; if the used-rate collapses while the produced-rate stays high, the system is making sludge and should be told to push exploration toward empty property-grid cells. Sonification works well here — map the property grid to a stereo field and each new substance to a struck tone, so a productive night sounds like rain on a roof and a RAF closure sounds like a chord.

On your phone

The headline object is the PROPERTY GRID: a small heatmap, hardness on one axis, toughness on the other, cells shaded by how recently something landed there, with tonight's new substances as bright dots. It is instantly legible and it shows progress as territory. Above it: `NIGHT 188 · SUBSTANCES 2,417 (+31, 4 useful) · PROCESSES 22 · RAF: yes (5 members)`. That RAF line is the one you check. Below: named new processes with docs. The binge view is the reaction hypergraph — feedstocks on the left, everything derived flowing right, RAF members highlighted in a cycle — plus a substance browser where you can tap any of the thousands of things your world has made and see its composition, its properties, the shortest recipe, and whether anything ever used it.

Your hardware

Chemistry is cheap: rule application is a few multiset operations, and you can evaluate millions of recipes per minute per core in plain Python if you intern substances by a canonical hash and memoise reaction results aggressively (this is the Infinite Craft cache lesson applied to a local rule engine — every pair-and-conditions tuple gets computed once, ever). 64GB is what lets you keep the entire substance table and the reaction memo resident, which is the difference between a fast sim and a slow one. RAF detection is a fixpoint iteration over the hypergraph, near-instant at these sizes. GPU: only the 4B namer, batched nightly, ~4GB. No training. Determinism is free here because chemistry is a pure function; budget search in node expansions and you get bit-identical replays regardless of laptop temperature.

What you have to do

Coffee and the property grid. The high-value nudge, which is genuinely fun and takes ten minutes, is ADDING A RULE — one new guarded transformation opens a whole region of substance space and the following week is visibly more productive. Do it monthly at most or you are just designing a tech tree slowly. Chore: none really, though you will want to skim the substance browser occasionally to check that the useful-fraction is not collapsing.

First weekend

Saturday: 12 elements, 15 rules, substance interning, and the reaction memo. Write no recipes. Then a brute-force random walk of 100k recipes to see what your rules actually generate — this alone is a delightful afternoon, because you will discover products you did not anticipate before any agent exists. Sunday: the survival predicate, enumerative recipe search, one Stitch pass, and the property grid rendered to a PNG on your phone. Delight moment: a named process and a substance in a grid cell you did not know was reachable. This is the fastest of the six to a genuine surprise, because the chemistry surprises you before the agents do.

Six months in

Tens of thousands of substances catalogued, a couple of dozen named unit processes forming a real process library, at least one and probably several RAFs, and a property grid with a distinctive filled shape that differs between your parallel settlements. What stays interesting is the frontier: there are always empty cells in the property grid and always a question of whether they are reachable. That is a genuinely open question about your own creation, six months in, which is the definition of what the builder is asking for.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Chemistry space is vast but FLAT, and this is the honest failure mode: the overwhelming majority of the 10^7 reachable substances are useless, and without discipline you get a beautiful mountain of sludge with a survival story bolted on. The property grid and the used-versus-produced ratio are the defences and you must build them early, not later. Second, rule-based chemistries have attractors — a handful of thermodynamically favoured products that everything collapses into — and you will notice by week three that 60% of recipes end in the same three substances. Fixing that means tuning rule guards, which is fiddly and is real work. Third, the RAF event may come early and then never recur meaningfully; one industry bootstrap is thrilling, and if the feedstock diversity is too low there is never a second. Fourth, be honest that the chemistry is a toy — it will produce things that are chemically absurd, and if you care about plausibility you will spend time on rules rather than on the civilization. My advice is to not care about plausibility at all and treat it as an alien chemistry, which also removes any temptation to author the outcomes you already know.

Stack

Python with substances interned as canonical tuples in a dict and reactions memoised (this is the whole performance story). Stitch over recipe s-expressions for the process library. RAF detection is ~50 lines: iteratively remove processes whose inputs or catalysts are not producible, until fixpoint. SQLite for substances, recipes, processes, RAF snapshots. The property grid and hypergraph render in canvas. llama.cpp + Qwen3.5-4B Q4 with GBNF for process naming — and this is where naming really earns its keep, because 'the process that holds a carbon-bearing mixture above 900 for 6 ticks' becoming `reduce-with-char` is what makes the chronicle readable. ntfy + Tailscale Serve + GitHub Pages as usual.

Verified

Verified: the RAF framework (Reflexively Autocatalytic and Food-generated sets, Hordijk & Steel) is well established and the specific paper that fuses it with combinatorial technology models is 'Emergence of autocatalytic sets in a simple model of technological evolution' (arxiv.org/pdf/2204.01059; J Evol Econ 2023, link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00191-023-00838-2), which reports that product transformation networks arising from combinatorial innovation models have a high probability of containing autocatalytic sets — i.e. the threshold event this design detects is a real, studied phenomenon and is likely to actually occur rather than being aspirational. 'Autocatalytic Sets Arising in a Combinatorial Model of Chemical Evolution' (PMC9695903, PubMed 36362857) gives the conditions under which they arise from random catalysis assignment. For the substrate style, Hiroki Sayama's Hash Chemistry line ('Non-Spatial Hash Chemistry as a Minimalistic Open-Ended Evolutionary System', arxiv.org/pdf/2404.18027, and 'Structural Cellular Hash Chemistry', arxiv.org/pdf/2412.12790) is the live precedent for computationally cheap open-ended artificial chemistry and is worth reading before you design your rules. On caching: the Infinite Craft architecture is exactly the pattern — the backend keeps every previously-combined pair in a cache and only novel pairs go to the expensive path (quuxplusone.github.io/blog/2024/02/08/infinite-craft/), with public datasets of 200k+ recipes existing; here the expensive path is your rule engine rather than an LLM, so caching makes it essentially free. Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch) handles the process-library compression unchanged, since recipes are just s-expressions.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Fastest to a genuine surprise, cheapest to run, and the most honest about its own failure mode — but the ceiling is nearer than the 10^7-substance figure implies, and it is in a different place than they think. The binding limit is not the size of composition space, it is the ARITY AND NONLINEARITY OF THE AUTHORED PROPERTY FUNCTIONS. Properties are fixed functions of composition and structure tag, so the property grid is a smooth image of a space you wrote down; filling it is hill-climbing a known landscape, and its Pareto frontier is determined the moment you write the functions. Expect the frontier to be essentially mapped in 3-6 weeks. The PROCESS library — the actual claimed tech tree — saturates harder and sooner: real metallurgy has a few dozen unit operations and so will this, so budget 20-40 named processes by week 6-10 and then permanent recombination. Also: reachability from ~5 feedstocks under 15 guarded rules is typically a few hundred to a few thousand substances, not tens of thousands, and most of those differ by one composition token. 'Tens of thousands of substances catalogued' at six months is off by roughly an order of magnitude unless the rules are far more generative than 15 guarded transformations usually are.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six, genuinely. Authoring rules rather than recipes is the correct move and it does produce objects the designer has not seen — the Saturday-afternoon 'brute-force 100k recipes and look at what came out' exercise is real discovery and it happens before any agent exists. Two caveats. First, the property functions are where taste re-enters invisibly: you choose hardness and toughness as axes because you know the metallurgy story you want, and then the survival predicates ask for hardness and toughness. Second, the RAF is at high risk of being an authored artifact rather than a discovery — with 15 rules and a handful of catalyst conditions, a self-sustaining set is usually either present from night one (char catalyses reduction which makes more char) or structurally impossible, and which one you get is decided by you when you write rule 7. A threshold event that fires once, early, and never again is a screenshot, not a mechanism.

Compute honesty

The only fully honest one in the set, and by a distance. Recipes are PURE functions of a substance record, so observational-equivalence dedup and memoisation are cheap in exactly the way DreamCoder's are and Emberlisp's are not — this is the single biggest architectural advantage in the document and it is barely mentioned. Interning substances by canonical hash plus memoising (rule, inputs, conditions) makes rule application effectively free after the first evaluation; millions of recipes per core-minute in plain Python is believable here in a way it is not anywhere else. RAF detection is a fixpoint over a hypergraph of a few thousand nodes: milliseconds. GPU: one 4B namer, ~4GB, batched nightly. Nothing needs the 8GB except the namer, and nothing needs a datacenter. Zero objections.

Nonsense risk

Split, and the split matters. SUBSTANCES: high — they concede the sludge problem and are right; worse, rule-based chemistries have thermodynamic attractors and you will find 60% of recipes terminating in three products by week three, which is real work to tune and is fiddly in a way that resembles content authoring. PROCESSES: LOW, and this is the idea's strongest claim. A recurring 'hold above T for D then quench' fragment is meaningful because the physics makes it meaningful, not because a language model named it — MDL and physical significance genuinely coincide when the corpus is process recipes over a reaction engine. Of the six ideas, this is the one where a compressed fragment is most likely to be a real thing rather than a syntactic coincidence.

Claims to re-check

(1) The RAF citation is the overreach. arXiv 2204.01059 / J Evol Econ 2023 finds high probability of autocatalytic sets in product transformation networks generated by COMBINATORIAL models with RANDOM catalysis assignment, and PMC9695903 gives conditions under random catalysis. Neither transfers to 15 hand-written guarded rules — the probability argument is over an ensemble you are not sampling from. Presenting 'the threshold event this design detects is likely to actually occur rather than being aspirational' as verified is not supported; it is exactly as aspirational as the writing event in idea 2, just cheaper to check. (2) '10^7-10^8 distinct substances' conflates the composition space with the REACHABLE set; report reachability from feedstock closure instead, and do it on the first Saturday, because that number decides whether this idea is interesting. (3) Sayama's Hash Chemistry papers (2404.18027, 2412.12790) are real and the right prior art, but note they achieve open-endedness through hash-based fitness with no property semantics at all — the opposite design choice from authored property functions, and worth confronting rather than citing. (4) The Infinite Craft caching analogy is apt and the blog post is real; the pattern is correct.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Stop authoring 15 rules and 7 property functions; author a GENERATIVE CORE of ~50 numbers instead — a per-element table of affinity, valence, mass, electronegativity-analogue and enthalpy contribution — and derive both the reaction guards and the property values from it by fixed formulas. Three payoffs at once: attractors become tunable by nudging numbers rather than by rewriting rule guards; adding a 13th element multiplies the reaction set instead of adding one recipe; and the chemistry becomes a MUTABLE GENOME, so the monthly 'add a rule' chore that this design depends on for longevity becomes an automatic axis (and composes directly with Thousand Dawns' outer loop). It also removes the smooth-authored-function problem — properties emerge from element interactions rather than from a landscape you already know the shape of.

▸

Thousand Dawns

Run thirty-two worlds at once, each with a mutated physics genome and its own tribe and its own library, and keep a MAP-Elites archive over WORLDS so compute flows to the ones still inventing. Then transplant libraries between worlds: a technology that survives transplant is a law, one that dies is a local trick — which gives you a computed, unauthored measure of how deep an invention actually is.

Not real depth
The world

A world is a genome: ~20 physics knobs (carrying cost, ignition threshold, melt thresholds, decay rates, day length, seasonal amplitude, predator pressure, resource abundance vector over 14 materials, water availability, terrain roughness) plus a terrain seed. Each world instantiates the Emberlisp substrate — property-record materials, ~12 physical rules, a typed survival lisp, one tribe, real death. Thirty-two of them run in parallel on the laptop, each cheap because each is a small grid with a few dozen agents. The knobs are not cosmetic: raising the ignition threshold past what percussion can deliver means FIRE IS IMPOSSIBLE IN THAT WORLD, and its tribe must find another way to be warm, or another way to eat, or die. Some worlds are unsurvivable and that is fine and informative.

How invention actually happens

Two nested searches. INNER: standard wake-sleep per world — enumerative synthesis over that world's library, Stitch compression into named abstractions, recognition model on fantasies. OUTER: MAP-Elites over world genomes. The archive is a grid indexed by behaviour descriptors computed from each world's HISTORY rather than its parameters: (library depth reached, number of physics dimensions the tribe's programs actually exploit, log time-to-first-abstraction-of-depth-3, count of distinct abstraction behaviour-signatures). Each cell holds the elite world for that behavioural niche, scored by an open-endedness metric rather than by survival — specifically, the rate of accepted novel abstractions over the last 200 nights. Every N nights: cull worlds whose invention rate has flatlined, and reseed those slots by mutating an elite's genome. Compute is thus continuously redirected toward worlds still producing. The third mechanism, which is the distinctive one, is TRANSPLANT TESTING: periodically inject a library entry (with its dependency closure) from world A into world B's tribe and record whether it executes, whether it helps, and whether it earns call-sites. Every abstraction accumulates a GENERALITY SCORE — the fraction of foreign worlds in which it remains useful. High-generality abstractions are laws of the substrate (leverage, containment, insulation, sequencing). Low-generality ones are parochial hacks that depended on one world's abundance draw. You did not label any of them; the archive computed it.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Three stacked sources. (1) Program space over each world's library: L^d, growing as before. (2) Physics space: 20 continuous knobs is a large space and MAP-Elites explores it adaptively rather than by grid search, so when a region of physics stops producing invention the archive moves. (3) The cross product: the same DSL primitive means something different under different physics, so an abstraction discovered in a high-gravity world can be transplanted into a low-ignition world and compose with ITS abstractions into hybrids neither lineage would reach. Crucially, this is the only one of the six ideas where the task distribution is refreshed STRUCTURALLY rather than by you: when a world saturates, you do not add physics by hand, the archive spawns a mutated world whose task distribution is different by construction. That is the honest answer to the DreamCoder plateau, and it is why this idea has the longest half-life.

The ladder

Week 1: four worlds, hardcoded genome differences, no archive — just four Emberlisp runs side by side and a page that compares their libraries. Already interesting, because the divergence is visible immediately. Rung 2: the MAP-Elites archive, cull-and-mutate, and the first time compute gets pulled off a dead world onto a live one automatically. Rung 3 (~week 3): the first transplant, and the first abstraction that FAILS to transplant — you learn something real about your own substrate the moment that happens. Rung 4: generality scores stabilise and you can sort the union of all libraries by universality; the top of that list is the substrate's genuine physics-level technology set, discovered by thirty-two independent civilizations, and reading it is a legitimate scientific pleasure. Rung 5 (~month 2): a world where fire is impossible invents something structurally different at the same depth — fermentation for calories, or insulation-by-composite for warmth — and the comparative chronicle writes itself. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: hybrid vigour — an abstraction that only exists because two transplanted foreign entries composed, achieving something no single-world lineage reached at any budget. And the endpoint that would genuinely astonish: the archive discovering a physics genome whose worlds reliably out-invent all others, i.e. your system finding what makes a universe generative.

What kills you

At the agent level, survival as always. At the world level, INVENTION RATE, not survival: a world where the tribe lives comfortably forever and stops inventing gets culled, which is the correct and slightly cruel selection pressure for this project, and it is directly borrowed from the open-endedness literature. At the abstraction level, generality across transplants. Three selection levels, all computed, none authored. The interaction between them is where the interest lives — comfortable worlds are boring, lethal worlds die before inventing, and the archive spends its time on the knife edge, which is exactly where you would want to be watching.

How the world changes

This idea IS the changing-worlds requirement, taken literally: worlds are born, mutate, and die on a schedule, and you can watch the population of universes turn over. Within a world, the Emberlisp non-stationarity (seasons, geology) still applies. And this is the natural home for human perturbation: the public page shows the archive, and anonymous visitors get one action per day — they can 'bless' a world (a small resource shower) or 'sunder' one (a drought), or vote a culled world back into the pool. Visitors become a slow, unpredictable environmental force acting at the level of universes, the guestbook becomes a record of divine intervention, and it gives the public artifact a genuine reason to exist. Anonymous, rate-limited, logged, and mechanically bounded so it can nudge but never break.

How it knows something is new

Archive-aware. (1) NEW NICHE FILLED: a world lands in a previously-empty MAP-Elites cell — a behavioural mode of civilization the system has never produced. Always push, with the world's genome diff from its parent. (2) TRANSPLANT SURPRISE: an abstraction that was useless in twelve worlds suddenly earns call-sites in the thirteenth — parochial tech turning out to be general is a great story and it is exactly what a generality score surfaces. (3) DIVERGENT SOLUTION: two worlds solve the SAME task predicate with abstractions whose behaviour signatures differ beyond a threshold — convergent problem, divergent technology, which is the comparative-history payoff. (4) ARCHIVE STAGNATION ALARM, the boredom detector as a first-class citizen: if no cell has been newly filled and no world's invention rate has exceeded its trailing baseline for K nights, the system tells you it is bored, widens genome mutation variance automatically, and only pings you if that fails too. (5) The per-world signals from Emberlisp, but scored against the ARCHIVE-WIDE distribution rather than the world's own, so a discovery only counts as new if thirty-one other civilizations have not already made it. That last point is what makes this the strongest novelty detector of the six — you have a population baseline, so 'genuinely new' is measurable rather than heuristic.

On your phone

The archive as a grid of tiles, one per world, each a tiny sparkline of library size with a colour for status (green inventing, amber plateaued, grey dead, white newly seeded). Thirty-two tiles fit on a phone screen and the whole state of your multiverse reads in five seconds. Header: `DAWN 44 · 32 WORLDS · 7 NICHES FILLED (+1) · 214 ABSTRACTIONS, 19 UNIVERSAL`. One headline card underneath for tonight's single most surprising event. Tap a world for its Emberlisp view (DAG, map, chronicle). Second tab, and this is the hour-long binge: the UNIVERSAL LIBRARY — every abstraction across all worlds sorted by generality score, each showing which worlds found it independently and how their bodies differ. That page is a comparative history of thirty-two civilizations and it gets better every week without you touching it.

Your hardware

This is the design that actually uses the 64GB and all the cores properly. Thirty-two small worlds at maybe 1-1.5GB of resident program bank each is a real fit for the machine, and it is the reason this idea would be impossible on a 16GB laptop. Run worlds as separate processes with a node budget each; allocate budget proportional to archive score, which is your compute-flows-to-interesting mechanism and costs nothing to implement. Stitch is called 32 times a night, still trivial. One shared Qwen3.5-4B Q4 instance on the GPU serving all naming requests in a nightly batch (~4GB, plenty of headroom). Absolutely no training, no finetuning; the recognition models are tiny per-world MLPs on CPU. Benchmark hygiene is CRITICAL here in a way it is not elsewhere: worlds compete for archive slots, so if fitness had any wall-clock component the thermal state of the laptop would decide which universes survive. Budget everything in node expansions, cost everything in interpreter steps, seed RNGs from (world_genome_hash, night), and the entire multiverse replays deterministically.

What you have to do

Read the tile grid with coffee; tap into whichever tile changed colour. Nudges: hand-author a world genome you are curious about and inject it into the archive (thirty seconds, high fun); pin a world so the culler cannot kill your favourite. One real chore, monthly at most: the behaviour descriptors that define the archive's axes will need one or two revisions as you learn what actually varies, and changing them resets the archive, so think before you do it.

First weekend

Only attempt this after Emberlisp exists — it is a multiplier on that substrate, not a from-scratch weekend. Given Emberlisp: Saturday, parameterise the physics as a genome vector, run four worlds with different genomes in four processes, build the tile grid. Sunday, transplant: copy an abstraction with its closure from world 1 into world 2, run a trial, record whether it earns call-sites. The delight moment here is unusually intellectual and unusually strong: you watch a technology fail to work in a different universe, and you realise you now have an experimental apparatus for your own physics.

Six months in

Hundreds of world-generations turned over, an archive with most niches filled and a documented map of which physics regimes produce generative civilizations, and a universal library where the top twenty entries are the genuine invariants of your substrate — found independently by dozens of tribes, and readable as a discovered periodic table of technology. This is the idea most likely to still be producing genuinely new things at six months, because it does not depend on you adding physics; it mutates its own.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The genome is finite: 20 knobs, and MAP-Elites will have illuminated the interesting corners within a few months. When that happens, novelty falls off across the whole archive at once rather than world by world, which is a starker plateau than Emberlisp's. Adding knobs is cheap but is a chore and each addition resets some archive structure. Second and more fundamental: the BASE DSL AND THE PHYSICS RULES ARE THE ONE THING THAT NEVER VARIES, and every technology in every world is ultimately a composition over those authored primitives. Transplantability only means anything because the base is shared. So the deepest honest statement about this whole family of ideas is that you author the alphabet and the system authors the literature — the literature is unbounded, the alphabet is not, and if the alphabet is impoverished the literature will be too. Spend your design effort on the base primitives, not on content. Third, per-world node budget is 1/32 of a single-world run, so each civilization is individually much shallower; you are trading depth for breadth, and if what you want is one tribe going far, this is the wrong idea. Fourth, the visitor-perturbation endpoint is the only piece of internet-facing surface in these designs and it needs rate limiting and bounded effects or someone will find it entertaining to break your multiverse.

Stack

Emberlisp's whole stack, times 32, orchestrated by a simple scheduler process. MAP-Elites is ~100 lines (a dict from discretised descriptor tuple to elite, with mutation by Gaussian perturbation of the genome vector). One SQLite per world plus one archive DB, which sidesteps the multi-writer contention problem entirely — a much better arrangement than The Long Teaching's shared DB. Transplant is a library-entry copy with dependency closure and a scored trial period. Frontend is the same PWA reading a single generated archive JSON; visitor actions go through a tiny node endpoint behind Tailscale Funnel or a Cloudflare Worker writing to a queue the sim drains at dawn.

Verified

Verified: MAP-Elites (Mouret & Clune) and Novelty Search with Local Competition are the two standard QD archive designs — MAP-Elites uses a static discretised mapping, novelty search an unstructured expanding archive; MAP-Elites supports both bounded and unbounded descriptor axes, which matters because library depth is unbounded (emergentmind.com/topics/map-elites-algorithm). ASAL, 'Automating the Search for Artificial Life with Foundation Models' (Kumar et al., Sakana AI + MIT + OpenAI + IDSIA + Ken Stanley, arxiv.org/abs/2412.17799, code at github.com/SakanaAI/asal, published in Artificial Life 31(3)) is the direct precedent for exactly this outer loop: searching a SPACE OF SIMULATIONS for temporally open-ended novelty and for illumination of a diverse space, demonstrated over Boids, Particle Life, Game of Life, Lenia and neural CAs — read this one before building the archive, it is the closest published thing to the outer loop here. 'Quality Diversity in the Amorphous Fortress' (arxiv.org/pdf/2312.02231) applies QD to evolving 0-player game complexity, which is nearly this project's genre. 'DejaQ: Open-Ended Evolution of Diverse, Learnable and Verifiable Problems' (arxiv.org/pdf/2601.01931) is recent work on evolving the problem distribution itself, which is the plateau fix. Inner loop unchanged: DreamCoder (PLDI 2021) + Stitch (POPL 2023, github.com/mlb2251/stitch, seconds on one CPU core, so 32 nightly calls are free). Hardware: 32 processes is the right shape for 64GB; the GPU only ever holds one Qwen3.5-4B Q4 (~3.4GB) for batched naming.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

The distinctive mechanism does not work as described, and the arithmetic is not close. MAP-Elites needs 10^4-10^6 evaluations to illuminate even a modest descriptor space; here ONE EVALUATION IS A CIVILIZATION RUN OF HUNDREDS OF SIMULATED NIGHTS. With 32 slots and a cull-and-reseed every N nights, you get on the order of 10-50 world-evaluations per month against a 20-dimensional continuous genome. That is not quality-diversity, it is random search with a leaderboard, and 'the archive discovers a physics genome whose worlds reliably out-invent all others' is unreachable by three to four orders of magnitude. Compounding it: the descriptors are computed from HISTORY, so each evaluation is expensive, noisy and non-i.i.d., which is the regime where MAP-Elites archives are known to fill with lucky seeds (elite inflation) — your archive will confidently report niches that a rerun would not reproduce. And per-world node budget is 1/32 of Emberlisp's, which was already the binding constraint, so each civilization stalls at depth 2-3 in the first fortnight. Ceiling: interesting for ~3 weeks (the four-worlds-side-by-side comparison), then 32 shallow libraries and a scoreboard that is mostly seed noise.

Is the discovery real?
