Their own honest_ceiling contains the correct diagnosis and then does not act on it: 'the base DSL and the physics rules are the one thing that never varies'. Twenty scalar knobs is not a physics space, it is a parameter sweep of ONE physics. Every technology in all 32 worlds is a composition over the same 28 primitives and the same 12 rules, which means the celebrated 'universal library sorted by generality' is largely a list of which compositions are robust to constant-tuning — real, computable, mildly interesting, but a much smaller claim than 'the discovered periodic table of technology'. The transplant test itself is the one part I would defend without reservation: it is cheap, it is honest, it produces an unauthored classification, and it is the single best measurement idea in the entire document. It just does not need MAP-Elites, or 32 worlds, to work.

Compute honesty

GPU: honest and trivial (one 4B, batched). CPU: this is where the pitch quietly overspends. 'Thirty-two small worlds at 1-1.5GB resident each' fits 64GB, fine — but a consumer laptop has ~8-16 usable cores, so 32 worlds is roughly HALF A CORE EACH, and the enumerator throughput problem from Emberlisp is now divided by 32 rather than merely being present. 'Allocate budget proportional to archive score' partially rescues this by starving dead worlds, but it also means most slots are near-frozen, which further reduces the effective evaluation count feeding the outer loop. The claim that this design 'actually uses the 64GB and all the cores properly' is backwards: it is the design most likely to be simultaneously RAM-comfortable and compute-starved. No datacenter or API bill is hidden here — the dishonesty is about what the available compute can accomplish, not about where it runs.

Nonsense risk

Moderate, with one specific hazard: the ARCHIVE-WIDE novelty baseline is genuinely the strongest anti-nonsense signal in the document — 'new only if 31 other civilizations have not found it' is a real population baseline and it is worth stealing for whichever idea gets built. But it only means something once the per-world libraries are deep enough to differ, and at 1/32 budget they are not. Early on, every world's library is the same six shallow abstractions, generality scores are all ~1.0, and the universal library reads as profound while containing nothing. Expect that state to persist for the first month and to be very easy to mistake for success.

Claims to re-check

(1) 'MAP-Elites supports both bounded and unbounded descriptor axes' — wrong as stated, and the citation is an AI-generated topic page. Canonical MAP-Elites requires a bounded, discretised grid; unbounded axes need CVT-MAP-Elites, expanding/unstructured archives, or novelty-search-with-local-competition. Since library depth is explicitly one of their axes and is unbounded, this is a real design bug, not a nitpick. (2) ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799) is real and IS published in Artificial Life 31(3) — verified, credit where due, and it is the right prior art. But note what ASAL actually searches: simulation parameters evaluated by a VLM over RENDERED FRAMES, with each evaluation costing seconds. The analogy breaks precisely on evaluation cost, which is the thing that kills the outer loop here. (3) DejaQ (arXiv 2601.01931, Röpke/Coward/Lupu/Foster/Rocktäschel/Foerster, Jan 2026) is real — verified — but it evolves synthetic MATH PROBLEMS against an LLM's learnability during RL training, with cheap verifiable evaluation. Citing it as 'the plateau fix' for a system whose problem instances cost a civilization-run each is an analogy, not a precedent. (4) 'Amorphous Fortress' (2312.02231) is real and genuinely the closest genre match. (5) 'This is the idea most likely to still be producing genuinely new things at six months' is the claim I would bet hardest against: it plateaus ACROSS THE WHOLE ARCHIVE AT ONCE, which they concede, and it gets there sooner than the single-world ideas because each world is shallower.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Put the ALPHABET in the genome, not the constants — and cut to 6-8 worlds. Make physics RULE SCHEMAS and base primitives mutable (add/remove/reparameterise a rule, add a primitive, change a type signature), so that different worlds genuinely have different languages rather than the same language with different thresholds. That does two things nothing else in this design does: it makes the transplant/generality test measure something deep (does this technology survive a change of PHYSICS, not a change of ignition threshold), and it means the outer loop can escape the 20-knob box that otherwise caps everything at a few months. Fewer worlds also gives each 4-5x the node budget, which is the difference between libraries that can differ and libraries that cannot. Accept that the result is no longer MAP-Elites — with ~30 evaluations a month it never was — and run it as an explicit, hand-read tournament of 8 physics variants instead.

▸

The Dreaming Stone

A single long-lived tribe consults an oracle — a local 4B model, grammar-constrained so it can only ever speak valid programs and machine-checkable goals in the tribe's own evolving language — and everything it says must survive execution in the world before it counts. Strangers on the internet write in a guestbook that the oracle formalises into the civilization's research agenda, so the tribe's future is literally set by prayers from people it will never meet.

Not real depth
The world

A single valley, the Emberlisp substrate (property-record materials, ~12 physical rules, typed survival lisp, permanent death), one tribe, running for months without reset. At the valley's centre is the Dreaming Stone: a site where an agent can spend ticks to 'dream', which in implementation means the tribe gets to consult the local language model. Dreaming is metered — a scarce in-world resource, so the tribe's LLM budget is itself part of the economy and a tribe that spends all its time dreaming starves. Around the stone, marks accumulate: the guestbook. Anonymous web visitors leave text; that text is visible in-world as inscriptions on the stone, and it is the raw material for the oracle's task proposals.

How invention actually happens

LILO with the world as the verifier, plus a task generator that is the honest fix for the DreamCoder plateau. Three grammar-constrained roles for the model, none of them trusted, all of them checked. (1) PROGRAM PROPOSER: given a task predicate and a rendered view of the current library (names + generated docs + type signatures), the model emits 8 candidate programs. It is decoded under a GBNF grammar AUTO-GENERATED FROM THE CURRENT DSL — regenerated every night as the library grows — so it is structurally incapable of emitting a syntactically invalid program or of calling a primitive that does not exist. Candidates are executed; failures are discarded silently; survivors are added to the corpus AND used to reweight the enumerative searcher's proposal distribution. The LLM is a proposal distribution, the world is ground truth, and nothing it hallucinates can enter the library. (2) ABSTRACTION PROPOSER: after Stitch's MDL pass, the model gets to suggest additional abstractions that are semantically meaningful but not maximally compressive — LILO's finding is that LLM-proposed, auto-documented abstractions produce more interpretable and more USABLE libraries than pure MDL. Each proposal must compile to base DSL and must earn call-sites within 20 nights or it is demoted. (3) TASK PROPOSER: this is the important one. Given the library docs, the current world state, and the guestbook, the model emits goal predicates — again grammar-constrained, so every proposed task is a machine-checkable predicate over world state, never prose. Proposals are filtered to those currently UNSOLVED but within a heuristic reach horizon (the Voyager auto-curriculum condition, plus DreamCoder's adjacent-possible framing), and scored later on whether they were eventually solved. A task proposer that only emits impossible or trivial goals gets its proposals downweighted. Optional fourth engine, the Ouroboros piece: weekly, take every verified (task, library-view, program) triple and QLoRA-finetune the 4B on its own wins; the new checkpoint must beat the old on a held-out set of last month's tasks measured in INTERPRETER STEPS TO SOLVE (deterministic, thermally invariant) or it is discarded.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Program space over a growing library is unbounded as in all of these, but the distinctive claim here is about the TASK distribution, which is where the other ideas plateau. An LLM reading a library of named, documented technologies will propose goals that reference those technologies, so the goal distribution grows with the library — a self-referential curriculum. When the tribe invents `bank-the-coals`, the oracle starts proposing tasks about carrying fire, storing fire, trading fire, hiding fire. That is a genuinely different plateau profile from pure MDL wake-sleep. Layered on top, the guestbook injects goals from outside the system entirely — a source of non-stationarity with no computational bound at all, because it is other people. Honest counterweight: the model is a 4B and its proposals are shallow; the grammar constrains syntax, not sense; and the reach-horizon filter is doing a lot of work. But the mechanism is real, it is the published LILO/Voyager finding, and it directly addresses the failure mode of ideas 1 through 4.

The ladder

Week 1: no LLM at all — Emberlisp's search plus one hardcoded task. Then bolt on the namer and get your first generated technology name. Rung 2: the GBNF grammar auto-generated from the DSL, and the program proposer; measure how often its candidates beat pure enumeration (they will, on deep tasks, and the measurement is the fun part). Rung 3: the task proposer, and the moment the tribe starts working on a goal that neither you nor the world's need-generator produced. Rung 4 (~week 4): the guestbook. Someone on the internet writes 'they should learn to bury their dead', the oracle formalises it into a checkable predicate, the tribe eventually satisfies it, and the chronicle records that a stranger's prayer was answered on night 312. That is a genuinely moving thing to build. Rung 5: the weekly QLoRA loop, with the held-out step-count eval as the gate — the oracle measurably gets better at its own world's language. Rung 6 / wildest plausible: the model's proposals become good enough that it starts suggesting REFACTORS — abstractions that do not compress the corpus but do make it teachable and composable, which is what a real engineer does and what MDL alone will never find. And the endpoint worth aiming at: the tribe develops a stable technical vocabulary that the model uses fluently, so the chronicle reads like a culture describing itself in its own invented words.

What kills you

Survival as always, plus three verifier gates that are the whole design philosophy: a program counts only if it executes and satisfies the predicate; an abstraction counts only if it earns call-sites within a window; a finetuned checkpoint counts only if it beats the incumbent on held-out interpreter-step cost. Nothing the model says is taken on faith at any level, which is what makes an LLM-in-the-loop design honest rather than a vibes machine. Dreaming is metered by an in-world resource so the oracle competes with food for the tribe's time — a nice pressure that makes consultation a strategic choice the search has to make.

How the world changes

Seasons and geology as in Emberlisp, but the primary non-stationarity here is HUMAN. The guestbook is a live external process: it changes when you post the link somewhere, it changes when a friend finds it, it goes quiet for a month and then a stranger writes something strange. That is genuinely unpredictable input that no amount of compute could have generated. Secondary: every ~4000 nights the valley resets with new terrain but the library, the oracle checkpoint, and the guestbook-scripture persist — so the tribe carries its technology and its religion into a new land, and you find out which parts of each still work.

How it knows something is new

(1) PRAYER ANSWERED: a guestbook-derived task predicate satisfied for the first time, reported with the original human text alongside the formalised predicate and the program that satisfied it. This is the flagship notification and it will be the one you screenshot. (2) The Emberlisp battery: first-ever event bloom filter, MDL shock, abstraction depth increase, long-unsolved-now-solved. (3) ORACLE SURPRISE: a program from the LLM proposer that the enumerative searcher had not found within its full node budget — i.e. the model contributed something search could not reach, which is the measurement that tells you whether the LLM is earning its keep. Track this as a running percentage; it is the honest metric for the whole hybrid design. (4) VOCABULARY DRIFT: embed the generated abstraction names nightly with a small local embedder and detect when the library's naming cluster structure shifts — the culture's technical vocabulary reorganising. (5) A hard rule for the feed: at most three pushes per day, ranked by surprise, everything else archived. Physical artifact fits this idea best of the six — a thermal receipt printer on the desk printing the dawn chronicle each morning, one paper strip: the date, the three events, any answered prayer with the stranger's words quoted. Sub-forty-pound printer, a few lines of ESC/POS over USB, and the check-in becomes a physical ritual with no screen at all.

On your phone

Deliberately quiet and text-forward, because this idea's payload is language. Top: `NIGHT 312 · POP 19 · LIB 94 · ORACLE v7 (won 31% of tasks search missed)`. Then, if there is one, the answered-prayer card: the stranger's original sentence in quotes, the formalised predicate underneath in monospace, and the solving program. Then up to two more event lines. Then a single button, 'leave a mark', which is the guestbook — so the phone view is also the input surface and visitors get the same page you do. Binge view: the full chronicle as a scrolling text history with the abstraction DAG inline, readable like a book. The guestbook history is its own page and over months it genuinely becomes scripture — a list of what strangers wanted from a civilization that cannot see them.

Your hardware

The most GPU-dependent of the six, and still comfortably inside 8GB. Qwen3.5-4B or Qwen3-4B at Q4_K_M, ~3.4-4.6GB resident, ~40 tok/s, running continuously as a llama.cpp server with the library dump kept in the KV cache across the night's calls (regenerate the cached prefix only when the library changes, which is once per night — this makes the per-call cost tiny). GBNF grammar regenerated nightly from the DSL; note the documented caveat that llama.cpp does runtime token checking for grammars and that this adds real overhead, so keep the grammar tight and expect maybe 25-30 tok/s under constraint rather than 40. Nightly volume of ~50-100k tokens local is free and takes under an hour. The weekly QLoRA on a 4B in 8GB is FEASIBLE BUT TIGHT and I want to be honest: Unsloth-style 4-bit QLoRA with gradient checkpointing, sequence length capped around 1024, batch size 1 with accumulation, expect 2-4 hours and expect to hit OOM the first few attempts. It is optional; the library is the real asset and the finetune is a garnish. If you want a stronger oracle occasionally, Claude Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per Mtok with prompt caching (cache hits at one tenth of input, batch API at 50% off) means a weekly hard-case pass with a 20k-token cached library prefix and a few thousand output tokens costs cents; a whole month of weekly calls is a couple of dollars. Everything timed for fitness is in interpreter steps, never seconds, which also means the QLoRA eval gate is unaffected by GPU thermals.

What you have to do

Coffee and a paper strip from the printer. The one genuinely enjoyable recurring act is leaving your own mark in the guestbook and seeing whether the tribe ever answers you. Chores, honestly: the weekly QLoRA will need babysitting the first few times (OOM tuning), and the guestbook needs a profanity/abuse filter and rate limiting since it is public and its content will be quoted onto your phone and your printer — build that on day one of making it public, not later. After the first month, near-zero.

First weekend

Assumes Emberlisp's substrate. Saturday: llama.cpp server, GBNF grammar generated from your DSL's type signatures, and the program proposer — then measure, on ten tasks, how many the model solves that enumeration alone did not. That measurement is the whole thesis of the idea and you can have it in an afternoon. Sunday: the task proposer with the machine-checkable-predicate grammar, and the guestbook as a single text box writing to SQLite with the oracle formalising entries at dawn. Delight moment: type a wish into your own guestbook on Sunday night, wake Monday to a formalised predicate the tribe is now working on.

Six months in

A tribe with a hundred-plus named technologies, an oracle that has been finetuned a dozen times on its own verified wins and speaks the civilization's invented vocabulary fluently, a guestbook that has become a genuinely strange document, and a shoebox of receipt strips. The thing that keeps it alive is the human input channel — the guestbook does not saturate, because people keep being unpredictable, and that is one non-stationarity source no other idea here has.

Where it stops (self-reported)

A 4B model is not smart. Its program proposals will be worse than enumeration on shallow tasks and only occasionally better on deep ones; the 'won X% of tasks search missed' metric might sit at 10-20% and you should be prepared for it to be low enough that the LLM is decoration. Measure it early and honestly, because if that number is near zero the whole idea reduces to Emberlisp with a nice namer — which is still good, but is not this. Second, the task proposer degenerates: LLMs asked repeatedly to propose goals converge on the same handful of themes, and after a few weeks you will see the same shapes of task recycled. The reach-horizon filter and the was-it-eventually-solved scoring help but do not fix it; the guestbook is the real defence, which means this idea's long-term novelty is partly dependent on other people showing up, and if nobody visits your page it plateaus like the others. Third, QLoRA on 8GB is a real time sink for uncertain gain; treat it as strictly optional and do not let it become the project. Fourth, public input means moderation, and quoting stranger text onto a physical printer in your house is a decision to make deliberately. Fifth, grammar-constrained decoding has known overhead and there is published work on how tokenisation interacts badly with grammar constraints, so expect some fiddling to get valid, fluent DSL out rather than valid, stupid DSL.

Stack

Emberlisp's substrate and stack, plus: llama-cpp-python running a persistent server with a nightly-regenerated GBNF grammar derived programmatically from the library's type signatures; a `proposals` table recording every LLM output with its verification outcome so you can measure the model's hit rate over time; a `guestbook` table with raw text, formalised predicate, moderation state, and satisfaction tick; Unsloth + PEFT for the optional weekly QLoRA with the held-out step-count eval as the accept gate; python-escpos for the receipt printer. Public surface via Tailscale Funnel or a small Cloudflare Worker queue that the sim drains at dawn, so the laptop never takes direct inbound traffic.

Verified

Verified: LILO, 'Learning Interpretable Libraries by Compressing and Documenting Code' (Grand, Wong et al., ICLR 2024, arxiv.org/abs/2310.19791) is the direct precedent — it integrates Stitch compression with LLM-generated auto-documentation in a search-compress-document loop and reports solving more complex tasks and learning richer libraries than DreamCoder; this is the published version of roles (1) and (2). LAPS (Wong et al., ICML 2021, code at github.com/lio-wong/laps_dreamcoder) is the language-guided-abstraction predecessor. Voyager (Wang et al., arxiv.org/abs/2305.16291, voyager.minedojo.org) supplies the auto-curriculum + ever-growing executable skill library + iterative prompting with execution-error feedback and self-verification — role (3) and the retry loop. Recent successor framing: skill-library agents with RL-based acquisition (SAGE) and the 2026 survey 'Agent Skills for Large Language Models' (arxiv.org/html/2602.12430) describing skills as bundles of instructions, scripts, docs and metadata loaded on demand. Grammar constraints: llama.cpp GBNF (github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/grammars/README.md) supports arbitrary custom DSL grammars and there is a good worked example of constraining a model to a custom assembly language (jamesdrandall.com/posts/gbnf-constrained-generation/); the documented caveat is runtime per-token checking overhead, and 'Lost in Space: Optimizing Tokens for Grammar-Constrained Decoding' (arxiv.org/html/2502.14969v1) covers the tokenisation pitfalls. Models: Qwen3-4B / Qwen3.5-4B Q4_K_M ~3.4-4.6GB, 40+ tok/s on 8GB, described as solid for coding on constrained hardware; Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M ~6.6GB is possible but leaves little room for context. Costs if you supplement with the API: Claude Haiku 4.5 is $1 input / $5 output per Mtok, prompt caching gives cache hits at one tenth of base input (5-minute and 1-hour cache writes at 1.25x and 2x), and the Batch API is 50% off — so a cached-library weekly pass is genuinely a few cents. Phone/push: ntfy.sh behind Tailscale is the standard hobby pattern and the ntfy web UI is itself installable as a PWA (docs.ntfy.sh/integrations); Tailscale Serve gives HTTPS with a stable URL and no router config.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Correctly identifies that the task distribution is where every other idea plateaus, then hands the fix to the one component that cannot deliver it. A 4B model, GBNF-constrained to a DSL that DID NOT EXIST DURING ITS TRAINING and whose primitive names were invented last week by a 4B model, has no prior to contribute. That is the crux and it is worth stating precisely: LLM priors are valuable in program synthesis exactly when the DSL is human-legible and maps onto world knowledge the model already has (this is why Voyager works — it writes JavaScript against a Minecraft API documented all over the internet). Here you have deliberately stripped every noun out of the substrate, so the model sees `(prop> (bank-the-coals x) 3)` and has nothing to reason with. The predicted 'won 10-20% of tasks search missed' is optimistic; expect 0-5%, and expect the wins to be on shallow tasks enumeration would have reached anyway. Grammar constrains SYNTAX, not sense. Strip the proposer and the finetune and what remains is Emberlisp with a namer and a guestbook — which is a nice project, and is not this one. Ceiling for the distinctive mechanism: measurable in the first week, and the measurement is the honest thing they should build first, exactly as their own first_weekend says.

Is the discovery real?

Inverted from the other five, and more insidious. The tech is not authored — the MEANING is. Two places. (1) The task proposer's reach-horizon filter is a hand-tuned heuristic that decides which LLM goals are 'within reach'; that filter, not the model, determines the curriculum, and tuning it is content authoring with extra steps. (2) The guestbook, which is the emotional centre of the pitch, is the worst offender: a 4B asked to formalise 'they should learn to bury their dead' into a predicate over calories, core temperature, position and property records will emit something syntactically valid, semantically unrelated, and eventually satisfiable — and then the system will print 'PRAYER ANSWERED' on a receipt, quoting a stranger, with a program that has nothing to do with burial. That is not discovery revealed in random order; it is manufactured meaning, and it is more corrosive than an authored tech tree because it is designed to be moving. Build the mapping so a human can inspect and reject it, or do not ship the printer.

Compute honesty

The most GPU-dependent design and still comfortably honest on 8GB — ~4GB resident for a 4B Q4, prefix-cached library dump, 25-30 tok/s under GBNF (their overhead estimate is realistic), ~100k tokens a night in about an hour. Two under-costings. (1) Volume: 8 candidates x N tasks x ~300 tokens scales faster than they budget; at 50 active tasks that is 120k OUTPUT tokens, which is over an hour of pure generation before the searcher gets a turn. Cap candidates per task, not tasks. (2) The QLoRA is worse than 'feasible but tight'. Qwen3.5-4B is a hybrid Gated-DeltaNet/gated-attention architecture with a vision path — Unsloth/PEFT support for that stack is not something to assume, and 'expect to hit OOM the first few attempts' will become 'expect to spend a weekend on kernel and adapter-target compatibility'. Separately, a weekly harvest of a few hundred verified triples is far too little data to move a 4B in any direction except overfitting, and the held-out interpreter-step gate will (correctly) reject nearly every checkpoint. Their advice to treat it as optional is right; my advice is to delete it. Also correct and worth repeating: costing fitness in interpreter steps rather than seconds is the right call and makes the whole thing thermally reproducible.

Nonsense risk

Highest of the six. Every other idea risks meaningless abstractions with plausible names; this one adds meaningless GOALS with plausible names, then a finetune that trains the model to produce more of them, then a receipt printer that renders the output as scripture. The three verifier gates (executes, earns call-sites, beats incumbent on steps) are genuinely good and I want to credit them — they make this the most epistemically careful LLM-in-the-loop design I have seen in a hobby spec. But note what they check: that a program RUNS and that a predicate is SATISFIED. Nothing checks that the predicate means what the words said, and the guestbook path makes that the load-bearing question.

Claims to re-check

(1) LILO (ICLR 2024, 2310.19791), LAPS (ICML 2021) and Voyager (2305.16291) are all real and correctly characterised. But LILO's LLM operates over Python-like DSLs with English-adjacent semantics and Voyager's over documented JavaScript APIs; neither is evidence for a 4B on an invented property-record lisp, and the design leans on them as if it were. (2) 'Agent Skills for Large Language Models' (arXiv 2602.12430) — February 2026, I cannot verify it and it is doing no load-bearing work; drop it. (3) 'Lost in Space' (2502.14969) on grammar-constrained tokenisation is real and the caveat is right — expect valid, stupid DSL, which is exactly the failure mode. (4) Qwen3.5-4B/9B: verified real (Mar 2026), sizes as stated. (5) Claude Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per Mtok with 0.1x cache reads, 1.25x/2x cache writes and 50%-off batch: correct, and it is the most useful fact in the entire research_notes — see below. (6) 'A source of non-stationarity with no computational bound at all, because it is other people' assumes visitors; a hobby page linked nowhere gets zero entries a month, and this idea's stated long-term novelty then reduces to the same plateau as the others. They admit this in the last paragraph, which is to their credit and should be read as the actual forecast.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Move the PROPOSER off the 4B and onto a weekly batched frontier-model call, and have it reason over a HUMAN-LEGIBLE rendering of the world rather than the invented DSL. Concretely: each week, render the last 7 nights as English (what the tribe tried, what killed them, what the new abstractions do, what the guestbook said), send it as a cached prefix to a batch API call, and ask for goals in natural language plus a mapping into a small FIXED predicate algebra that you wrote and can read. Their own numbers say this costs a couple of dollars a month. Three things change: the model now has priors that actually apply (it knows what winter, storage and burial mean, even if your primitives do not), the formalisation step becomes inspectable rather than a 4B's private guess, and the 'oracle won X% of tasks search missed' metric has a chance of being non-zero. Keep the local 4B for naming, where a small model is sufficient and its errors are cosmetic. And run that measurement on day one, before building anything else — if the number is still near zero with a strong model, the whole LLM branch is decoration and you should go build Glyphworks instead.

Agent societies

Culture and knowledge compounding across generations, lossily

▸

Embertongue

A cold basin where technology is literally a library of named programs that agents discover, and every entry lives in specific people's heads until someone invents writing. DreamCoder's wake/sleep compression loop is the tech tree: nightly, Stitch compresses the tribe's successful action-programs into new named primitives, and those primitives become tomorrow's vocabulary — forever.

Ceilinged
The world

A 96x96 hex basin, deterministic, 1 tick = 1 hour, seeded RNG, no authored recipes anywhere. The substrate is CONTINUOUS FIELDS plus typed matter: a heat field, an airflow field, a moisture field, a load/stress scalar per structure, and ~9 element-ish material tags (carbon, silica, clay-mineral, iron-oxide, tallow, cellulose, keratin, water, salt). Every physical object is a struct: {mass, temp, composition_vector, hardness, plasticity, porosity, wetness, integrity, decay_rate}. Physics is ~400 lines of threshold rules over those numbers — clay above 900C for 4h becomes irreversibly hard; cellulose heated with airflow below a threshold becomes carbon-dense charcoal instead of ash; iron-oxide + carbon + airflow above 1200C yields a bloom. You never write "pottery" or "iron" as a concept; the sim only knows numbers, and the tribe names what it notices. Agents are bodies (kcal, core temp, injury, age, pregnancy, skill floats) plus a MIND: a memory stream (generative-agents style, but 90% of entries are sim events not prose) and a personal library of program entries each with a mastery float. All actions are programs in a typed DSL over the substrate: heat, strike, bind, twist, soak, dry, bury, cover, carry, wait, place, split, mix, plus control flow (seq, repeat_until, if_state).

How invention actually happens

Wake/sleep library learning, straight out of DreamCoder (Ellis et al., PLDI 2021) with Stitch (Bowers et al., POPL 2023) as the compressor. WAKE: an agent has a need (cold, hunger, broken tool, a child dying). It searches for a program that reduces that need. Search is a hybrid: (a) enumerative bottom-up search over its OWN library, weighted by a learned prior (a simple bigram model over primitives, updated nightly), and (b) a proposal from the local Qwen3-4B, grammar-constrained by a GBNF compiled directly from the DSL so it CANNOT emit an invalid program. Candidate programs execute against a cheap copy of local world-state (this is the agent's 'imagination'); the winner executes for real, in the real deterministic sim, and reality gets the final vote. Every program that measurably reduced a need is appended to corpus.jsonl with its context and its measured delta. SLEEP (nightly, 03:00, ~4 minutes of CPU): run stitch_core.compress() over the last N=8000 successful programs. Stitch returns abstractions ranked by compression gain. The top-k (k~3, gated by minimum utility) are minted as NEW NAMED PRIMITIVES and injected into the DSL, the GBNF grammar is regenerated, and one Haiku call names each one and writes a one-line etymology in-world ('they call it emberkeep — to smother the fire so it wakes'). Those primitives are now vocabulary. Tomorrow's search operates one level higher. That recursion is the entire compounding mechanism, and it is the same mechanism that made DreamCoder rediscover Newtonian laws and classic list routines from scratch. THE CULTURAL TWIST that makes it a civilization instead of a library: an abstraction does not belong to the tribe, it belongs to the MINDS that hold it. carriers = set of agent ids with mastery > 0.3. Teaching is a scheduled interaction costing labor-hours; the child receives the abstraction with mastery = parent_mastery * (1 - noise) AND with probability p a corrupted AST (one argument permuted, one subterm dropped, one constant jittered). Corruptions usually fail. Occasionally a corruption is better — that is a second, independent invention channel, and it is pure cultural transmission noise doing the work. If every carrier of an abstraction dies before teaching it, it is deleted from the world. The tribe can lose fire. Writing is not authored either: if search ever finds a program whose effect is a durable, legible mark on a low-decay_rate surface, the engine detects that the program's output object satisfies (legibility > 0.5 and decay_rate < eps) and unlocks inscribe(surface, library_entry) as an affordance. From that moment abstractions can be stored outside heads at mastery 1.0, decaying at the medium's rate. The invention-rate curve visibly changes regime the night that happens.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The reachable space is programs of bounded depth d over a vocabulary V, which is roughly |V|^(b*d) — and |V| GROWS every night as compression mints primitives. Each new primitive is a chunk that used to cost 6 nodes and now costs 1, so a depth-8 search after 200 abstractions reaches what would have been depth-40 raw programs. That is not a list a designer wrote; it is a recursive re-parameterization of the search space by its own successes, and it is the same argument Arthur makes in The Nature of Technology (combination begets combinables) and Kauffman makes about the adjacent possible (each new thing enlarges the set of next things). WHERE IT ACTUALLY BOTTOMS OUT, honestly: the BASE primitives and the physics' state variables are a hard wall. If your physics has no pressure field, no steam engine is reachable, ever, no matter how much compression happens. The library can only get deeper inside the expressiveness you shipped. This is why the substrate is continuous fields and a composition vector rather than an item list — the number of DISTINGUISHABLE material states is then astronomically large and the wall sits very far out, but it exists and you should know exactly where it is: it is the set of numbers in the object struct.

The ladder

WEEK 1 (with a 10-primitive DSL and no LLM yet): agents carry water, feed fire, huddle, dry meat, and Stitch mints its first abstraction — probably something dull-but-real like 'carry-then-place-near-heat'. Delight moment: it names a chunk you never thought of, because it noticed the chunk repeated 400 times. REAL RUNGS, in the order they will plausibly fall: banked fire (cover + ash + wait, discovered because agents keep waking to cold embers); cordage (twist + soak + dry over cellulose fibers); hafting (bind + strike composed); fired clay (kiln failures teach that an open fire never crosses 900C, which forces the earthwork); charcoal (cellulose + starved airflow — the sim's airflow field makes this a genuine discovery, not a recipe); bellows (hide + cord + chamber, only reachable AFTER cordage and hide-working); bloom iron (charcoal + iron-oxide sand + forced airflow above threshold); lime/plaster if you include a carbonate; and inscription, which changes everything. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT with a load/stress and flow field in the physics: a water-driven trip hammer — because 'apply repeated strike without labor-hours' is a compression the system will WANT once labor is the binding constraint; a granary with a stored inscribed corpus (a library building whose presence multiplies teaching bandwidth); and a specialist caste that emerges because the abstraction depth exceeds what one lifetime can master, which the sim measures directly as teaching-hours-required > remaining-lifespan.

What kills you

Kcal and core temperature, on an hourly tick, with a winter that is genuinely lethal. Childbirth and childhood mortality. Injury from failed programs (a bad hafting throws the axe head). And competition: 2-5 tribes share the basin, wood and clay deposits are finite and rivalrous, and a tribe holding more high-utility abstractions produces more kcal per labor-hour, so it outbreeds and out-settles. Explicit extinction is the point — a tribe that loses its cordage abstraction in a bad flu season will visibly fall down the ladder, and you will read about it. Abstractions themselves are under selection: each carries carriers_alive, teach_count, and measured kcal-per-labor-hour delta; low-utility ones stop being taught and quietly die.

How the world changes

Three layers. (1) SLOW: a deterministic climate signal per world seed — three superimposed sines at ~40, ~400 and ~4000 day periods — so there are hard centuries and soft centuries and the tribe cannot tune to a static optimum. (2) SHOCK: seeded events sampled from a fixed table with long return periods — ashfall that zeroes photosynthesis for 90 days, a river avulsion that moves the clay deposit, a blight that removes one food species permanently. (3) NEW BASINS: every ~2 weeks of wall-clock a new basin is generated (different resource abundances AND different threshold constants — the clay here fires at 1050C, the ore is manganese-rich) and a gate opens. Tribes may migrate carrying only what their living members know. Abstractions tuned to the old constants FAIL in the new basin; the tribe keeps teaching them anyway for a few generations, which the engine labels ritual. Every basin runs as its own deterministic process; a world-index page shows all of them.

How it knows something is new

Three layers, and the third is the good one. (1) FIRST-EVER: canonicalize the program AST (alpha-renamed, sorted commutative args), hash it, check a bloom filter over all history. Cheap, high recall, high noise. (2) BAYESIAN SURPRISE: maintain Dirichlet counts over event-type bigrams and over binned world-state features; push when KL(posterior || prior) for the day exceeds a running 99th percentile. This catches regime changes (the day everyone starts sleeping indoors) without knowing what to look for. (3) ASSEMBLY INDEX (Sharma/Cronin/Walker, Nature 2023): for every physical object in the world, compute the minimum number of DSL steps to build it from freely-available materials — that is literally the assembly index, and it is cheap because you already have the build DAG. Track max_assembly_index and the copy_number of every high-index object. A push fires when the basin's max assembly index increases, or when a high-index object's copy number crosses a threshold (meaning it is being REPRODUCED, i.e. it is now technology and not an accident). This is the single best civilizational-depth metric I know of that is actually computable, and it is what the phone card leads with. Haiku is called only for items that clear these filters — typically 5-30 a day, not thousands.

On your phone

One card, no scrolling. Line 1: 'Day 611 · Ashpan · 2 tribes · 47 alive · winter'. Line 2, the headline, always the highest-novelty event: 'NEW: emberkeep (assembly 9). Sura taught it to 3 children, then died at 41.' Line 3: max assembly index sparkline (the civilization's depth over time) and population sparkline, 60px tall. Line 4: three-node diff of the library graph — what was minted, what went extinct, what is down to one carrier ('cordage: 1 carrier left, age 63' is the most alarming sentence the app can print, and it should be red). Line 5: the Haiku chronicle paragraph, 40 words, written in a chronicler's voice. Tap anywhere → full force-directed library graph colored by carrier count, tap a node → its etymology, its inventor, its death. ntfy push fires at most 3x/day and only on layer-3 novelty.

Your hardware

Honest sizing for 8GB. llama.cpp server with Qwen3-4B-Instruct at Q4_K_M (~2.5GB weights), -np 6 parallel slots, 4k context each, KV cache at q8 (~0.6GB) → ~3.5-4GB VRAM, leaving room for the browser and the compositor. Expect roughly 90-130 tok/s single-stream on a laptop 5070 and maybe 250-400 tok/s aggregate across slots; the published 5070-class benchmark I found is ~86 tok/s for an 8B at Q4_K/4k, and a 4B should be comfortably faster, but a LAPTOP 5070 is thermally limited and will sit below desktop numbers. Budget planning number: 5-10M generated tokens/day, i.e. ~60-100k short grammar-constrained program proposals. That is far more than the sim needs — the sim is the bottleneck, not the GPU. The sim itself is CPU: numpy fields (or a Rust core via pyo3 once you care), and 64GB RAM means the whole event log stays in page cache. Stitch is Rust, runs on CPU, seconds to a couple of minutes over 8k programs. NO TRAINING, so no VRAM contention. Set a 65% GPU duty cycle so the fans are livable overnight. Haiku: naming (5-30/day), the chronicle (1/day), a Sunday essay (1/week) — 150-300 batched calls/day. At $1/$5 per MTok with the Batch API's 50% off, ~300 calls x (1k in / 600 out) ≈ $0.15 in + $0.90 out ≈ under a dollar; cut it to 150 calls if you want headroom. Prompt caching (90% off reads) helps the live path but not batch, so lean on batch and short prompts.

What you have to do

Zero required, ever. The thing runs 24/7 and writes to you. Optional god-tier nudges, all one-tap from the PWA: drop a resource seam, trigger a climate shock, open a gate to a new basin, kill a tribe, or 'pin a question' that the nightly chronicler must answer in tomorrow's paragraph ('is anyone still teaching cordage?'). Realistically the only recurring chore is the one you will actually enjoy: extending the DSL and the physics when you see the library plateau, maybe an hour every few weeks.

First weekend

Saturday: physics with 6 materials, a heat field, hunger, cold, death; 8 DSL primitives; a tree-walking interpreter; one tribe of 12; enumerative search only, no LLM at all. Get them to keep a fire alive. Sunday morning: log every successful program to corpus.jsonl. Sunday afternoon: pip install stitch_core, dump 500 programs in, print the abstractions. THAT is the moment — Stitch will hand you a named chunk you did not author, used 200 times, and you will immediately want to know what to call it. Wire one Haiku call to name it, print the etymology to the terminal, and you have the whole loop in miniature. LLM proposals, teaching, mortality of knowledge, and the PWA are week two.

Six months in

Four or five basins running as separate seeds. A library graph of 150-400 abstractions with real depth structure — you can see the trunk (fire, cord, heat-holding) and the branches. At least one lineage that invented inscription and one that did not, and the difference is visible in their invention rate as a kink in the chart. A dozen extinction events where an abstraction died with its last carrier, each one a story. Tribes migrating between basins carrying knowledge that no longer applies. You will have extended the DSL twice and the physics once. The morning ritual is real: coffee, one card, 'wait, they did WHAT'.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Two plateaus, and they arrive at different times. (1) COMPRESSION PLATEAU, ~6-10 weeks: once the task distribution is stationary, Stitch stops finding high-gain abstractions and mints junk. Climate cycles and new basins are the deliberate fix, and they buy months, not years, because non-stationarity from a fixed generator is itself eventually stationary. (2) EXPRESSIVENESS WALL, ~3-6 months: the physics' state variables are the hard limit on what can ever be invented. This is the one that ends the project if you ship 6 materials and 2 fields; ship 9 composition dimensions and 4 continuous fields and the wall moves out a long way, but it is still a wall you built. There is also a search-depth problem: a 4B's program proposals get weak past depth ~8, at which point enumeration dominates and this quietly becomes a compute-bound search project rather than an LLM project. That is fine, but know it. Real work: DSL and physics design is the actual craft here and it never fully ends. Expect a debugging week where compression mints degenerate abstractions (constants baked in, arity-0 wrappers) and you add utility gates.

Stack

Python 3.12 sim core (numpy for the fields), SQLite in WAL mode as the event/lineage store (tables: agents, kin_edges, abstractions, carriers, teachings, programs, artifacts, events, novelty_scores) — one file, greppable, trivially backed up. stitch_core (pip) for compression. llama.cpp server for Qwen3-4B with a GBNF grammar auto-generated from the DSL on every library update. FastAPI read-only JSON API. Vanilla-JS canvas PWA (your home turf) served over Tailscale Serve for the live view, PLUS a nightly static snapshot committed to GitHub Pages so the phone still works when the laptop sleeps. ntfy.sh topic for push. This is the shared substrate you reuse across all future projects: headless deterministic sim + SQLite event log + novelty scorer + PWA chronicle + ntfy. Benchmark hygiene: NOTHING in the fitness path touches wall-clock — utility is measured in kcal per labor-hour and sim ticks, both deterministic, so thermal throttling cannot corrupt evolution.

Verified

VERIFIED: DreamCoder — Ellis et al., 'DreamCoder: bootstrapping inductive program synthesis with wake-sleep library learning', PLDI 2021, dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454080 (wake/sleep + refactoring via e-graph matching to build a deepening library). Stitch — Bowers et al., 'Top-Down Synthesis for Library Learning', POPL 2023; repo github.com/mlb2251/stitch; Python bindings stitch_core (pip), docs stitch-bindings.readthedocs.io, API is stitch_core.compress(programs, iterations=...) returning abstractions + usage stats; the paper explicitly claims scaling to corpora intractable for prior deductive approaches, which is exactly the regime you need. LILO (arxiv.org/abs/2310.19791) is the LLM-augmented successor that also auto-DOCUMENTS abstractions — directly relevant to naming them in-world. Assembly theory — Sharma, Czégel, Lachmann, Kempes, Walker, Cronin, Nature 2023, arxiv.org/abs/2206.02279: assembly index = minimal steps to build an object from basic parts, copy number = observed instances; the pair is designed to distinguish selection-driven complexity from noise, which is precisely a novelty detector for an invention sim. llama.cpp GBNF grammars: github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/grammars/README.md — JSON-schema-to-GBNF conversion is built in and the server accepts response_format; server has slot-based parallel decoding (-np). Model sizing: Qwen3-4B Q4 is ~2.5-3.4GB; a published RTX 5070 (desktop, 12GB) recipe reports ~85.8 tok/s for Qwen3-8B Q4_K at 4k context (smeltcore.com) — treat that as an optimistic upper bound for the 8GB laptop part. Haiku 4.5 pricing confirmed at anthropic.com/claude/haiku: $1/MTok input, $5/MTok output, up to 90% off with prompt caching, 50% off with the Batch API. Not used but checked: Voyager (arxiv.org/abs/2305.16291, github.com/MineDojo/Voyager) is the skill-library precedent; Project Sid (arxiv.org/abs/2411.00114) is the many-agent precedent.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two stops, and the real one is far earlier than they think. (a) The advertised wall is the physics threshold table; the ACTUAL first wall is sparse reward over long horizons. Fitness = 'a program that measurably reduced a need' gives literally zero credit for building a kiln, gathering ore, or a 4-hour firing that pays off tomorrow. Enumerative search plus a 4B proposer cannot cross a 6-step delayed-payoff chain with no gradient, so the library plateaus at depth-2/3 compositions of immediate-payoff actions (fetch-place-heat, huddle, dry-meat) at roughly WEEK 2-4 — before charcoal, and nowhere near bellows or bloom iron. Those ladder rungs are unreachable without reward shaping the designer would have to author, at which point he has authored the tree. (b) Even if solved, the discoverable set equals the count of qualitatively distinct threshold rules in the 400-line physics — call it 15-30 — exhausted in 8-14 weeks. (c) Deadliest detail: Stitch keeps minting abstractions after both stops, so the 'library is growing' chart stays healthy while nothing new happens. That is the most dangerous failure mode in the whole batch because it looks exactly like success.

Is the discovery real?

High and under-acknowledged. 'No authored recipes anywhere' is false. 'Clay above 900C for 4h becomes irreversibly hard' and 'iron-oxide + carbon + airflow above 1200C yields a bloom' ARE recipes — written in numbers instead of names. The depth_ladder is a list of things the author put in the physics file, in the order he put them there. What IS genuinely unauthored is the path and the chunking: Stitch's abstractions are real structure nobody wrote, and that part is honest. So: real search over an authored landscape. Better than a tech tree walked by bots; not open-ended. The writing unlock is worse — legibility>0.5 AND decay_rate<eps is an authored affordance with a physics-shaped trigger, i.e. a tech-tree node in a trench coat. Deepest conceptual error: DreamCoder's compounding came from a human-authored curriculum of increasingly hard task batteries, not from the library. Compression is a POSTERIOR operation — it re-encodes what already succeeded and never proposes a harder problem. Here the task distribution is homeostatic and satiable: once kcal/labor-hour clears winter, nothing pulls programs deeper. Their own 6-10 week compression plateau prediction is therefore structural, not tunable.

Compute honesty

Mostly honest — GPU sizing is conservative, Stitch on 8k programs is seconds-to-minutes on CPU, SQLite is right. Two real omissions. (1) They dropped DreamCoder's neural recognition model ('NO TRAINING'), which is the half of the loop that keeps search tractable as |V| grows. Without it, adding primitives INCREASES branching factor, so search degrades over time — the exact opposite of 'depth-8 reaches what would have been depth-40'. A 4B with a GBNF over 200 invented primitives named 'emberkeep' has no useful prior over them; by month two this is a CPU enumeration project with an LLM ornament. (2) Unbudgeted: imagination rollouts. Candidates x sim-steps over continuous fields with multi-hour processes, per agent, per need. That is the real bottleneck and it is unsized. Also a metric bug: assembly index measured in DSL steps is not comparable across nights once minting collapses 6 steps into 1 — max_assembly_index will move on abstraction events rather than on invention, corrupting the headline chart.

Nonsense risk

Medium. Programs are typed and physics-grounded, so outputs are not drivel. But Stitch abstractions are frequent-substring artifacts and only sometimes functional units; Haiku naming plus an in-world etymology confers apparent meaning on syntactic noise, and the chronicle will render it as culture. Fix before shipping the namer: require an abstraction to have a measurable, context-independent effect delta before it earns a name.

Claims to re-check

DreamCoder 'rediscovered Newtonian laws' — true, but inside a curated symbolic-regression battery; it is not evidence of open-endedness and is used here as if it were. Assembly index 'is literally the assembly index and it is cheap because you already have the build DAG' — WRONG: the build DAG yields the shortest OBSERVED construction (an upper bound); computing the true minimal assembly index is NP-complete (npj Complexity 2025 / arXiv 2406.12176), and assembly theory's claim to detect selection is actively contested (Zenil et al., PLOS Complex Systems; arXiv 2403.06629, 2408.15108). Fine as a depth proxy, do not cite it as rigorous. Cost math: 180k output tokens at the batch rate ($2.50/MTok) is $0.45, not $0.90 — they used list price after claiming the discount. 'Prompt caching helps the live path but not batch' is likely wrong and directly contradicts Grist's own note; caching applies within Batch requests, and Grist's version is the correct one. The RTX 5070 ~85.8 tok/s figure is an unverifiable vendor blog; a ~115W laptop 5070 with 8GB will be materially slower, and -np 6 x 4k KV on top of a 2.5GB model is tighter than stated.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Ship an autocurriculum. Replace the satiable homeostatic objective with pressure that scales with capability: unbounded population growth against rivalrous depletable resources, plus an explicit proposer that logs UNMET needs as standing tasks, plus credit for programs that expand the reachable state space (not only kcal delta) so multi-step chains get partial credit. DreamCoder's depth came from its curriculum, not its library — without one, Stitch compresses junk by week 8 no matter how good the physics is.

▸

Saltmark

An artificial chemistry with an infinite molecule space, and tribes who can only exploit it as fast as their LANGUAGE lets them talk about it — so the real invention being made, generation by generation, is a compositional naming system. You watch a lexicon go from holistic grunts to 'ash-bitter-thick' and watch the discovery rate jump the week it happens.

Ceilinged
The world

No grid needed at first; a small set of hearth-sites connected by travel time. The substrate is a deterministic artificial chemistry: molecules are strings over a 6-symbol alphabet; reactions are ligation (concat) and cleavage (split), and whether reaction R is catalysed by molecule M is decided by a fixed hash rule — hash(M, R) < threshold. This is Kauffman's binary polymer model in its cheapest computable form (cf. Sayama's 'Non-Spatial Hash Chemistry as a Minimalistic Open-Ended Evolutionary System', arXiv 2404.18027). No content is authored: the entire chemistry is a hash function and two operators. A pot at a hearth is a multiset of molecules with a temperature and a duration; ticking a pot runs mass-action kinetics for those molecules. Each molecule gets a small number of LEGIBLE physical properties derived deterministically from its string — nutrition, toxicity, adhesion, burn temperature, dye hue, bitterness — via fixed projections, so that agents (and you) can actually perceive it as food/poison/glue/pigment rather than as noise. Agents are bodies with hunger, poisoning load, and a LEXICON: a mapping from signals (strings over a syllable inventory) to meanings (regions of property space), plus a set of remembered procedures.

How invention actually happens

Two engines, coupled, and the coupling is the idea. ENGINE A — CHEMISTRY DISCOVERY: agents mix, heat, wait, strain, and re-seed pots. What comes out is computed by the chemistry, not chosen by anyone. Nightly, run a RAF detection pass (reflexively-autocatalytic and food-generated set detection — Hordijk & Steel's algorithm is polynomial and about 200 lines) over the reaction network the tribe has actually realised, to identify self-sustaining loops. A discovered RAF is a *living* technology: a starter culture, a fermentation, a self-renewing catalyst supply. Losing it means losing an entire industry, and it can be lost by letting a pot go cold. ENGINE B — ITERATED LEARNING OF NAMES: this is the cultural engine. Each generation, adults produce signals for substances; children observe only a BOTTLENECK subset of (signal, substance) pairs and must generalise. Implemented concretely: the local Qwen3-4B plays teacher and learner in short referential games (teacher sees a substance's property vector and its lexicon, emits a signal; learner picks from N distractors), and the resulting learner lexicon is stored as an explicit trie, not as vibes. The bottleneck plus a communication pressure is the classic Kirby iterated-learning setup, and it reliably produces COMPOSITIONAL structure — signals decompose into morphemes that track property dimensions. Recent work replicates this specifically with LLM agents (Kouwenhoven et al. 2025, iterated referential games with generational transmission; 'From Signals to Structure', arXiv 2607.00233, finds compositionality needs BOTH a discriminability pressure and a compression pressure). THE COUPLING: a compositional lexicon lets an agent describe a substance it has never seen to someone who has never seen it, which mechanically unlocks (a) recipes transmitted verbally without co-presence, (b) trade orders for substances by description, (c) a child correctly PREDICTING a substance's properties from its name before tasting it — which stops it dying. So language quality feeds directly into discovery rate and survival, and you can plot the two curves on the same axes. That plot is the artifact.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The molecule space is strings over an alphabet: countably infinite, with reachable depth limited only by energy and time. RAF sets nest — a RAF whose food set includes the output of another RAF is a strictly deeper industry, and there is no upper bound on nesting. The lexicon is an open vocabulary over an open meaning space. Nothing here is a list. HONEST BOTTOM: most of an infinite chemistry is uninteresting slop, and 'unbounded' is worthless if 99.999% of it is indistinguishable garbage. The *interesting* subspace is bounded by the number of legible property projections you define (I said six) and by the fitness pressures attached to them. Two, maybe three of those properties will carry all the drama. Also, the language transition happens ONCE per world — compositionality, once achieved, is stable (that is the whole point of iterated learning). So the repeatable, evergreen content is language BIRTH, which means new worlds with new chemistries are not a bonus feature here, they are the content pipeline.

The ladder

WEEK 1: agents eat random strings, poison each other, and one hearth accidentally keeps a pot warm long enough to produce something with nutrition > 0. First names are holistic — one arbitrary syllable-blob per substance, no structure, and children mostly fail to learn them. REAL RUNGS: a two-step preparation (leach a toxin out, then heat) that no single agent designed; a fermentation that runs overnight (first RAF); a catalyst that can be harvested but not made, which creates a place worth walking to and therefore a trade route; the same catalyst becoming makeable, which collapses the trade route and impoverishes a hearth; the first MORPHEME — a syllable that reliably means 'bitter' across many names — detectable as a spike in topographic similarity between signal-space and meaning-space distances; recipes transmitted by description alone to a hearth that has never seen the ingredients; a nested RAF (industry A's waste is industry B's food); written marks for morphemes, i.e. an alphabet, which is a much more natural origin story for writing than 'they invented writing'. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT: a seed culture — a living autocatalytic starter physically passed down for 40 generations, maintained by a ritual nobody understands, whose loss during a famine causes a measurable civilizational regression; and a schism over two dialects whose morphemes carve the property space differently, so the two tribes literally cannot exchange recipes and diverge into different chemistries. That is emergent religion and emergent ethnolinguistics out of a hash function and a bottleneck.

What kills you

Toxicity kills fast, malnutrition kills slow. Catalysts are scarce and rivalrous. Pots need fuel and attention, so labor allocation is a real constraint. Between tribes: the one whose children learn the lexicon faster loses fewer people to poisoning and can run deeper pathways, because deep pathways need reliable multi-person coordination which needs reliable naming. Learnability is fitness — that is the pressure that drives compositionality, and it is grounded in corpses, not in a loss function.

How the world changes

The FOOD SET (freely available molecules) drifts seasonally and differs per region, so pathways go dead and dormant knowledge becomes valuable again later. Each new world is a new hash seed, which means a completely different chemistry — nothing transfers except the IDEA of naming, and the tribe's morphology (their syllable inventory, their morpheme-slot order) does transfer, so you get language families with shared grammar over unrelated vocabularies. That is a gorgeous, cheap, and completely mechanical way to have 'multiple worlds' mean something. Run 3-6 seas concurrently; the world index page is a map of chemistries.

How it knows something is new

(1) First synthesis of a molecule whose assembly index (min ligation/cleavage steps from the food set — the chemistry gives you this exactly) exceeds the world record. (2) RAF-set jump: nightly RAF detection returns a set whose size or nesting depth increased. (3) THE LANGUAGE METRIC, and this is the special one: topographic similarity (Spearman correlation between pairwise edit distance in signal space and pairwise distance in meaning space) measured over each generation's lexicon, plus held-out generalisation accuracy — can this generation's children correctly name a substance NO ONE has named before? A jump in either is the compositionality transition and it deserves a push notification with a fanfare. (4) Bayesian surprise over event-type counts for everything else. Push budget: 2-3 a day max.

On your phone

Card: 'Sea of Kell · Day 1,204 · 3 hearths · 38 alive'. Headline slot alternates between chemistry and language: 'FIRST: a substance 11 steps deep. They call it *kessa-thu-mar*.' Then two sparklines side by side that are the whole thesis: max assembly index, and topographic similarity of the lexicon — when the second one jumps and the first one follows two weeks later, you have SEEN culture cause technology, and no caption is needed. Then a 6-word gloss of the newest morpheme ('-mar now means: sticks to skin'). Tap → the lexicon trie, and the reaction network rendered as a graph with RAF loops highlighted. Also a LISTEN button: the sonification station (see tech) is genuinely the best passive check-in of the six ideas, because a chemistry is already a stream of discrete timed events.

Your hardware

The lightest of the six. The chemistry is pure integer/hash work — numpy or a 300-line Rust core does millions of reactions per second on CPU, and 64GB RAM lets you keep the entire realized reaction network resident. The GPU only does referential games: Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M with -np 8, short prompts (a property vector, a lexicon slice, 4 distractors), ~80 output tokens per exchange. A generation of 30 children x 200 exchanges = 6000 calls, ~500k tokens, which is under 30 minutes of GPU. You can run a full linguistic generation every few hours comfortably and still have the GPU idle most of the day. Realistically you could run 4-6 seas concurrently on one laptop. Haiku: the nightly chronicle plus a WEEKLY LINGUIST'S REPORT (give it the lexicon diff, the topsim numbers, and 20 example names; ask for a field-linguist's memo) — that report is the single most enjoyable output any of these six ideas produces, and it costs about 3 calls x 2k output. Total Haiku well under $0.30/day, leaving budget to run more seas.

What you have to do

Zero. Optional nudges: introduce a new food molecule, poison a well, split a tribe, or force a migration between seas. One genuinely fun manual lever: you can ADD a syllable to the world's phonology and see whether it gets adopted.

First weekend

Saturday morning: the chemistry — strings, ligation, cleavage, hash-catalysis, property projections. Print a random walk of it and tune the threshold until reactions are neither dead nor exploding (this tuning IS the weekend's real work, budget 3 hours). Saturday night: pots, hunger, toxicity, 12 agents, death. Sunday: the referential game with the 4B and a bottleneck, run 5 generations, compute topographic similarity per generation, plot it. If the line goes up, you have replicated a 20-year-old cognitive-science result on your gaming laptop over a weekend, in a world where being wrong kills you. That is the delight moment and it is very achievable.

Six months in

Five seas with five unrelated chemistries and five language families. A couple of hundred generations of lexicon history you can scrub through. At least one documented case of a tribe losing a starter culture and regressing, and at least one schism. The weekly linguist's memo has become the thing you look forward to. You will have added property projections twice and probably added a spatial layer so trade routes are physical.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The compositionality transition is a ONE-TIME event per world — expect it at week 2-6 per sea, and after it happens the language mostly just accretes vocabulary. Chemistry discovery plateaus when the tribe has exploited the shallow high-nutrition region and deeper molecules stop paying for their labor cost; realistically 2-4 months per sea. So the honest model of this project is: each sea is a 2-3 month STORY with a clear arc, and the project's longevity comes from running new seas, not from one sea deepening forever. That is a real limitation and it is also, arguably, fine — it makes each world a novel rather than a soap opera. Second risk: hash chemistry can feel meaningless. If your property projections are badly chosen the whole thing reads as noise with names on it, and no amount of LLM prose will fix that. Spend the tuning time.

Stack

Rust or numpy chemistry core with a fixed hash (xxhash, seeded per world) — determinism is non-negotiable and must be tested with a replay-from-seed check in CI. SQLite for molecules, reactions, lexicons-per-generation, teachings, deaths. RAF detection: Hordijk-Steel closure algorithm, ~200 lines, runs nightly. llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B with GBNF constraining signals to the world's phonology (so the model literally cannot emit a syllable that does not exist in this culture — a lovely use of grammars). topsim via scipy.stats.spearmanr. PWA over Tailscale + nightly GitHub Pages snapshot + ntfy. SONIFICATION: map each molecule to a timbre by hashing its string into (fundamental, harmonic profile, decay), each reaction to a note event, each hearth to a stereo position; render 24h of the day's reaction log to a 3-minute Ogg with a tiny WebAudio offline render or sox, and publish it as a podcast RSS feed the phone can subscribe to. Leave it on while you work; you will learn to hear a fermentation.

Verified

VERIFIED: Sayama, 'Non-Spatial Hash Chemistry as a Minimalistic Open-Ended Evolutionary System', arXiv 2404.18027 — hash-based catalysis is the cheapest known way to get an open-ended artificial chemistry, no reaction table needed. Kauffman binary polymer model + RAF theory: Hordijk & Steel's autocatalytic-set line of work (polynomial RAF detection); see also 'Autocatalytic Sets and Assembly Theory: A Toy Model Perspective', Entropy 26(9):808, mdpi.com/1099-4300/26/9/808, and 'From correlation to causation: unraveling the impact of closure on open-ended evolution within the Kauffman model' (biorxiv 2024.10.30.621154 / BioSystems 2025) — both confirm this substrate is actively used for open-ended-evolution research right now, not a dead 1990s toy. Assembly index for depth: Sharma et al., Nature 2023, arXiv 2206.02279. Iterated learning: Kirby's framework; 'Compositionality and systematicity emerge from iterated learning in deep linear networks', PNAS 2025 (doi 10.1073/pnas.2509739123); Kouwenhoven et al. 2025 — LLMs in an iterated referential game with generational transmission go from holistic to compositional; 'From Signals to Structure: How Memory Architecture Drives Language Emergence in LLM Agents', arXiv 2607.00233 — compositionality needs BOTH communication (discriminability) and compression (learnability) pressure, which tells you exactly what two pressures to build. Topographic similarity is the standard emergent-communication compositionality metric (see 'Emergent Language: A Survey and Taxonomy', arXiv 2409.02645). llama.cpp GBNF for constraining generated signals to a phonology: github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/grammars/README.md.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The chemistry has no gradient, and that is the whole ballgame. Properties are fixed projections of a string under hash-based catalysis, so either the projection is hash-like — structure-to-function is white noise, an 11-step molecule is no better in expectation than a 3-step one, and NOBODY has any reason to go deep — or it is a readable projection (count of symbol A drives nutrition), in which case one degenerate optimum is found in week 1. The '3 hours of tuning' budgeted to find the middle is an open research problem, not a Saturday afternoon. Absent a structured structure-to-function map, chemistry discovery bottoms out once the shallow high-nutrition region is enumerated: 3-6 weeks per sea, not the claimed 2-4 months. RAF nesting is the one genuinely unbounded axis, but nested RAFs only MATTER if deeper products pay, which loops straight back to the gradient problem. The language side stops harder: they concede compositionality is a one-time transition per world, and over 6 legible dimensions of which 2-3 are load-bearing, the lexicon saturates within days of the transition. Honest model: each sea is a 4-8 week arc, and the project's longevity is a content pipeline, not depth.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six on chemistry — a hash function plus ligation/cleavage is genuinely unauthored, and the reaction network will surprise its author. But MEANING is entirely authored: the six property projections are the world's whole semantics, so the interesting subspace has exactly the dimensionality the designer typed, and he already predicts 2-3 will carry all the drama. The fatal risk is elsewhere and it is a VALIDITY problem, not an authoring one: Kirby-style iterated learning requires a fresh, capacity-limited learner each generation. Here every generation is the same frozen Qwen3-4B carrying English morphology priors, prompted. Nothing is reset; the bottleneck is not at the level the theory requires. Topographic similarity will rise because a pretrained LM composes by default, and the 'compositionality transition' push notification will be measuring Qwen's prior rather than this world's cultural evolution. The headline plot — topsim jumps, assembly index follows — is confounded by construction unless he ships a null control (identical pipeline with no bottleneck; and a scrambled-meaning control).

Compute honesty

Yes — the most honest of the six. Integer/hash chemistry genuinely does millions of reactions/sec on CPU; 6k short referential exchanges per generation is well under 30 minutes of GPU; RAF detection is genuinely polynomial and small; 4-6 seas concurrently is plausible. Haiku spend under $0.30/day is right. Sonification is free. No hidden datacenter anywhere in this design.

Nonsense risk

Highest of the six, and he half-admits it. Hash chemistry buys combinatorial novelty, not functional novelty — Sayama's actual reported result is unbounded growth in the SIZE of replicating higher-order entities, which is precisely novelty-without-meaning. Names attached to hash outputs are drivel with syllables. 'FIRST: a substance 11 steps deep, they call it kessa-thu-mar' is a meaningless headline unless depth correlates with something an agent can want. Everything downstream — trade, recipes-by-description, the linguist's memo — inherits whatever meaning the six projections carry and no more.

Claims to re-check

arXiv 2607.00233 EXISTS (Talebirad, Redman, Parsaee, Zaiane, 30 Jun 2026) but is materially mischaracterized: it is about MEMORY ARCHITECTURE (persistent private notebook vs stateless) in two-agent Lewis signaling games, not 'compositionality needs both discriminability and compression pressure' and not generational transmission. That claim is Kirby et al. 2015, echoed by Kouwenhoven's finding that LLMs go degenerate without compression pressure. Do not build the design's two-pressure justification on 2607.00233. PNAS 2025 (Jarvis, Klein, Rosman, Saxe; doi 10.1073/pnas.2509739123) is real, but its actual conclusion cuts AGAINST the design: iterated learning yields only 'weak systematic generalization' and requires an extremely large dataset — the opposite regime from 30 children x 200 exchanges. Sayama 2404.18027 is real (IEEE CEC 2024) but delivers unbounded growth in entity size, a weaker result than 'cheapest way to open-ended chemistry' implies. Assembly index: NP-complete to compute exactly, and contested as a selection detector (see Embertongue).

The one change that raises the ceiling

Kill the hash property projections. Derive properties from a cheap STRUCTURED map — fold the string with an HP-lattice-style rule and read nutrition/toxicity/adhesion/burn off features of the folded shape. Then similar strings usually behave similarly (exploration has a gradient, and a compositional name is genuinely PREDICTIVE, which is the exact thing the whole design exists to demonstrate) while rare folds flip behaviour (real discoveries, real poisonings, real cliffs). One change; it simultaneously gives the chemistry a reason to be deep and gives the language something worth being compositional about.

▸

Grist

Infinite Craft's unbounded combination space, but every verdict the LLM issues is compiled into a row of NUMBERS in a deterministic materials ledger and then never asked again — so the world is infinite in what can exist, and hard-physical in whether it helps you survive the winter. Recipes are secrets held by guilds, taught lossily, stolen, traded, and lost.

Not real depth
The world

A river valley with clay, timber, stone, ore-sand, reed, hide, tallow, grain, and salt. Tiled, deterministic, hourly tick. Everything that exists is a row in the KINDS ledger: {id, name, tags[], mass_per_unit, hardness, temp_stable_c, energy_density, workability, toxicity, decay_per_day, requires_tool_hardness, era_tier}. Agents act with a small verb set — combine(a, b, method, tool) where method ∈ {heat, strike, grind, soak, ferment, twist, cut, bury, mix} — plus gather, build, eat, teach, trade, hide, spy. Survival math is entirely numeric: kcal from energy_density, tool effectiveness from hardness ratio, shelter warmth from a structure's material properties, poisoning from toxicity. The LLM never touches survival math. It only ever answers one question, and only once per question: 'what KIND results from doing this to these?'

How invention actually happens

THE COURT OF KINDS. When an agent attempts combine(a, b, method, tool): look up the triple in the verdicts table. Hit (which after two weeks is >99% of attempts) → deterministic result, zero tokens, microseconds. Miss → the attempt is queued for the Court. Overnight, the queue is adjudicated in a Batch API run: Haiku 4.5 receives a CONSTITUTION (the world's physical laws, ~700 tokens, fixed forever, cached), the two full ledger rows, the method, the tool hardness, the ambient temperature, and the current era_tier, and must return either NOTHING_HAPPENS or a new ledger row under a strict JSON schema. Then MECHANICAL GATES reject bad verdicts before they ever enter the world: mass conservation within tolerance; energy_density cannot exceed max(inputs) by more than a fixed factor; hardness cannot exceed the hardest input unless the method is heat AND an ore tag is present; era_tier cannot exceed current_max_era + 1 (this is the anti-'they invented plasma' gate, and it is essential); name must not collide, must not contain banned modern vocabulary, must be derivable from the culture's existing morphemes. One repair retry, then NOTHING_HAPPENS. A surviving verdict is compiled into the ledger and is now physics, permanently, for every agent in every future run of that world. PRECEDENT AND DRIFT: monthly, re-adjudicate a random 1% of historical verdicts with a fresh call; if the new verdict contradicts the old, the OLD ruling stands (correct engineering: determinism beats freshness) and the contradiction is logged as a heresy — a visible drift metric, and in-world flavour. THE CULTURAL LAYER, which is what makes this a civilization: a verdict entering the ledger does not mean anyone KNOWS it. Knowledge of recipe R is held by specific agents with a mastery float. Recipes are taught (lossy: mastery decay, and with probability p the taught recipe has a wrong parameter — wrong temperature, wrong duration — which produces a DIFFERENT, sometimes better, verdict, which is itself an invention channel). Guilds form endogenously: when a recipe's kcal-per-labor-hour value exceeds a threshold, holders begin refusing to teach outside kin (a two-line rule that produces guild secrecy, apprenticeship, and industrial espionage as emergent behaviour). Recipes can be stolen by observation, traded, and permanently lost. Writing, once a low-decay legible medium exists in the ledger, turns recipes into objects that can be burned, buried, and rediscovered.

Why it doesn't bottom out

With |S| substances and |M| methods there are ~|S|^2 * |M| / 2 experiments, and |S| grows every time one succeeds — so each new kind adds ~2|S||M| new unexplored triples. The space grows superlinearly forever and Infinite Craft is the live proof that an LLM adjudicator never runs out of nameable outputs. The critical difference from Infinite Craft, and the reason this is not a novelty toy: an invention is not real because the LLM said a word, it is real because the numbers it emitted make a tribe eat more per labor-hour, and the SIM decides that. Most verdicts are useless kinds that nobody makes twice. HONEST BOTTOM: the ceiling is the model's imagination and its characteristic failure modes. Left unconstrained, an LLM adjudicator drifts toward pop-culture nouns and toward compressing 10 real steps into one magic word — Infinite Craft famously degenerates that way. The era_tier gate, the numeric conservation gates, and the morpheme-derivation naming rule are what hold it back, and they are a real, ongoing engineering burden. Also: verdict SPACE is unbounded but verdict QUALITY is not; after a few thousand kinds you will notice the model producing variations on a theme rather than new categories. That is the true ceiling and it is about taste, not tokens.

The ladder

WEEK 1: sharpened stick, fire, cooked grain, dried hide, cordage. Boring, correct, and it proves the loop. REAL RUNGS: charcoal (heat timber with a bury method — note the method set makes this discoverable rather than authored); a kiln as a BUILT structure whose material properties determine achievable temp_stable, which gates everything above; fired clay; a crucible; a copper-ish alloy once the ledger contains an ore and a flux; lime mortar; a lever/press (mass and hardness give you mechanical advantage without new physics); soap from tallow + ash-leachate (a genuinely surprising one that the Court gets right); parchment or fired tablets → WRITING → the recipe-book regime change; a guild that controls a crucible recipe and prices it; a rival tribe that reverse-engineers it from a stolen sample by attempting combinations the sample's tags suggest.
