WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT within honest gates: a water-powered mill, glass, a rudimentary chemistry of acids and salts with real numeric effects, a monetary standard emerging from the highest value-density kind, and — my favourite plausible outcome — a codified STANDARD: a written specification of a kind's parameters that lets two guilds in different towns make interchangeable parts. Standardisation is the actual hinge of industrial history and it falls naturally out of writing plus trade plus numeric tolerances.

What kills you

Winter, injury, disease with a seeded return period, and rival towns. Tools wear out (integrity decays with use), so a tribe must continuously reproduce its technology or fall back down the ladder — this is the single most important rule in the design, because it means technology must be MAINTAINED, not just discovered. Kcal per labor-hour is the master fitness number and it is what the phone card charts. A guild that hoards too hard gets outcompeted by a town that teaches freely and scales; a town that teaches freely gets its recipes stolen. That tension is not authored, it falls out of the numbers.

How the world changes

Worlds differ by their BASE ledger — which raw kinds exist and with what numbers. A world with no ore-sand can never reach metals and will go deep on ceramics, fiber and chemistry instead, and its tech tree will look genuinely, structurally different because the Court is adjudicating from a different starting set. That is the cheapest possible way to make multiple worlds mean something and it is very strong here. Within a world: seasons, floods that destroy stored goods (destroying stored goods is how you get regression stories), a trade caravan that arrives with a foreign kind whose recipe nobody knows, and web visitors — an anonymous visitor to the public site can leave one OMEN per day, a short phrase, which is deposited in the world as a portent that biases which combinations agents try next (implemented as a prior boost over triples whose tags match the omen's embedding). Visitors are cheap non-stationarity and they will absolutely try to make the tribe invent something stupid, which is the fun.

How it knows something is new

Four gates, ANDed down to a trickle. (1) A verdict that is not NOTHING_HAPPENS (already rare). (2) The kind gets MADE A SECOND TIME by someone other than its inventor — reproduction is the line between accident and technology, and it is exactly assembly theory's copy-number criterion. (3) The kind's introduction moves the town's kcal-per-labor-hour or winter-survival projection by more than 2% — an ECONOMICALLY significant invention. This is the best filter in any of these six designs because it is unarguable. (4) Assembly index (min steps from base ledger) exceeds the world record. Also fire on: guild formation, first theft, first written recipe, recipe extinction (last holder died), and heresy events from drift checking. Everything else goes in the log and never touches your phone. Expect 1-4 pushes a day.

On your phone

Card: 'Vetch Ford · Day 388 · 2 towns · 71 alive · kcal/labor-hr 4.1 ↑'. Headline: 'NEW KIND: greyglaze (assembly 7). Made twice. Winter survival +6%. Held by 3 people, all Orrin's kin — they have stopped teaching it.' Then the master sparkline: kcal per labor-hour, the one number that means 'is this civilization actually getting better'. Then a 3-row ledger diff. Then the chronicle sentence. Tap → the ledger as a searchable table (surprisingly fun to browse — it is a materials encyclopedia of a world that does not exist) and the recipe-knowledge graph showing who knows what. Second tab: THE COURT DOCKET — last night's rulings, including the rejected ones with their rejection reason. Reading the rejects is genuinely hilarious and it is free.

Your hardware

The local 4B does agent decision-making (what to try next, who to teach, whether to trade) with tight grammar-constrained outputs — 60-120 tokens per decision, a few thousand decisions a day, trivial for a 5070 at Q4. Optionally use the local 4B as a FIRST-PASS judge: it adjudicates the combination, and you only escalate to Haiku when the local verdict fails the mechanical gates or when the local model's logprob-confidence is low. That cuts Haiku volume by maybe 60% at the cost of a duller ledger; I would run Haiku-only for the first month to establish a good corpus of precedent, then switch to escalation. COST: the honest math. Novel adjudications actually attempted per day settle around 200-500 once the cache warms (agents only try what their culture suggests, which is a narrow beam). At 500/day via Batch API with a cached 700-token constitution: input ~500 x 1.1k = 550k @ $0.50/MTok = $0.28; output 500 x 250 = 125k @ $2.50/MTok = $0.31. Plus chronicle and drift checks: call it $0.70/day. Hard-cap it: a daily adjudication quota that, when exhausted, puts the Court IN RECESS — a diegetic rate limiter that shows on the phone as 'the Court sits again at dawn'. That is both honest engineering and good writing. GPU sits at maybe 20% duty cycle; you can run three valleys.

What you have to do

Zero required. The one thing worth doing occasionally (monthly, 20 minutes, and it is fun not chore-like) is reading the Court docket and editing the constitution — tightening a law because you saw a verdict you disliked. That is god-tier nudging in its purest form: you are amending physics.

First weekend

Saturday: the ledger, the verb set, the survival math, 12 agents, hunger, winter. Hard-code 8 base kinds and ZERO recipes. Saturday night: wire the Court with a 20-line constitution and no gates yet, let it rip for 200 sim-days, and read the ledger. It will be delightful and slightly broken — that is the delight moment, day one. Sunday: add the mechanical gates and the era_tier cap, watch the ledger get sane, add the second-time-made novelty filter, and put a single card on your phone via ntfy. You will end Sunday with a world that invented something you did not author and told your phone about it.

Six months in

A ledger of a few thousand kinds, of which maybe 200 are actually load-bearing technology that gets reproduced. Three valleys with structurally different tech because of different base ledgers. Guilds, at least one recipe monopoly and at least one recipe that died with its last holder in a flu winter. An omen guestbook full of strangers trying to make your tribe invent cheese. The kcal-per-labor-hour chart is a real, legible civilization curve with plateaus and jumps, and you can point at each jump and name it.

Where it stops (self-reported)

2-4 months before the ledger reads as variations rather than kinds — this is the sharpest of the six ceilings and it is a taste problem, not a tokens problem. Verdict drift and the model's pull toward anachronism require active gate maintenance; budget an evening a month. The Court's numeric attributes are also only as meaningful as your survival math consumes: if hardness only matters for one thing, the LLM's careful hardness numbers are decoration. Realistically only 4-6 of the ledger's attributes will ever be load-bearing, so define those well and treat the rest as flavour. Finally: this idea is the most dependent on paid tokens of the six, so if you ever want a fully-offline run, plan the local-4B-as-judge escalation path from day one.

Stack

Python sim + SQLite (kinds, verdicts, recipes, holders, guilds, trades, thefts, events). Anthropic Batch API for the nightly Court (submit at 02:00, poll, compile at 06:00 — the 24h SLA is irrelevant since results typically land in minutes-to-hours and the sim just stalls those experiments until dawn, which is diegetically perfect). JSON-schema-constrained output on the Haiku side; GBNF on the local side. FastAPI + vanilla-JS PWA over Tailscale Serve, nightly static export to GitHub Pages, ntfy push. Public read-only mirror with the omen guestbook (rate-limited, moderated by a cheap classifier call). Same shared spine as the other ideas: deterministic sim + SQLite event log + novelty scorer + PWA + push.

Verified

VERIFIED: Infinite Craft's mechanism is exactly per-pair caching with LLM fallback — Arthur O'Dwyer's analysis (quuxplusone.github.io/blog/2024/02/08/infinite-craft/) confirms the backend caches every pair so the same pairing always gives the same result globally and only NEW pairings hit the model; open clones exist and are readable: github.com/bufferhead-code/opencraft, github.com/CharlesFauman/infini-craft (the latter runs a local Llama for adjudication, which is the precedent for the local-first-pass judge). Anthropic pricing verified at anthropic.com/claude/haiku: Haiku 4.5 is $1/MTok in, $5/MTok out, prompt caching up to 90% off reads, Batch API 50% off both directions and stacks with caching. llama.cpp supports JSON-schema→GBNF conversion natively and the server exposes response_format (github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/tools/server/README.md), so both judges can be schema-locked. Assembly theory's copy-number criterion (Sharma et al., Nature 2023, arXiv 2206.02279) is the formal justification for the 'made a second time' novelty gate. W. Brian Arthur, The Nature of Technology (2009) — technologies are combinations of existing technologies, and new combinations enlarge the combinable set — is the theoretical frame; Kauffman's adjacent possible is the same claim from the biology side.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

The invention space is the model's memory of human technological history, and the design has built rails to walk it in order. era_tier <= current_max_era + 1 IS a hand-authored tech tree with the node names left blank for Haiku to fill in. The listed ladder — charcoal, kiln, fired clay, crucible, alloy, lime mortar, glass, water mill — is not a prediction about the sim; it is a recitation of what the model already knows comes next, and it will emerge in that order because the gate forbids any other order. Where it stops: when the era ladder outruns what the survival math can consume. He concedes only 4-6 attributes are load-bearing, so anything whose value is mechanical power, chemistry, information or institutions has nowhere to land numerically. Ledger reads as variations at 3-8 weeks; the ECONOMICALLY significant invention rate (his own filter #3, the best filter in the batch) goes near zero well before that. Note the second authoring channel: every anachronism he dislikes becomes a new gate rule, so six months in the constitution IS the tech tree, hand-written by him one rejection at a time.

Is the discovery real?

Maximal, and it is exactly the failure mode named in the brief — except the author is the pretraining corpus rather than the designer. This is Wikipedia's tech tree, revealed in an order the designer constrained to be historical, then filtered for whether it helps. To be fair, the filter is real and it is the good part: kcal/labor-hour as master metric, tool wear forcing continuous REPRODUCTION of technology, guild secrecy from a two-line threshold, recipes dying with their last holder, theft and reverse-engineering. Those are the best-specified social/economic dynamics of the six and they are genuinely emergent. Just do not call the ledger discovery: it is retrieval with an economic referee.

Compute honesty

Yes on tokens; the arithmetic checks out at roughly $0.60-0.70/day (~$250/yr) — cheap but the only design that stops working when payment stops. Two soft spots. (1) '200-500 novel adjudications/day once the cache warms' is an assumption, not a measurement; a curious-agent policy over |S|^2 x |M| triples can blow through it on any day the ledger jumps, and 'the Court is in recess' means the invention rate is set by a budget cap — a strange thing to have sitting in the fitness path. (2) The offline escalation path is a fiction: a 4B emitting calibrated hardness / energy_density / temp_stable_c for a novel material is producing plausible noise, and the mechanical gates check conservation, not correctness, so escalation mode silently degrades the ledger to random numbers that pass.

Nonsense risk

The names will be fine; the NUMBERS are the problem and this is what I would fix first. Each verdict is an independent LLM guess with no global consistency constraint. After 3,000 kinds you hold 3,000 uncalibrated hardness estimates produced in 3,000 different contexts, and the survival math takes RATIOS of them (tool hardness vs requires_tool_hardness). The ledger is not a physics, it is a scatter of vibes, and it will become quietly incoherent — tools that should work won't, soft materials will gate ore. Pairwise mass/energy gates cannot detect this class of failure, and it will look like bad luck rather than a bug.

Claims to re-check

Infinite Craft's per-pair caching mechanism and Haiku pricing are correct. The era_tier gate is circular: it prevents anachronism using an era label assigned by the very model whose anachronism it polices. Assembly-theory copy-number justification: the 'made a second time' heuristic is sound on its own merits, but AT's claim to distinguish selection from noise is contested (Zenil et al., PLOS Complex Systems; arXiv 2403.06629 / 2408.15108) and minimal assembly index is NP-complete — 'min steps from base ledger' is an upper bound from observed recipes. On Batch + prompt caching, Grist's note (they stack) is right and Embertongue's contradicting note is wrong.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Stop letting the model emit properties. Restrict the Court's output to COMPOSITION + PROCESS — a vector over ~10 elements/phases plus temperature, duration, and mechanical work — and compute every numeric property deterministically from that composition. The ledger becomes one consistent physics, hardness ratios mean something, anachronism becomes structurally impossible (you cannot reach steel without reaching the composition, so the era gate can be deleted), the offline 4B path becomes viable because the model is only proposing a plausible composition, and the LLM is demoted from oracle to namer and chemist's-intuition proposer, which is the only job it is actually good at.

▸

Kithmind

Each tribe's culture is not text — it is a QLoRA adapter, trained every week on only the trajectories that survived. Tribes diverge into genuinely different minds you can measure, teaching is weight-level distillation through a bottleneck, conquest is model merging, and a dead civilization's adapter lies in a barrow waiting to be dug up and partially remembered.

Not real depth
The world

Deliberately modest and hard: a 64x64 tiled valley, hourly tick, deterministic, with a small but real survival economy — forage, hunt, store, build, keep warm, birth, teach, raid, trade. Six to ten tribes. The SYMBOLIC technology layer is borrowed wholesale from Embertongue or Grist (a growing library of named programs, or a materials ledger) — you need a visible tech tree, because weights alone are not watchable. The novel layer is the MIND. All tribes share one frozen base (Qwen3-4B-Instruct, Q4 for inference, 4-bit for training) and each tribe owns a LoRA adapter, rank 16, maybe 40MB. The adapter is queried for every non-trivial agent decision, always grammar-constrained. Two tribes in the same situation with the same knowledge will act differently, and the difference is in the weights, which is exactly what 'culture' means and is very hard to fake with prompts.

How invention actually happens

Two coupled engines. SYMBOLIC (the visible tech tree): whichever engine you borrow — library compression or the ledger — produces the named technologies. SUBCONSCIOUS (the novel part): STaR / rejection-sampling self-training with SURVIVAL as the verifier. Every week of sim time, for each tribe, collect the decision trajectories from agents who are still alive and whose tribe's stores went UP, filter to trajectories that mechanically passed (the child lived, the tool worked, the winter was survived), and QLoRA-finetune that tribe's adapter on them with Unsloth. New checkpoint must beat the old on a FROZEN held-out benchmark of 200 seeded survival scenarios, scored by sim outcome (days survived, kcal stored) — not by loss, and never by wall-clock. Fails the gate → discard, keep the ancestor, log it as 'a generation that learned nothing'. TEACHING AS DISTILLATION THROUGH A BOTTLENECK: a child tribe (a splinter settlement) does not inherit the parent adapter. It trains a fresh adapter on ONLY the transcripts the elders produced while teaching — a small, biased, deliberately incomplete sample of the parent's competence. That is iterated learning implemented at the weight level, and it guarantees generational loss and drift, which is the whole point. CONQUEST AND SYNCRETISM: when tribe A absorbs tribe B, merge the adapters (TIES-merge or DARE, weighted by population) — the resulting culture is a real hybrid with real interference artifacts, which is a much more honest model of syncretism than concatenating two prompt strings. BARROWS: when a tribe goes extinct its adapter is archived, not deleted. A later tribe that excavates the ruin gets a low-weight partial merge (alpha 0.1-0.2) — mechanically, they start doing things the old people did, without knowing why. That is archaeology and revival as a weight operation and I have not seen anyone do it.

Why it doesn't bottom out

I will be straight with you: the WEIGHT layer is not unbounded and claiming otherwise would be dishonest. Adapter space is vast but capability on a narrow sim saturates fast. The unboundedness in this project comes from the borrowed symbolic engine (library compression, which is genuinely recursive) — the adapters supply DIVERGENCE, not depth. And divergence is genuinely combinatorial in an interesting way: with 8 tribes, a merge/split/extinct/revive lineage process over months produces a phylogeny whose branch structure never repeats, and behavioural distance between tribes on a fixed probe set is a real, growing, measurable quantity. So: unbounded in cultural PHYLOGENY, bounded in individual capability. Frame it as a culture-divergence engine and it is honest and still exciting; frame it as a capability engine and it will disappoint you by week 6.

The ladder

WEEK 1: all tribes are identical because they share the base model; the first adapter round is where it gets interesting — after one finetune the tribes are already measurably different on the probe set even though nothing in the world differed much. REAL RUNGS: measurable behavioural divergence (KL between tribe policies on 100 fixed probe states) crossing a threshold; a tribe that develops a stable BIAS you can name from behaviour alone (hoarders vs. sharers, cautious vs. exploratory) and that you did not prompt; a splinter tribe whose bottlenecked adapter is visibly a degraded, superstitious version of its parent; a merge that produces a genuinely weird hybrid; a barrow excavation that partially restores an extinct behaviour pattern; and — the deep one — a tribe whose adapter has internalised a TABOO (it reliably avoids an action that once killed many of its ancestors, even in situations where that action is now safe), which is maladaptive culture measurable as a fitness cost. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT: a probe-set phylogeny with 20+ nodes that matches the historical lineage tree you never told it about; and a documented case of two tribes whose merged adapter performs WORSE than either parent, i.e. a cultural collision you can point at with numbers.

What kills you

Standard survival plus raiding plus the held-out benchmark gate on every adapter update. Crucially, tribes with worse adapters actually die, so weight-space selection has real teeth. Model collapse is a genuine failure mode here and it is also, mechanically, a civilizational decline story — a tribe whose training corpus became too self-similar loses behavioural diversity, stops exploring, and gets outcompeted. You can literally measure entropy of its action distribution and predict its fall.

How the world changes

New valleys with different resource layouts and hazard tempos, entered by migration. The interesting bit: an adapter trained in a forest valley is measurably WRONG in a coastal one, so migration produces a visible competence crash followed by a re-training recovery, which is exactly the arc of a people arriving somewhere new. You can also do a 'dark age': hold adapter training for 20 sim-generations and watch the symbolic library and the weights decay together.

How it knows something is new

(1) Behavioural distance jumps: nightly, run every tribe's adapter over a FIXED 200-state probe set (cheap — 200 short generations per tribe), embed the action distributions, and push when any tribe's distance from its own past self, or from the population centroid, jumps past a threshold. This is a proper, quantitative 'a culture changed' signal and it is the heart of the project. (2) Benchmark gate outcomes: pushes on 'the Weft learned nothing this generation' or 'the Kell's new mind beat its ancestor by 14%'. (3) Symbolic-layer novelty inherited from the borrowed engine (assembly index, first-ever programs). (4) Merge/split/extinction/excavation events. Push volume naturally low: maybe 1-2 a day.

On your phone

Card: 'Kell Valley · Day 244 · 6 tribes · 118 alive'. Headline is always a MIND event, not a world event: 'The Weft trained last night and FAILED the gate — their new mind was worse than their grandparents'. They keep the old one. Third failure running.' Below it, the one visual that carries this whole project: a small dendrogram of the six tribes by behavioural distance, drawn from last night's probe run, with branch lengths that grow over weeks — you can see cultures separating at a glance, and it is legible in three seconds. Then two numbers: population and mean kcal/labor-hour per tribe as tiny bars, plus a red dot on any tribe whose action-entropy is collapsing (the early-warning sign of a culture going rigid). Then the chronicle sentence. Tap → the full phylogeny with merges, splinters, extinctions and barrow excavations drawn as edges; tap a tribe → its probe-set fingerprint versus its ancestor, with the 5 probe states where they most disagree shown as literal quoted answers, which is the most fun screen in the project because you are reading two cultures answer the same question differently.

Your hardware

THIS IS THE ONE WITH REAL 8GB PAIN, and you should know it going in. Inference: base Qwen3-4B Q4 + N LoRA adapters is fine — llama.cpp supports hot-swapping LoRAs, or you keep 3-4 hot and swap the rest, ~3-4GB total. Training: Unsloth 4-bit QLoRA on a 4B fits comfortably in 8GB (their published table has 8B at ~6GB and 11B at ~7.5GB for 4-bit QLoRA), BUT you must stop the inference server during training — you cannot hold both. So the schedule is: sim runs 23h with the server up; at 04:00 the sim pauses, the server unloads, one or two tribes train for 20-40 minutes each, the gate benchmark runs, the server reloads. Round-robin so each tribe trains every 4-5 days. Blackwell sm_120 is a real install hazard: stock PyTorch wheels historically top out below sm_120 and you need the CUDA-matched wheels (uv's --torch-backend=auto, or the Blackwell-specific install path Unsloth documents) — budget an evening for this and do it BEFORE building anything else, because if it does not work the whole idea is dead. Thermals: training on a laptop at 04:00 is fine; just never let wall-clock into the fitness signal (the gate is sim outcomes, so you are safe by construction). Haiku: chronicle plus a weekly 'anthropologist's report' comparing tribes' probe-set behaviour — maybe 30 calls/day, well under $0.30.

What you have to do

Zero required, but this one has the highest chance of needing a rescue: if adapters collapse, you intervene. Realistically an hour every couple of weeks tuning LoRA rank, learning rate, and the trajectory filter. Be honest with yourself that this is the least chore-free of the six.

First weekend

Weekend 1 is an install weekend and you should accept that: get Unsloth training a 4B QLoRA on the Blackwell laptop, end to end, on toy data. Then a minimal sim (hunger, forage, winter, 2 tribes), collect 2000 decision trajectories with the base model, filter to survivors, train two DIFFERENT adapters on two tribes' data, and run both over 50 probe states. The delight moment is the diff: two minds, same base, measurably different answers to the same question, and the difference came from who survived. That is achievable in a weekend IF the install cooperates.

Six months in

A phylogeny of 20-40 adapters with a real lineage tree, merges, splinters, extinctions and two or three excavations. A behavioural distance matrix you can render as a dendrogram and that actually recapitulates history. A couple of named cultural traits that emerged with no prompting. Several hundred MB of barrows. The weekly anthropologist's report is the artifact you would show someone.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The shortest capability ceiling of the six: a 4B self-training on a narrow sim saturates in roughly 4-8 weeks, after which the gate rejects most checkpoints and the charts flatten. What KEEPS going is divergence and phylogeny, which stays interesting for months but is a subtler pleasure than 'they invented the kiln'. Model collapse (documented as a cultural-evolution phenomenon in arXiv 2605.23054) is a live risk, not a theoretical one — expect at least one tribe to degenerate into repetitive behaviour and plan the entropy monitor from the start. Operationally this is the highest-maintenance idea and the one most likely to break on a driver update. My honest recommendation: build Embertongue or Grist FIRST, get the world working, and add Kithmind as a second-year layer on top — the adapters are much more interesting when there is already a technology tree for them to be differently good at.

Stack

Sim: Python + SQLite as elsewhere. Inference: llama.cpp server with LoRA hot-swap, or vLLM if you want multi-LoRA serving (tighter on 8GB — llama.cpp is the safer call). Training: Unsloth + peft, 4-bit QLoRA, rank 16, on trajectory JSONL. Merging: mergekit (TIES/DARE) or 40 lines of task-arithmetic on the LoRA deltas directly — for LoRA deltas the arithmetic is simple enough to write yourself and you will understand it better. Probe-set evaluation harness with fixed seeds. PWA + Tailscale + GitHub Pages snapshot + ntfy. Adapters and their lineage stored as files with a manifest table — the barrow is literally a directory.

Verified

VERIFIED: Unsloth's published minimum-VRAM table has 8B at ~6GB and 11B at ~7.5GB for 4-bit QLoRA, so a 4B on 8GB has real headroom; their December-2025 release claims ~3x speedup and ~30% less VRAM from new Triton kernels and padding-free packing. Blackwell/sm_120 is a documented install hazard — stock stable PyTorch wheels have lagged sm_120 and installs need the CUDA-matched path (uv --torch-backend=auto or Unsloth's Blackwell section); do not assume pip install works. STaR-style rejection-sampling self-training with a mechanical verifier is the same skeleton as the builder's Ouroboros idea; the open-ended-archive framing (keep every ancestor, allow revival from the archive rather than only the current best) is from the Darwin Gödel Machine, arXiv 2505.22954, repo github.com/jennyzzt/dgm — its central empirical lesson is that the ARCHIVE plus open-ended exploration beats greedy hill-climbing, which is exactly why barrows should be revivable. Model collapse as cultural evolution: arXiv 2605.23054 — treat it as a mechanic, not just a bug. Iterated learning at the weight level (resetting/retraining a learner each generation induces easier-to-learn, more compositional representations) is supported by the PNAS 2025 deep-linear-network result (doi 10.1073/pnas.2509739123) and the LLM iterated-learning literature. Multi-agent-society precedent for emergent specialization and cultural memes: Project Sid, arXiv 2411.00114 (PIANO architecture; specialization emerged only WITH social awareness — a strong argument for making tribe minds differ).

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

He is honest that weights are not a depth engine; I will go further and say the advertised phenomena mostly will not appear. A rank-16 LoRA finetuned on a few thousand short grammar-constrained decisions produced by its OWN base model, filtered by survival, is a weak nudge on an already-fixed policy. What gets measured after round 1 is drift — divergence driven by sampling noise in each tribe's trajectory set — and there is no specified way to distinguish that from culture. The named payoffs are the least likely parts: a 'taboo' requires state-conditional avoidance that generalizes counterfactually to newly-safe situations, which is a capability claim a 4B+LoRA on a few thousand examples will not satisfy. Hoarder-vs-sharer might appear weakly because it is a one-dimensional prior shift. His 4-8 week saturation estimate is generous; the gate will start rejecting checkpoints within 2-3 rounds, which the design cleverly reframes as 'a generation that learned nothing' — a narratively perfect way to never notice the engine has stopped. Structurally: this idea has NO invention engine, it borrows one, so it cannot answer the brief at all. It answers a narrower question (do cultures diverge measurably) rather well.

Is the discovery real?

Low on the mind layer — genuinely unauthored, the weights really do differ, and merge/barrow/excavation are real operations rather than prose, which is more than any prompt-based 'culture' can say. High on the READOUT layer: a nightly Haiku 'anthropologist's report' narrating probe-set diffs will manufacture compelling cultural personality out of noise every single night, and he will believe it. This is the design most at risk of self-deception, and the risk sits in the interpretation, not the mechanism.

Compute honesty

The only one with real 8GB pain and he says so — credit for that. Unsized costs: the gate is 200 seeded scenarios SCORED BY SIM OUTCOME, i.e. 200 full rollouts x many LLM decisions each, per candidate checkpoint, plus a nightly 200-state probe run x 6 tribes with adapter swaps. That is plausibly more GPU-hours than the training itself and it lands inside the window when the sim is paused. Round-robin at 20-40 min/tribe means each tribe updates every 4-5 days, so ~35 generations in six months — a thin phylogeny for the dendrogram he wants. llama.cpp LoRA hot-swap works but quantized-base + adapter has quality caveats; multi-LoRA vLLM on 8GB is not realistic (he agrees). TIES/DARE over LoRA deltas from a shared base is arithmetically fine.

Nonsense risk

Not drivel — outputs are grammar-constrained sim decisions. The risk is the semantics of the METRIC: behavioural distance between adapters is uninterpretable without a null model. Train two adapters on random splits of the SAME tribe's data and show within-tribe distance is much smaller than between-tribe distance; without that permutation control, every number on the phone card and every line of the anthropologist's memo is unfalsifiable.

Claims to re-check

Blackwell sm_120 PyTorch lag is OUTDATED — CUDA 12.8 wheels have shipped sm_120 since 2025, so 'budget an evening and if it fails the idea is dead' over-weights a solved problem. Unsloth's VRAM table is roughly right; the December-2025 3x/30% claims are unverifiable marketing. arXiv 2605.23054 'Model Collapse as Cultural Evolution' (Guo, Wu, Yiu, 21 May 2026) is real and actually SUPPORTS the design better than cited: its finding is that compositionality follows a non-monotonic trajectory under self-training and is sustained only by TASK-GROUNDED filtering, not random filtering — that is a direct argument for the survival gate, so cite it as justification, not merely as a risk. PNAS 2025 iterated learning establishes only weak systematic generalization at very large data scale; do not lean on it for weight-level iterated learning at this scale. Project Sid (2411.00114) is a non-peer-reviewed industry report with heavily scaffolded emergent-religion/specialization claims — weak support for 'make tribe minds differ'.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Change the training objective. Imitating your own surviving trajectories is self-distillation on a satiable task: it saturates in two or three rounds and collapse is the documented endpoint. Train instead on a dense, non-satiable objective — next-state and outcome PREDICTION over sim transitions the tribe has observed (a tribe-specific world model) — and keep survival only as the acceptance gate. Prediction error never runs out, it improves planning rather than mimicking it, the corpus is ~100x larger, and tribes that have lived in different places end up with genuinely different world models, which is a far better mechanistic account of culture than copying whoever failed to die. Ship the within-tribe null control in the same commit.

▸

Marginalia

A civilization whose entire technology lives in physical, decaying, hand-copied texts — so the mutation operator of its evolution is the scribal error, and the selection operator is whether the misread recipe kills you. Strangers on the internet can drop one line a day into the world, and you will watch a joke become scripture and then become a working technique.

Ceilinged6.2
The world

A river valley of three or four settlements, deterministic hourly tick, ordinary hard survival economy (kcal, warmth, injury, childbirth, seasons, floods). What is unusual is where knowledge lives. Every piece of knowledge is a TEXT OBJECT: {content (a program in a survival DSL, plus prose gloss), medium_id, location, legibility 0..1, decay_per_day, copy_of_id, generation}. Media are physical and differ: bark (decay 0.01/day), fired tablet (0.0001), hide (0.002), stone (~0). A text exists at a place. To use knowledge, an agent must be AT the text, with enough light, and be literate (a learned skill with a mastery float), OR have it in personal memory from having been taught (also lossy, also decaying). Copying is an action costing 3-8 labor-hours that produces a child text with errors. Nothing about competence is free-floating: an illiterate valley with a burned archive is genuinely back at zero, and the sim will show you that.

How invention actually happens

COPY ERROR + INTERPRETATION, which together form a complete evolutionary algorithm over a program population. VARIATION: the scribal error model, drawn from real paleography and delightfully cheap to implement — dittography (a line copied twice), haplography (a line skipped), homoeoteleuton (the eye jumps between two lines with the same ending — devastating and very real), transposition, numeral corruption (a temperature or duration digit changes), and marginal gloss absorption (a later copyist's note gets copied INTO the body as if it were part of the recipe, which is historically the single most productive corruption there is). Error rates scale with legibility, scribe mastery, light, and fatigue. HEREDITY: copy_of_id gives you an exact stemma — a manuscript family tree — for free. SELECTION: a corrupted recipe is executed in the hard sim. Mostly it fails or kills someone. Occasionally the dropped step or the changed numeral produces something BETTER, and that variant gets copied more because it works. INTERPRETATION, which is where the LLM earns its keep and where genuine novelty enters: when an agent reads a damaged or corrupted text, the local Qwen3-4B must turn ambiguous marks into an EXECUTABLE program, grammar-constrained by the DSL. It is filling gaps, guessing at a smudged numeral, reconciling a gloss with the body. Interpretation of noise is a creative act, and it is a cheap, high-yield source of variation that is unlike random mutation — it is BIASED toward things that make sense given the reader's prior knowledge, which is exactly how real reinterpretation works. Then reality adjudicates, with no appeal. CANON: texts accumulate copy counts. When a text's copy count and geographic spread cross thresholds it is flagged CANONICAL, and canonical texts get copied preferentially (a rich-get-richer rule with one line of code), which produces a canon, an apocrypha, and eventually a schism when two settlements' canonical versions of the same ancestor text have diverged past a similarity threshold. Institutions — a scriptorium building that lowers error rates, a scribal caste, a rule against copying non-canonical texts — emerge from cost-benefit thresholds, not from authored content.

Why it doesn't bottom out

It is an evolutionary algorithm over programs, with a mutation operator (scribal error), an interpretation operator (a language model filling gaps), and a fitness function (survival). Program space is unbounded; the population is a library. What keeps it from converging is that the fitness landscape moves (seasons, floods, migration) and that the interpretation operator injects structured, prior-biased variation rather than white noise. HONEST BOTTOM: this is hill-climbing with a clever mutation operator, and hill-climbing on a fixed landscape plateaus. Marginalia is genuinely deeper on TRANSMISSION than on INVENTION — the tech tree it grows will be shallower than Embertongue's unless you pair them. And you should pair them: run Embertongue's DSL and its nightly Stitch compression as the invention engine, and Marginalia as the transmission layer. Then the abstractions are minted by compression AND corrupted by copying, and you have the best of both. As a standalone, expect the depth to come from the corpus dynamics, not the technology height, and price your expectations accordingly.

The ladder

WEEK 1: agents scratch marks on bark, most of it rots, someone copies something and gets it slightly wrong, and the first person dies of a misread. REAL RUNGS: the first text that survives its author; the first COPY that outperforms its parent (push-worthy, always); the discovery that fired tablets outlast bark, which reorganises the whole economy around clay; a scriptorium; a canonical text with 20+ descendants and a visible stemma; a settlement-level schism from divergent canon; the first CRITICAL act — an agent who owns two divergent copies and reconciles them into a third (textual criticism, emerging because reconciliation measurably improves outcomes); an INDEX (a text whose content is the locations of other texts, which mechanically multiplies retrieval and is a genuine phase change); and a marginal gloss absorbed into a recipe body that turns out to be an improvement, which is my favourite possible event in any of these six designs. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT: a library building whose destruction in a flood causes a measurable multi-generation technological regression that the survivors partially recover from by collecting outlying copies from other settlements — i.e. you get to watch a dark age and a renaissance, mechanically, with numbers.

What kills you

Ordinary mortality plus a very specific pressure: knowledge that is not copied dies with its medium, and copying costs labor-hours that could have been food. So a settlement is constantly choosing between eating and remembering, which is a real and beautiful tension and it is one line of the labor allocator. Between settlements: the one with better transmission infrastructure recovers faster from shocks, and shocks are frequent. Texts themselves are under selection — copy count is fitness, and legibility, usefulness, and brevity all raise it.

How the world changes

Floods (destroy caches, and are the engine of dark ages), fires, and migration to new valleys where the ecology differs so inherited recipes are wrong. The strongest world-change lever here is MEDIUM: introduce a new material with a different decay rate and the entire information economy reorganises. VISITORS AS PERTURBATION, and this is the best public hook of the six: the public site has a guestbook where an anonymous visitor may leave one line per day. That line is deposited into the world as a SKYFALL TABLET at a random location — a stone with words on it that no one wrote. Agents may find it, fail to read it, copy it, misread it, gloss it, and canonise it. The sim treats its content as a text like any other, so a visitor's line can end up in a recipe body via gloss absorption. You will get scripture from strangers, and it costs you nothing but a rate limiter and a moderation call.

How it knows something is new

(1) A copy outperforms its parent on the sim's fitness measure — the core event, always pushed. (2) Canon formation: copy count crosses threshold, or a text hits 50 descendants. (3) Schism: two settlements' canonical versions of a common ancestor drop below a similarity threshold (normalized edit distance on the AST, not the prose). (4) A 'classic': a text transmitted N generations with fewer than k mutations — rare and remarkable, and worth a push. (5) Skyfall canonisation: a visitor's line entered a canonical text. (6) Assembly index of the deepest reliably-reproducible artifact, as elsewhere. (7) Bayesian surprise over everything else. This idea has the richest and most legible novelty signals of the six because textual events are inherently narrative.

On your phone

Card: 'Ashwater · Day 903 · 4 settlements · 88 alive'. Headline: 'A MISCOPY WORKS. Line 3 of *emberkeep* was skipped by a tired scribe; the result cooks faster. 6 copies of the error now exist; 2 of the original.' Then: canon size, literacy rate, and total surviving text-mass sparklines (text-mass is a gorgeous civilizational health metric — it drops during floods and famines). Then one line: 'Skyfall tablet from visitor "hannah" was inscribed into the Book of Rains.' Tap → the STEMMA VIEWER, a manuscript family tree of any text, with diffs between versions highlighted, which is the single most binge-able screen in any of these designs. You will lose an hour to it.

Your hardware

Very light. The local 4B does interpretation (read damaged text → emit program, grammar-constrained, ~150 output tokens) and short teaching dialogues. Interpretations happen maybe a few thousand times a day; at 4B Q4 on a 5070 with -np 6 that is well under an hour of GPU. Everything else — copying, decay, stemma tracking, similarity metrics — is CPU string work and is free. 64GB RAM means the entire corpus with full version history stays in memory. Haiku: the daily chronicle written IN THE VOICE OF A LATER HISTORIAN working from the surviving texts (which is a genuinely great constraint — give it only the texts that survived, not the ground truth, so the chronicle is itself lossy and sometimes WRONG, and you can diff the chronicle against the sim's truth for extra delight), plus moderation of guestbook lines. ~50 calls/day, well under $0.30. You could run four valleys.

What you have to do

Zero. Optional god-nudges: burn an archive, flood a settlement, gift a new medium, or drop your own skyfall tablet. The guestbook means OTHER people supply the nudges, which is the point.

First weekend

Saturday: the survival sim, the DSL, texts as physical objects with decay, and the copy action with three error types. No LLM. Sunday: give agents a starting text that works, let them copy it for 200 sim-days, and render the stemma. The delight moment is watching a family tree of a recipe form, seeing branches die, and finding the one branch where a dropped line accidentally improved things. Sunday afternoon: add LLM interpretation of damaged texts and watch it invent readings. Very achievable.

Six months in

A corpus of thousands of texts with a deep, browsable stemma. A canon, an apocrypha, at least one schism, at least one dark age with a partial recovery. A guestbook-derived scripture that regular visitors recognise and contribute to. The historian's chronicle, which is sometimes wrong in interesting ways because the evidence rotted. A shoebox of receipt-printer strips.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Standalone, the TECHNOLOGY plateaus in roughly 6-10 weeks because copy-error hill-climbing exhausts the local optima of whatever DSL you shipped; the CORPUS dynamics stay interesting much longer (6+ months) because they are driven by mortality, geography and visitors, which do not converge. So the honest description is: a great transmission simulator with a mediocre invention engine, which is why the strong play is to bolt it onto Embertongue's compression engine and let the two operate on the same DSL. Second honest note: the interpretation step can produce garbage that is neither interesting nor fatal, and you will need a quality floor (reject interpretations that fail static type checks) or the corpus fills with noise. Third: guestbook moderation is a real ongoing obligation the moment the site gets any traffic — automate it on day one, not after.

Stack

Python sim + SQLite (texts, copies with copy_of_id, media, readings, literacy, settlements, canon_flags, guestbook). The DSL and executor shared with Embertongue (this is the shared-substrate argument in action — one DSL, two projects). Error model: ~120 lines. Stemma: recursive CTE in SQLite, rendered as a d3-less hand-rolled canvas tree in the PWA (your home turf). Similarity: normalized tree edit distance on ASTs. llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B with GBNF. Public mirror on GitHub Pages with a tiny serverless guestbook (or a Tailscale Funnel endpoint with a rate limit and a cheap Haiku moderation call). ntfy for push. RECEIPT PRINTER: this is the idea that most deserves one — a cheap ESC/POS thermal printer on USB, printing one text per morning (the day's most-copied passage, with its corruptions marked) on a strip of paper you find with your coffee. That is the physical artifact, and for a manuscript civilization it is exactly right.

Verified

VERIFIED CONTEXT: iterated learning / cultural transmission with a bottleneck is the mechanism this idea mechanises — Kirby's framework, plus PNAS 2025 'Compositionality and systematicity emerge from iterated learning in deep linear networks' (doi 10.1073/pnas.2509739123) and 'From Signals to Structure' (arXiv 2607.00233), which together establish that transmission NOISE plus a learnability pressure is a generative force, not just degradation. Generative agents' memory-stream/reflection architecture (Park et al., arXiv 2304.03442) is the ancestor of the per-agent memory here, but note that the original Smallville study cost thousands of dollars in API calls; Affordable Generative Agents (arXiv 2402.02053) and Lyfe Agents (arXiv 2310.02172) report 30-100x cost reductions via aggressive caching and cheap policies — the design principle I have applied throughout is that the LLM should be called only at the moment of INTERPRETATION, never for bookkeeping. Program-population evolution with library compression as the complementary engine: DreamCoder (PLDI 2021) + Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, pip stitch_core). Assembly index for artifact depth: Sharma et al., Nature 2023 (arXiv 2206.02279). Scribal error types (dittography, haplography, homoeoteleuton) are standard textual-criticism categories and are trivially implementable; stemmatics gives you the family-tree rendering for free. llama.cpp GBNF grammars keep every interpretation type-valid: github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/grammars/README.md.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Correctly self-diagnosed, and the correct diagnosis is severe: this is hill-climbing whose mutation operator is line-level edits to ASTs. Program space has no smooth genotype-phenotype map — a dropped line is almost always type-valid and useless — so the beneficial-mutation rate sits orders of magnitude below what evolutionary intuition suggests. Worse, the beneficial mutations you DO get are overwhelmingly deletions of redundant steps: efficiency, not capability. The promised recurring headline ('A MISCOPY WORKS') arrives a handful of times, says 'it cooks faster', and the technology curve is flat by week 4-8 at whatever local optima the shipped DSL contains. LLM interpretation adds prior-biased variation, which beats white noise, but it is variation toward what the model expects a recipe to say — regression toward plausibility is a contracting operator, not an expanding one. What genuinely does NOT converge: stemma structure, canon/apocrypha, schism, literacy rate, text-mass. Those are low-order statistical processes (preferential attachment, drift, spatial isolation) that reliably produce structure and keep producing it past six months. This is the design most likely to actually deliver its promised screens — they are just philological screens, not technological ones.

Is the discovery real?

Mixed, and worth being blunt. The corruption operator is genuinely unauthored and is the best mutation model in the six — dittography, haplography, homoeoteleuton, numeral corruption and especially marginal-gloss absorption are real, cheap, and structurally productive. But the INSTITUTIONS are authored: 'a scriptorium emerges from cost-benefit thresholds' means he wrote a scriptorium building with an error-rate modifier and a cost and agents found it worth building; canon is a rich-get-richer rule he wrote; schism is a threshold he chose on tree-edit distance. These will feel emergent and are mostly parameterized authorship. And the base recipes are borrowed outright — Marginalia has no invention engine at all, only a transmission engine, exactly as stated.

Compute honesty

Yes, genuinely light and correctly costed: a few thousand 150-token interpretations/day is well under an hour of GPU, and decay/copying/stemma are CPU string work. One undersized item: normalized tree edit distance is roughly O(n^2) per pair and schism detection wants it pairwise over a growing corpus — cache or sample aggressively or it becomes the CPU hot spot by month two. The historian-chronicler-restricted-to-surviving-texts idea is free and is the best writing constraint in any of the six documents.

Nonsense risk

Low-moderate. Type-checked interpretations keep the corpus executable and the prose glosses stay anchored to programs. The real hazard is pollution by interpretations that are neither interesting nor fatal (he flags it): you need a UTILITY floor, not just a static type check, or the stemma viewer fills with noise variants and the schism metric starts firing on garbage lineages. Also note the risk that the most-cited emergent events (canon, schism) are threshold artifacts — vary the thresholds and confirm the phenomena persist before believing them.

Claims to re-check

Same iterated-learning caveats as Saltmark: PNAS 2025 (doi 10.1073/pnas.2509739123) shows only WEAK systematic generalization requiring very large data, and arXiv 2607.00233 is about memory architecture in two-agent Lewis games, not 'transmission noise plus a learnability pressure is a generative force' — that claim traces to Kirby et al. 2015. The generative-agents cost figure and the AGA (2402.02053) / Lyfe (2310.02172) reduction claims are roughly right, and the derived principle (call the LLM only at interpretation) is the soundest engineering instinct in the whole batch. Scribal error taxonomy is accurate paleography. Unstated risk: the skyfall guestbook is the best public hook here and moderation is a permanent adversarial obligation — 'a cheap classifier call' is not sufficient for a public write endpoint feeding a corpus you publish.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make texts carry CLAIMS about the world, not just recipes. Let a text assert a testable proposition — a threshold, a numeral, a condition ('clay holds at four hands of heat') — and let agents test claims against the physics. Then copying, reconciliation of two divergent copies, and index-building do real epistemic work: the corpus becomes a growing, testable model of the world instead of a population of programs stuck at a local optimum, textual criticism becomes measurably profitable instead of a scripted rung, and depth is bounded by the physics rather than by DSL local optima. It also makes the lossy historian's chronicle diffable against ground truth in a way that means something.

▸

The Ark of Constants

The unbounded space here is not the tech tree — it is the space of WORLDS. An LLM writes new physics modules as code, a mechanical filter keeps only the ones that are survivable-but-not-trivial, and civilizations migrate between them carrying an ark of knowledge, most of which turns out to be wrong. What they keep doing anyway becomes ritual, and ritual has a measurable cost.

Ceilinged
The world

A world is a Python module implementing a narrow, frozen interface: resource fields and their regeneration, a reaction/threshold table over material properties, hazard schedules, seasonality, and a small set of affordances. Everything else — agents, bodies, the DSL, hunger, death, teaching, the library — is SHARED and fixed across all worlds. So a world is a few hundred lines of constants and rules, and the space of worlds is the space of those programs. Worlds are generated, validated, frozen, and then absolutely deterministic forever. Between worlds are GATES; a civilization can send an ARK through a gate: a chosen subset of its library, its lexicon, its written texts, and a handful of living elders. The rest is left behind and is playable later as a ruin.

How invention actually happens

TWO LEVELS, and the outer one is the point. INNER (per world): whatever engine you like — I would use Embertongue's Stitch-compression library, so within a world the tribe genuinely invents. OUTER (across worlds): OMNI-EPIC applied to physics. Haiku writes a candidate world module as CODE, given (a) the fixed interface, (b) a description of the archive of existing worlds and what makes them different, and (c) an explicit instruction to produce something LEARNABLE and INTERESTING relative to the current civilization's competence. Then MECHANICAL VALIDATION, which is what makes this honest rather than vibes: the module must import, must be deterministic under replay (run it twice from a seed and diff), must be survivable — a scripted baseline agent must last at least N days — and must NOT be trivial — the same baseline must NOT thrive without inventing anything. Fail → one repair round with the traceback, then discard. Pass → the world is frozen, hashed, and added to the archive. To stop the archive from converging to desert/ice/volcano variants, keep it as a MAP-Elites grid over a behavioural characterization computed by RUNNING the baseline, not by reading the prose: axes like (resource sparsity, hazard tempo, minimum reaction depth to reach a survivable calorie source, material-property variance). A new world is only admitted if it fills an empty cell or beats the incumbent in its cell. That is a real novelty archive, not an LLM saying 'this one is interesting'. THE CULTURAL PAYOFF — RITUAL AS A MEASURABLE OBJECT: when an ark arrives, every inherited library entry is silently re-evaluated against the new physics. Entries that still improve fitness are KNOWLEDGE. Entries that no longer do anything, but are still taught and still performed, are RITUAL — and the engine labels them automatically, tracks ritual_labor_hours as a fraction of total labor, and lets that fraction impose a real cost. High ritual load makes a tribe poorer, which creates selective pressure for reform; reform means an agent stops teaching an ancestral practice, which other agents may punish (a social-cost rule). So you get orthodoxy, reformation, and schism as consequences of an accounting identity. That is the most grounded route to emergent religion I know of.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The space of world-generating programs is unbounded in the same way any program space is, and OMNI-EPIC (ICLR 2025) is the existence proof that an LLM plus an interestingness filter can keep producing new, learnable, non-repeating environments rather than sampling from a narrow authored distribution. And the CROSS-WORLD dimension adds something no single-world design has: the quantity being optimised over time is TRANSFERABLE knowledge, which is a strictly harder and more interesting target than knowledge that works here. HONEST BOTTOM, and it is a real one: LLM-generated worlds DO converge in flavour — you will see the desert/ice/tidal/toxic-atmosphere cluster within the first twenty. The MAP-Elites behavioural archive is what fights this, and it only partly wins, because the model's prior shapes what code it writes no matter what you ask for. Expect the archive to fill its easy cells fast and then produce diminishing returns; expect to hand-write two or three genuinely alien worlds yourself to reseed the distribution, and consider that a feature (you get to be a god who occasionally makes a world by hand). Second honest bound: the shared interface caps what a world can BE. A world cannot introduce a new physical dimension you did not put in the interface, so the interface design is the real creative act and it is yours, not the model's.

The ladder

WEEK 1: two hand-written worlds, one gate, one tribe that walks through and immediately does badly. REAL RUNGS: the first automatically-generated world that passes validation without repair; the first ark arrival where you can see the transfer score (what fraction of the library still works) — this number is the project's heartbeat; the first RITUAL detected automatically (they still cure hides with a plant that does not exist here, using a substitute that does nothing); ritual load crossing 10% of labor and a reformer emerging; a schism where half the tribe goes back through the gate; a world that DEFEATS a civilization entirely, and the ruin left behind; a later ark finding that ruin and recovering part of a lost library from its written texts. WILDEST PLAUSIBLE ENDPOINT: a civilization that has crossed eight worlds and whose library has been filtered by eight independent physics — what survives that gauntlet is, by construction, ABSTRACT knowledge (search strategies, teaching methods, how to test a hypothesis) rather than concrete recipes, because only the abstract transfers. Watching a culture become epistemically sophisticated because concreteness kept failing is the wildest and most plausible endpoint of all six ideas, and it falls straight out of the mechanism.

What kills you

Survival in each world, plus the cross-world pressure: arks are small, so a civilization must CHOOSE what to carry, and that choice is made by the culture's own valuation (most-taught, most-used entries get carried) which may be wrong. Ritual load is a direct fitness tax. Between civilizations: run 2-3 arks through the same archive on different paths and compare their transfer scores over time — the one that learned more abstractly wins, measurably.

How the world changes

This IS the changing-worlds idea; it is the answer to that part of the ask. Three timescales: within a world (seasons, hazards), between worlds (gate crossings every few sim-generations), and the archive itself growing as new worlds are generated nightly. You can also RETURN to a world you left, hundreds of years later, and find your ruins and your left-behind population, because worlds are frozen and deterministic and can be resumed from a snapshot.

How it knows something is new

(1) TRANSFER SCORE on arrival — the fraction of the library that still improves fitness. A high score means the world is similar (less interesting); a very low score means a crisis (very interesting). Push on both tails. (2) Automatic ritual detection — a new entry crossing from knowledge to ritual is a story every time. (3) Archive cell fills — 'a world unlike any before it' is literally 'a previously empty MAP-Elites cell', which is a rigorous claim you can make on the phone. (4) Inner-world novelty from the borrowed engine (assembly index records, first-ever programs). (5) Extinction and ruin-discovery events. (6) Reformation/schism events triggered by the ritual-load accounting. Push volume: 1-3 a day.

On your phone

Card: 'Ark of the Weft · World 14 "Saltglass" · day 61 in-world · 34 alive'. Headline, always the transfer score on a fresh arrival: '61% of their library still works here. Fire-hardening now does nothing. They still do it — 4% of all labor.' Then a horizontal strip of the world archive as small tiles colored by whether an ark survived there, which is the single best 30-second civilizational overview I can imagine: you see the whole multiverse and where your people died. Then the ritual-load gauge and the chronicle sentence. Tap → the archive map (MAP-Elites grid, hover a cell to see the world's generated description and its baseline stats), and the ark's library colored knowledge/ritual/dead.

Your hardware

Sim and validation are CPU-bound and parallel — with 64GB RAM you can validate 8 candidate worlds concurrently in separate processes, and validation is a few minutes each. The local 4B does the inner-world agent thinking as in Embertongue (~3-4GB VRAM, GBNF-constrained, comfortable). World GENERATION is the only thing that wants a strong model: Haiku writing a world module is ~1.5-3k output tokens plus a repair round. At 2 new worlds a day that is maybe 8-10 calls, ~25k output tokens = ~$0.06 batched. Add the chronicle and transfer analysis: total well under $0.50/day, so this is one of the cheaper designs despite feeling like the most ambitious. Note: you MUST sandbox generated world code — run it in a subprocess with a restricted import set, a CPU/memory rlimit, and no network. Do not skip this; it is 30 lines and it is the difference between a fun project and a bad evening.

What you have to do

Zero. The best optional nudge in any of these six: WRITE A WORLD YOURSELF. Drop a hand-written module in the worlds directory and it enters the archive alongside the generated ones, and your ark may wander into it. Also: open a gate manually, force a migration, or resurrect a dead world's population.

First weekend

Saturday: the world interface, TWO hand-written worlds, the shared agent/survival layer, and a gate. Get an ark to walk from world A to world B and compute the transfer score — even with a library of six hand-written entries, seeing '3 of 6 still work' is the delight moment and it is a Saturday-night result. Sunday: the ritual detector (it is genuinely about 20 lines: entry is taught AND performed AND its measured fitness delta is ~0), then one Haiku call to generate world C, and the validation harness. If a generated world passes validation and your ark survives 30 days in it, you are done for the weekend and you have already built the outer loop.

Six months in

An archive of 100-300 worlds with a filled MAP-Elites grid you can browse like a star map. Two or three ark lineages with divergent histories, several extinctions, a couple of ruins rediscovered. A civilization whose surviving library is visibly more abstract than it was in world 1, with the transfer-score curve to prove it. A ritual ledger that reads like a religion. You will have hand-written five worlds yourself because it is fun.

Where it stops (self-reported)

World-generation flavour convergence at roughly 20-40 worlds; the MAP-Elites archive keeps admitting new cells for a while but the model's prior is a real limit and the marginal world gets less surprising. Interface expressiveness is the hard wall, same as Embertongue's physics wall but one level up. Inner-world depth is inherited from whichever inner engine you pick, with its own ceiling. Validation is a genuine cost: a few CPU-minutes per candidate and a nonzero rate of subtly-broken worlds that pass validation and are just tedious. And the biggest honest risk is DILUTION — with a hundred worlds, no single world gets enough sim-time for a deep civilization, so you must choose: many shallow worlds or few deep ones. My recommendation is a hard rule of at most three active arks and long residencies (weeks of wall-clock per world), with the archive growing in the background as a place to GO rather than a treadmill. Get that dial wrong and this becomes a screensaver.

Stack

Python throughout (generated world modules are Python, so the interface must be small and typed — use a Protocol plus a validation harness). Subprocess sandbox with resource limits. SQLite for the archive, arks, transfer scores, ritual ledger, lineage. MAP-Elites grid: a dict keyed by discretized behavioural characterization, ~80 lines, no library needed. Inner engine: Embertongue's DSL + stitch_core, shared code. llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B for agents; Haiku (Batch API) for world generation and chronicle. PWA over Tailscale + nightly GitHub Pages static export + ntfy. Determinism test in CI: every world module must produce identical event hashes on replay, and this test is what keeps the whole multiverse trustworthy.

Verified

VERIFIED: OMNI-EPIC — Faldor, Zhang, Cully, Clune, 'Open-endedness via Models of human Notions of Interestingness with Environments Programmed in Code', ICLR 2025, arXiv 2405.15568, repo github.com/maxencefaldor/omni-epic. Its core claim is exactly this design's outer loop: foundation models autonomously generate CODE specifying the next learnable (not too easy, not too hard) and interesting task/environment, escaping the manually-predefined narrow environment distributions that limited prior work. The learnability filter (a baseline must partially succeed) is theirs and it is the load-bearing piece. Darwin Gödel Machine (arXiv 2505.22954, github.com/jennyzzt/dgm) supplies the archive lesson — keep every variant, branch from anywhere, because open-ended archives beat greedy improvement; that is why dead worlds and dead arks stay resumable here. MAP-Elites / quality-diversity is the standard way to keep a generated archive diverse along behavioural axes rather than along an LLM's self-report. Inner engine: DreamCoder (PLDI 2021) + Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, pip stitch_core). Cultural-transmission-across-environments framing and the emergence of religion/memes in agent societies: Project Sid, arXiv 2411.00114 (agents spread cultural memes and a religion across a 500-1000 agent population; specialization required social awareness). Cost: Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per MTok with Batch API 50% off (anthropic.com/claude/haiku) makes 2 worlds/day cost pennies. Sandboxing generated code is not optional — subprocess + rlimits + import allowlist.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two hard stops and one self-inflicted wound. (a) The archive is a MAP-Elites grid over FOUR axes he chose, so 'a world unlike any before it' means 'an empty cell in a space I defined'. The unbounded space of world-programs is projected onto a bounded grid, and the grid fills — easy cells in 2-4 weeks, diminishing returns after; his 20-40-world flavour-convergence estimate is right and the archive measures the convergence rather than preventing it. (b) The frozen interface is Embertongue's expressiveness wall one level up, and worse, because generated worlds can only permute constants inside it. (c) The self-inflicted wound: the outer loop SUBTRACTS depth. The brief asks for fire, then houses, then tools, then something wild — which requires long uninterrupted residency and accumulated library depth. Gating a civilization into a new world every few generations, where 40% of its library stops working, is an explicit anti-accumulation mechanism. His dilution warning understates it: this is not a dial to tune, it is a direct conflict between the headline mechanism and the stated goal.

Is the discovery real?

The world modules are genuinely unauthored, and OMNI-EPIC is a real existence proof for LLM-generated code environments under a learnability filter. But the validation filter is very weak — 'baseline survives N days AND does not thrive without inventing' admits an enormous space of tedious worlds, and nothing checks that a world is LAWFUL rather than arbitrary. Meanwhile the ritual mechanic is the single best idea in all six documents and is not authored at all: knowledge-vs-ritual as an accounting identity (taught AND performed AND measured fitness delta ~ 0), with ritual_labor_hours as a real tax producing orthodoxy, reform and schism as consequences rather than as content, is about 20 lines and I have not seen it done. Extract that mechanic and put it in whichever design actually gets built.

Compute honesty

Honest on tokens (2 worlds/day is pennies) and the sandboxing requirement is correctly stated and non-negotiable. Undersized: the INNER engine is the expensive part and now runs in N worlds; validation at a few CPU-minutes each, x8 concurrent, at a rate high enough to fill a grid, is a permanent background load competing with the sims he actually cares about. Every generated module is also a maintenance surface — subtly-broken-but-valid worlds pass validation, and debugging someone else's generated physics is the least fun failure mode in the batch.

Nonsense risk

The specific risk is ARBITRARINESS, and it silently kills the payoff. Transfer score is the project's heartbeat and its interpretation depends entirely on worlds sharing latent structure. If Haiku samples constants and rules freely, '61% of their library still works here' is a draw from a random distribution and carries no information about the civilization's epistemics. Worse, the marquee endpoint — a culture becoming abstract because only abstract knowledge transfers — requires learnable cross-world regularity to abstract TOWARD. Random worlds select for nothing; they merely delete libraries. As specified, the mechanism cannot produce its own best outcome.

Claims to re-check

OMNI-EPIC (2405.15568, ICLR 2025) is real and fairly described, but it generates ROBOT TASKS in a fixed simulator, filtered by a model of human interestingness, using frontier-class code models; extrapolating to 'Haiku writes coherent civilizational physics modules' is a substantial stretch, and its learnability filter was calibrated to a single RL agent's partial success, not to a multi-agent civilization. Darwin Godel Machine (2505.22954) is real and the archive lesson is fairly drawn. Project Sid (2411.00114) is a non-peer-reviewed industry report; citing it for emergent religion is unnecessary here, since the ritual accounting in this very design is more rigorous than anything in that paper. Assembly index carries the usual caveats (NP-complete exactly; contested as a selection detector). MAP-Elites is described correctly.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Stop generating free-form world code. Define ONE shared generative physics with a structured parameter space — identical constitutive laws everywhere, worlds differing in constants, topology, resource distributions and which couplings are active — and let the LLM sample within that family (writing flavour, and proposing new couplings for human review). Then cross-world invariants genuinely exist, abstract knowledge genuinely transfers better than concrete recipes, transfer score becomes an informative measurement instead of a random number, validation collapses to bounds-checking instead of executing untrusted code, and the 'epistemically sophisticated civilization' endpoint becomes reachable rather than rhetorical. Pair it with a hard residency rule (at most 3 active arks, weeks of wall-clock per world) so depth has time to accumulate.

Embodied learning

Technology as learned behaviour under survival pressure

▸

KINDLING

A gridworld with real physics and zero crafting recipes, where a tribe's technology is a growing library of named, executable behavior programs that a nightly compressor carves out of whatever kept them alive. Fire is not an item you unlock; it is a lambda term the system discovered, named, and can now call.

Ceilinged
The world

96x96 toroidal grid, struct-of-arrays cells: material_id (uint8), temperature (f16), moisture, integrity, occupancy. Materials come from a per-world table of ~24 types x 8 continuous properties (hardness, ignition_temp, conductivity, calorific value, density, friability, toxicity, decay). Nothing is named 'flint' in code — flint is whatever material happens to be hard and spark-prone this world. Agents: 3-8 tribes x 8-24 bodies; body = pos, facing, 2 hand slots, stamina, core_temp, hunger, hp, 8 int8 memory registers. Plants regrow on a moisture/heat curve; prey with flee policies; mineral outcrops; deadwood that dries with heat and wets with rain. A coarse 16x16 climate lattice advects heat/moisture with a 3000-tick seasonal cycle.

How invention actually happens

Nested wake/sleep. WAKE (GPU, nightly, JAX): each tribe carries a Library L of typed lambda terms over a base DSL of ~22 primitives — MOVE(d), TURN, GRAB, DROP, STRIKE(a,b), RUB, PLACE, DIG, EAT, THROW, WAIT, SENSE_TEMP(d), SENSE_MAT(d), SENSE_AGENT, IF, bounded WHILE, SEQ, REPEAT(n), LET, GOTO_NEAREST(pred), numeric ops. The controller is a tiny conv+GRU policy (~300k params) whose action head does NOT emit primitives — it emits an index into L ∪ base plus arguments. PPO (purejaxrl single-file style) over 2048 parallel worlds. Adding an abstraction literally enlarges what one decision can do: that is the compounding. SLEEP (CPU, ~20 min, while the GPU cools): expand top-quartile survivors' action traces to base primitives, run Stitch (stitch_core, Bowers et al. POPL'23) for top-k abstractions by MDL. Admit any that saves > tau bits and is not alpha-equivalent to an existing entry; do net surgery (new row in the action head, initialized near the mean of its constituent primitives' rows); name it with a one-shot local Qwen3-4B call that sees the term plus a 6-frame render of it executing ('BANK_THE_COALS'). The name is cosmetic; the term is the technology. Verification is the sim: re-run on 256 held-out world seeds; if reward drops, roll the library edit back. That is Ouroboros Forge's rejection-sampling hygiene transplanted onto physics instead of unit tests.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two arguments. (i) Compression compounding: abstractions are built from abstractions, so after n nights the set of behaviors expressible in <=8 tokens grows super-exponentially in |L|. This is DreamCoder's argument and it is empirically demonstrated. (ii) The target is not a task list — it is survival in procedurally regenerated physics, so the adjacent possible moves as the world moves. Where it honestly bottoms out: the PHYSICS, not the library. With 8 material properties and 22 primitives I expect a few hundred qualitatively distinct useful behaviors, not infinity. Mitigation is to grow the physics on a slower clock than the library (add a field — pressure, charge — every few weeks), but be clear-eyed: that is you adding physics, not the system inventing it.

The ladder

Week 1: grid + PPO + base DSL, no library learning. Agents seek food and huddle on warm cells at night; first unauthored thing is dragging deadwood into sunlit cells to dry it — you never wrote 'dry'. Weeks 2-3: Stitch loop on, first 5-10 abstractions (GATHER_NEAREST_EDIBLE, DRY_AND_STACK, STRIKE_REPEAT). Somewhere here, ignition: hold hard-material, strike against hard-material, adjacent to low-moisture high-calorific material, temperature crosses ignition_temp. Fire, unauthored. Month 2: fire-dependent abstractions — KEEP_FIRE_ALIVE (feed before integrity hits 0), COOK, SMOKE_OUT_PREDATOR; shelter as PLACE patterns that lower a cell's heat loss; WINDBREAK. Months 3-4: 40-80 abstractions, 3-4 levels of nesting, a recognizable toolchain where abstractions produce intermediates other abstractions consume; sustained high temperature transforms materials if you add a small temp/pressure phase map (one function, not a tech tree). Wildest plausible at 6-12 months: tribes specializing (extraction vs fabrication), trade emerging as an abstraction (GIVE + EXPECT), and a library entry that is ABOUT other entries — TEACH(x), which runs an abstraction slowly beside a naive agent. Ceiling of the ladder: agriculture, metallurgy-as-phase-change, proto-teaching. Not writing, not electricity.

What kills you

Hunger drains per tick with a cold penalty proportional to (comfort_temp - core_temp). Night predators spawn away from heat sources. A 3000-tick winter zeroes plant regrowth — only stored/cooked food and heat survive it. Tribes share one map: flint outcrops and dry deadwood are finite and contested, and a tribe that burns a forest denies fuel to its neighbour (real niche-construction externality). Tribe hits zero members: its library is archived as a ruin (dead branch in the DB) and its territory reseeds. Fitness = tribe-ticks survived across a held-out seed set. Deterministic tick budget, never wall-clock — this laptop thermally throttles and any timing-based fitness would silently drift with ambient temperature.

How the world changes

Three levers. (1) A world genome per run: material property table, climate amplitude, predator aggression, all seeded. Every Sunday the outer loop spawns a new genome and forces surviving libraries to transfer cold; abstractions that survive transfer are the DEEP ones, and the archive records 'portable' vs 'parochial' technologies — itself a great phone stat. (2) POET-style co-evolution: keep an archive of world genomes, mutate those where the current library is neither trivially winning nor hopeless. (3) Concurrent shards: 4 worlds at once are just vmap batches with different property tables, so you get parallel civilizations to compare.

How it knows something is new

Three signals ANDed before any push. (a) MDL: Stitch reports bits saved; require bits_saved > tau and a canonical de Bruijn hash never seen before. (b) First-ever world-event ledger: a small set of physics predicates (a cell exceeded ignition_temp with an agent adjacent; a manufactured material appeared; a tribe survived a whole winter) each carrying a 'first time in project history' flag in SQLite. (c) Usage gate: an abstraction only becomes a TECHNOLOGY once it appears in >=5% of surviving-tribe traces for 3 consecutive nights — this kills the flood of one-off compressions. The morning ntfy is at most 3 lines and often says nothing, which is the point.

On your phone

One screen. Top: a 200x200 PNG of last night's most eventful tick. Middle: the tech tree as an actual growing DAG (abstraction -> children) in SVG, newest node pulsing. Bottom: three counters — living tribes / library size / nights since last genuine novelty. Tap a node for the lambda term, its Qwen-given name, a 3-second GIF of an agent executing it, and its birth night. Served static over `tailscale serve`, mirrored to GitHub Pages each morning so friends can look.

Your hardware

Ideal 8GB workload. Obs is an 11x11xC int8 egocentric patch, policy ~300k params, 2048 parallel envs sits under 3GB. Craftax-Classic does 1B env steps in under an hour on one GPU and Craftax-MA does 250M steps in 57 min on an L40S, so an 8-hour overnight run on a 5070 laptop realistically lands 1-3B steps. Stitch is Rust on CPU, seconds-to-minutes on thousand-program corpora — perfect use of 64GB RAM and an idle GPU. Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M is ~3GB and only runs during the naming pass after RL stops; never concurrently. Blackwell caveat: sm_120 needs CUDA 12.8+ wheels and PyTorch only got there via nightlies (pytorch/pytorch#164342) — budget a full setup day and keep a CPU-JAX fallback (64GB RAM runs a 64x64 world at tens of thousands of steps/s, enough to debug).

What you have to do

Fully autonomous nightly. Optional god-nudges: add one physics field or one base primitive when the tree visibly plateaus (a 20-line change); veto a degenerate library entry from the phone (writes to a blacklist table); hand-author a world genome when you want to see something specific ('make flint rare').

First weekend
