Saturday: the env (grid arrays, 6 materials, heat diffusion as a 3x3 conv, agent step, hunger/cold) plus a random-policy renderer writing GIFs. Sunday: purejaxrl PPO with a flat base-primitive action space, the SQLite event ledger, and a cron-pushed static page with last night's GIF and the first-time-ever list. You already get an unauthored moment: agents learning to spend nights on sun-warmed rock.

Six months in

60-150 abstractions across a handful of lineages; a visibly branched DAG where two isolated worlds independently found ignition via different terms (convergent invention, a genuinely thrilling push); a set of portable technologies that survive world-genome swaps and a graveyard of parochial ones. You will be adding physics fields occasionally and each one buys weeks of new branches.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The library grows forever but interestingness per abstraction decays. Realistically 4-6 qualitatively distinct capability tiers with a 22-primitive DSL and 8 material properties: forage/thermoregulate, object and material manipulation, fire and heat management, shelter/construction, transformed materials (cook / fire-harden / smelt-as-phase-change), crude social tech (share, defend, teach). Anything resembling writing, selective agriculture, or machines needs physics you do not have. Expect novelty pushes to get sparse around month 3-4 unless you feed new physics. Also honest: PPO over a GROWING action space is finicky — real debugging time will go into net surgery and into abstractions that get learned then catastrophically forgotten.

Stack

Python + JAX, single-file purejaxrl-style PPO, Flax or Equinox for the net, a hand-written grid env (do NOT fork Craftax — its fixed recipes are exactly what you are removing; read it for structure only), stitch_core for compression, SQLite for the lineage/event DB, llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B-Instruct GGUF for naming only, FastAPI + vanilla-JS PWA, ntfy.sh, Tailscale. This is the shared substrate for everything else: sim core + SQLite event/lineage DB + PWA chronicle + push.

Verified

Craftax (arXiv 2402.16801, craftaxenv.github.io): 1B env interactions in under an hour on a single GPU, ~250x faster than Python Crafter — this is the speed budget the whole idea rests on. Multi-Agent Craftax (arXiv 2511.04904, github.com/BaselOmari/MA-Craftax): 250M steps in 57 min on one L40S with 4 agents; also confirms IPPO/MAPPO/PQN all fail its long-horizon credit assignment, so do not expect a stock algorithm to just work. Stitch (Bowers et al., POPL 2023, mlb2251.github.io/stitch.pdf): 3-4 orders of magnitude faster and 2 orders less memory than DreamCoder's compressor, with equal or better compressivity — this is what makes a nightly CPU sleep phase realistic on a laptop. LILO (arXiv 2310.19791) is the precedent for using an LLM to name/document a Stitch-learned library. purejaxrl for the PPO skeleton. Avoid Neural MMO: 2.0 soft-launch, env repo last active Aug 2024, baselines Jul 2024, no 3.0 — do not build on it. sm_120 friction: pytorch/pytorch#164342, cu128 required.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Hard stop is architectural, not physical, and it is earlier than the pitch says. Stitch consumes typed lambda terms with variables, IF and WHILE; what you feed it is a FLATTENED execution trace. Flattening destroys exactly the structure DreamCoder compounds over — a trace records the branch taken, not the IF; the unrolled body, not the WHILE; a concrete argument, not a binder. So the library can only ever contain straight-line macro-actions (options / action n-grams). That is a well-trodden and famously flat literature: macros buy a constant-factor horizon reduction, never compositional growth. 'Behaviors expressible in <=8 tokens grows super-exponentially in |L|' is true of a lambda library and false of a macro table. Practical stop: useful macro-actions over 22 primitives and 8 material properties saturate around 30-60 entries. Forage/thermoregulate by week 2, ignition weeks 3-6, fire-tending + shelter + cook by weeks 8-14, then the DAG keeps adding nodes that are re-parameterizations of existing ones. Novelty pushes go sparse at ~week 10-14, earlier than the stated month 3-4, and PPO net surgery on a growing head will cost you weeks of the interval by forcing re-exploration each time a row is added.

Is the discovery real?

Medium-low, and honestly disclosed. Fire really is a consequence of a property table rather than a recipe — that part is legitimate and is the best thing in the design. But the set of possible KINDS of outcome is exactly the authored physics: every tier in honest_ceiling maps 1:1 to a function you will write (heat diffusion, ignition, the temp/pressure phase map). The system discovers routes, not outcomes. Sharper problem in the notification layer: the world-event ledger is a list of hand-written predicates, so you can only ever be surprised by things you already wrote a detector for. Qwen naming is correctly labelled cosmetic.

Compute honesty

Partly dishonest by an order of magnitude, in the throughput claim rather than the memory claim. 2048 envs x 300k params x 11x11 int8 obs under 3GB: fine. Stitch on CPU: fine. Qwen3-4B after RL releases the GPU: fine. But '1-3B steps overnight' borrows Craftax's single-agent number for a sim with 3-8 tribes x 8-24 bodies (up to ~190 agents per world). The right reference is the paper this idea itself cites: Craftax-MA does 250M interactions in under an hour with FOUR agents on an L40S, and a 5070 laptop is roughly a quarter of that card. Realistic overnight budget: 20-80M multi-agent steps, one to two orders below the stated figure — which matters, because the whole depth-per-night argument is built on the larger number.

Nonsense risk

Low for drivel, high for triviality. Every library entry is executable and sim-verified against 256 held-out seeds, so nothing meaningless can survive — but nothing forces entries to be interesting either, and a macro table fills with GRAB-then-MOVE-then-EAT micro-motifs. The 5%-of-traces-for-3-nights usage gate is the right instrument and will correctly leave most mornings silent.

Claims to re-check

(1) 'Avoid Neural MMO, no 3.0, dead since Aug 2024' is WRONG. Neural MMO 3 is alive and is a headline showcase of PufferLib (PufferLib 2.0, RLC 2025; continued NMMO3 work through 2025). You may still not want to build on it, but not for the stated reason. (2) 'DreamCoder's compounding argument is empirically demonstrated' — demonstrated on curated task suites with an expensive enumerative wake search, never on RL traces, and no published result shows super-exponential growth of USEFUL behaviors. (3) Craftax-MA (2511.04904) is real and does report existing algorithms failing on long-horizon credit assignment, but its published throughput figure is '250M interactions in under an hour'; the '57 min on one L40S with 4 agents' precision is an unverified paraphrase and it argues against your speed budget, not for it. (4) The pytorch sm_120 issue is a red herring for a JAX stack — jaxlib cu12 wheels are the actual dependency.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Stop flattening. Log the PROGRAM the policy executed, not the action sequence it produced: have the action head emit a small parameterized sketch (primitive + argument + optional sensor guard) so traces retain conditionals and binders, and Stitch has real structure to abstract over. That single change is the difference between a macro table that saturates at ~50 entries and a library that actually compounds — it is the load-bearing assumption of the entire idea and right now the pipeline destroys it before Stitch ever sees it.

▸

JOINTMAKERS

Agents in a real 2D rigid-body world whose only superpower is joining things together — a technology is an evolved physical assembly plus the policy that wields it, and one search discovers both. Hammers, levers, deadfall traps and catapults come out of a MAP-Elites archive, not a recipe list.

Ceilinged
The world

Jax2D (the physics engine inside Kinetix, FLAIROx) — deterministic impulse-based rigid-body sim in pure JAX: circles, polygons, revolute and fixed joints, thrusters. The arena has terrain polygons, scattered loose bodies (stones of varying density, sticks of varying length and stiffness), and food nodes gated by physics rather than by inventory: a nut needing >40 N impact, a burrow slab needing a 3-body lever to lift. An agent is itself a small articulated ragdoll (3-5 bodies, 2-4 motorized joints), so morphology is part of the genome and bodies co-evolve with brains.

How invention actually happens

Genome = (morphology graph, attachment policy, control policy). Two verbs beyond locomotion: WELD(hand, body) instantiates a real joint constraint in Jax2D, CUT(joint) removes one. An agent can therefore build an assembly from loose bodies, attach it to itself or to the ground, and must then LEARN to use it. Search is MAP-Elites whose behaviour descriptors are physical: peak impulse delivered, reach, assembly mass, joint count, energy per unit work. Elites' controllers are refined by short PPO bursts — this is PGA-MAP-Elites, which already exists in QDax. A technology is an archive cell's elite: a canonicalized joint graph with parameters. New technology = a newly filled cell, or a >15% improvement to an occupied one. Crucially, assemblies persist in the world after their builder dies, so a clan inherits its infrastructure and can steal or demolish a rival's.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The design space is (graph topology x continuous geometry x attachment points x control policy). Kinetix's own claim is that this substrate procedurally contains tens of millions of distinct TASKS; the assembly space is strictly larger than the task space, and a joint graph over n bodies has super-exponentially many topologies each carrying continuous edge parameters. There is no enumerable list. Where it bottoms out honestly: the physics is 2D, rigid, frictional — no chemistry, no fields, no fluids. The FUNCTIONAL space is mechanics: leverage, impulse storage (springs via joint stiffness), containment, projectile launch, traps, wheeled transport. That is about three real capability tiers. Genuinely unbounded in variation, genuinely bounded in kind, and I would rather say that plainly than sell you a tech tree.

The ladder

Week 1: Jax2D arena + PPO locomotion + a nut needing impulse; agents learn to body-slam. Weeks 2-3: WELD enabled; first tool is pick up heavy stone, weld to limb, swing. It looks exactly like a hammer. Month 2: leverage (stick welded to a fulcrum stone to lift a slab) and the deadfall — an assembly storing potential energy that releases on contact, i.e. a machine that works while the agent is elsewhere. Months 3-4: multi-agent assemblies (two agents cooperatively hold and weld a structure neither can build alone), inherited infrastructure (a bridge across a chasm that outlives its builder, and a clan that only survives because a dead ancestor built it). Wildest plausible: a wheeled sledge for hauling heavy food, a torsion catapult, and counter-technology — a clan evolving assemblies specifically to demolish a rival's bridge. Physical arms races.

What kills you

A caloric economy where the high-value food is only accessible above a force or height threshold. A chasm separating rich from poor terrain. Rival clans sharing an archive but holding separate territory. Joint fatigue means assemblies decay, so infrastructure must be maintained or lost. And POET-style pressure: arenas live in their own co-evolving archive, so a clan that solves everything is handed a harder arena.

How the world changes

Kinetix arenas are just data — mutate gravity, friction, body density distributions, terrain. Run an explicit POET loop: an archive of (arena, clan) pairs, mutate arenas, transfer clans across arenas, keep pairs scoring in a goldilocks band. Multiple worlds is free, they are vmap batches. Publicly, let visitors vote weekly on one arena mutation ('raise gravity 10%', 'make sticks brittle') — cheap non-stationarity with a human story attached to it.

How it knows something is new

Canonical hash of the assembly's joint graph (Weisfeiler-Lehman hash on the labelled graph, continuous params bucketed) plus archive-cell events. Push when: a never-seen graph hash becomes an elite; a previously empty archive region gets colonized; or a functional first fires — first assembly delivering >100 N, first assembly that continues doing work with no agent touching it. That last predicate is a one-line detector for 'they built a machine', and it is the best notification in this design.

On your phone

A 6-tile grid of last night's newest elites, each a 2-second autoplaying WebM of the assembly in use, labelled with its archive cell and the record it broke. One headline line: 'Clan Basalt built something that works without them.' Beneath it, the archive heat-map as a raw 2D image (behaviour space coloured by fitness) — that single image is a 3-second read of six months of progress.

Your hardware

Jax2D is the entire reason this fits 8GB: Kinetix trains general agents through billions of steps on a single GPU. Keep bodies per scene <=12 and parallel scenes <=1024; VRAM is not the binding constraint here, wall-clock is. The MAP-Elites archive lives in RAM, where 64GB is luxurious (a 50k-cell archive of small genomes is nothing). Video encoding on CPU overnight. Same sm_120/CUDA-12.8 setup tax as any JAX project on this laptop.

What you have to do

Autonomous. Nudges: add a body type (rope? a soft joint?), add one verb, or hand-place a puzzle arena you want solved and see whether the archive can reach it.

First weekend

Saturday: install Kinetix, get its arena editor running, hand-build the nut arena, train PPO to smash it. Sunday: implement WELD/CUT as env actions, run vanilla MAP-Elites over three descriptors, export the top 6 elites as GIFs to a static page. This idea has the fastest delight-to-effort ratio of the six — welding a rock to your arm and hitting things is instantly legible on a phone.

Six months in

An archive of a few thousand distinct assemblies with a scrollable visual museum, several convergently rediscovered classics (hammer, lever, deadfall, ramp) plus a long tail of things you have no name for, and inter-clan demolition wars. The museum page is the artifact strangers will actually share.

Where it stops (self-reported)

2D rigid-body mechanics, full stop. Hammers, levers, traps, bridges, sledges, catapults — yes. Fire, chemistry, agriculture, writing, electricity — never, the substrate has no channel for them. Three realistic tiers (held tool -> static structure -> energy-storing machine), and from month 4 you are mostly getting variation inside tier 3. Second warning: MAP-Elites archives cheat. OpenAI's hide-and-seek agents famously exploited physics (box surfing, ramp abuse) and yours will too — budget for a physics-exploit blacklist and a hard cap on joint impulse, or your museum fills with infinite-energy joints.

Stack

Jax2D + Kinetix (github.com/FlairOx/Kinetix), QDax for PGA-MAP-Elites, JAX/Flax, evosax if you want ES variants, SQLite for archive metadata and lineage, ffmpeg for clips, the shared PWA + ntfy + Tailscale stack.

Verified

Kinetix (arXiv 2410.23208, ICLR 2025 Oral; github.com/FlairOx/Kinetix; kinetix-env.github.io) — introduces Jax2D, a deterministic impulse-based 2D rigid-body engine in pure JAX, procedurally generates tens of millions of physics tasks, trains a general agent that zero-shot solves unseen human-designed levels, all on accelerator hardware. QDax provides MAP-Elites, CVT MAP-Elites, PGA-MAP-Elites and CMA-MEGA in JAX. evosax (arXiv 2212.04180, github.com/RobertTLange/evosax) has 30+ ES implementations under a jit/vmap ask-eval-tell API if you want ES rather than PG refinement. OpenAI's emergent tool use / hide-and-seek is the precedent that pure multi-agent competition produces ramp use, box locking AND documented physics exploits — treat the exploits as an expected outcome, not a bug you failed to prevent.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two hard walls the pitch does not name. (1) Jax2D is statically shaped: every scene compiles to fixed-size arrays with a maximum shape and joint count (Kinetix's largest preset is on the order of a dozen shapes and a handful of joints). Runtime WELD must consume a preallocated joint slot, so maximum assembly complexity is a compile-time constant, realistically 6-8 joints. Your catapult has a hard part count and there is no version of this design with a 30-part machine. (2) MAP-Elites over a FIXED 5-descriptor space has a literal, countable ceiling: the number of cells. Once coverage saturates, 'new technology' degenerates into the >15% fitness-improvement clause, i.e. grinding. Illumination is not accumulation — nothing lets assembly A become a reusable component of assembly B with A's controller intact, so the archive gets wider forever and deeper never. Hammer week 2-3, lever and deadfall month 2, coverage saturating month 3-4, and from there it is variation inside tier 3, which the author already concedes.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest in the set alongside BYTE REEF, and the pitch is honest about kind-boundedness. The one real authorship: the five behaviour descriptors ARE the coordinate system of the tech tree. 'New technology = newly filled cell' means new point in a space you drew, and peak-impulse/reach/mass/joint-count/energy-per-work is a compact statement of what you already expect mechanics to be about. Multi-agent cooperative welding (two agents holding a structure neither can build alone) is asserted with no mechanism and is a much harder joint-exploration problem than anything else in the ladder — treat it as speculative, not month-3.

Compute honesty

The weakest 'it fits on 8GB' claim of the six, and the framing hides where the cost is. VRAM is genuinely not binding; the binding cost is that PGA-MAP-Elites means per-elite PG refinement across an archive of thousands. With morphology in the genome, every genome has a different observation and action shape, so you cannot vmap one policy over the archive without padding to a max morphology and masking, and each variation batch is a fresh short PPO run with its own optimizer state. Filling a 5-D archive plus refinement is days-to-weeks per frontier, not one overnight. Citing OpenAI hide-and-seek as precedent compounds this: that result took on the order of hundreds of millions of episodes on a large distributed cluster, and it is used here as evidence for what a laptop will produce.

Nonsense risk

Low nonsense, high exploit — and the two interact badly. Runtime joint creation in an impulse solver is a textbook energy-injection bug: welding overlapping bodies produces an instantaneous penetration-resolution impulse. Since 'peak impulse delivered' is itself a descriptor, the archive will actively select for the solver artifact. Your museum's top exhibits will be free-energy joints unless the impulse cap and an overlap check land before the first long run, not after.

Claims to re-check

Kinetix claims check out: arXiv 2410.23208, ICLR 2025 Oral, Jax2D, 'tens of millions of procedurally generated tasks', 'billions of environment steps' — all confirmed (repo is FLAIROx/Kinetix, not FlairOx). The unsupported step is the inference from those numbers: 'the assembly space is strictly larger than the task space' is true and irrelevant, because reachability under MAP-Elites is bounded by descriptor cells and joint slots, not by the size of the underlying space. The hide-and-seek citation is used for two opposite purposes at once (proof emergence works, warning that exploits happen) and only the second is safe.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Add composition. Let a finished elite be inserted into a new genome as a single reusable macro-part (its body subgraph plus its trained controller, frozen and callable), with a hierarchical or nested archive over parts-of-parts. That is the only route past both walls at once — it converts illumination into accumulation, and it sidesteps the joint-slot cap because a composed part occupies one slot in the parent genome.

▸

THE KILN

Embodied tribes run physical processes — heat this, crush that, soak the other for a while — and when a process produces a combination nobody has ever made, a language model is asked, once and forever, what it becomes and what its properties are. The model never decides what happens next; it only expands the periodic table, and everything downstream is ordinary physics.

Not real depth
The world

2D JAX grid. Every substance is a 12-float property vector (density, hardness, melting_pt, ignition_pt, conductivity, toughness, calorific, toxicity, reactivity, opacity, elasticity, decay). The world begins with 14 base substances. A PROCESS is a situation the sim can detect: substances A and B co-located in a cell whose (temperature, pressure, moisture, duration, mechanical work) falls in a bucketed region. The sim quantizes that into a key. Key present in the SQLite recipes table -> apply the cached outcome. Key absent -> enqueue an oracle query. Tribes are PPO policies with bodies, hunger, tool durability, and territory.

How invention actually happens

The oracle, tightly leashed. One batched call per night over all cache misses, with a rigid structured-output schema: given the two input property vectors, their discovered names, and the process conditions, return {product_name, product_property_vector, yield_fraction, byproducts}. Three guardrails keep it from being slop: (a) conservation — mass in >= mass out, and a hard clamp so no property may exceed the max of its inputs by more than 1.5x unless the process supplied the energy for it; (b) two independent samples must agree within tolerance on the property vector or the pair is marked inert (Ouroboros Forge's mechanical verifier, applied to physics); (c) once written, a recipe is immutable and cached, so the world's physics is append-only and consistent forever. The embodied half is the actual game: the LLM tells you THAT sustained 900 degrees on high-reactivity ore yields a hard tough substance; the tribe still has to independently learn, via PPO, to build a structure that holds 900 degrees for 400 ticks. Knowing a recipe and being able to perform it are separate problems, and only the second one kills you.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The recipe space is |substances|^2 x |condition buckets|, and |substances| grows with every accepted recipe, so reachable space grows super-quadratically with discoveries. Infinite Craft is the existence proof that this does not run out — its community-mined recipe dataset already exceeds 200k entries. Where it genuinely bottoms out is not size but COHERENCE: LLM-invented property vectors drift toward cool-sounding things with high numbers, and after a few thousand recipes you get a smooth gradient of super-materials with no interesting structure. The two mitigations that actually help are the conservation clamp and a usage tax: any substance no tribe has physically used in 20 nights is deleted and its recipes marked dormant. Physics nobody uses is not physics.

The ladder

Week 1: grid + 14 substances + 6 hand-written recipes; PPO tribes learn to stay warm. Week 2: oracle wired in, 30-50 cache misses a night; the first LLM-born material appears (usually charcoal-like, then a ceramic). Months 1-2: tribes discover that MAINTAINING conditions pays — the first kiln, which is a wall geometry the policy learned because it holds heat. Month 3: two-stage processes, where a product becomes an input to another process. That is the moment the tech tree stops being flat. Month 4+: material lineages tens deep, tribes specializing, trade emerging because one territory has the ore and another has the fuel. Wildest plausible: a material with a genuinely weird vector — very high conductivity, very low density — and a tribe whose entire survival strategy reorganizes around it. You will read that push and laugh out loud.

What kills you

Durability. Tools wear at a rate set by hardness and toughness, so better materials are a survival edge rather than a score. Winters, predators, and finite ore veins that EXHAUST — which is what makes tribes migrate and fight. A tribe that loses its kiln loses the ability to make the material its tools depend on and visibly regresses; technological collapse becomes a legible, filmable event rather than a stat.

How the world changes

Geological eras every ~2 weeks: veins exhaust, new strata expose, the climate band shifts. Public perturbation: the world has a sky that anonymous web visitors influence — a guestbook where each visitor's message is hashed into a small weather nudge (rain, heat, wind), and the accumulated messages render on the site as the world's omen log. Cheap non-stationarity plus an actual reason the page is public.

How it knows something is new

Three tiers. (a) A cache miss is a candidate, not news. (b) A new substance is news only if a tribe USED it to survive — present in a surviving tribe's tool or structure for 3 consecutive nights. (c) Rank survivors by property-vector novelty: k-NN distance in the normalized 12-d space against every existing substance, and push the top one per night as a rendered material card. That yields roughly one interesting message per morning and silence on boring ones.

On your phone

A materials trading card: new substance name, radar chart of its 12 properties, the process that made it, the tribe that first used it, and a thumbnail of the structure they used it in. Plus one sparkline — substances discovered versus substances actually used. The gap between those two lines is the honesty metric of the whole project, and watching it is oddly compelling.

Your hardware

The RL half is small (same profile as KINDLING; 8GB is plenty). The LLM is the scheduling problem: run it only after the RL job releases the GPU. Local Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M is ~3GB at 40+ tok/s, so 50 recipes a night at ~300 output tokens each is about 7 minutes, free. If you would rather have coherence, claude-haiku-4-5 is $1/MTok in and $5/MTok out: a query with a cached system prompt is roughly 600 in / 250 out, about $0.0019 each, so 50/night is ~$0.10/night, ~$3/month — and the Batch API halves that while prompt caching (cached reads at ~0.1x) makes the fixed physics-rules prefix nearly free. This is the rare LLM-in-the-loop design whose bill is genuinely rounding error.

What you have to do

Autonomous. Nudges: tighten or loosen the conservation clamp, kill a runaway super-material from the phone, or hand-write a recipe when you want to seed a direction.

First weekend

Saturday: grid, substances-as-vectors, the process-key quantizer, six hardcoded recipes, a PPO tribe that survives one night. Sunday: swap the hardcoded table for the oracle behind an aggressive cache, and watch the first non-authored material get born and written into SQLite. That is a hell of a Sunday-night moment and it is reachable.

Six months in

Several hundred substances, maybe 60-100 of them load-bearing; a materials wiki that generated itself; tribes with genuinely different technological identities per world; and a graveyard table of deleted materials nobody ever used. The self-written wiki is the shareable artifact.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The LLM will eventually produce mush — expect visible coherence decay past roughly 500 substances without the usage tax, and even with it the property space is 12 floats and everything eventually becomes interpolation. Realistic tiers: fuels and fire, fired ceramics, alloys and composites, one or two genuine exotics. Four tiers, not twenty. The bigger risk is temptation: it is very easy to start letting the LLM adjudicate BEHAVIOUR too, and that is the exact moment the project turns into a chatbot playing pretend. Keep the model strictly on the property-vector side of the line, forever.

Stack

JAX grid env + PPO; SQLite tables substances / recipes / process_keys / usage; llama.cpp with Qwen3-4B GGUF, or the anthropic Python SDK with output_config.format structured outputs plus prompt caching plus the Batch API; shared PWA + ntfy + Tailscale stack.

Verified

Infinite Craft's architecture is the precedent: the backend asks an LLM what a combination produces, then caches it permanently, so only genuinely new pairs cost anything; community-mined datasets of 200k+ recipes exist. Open clones to read before building: github.com/bufferhead-code/opencraft, github.com/CharlesFauman/infini-craft, and github.com/ngruychev/finite-craft (explicitly runs on a laptop without a good GPU). Pricing verified: claude-haiku-4-5 at $1.00/$5.00 per MTok, claude-sonnet-5 at $3.00/$15.00, cached input reads ~0.1x with ~1.25x write cost and a ~1024-token minimum cacheable prefix, Batch API at 50%. Local alternative: Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M fits ~3GB VRAM; 8B Q4_K_M hits 40+ tok/s on 6-8GB cards, so 4B is comfortable. Theory being operationalized: Kauffman's adjacent possible and W. Brian Arthur's 'technology is combination'.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Two ceilings, and the near one is not the one the pitch worries about. The pitch fears coherence decay past ~500 substances; the real failure is DIMENSIONAL COLLAPSE at 30-80 substances, weeks 3-6. Twelve floats are only real if the sim implements consequences for each. Realistically hardness and toughness feed tool wear, calorific and ignition feed fire, density and conductivity feed heat — opacity, elasticity, toxicity and reactivity are decoration unless you write dynamics for them, which is as much work as the rest of the sim. So the effective material space is ~4-D, and the conservation clamp makes it monotone and bounded. Every new material is then a point on a scalar quality ladder: to the simulator, 'bronze' and 'unobtainium' are the same object with slightly better numbers. That is a stat progression, not a tech tree, and it stops climbing when the clamp ceilings bind. Two-stage processes (month 3, the claimed moment the tree stops being flat) do not fix this — a chain of processes that each nudge four numbers upward is still one dimension of progress.

Is the discovery real?

Fatal, and it is precisely the flaw the brief warns about — with the twist that the author is a language model rather than the designer. Ask any competent model 'high-reactivity ore, sustained 900C, with charcoal' and you get metallurgy, because it memorised Wikipedia. The recipe table is human technological history retrieved from pretraining and revealed in the order the tribes happen to physically explore. The pitch calls this 'expanding the periodic table' as if that were the harmless half; expanding the periodic table IS the tech tree. The verifier does not save it: two samples agreeing is self-consistency, not truth — models are mode-collapsed at low temperature, agreement is near-guaranteed, and it measures nothing. Ouroboros-style rejection sampling works because the unit tests are an oracle independent of the generator; here there is no independent oracle at all. What IS genuine discovery is the embodied half — learning a wall geometry that holds 900C for 400 ticks is real, unauthored, and the best moment in the design. But that is one discovery, re-run against a retrieved catalogue.

Compute honesty

Yes — the most honest budget in the document, and the numbers are essentially right. Verified: claude-haiku-4-5 is $1.00/$5.00 per MTok, claude-sonnet-5 $3.00/$15.00, Batch API 50%, cached reads ~0.1x with ~1.25x write cost, minimum cacheable prefix ~1024 tokens. ~$0.10/night at 50 recipes is correct in magnitude. One self-contradiction: at the stated 600-in/250-out per query the request is BELOW the ~1024-token cacheable minimum, so prompt caching cannot engage at all — you would have to pad the physics-rules prefix past 1024 tokens for the 'nearly free prefix' claim to be true, which also raises the uncached cost of the first call. Local Qwen3-4B at ~3GB after the RL job releases the GPU is fine.

Nonsense risk

High — but the failure mode is not gibberish, it is plausible-sounding interpolation. You will get an unbroken gradient of reasonable-looking materials with monotonically nicer stats and no structure, which reads as depth on a trading card and is flat in the simulator. The usage tax (delete anything unused for 20 nights) is a genuinely good instrument and the discovered-vs-used sparkline is the most honest telemetry proposed anywhere in this document — it will show the gap widening, which is the correct diagnosis arriving too late to fix the architecture.

Claims to re-check

Infinite Craft precedent and the 200k+ community recipe datasets are real, but the precedent proves the wrong thing: Infinite Craft never runs out because it is a naming game with no simulator downstream, so nothing has to MEAN anything. Here everything downstream is physics that reads four numbers. 'Recipe space is |substances|^2 x buckets, so growth is super-quadratic' counts syntactically distinct keys, not distinguishable outcomes — the honest count is the number of reachable points in the sub-space of properties the sim actually consumes. 'Mechanical verifier' is a misuse of the Ouroboros Forge analogy as described above.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Invert the oracle so the model cannot retrieve. Two coupled moves, one idea: never show it names or human-legible property labels (feed anonymised input vectors and bucketed conditions), and restrict its output to a RULE in a tiny closed algebra you wrote — which conserved quantity moves where, which phase boundary is crossed, what fraction transfers — from which the product vector is derived mechanically. Novelty then comes from your algebra composing rather than from the model's memory, the result can surprise the model, and the tree stops being a walk through human metallurgy in a random order.

▸

PALIMPSEST

An archipelago of small worlds where a technology only exists as long as someone alive can perform it and someone young is watching — inventions are memes with a physical carrier, and the drama is transmission, drift, loss and rediscovery. The tech tree is a phylogeny, not a checklist, and it has dark ages.

Ceilinged
The world

5-9 island grids, each 64x64, all vmapped as one batch, separated by water. Each island holds one lineage of 12-40 embodied agents with real lifespans (~2000 ticks), age, and death. The physical substrate is KINDLING's — materials with property vectors, heat, moisture, food, predators — but the invention engine is completely different.

How invention actually happens

Three coupled mechanisms. (1) Individual innovation: an agent occasionally mutates a program it knows (subtree swap in a small behaviour DSL) at a low base rate that RISES with hunger — necessity as the mother of invention, literally parameterized. Most mutations are useless; the sim is the only filter. (2) Transmission with a real bottleneck: knowledge is never shared by copying parameters. A knower must physically PERFORM the program adjacent to a learner, repeatedly, and the learner runs a small behavioural-cloning update on the observed (obs, action) pairs — DeepMind's cultural-transmission 'starter kit' of memory, expert dropout, and attentional bias toward the expert. The copy is noisy. That noise IS cultural drift and it is the source of variation. (3) External storage: an agent can inscribe a program onto a carved token at a cost. A token is a real physical item — carried, traded, stolen, buried, burned. Reading one gives a cleaner copy than watching a person. So writing is not a milestone you author; it is a strategy that either pays or does not, and you find out by watching.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The space is programs, which is combinatorially open as in KINDLING, but what makes it never settle is the DYNAMICS: transmission noise plus finite lifespans plus island isolation means each lineage random-walks a different region of program space, and contact events (rafts, storms carrying a body between islands) horizontally transfer memes into contexts that recombine them. This is exactly the iterated-learning / cultural-accumulation regime that 'Artificial Generational Intelligence' formalizes. It bottoms out where the DSL and physics do — the same honest bound as KINDLING — but the HISTORY it generates is far richer per unit of technology, because the same 40 technologies produce a different story on every island every month.

The ladder

Week 1: one island, agents with lifespans, one hand-seeded skill held by one agent — watch it die out when the knower dies. Technology visibly going extinct is the first delightful moment and it arrives in days. Week 2: imitation channel on; measure whether a skill survives 5 generations (the standard cultural-transmission metric). Weeks 3-4: innovation on; the first lineage-original technology. Month 2: multiple islands, first contact event, first horizontal transfer. Month 3: dark ages — a famine kills Island 3's elders, fire-making is lost for 40 generations, then independently reinvented by a different term. That single push is the best notification anything in this document can produce. Month 4+: inscription becomes adaptive on some islands and not others, and a literate island out-accumulates its neighbours in a way the phylogeny makes visible. Wildest plausible: a token economy, tokens as inter-island trade goods, and an island whose survival strategy is acquiring other islands' tokens — emergent cultural imperialism.

What kills you

Individual death from hunger, cold, predation. Lineage extinction at population zero, after which the island reseeds from a neighbour's raft — itself a transmission event. Teaching has an explicit opportunity cost: the elder is not foraging. That creates the innovate/imitate/forage tradeoff which the cultural-accumulation literature says is the precondition for accumulation to happen at all. Islands differ in resource richness, so poor islands are innovation-pressured and rich islands are conservative, and you can watch that difference in the phylogeny.

How the world changes

The archipelago IS multiple worlds, changing independently. Sea level oscillates slowly: low sea level opens land bridges (mass contact, tech homogenization), high sea level isolates (divergence). Visitors are travellers — an anonymous web visitor may once a day 'sail', and the site picks a random agent and moves it, with everything it knows, to another island. And the guestbook literally becomes scripture: a visitor's message is inscribed on a token that enters the world as a physical object, and the site shows which islands currently hold which human-written tokens. That is a genuinely good reason for the page to be public and for strangers to come back.

How it knows something is new

Run it on the phylogeny, not the world state. Push on: (a) convergent invention — two lineages independently reach behaviourally equivalent programs with different terms, compared by action-trace equivalence on a fixed probe set rather than by syntax; (b) resurrection — a technology extinct for more than N generations reappears; (c) horizontal transfer — a meme's first appearance in a lineage whose ancestors never had it; (d) lineage collapse — meme-pool entropy dropping more than 30% in one night. Each is one SQL query over the meme table and each produces a headline a human actually wants to read.

On your phone

The archipelago as a small map, each island tinted by meme-pool size, plus one headline event. Below it a CHRONICLE: reverse-chronological one-sentence historical events auto-generated from the event table, phrased by a Qwen3-4B pass — 'On night 214, the last woman who knew how to bank a fire died on Ashen Isle.' The chronicle is the binge-for-an-hour artifact, and it is exactly what a thermal receipt printer wants: a $30 printer on the desk emitting the previous night's three chronicle lines every morning is the best off-screen check-in ritual of any idea here.

Your hardware

Tiny. Islands are vmap batches of a small grid; imitation is a few BC gradient steps per teaching event, not a training run. This is the idea that most comfortably runs 24/7 on 8GB while leaving room for the LLM chronicler to share the GPU. 64GB RAM matters more than VRAM: the meme lineage DB and per-agent program stores are the memory hogs and they live CPU-side.

What you have to do

Autonomous; the phone action is READING, which is the point. Nudges: trigger a catastrophe, seed a technology onto one island to see whether it spreads, change the sea-level period.

First weekend

Saturday: one island, agents with age and death, one hardcoded skill held by one agent, and the imitation channel. Sunday: the meme table, the extinction detector, and a page that prints the chronicle. You will watch a technology die on Sunday evening, and that alone justifies the weekend.

Six months in

A phylogeny of thousands of meme nodes across 9 lineages, a chronicle holding a year of readable history, several documented dark ages and rediscoveries, and a visible difference between islands that a stranger perceives in ten seconds. The chronicle, printed and bound, is a physical book of a world's history.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The technologies themselves stay simple. This design spends its complexity budget on transmission dynamics rather than depth of invention, so expect 20-40 distinct technologies per lineage at the fire/shelter/tool tier and no further. If you want deep tech AND deep culture you must merge this with KINDLING's Stitch loop — the meme becomes the abstraction, which is a real and attractive option but roughly doubles the build. Second honesty: imitation learning between tiny agents is fiddly, and getting transmission fidelity into the goldilocks band (not zero, not one) will take real tuning. Too faithful and you get no drift, no loss, and no story at all.

Stack

JAX for the island sims, Flax or PyTorch for the small BC updates, SQLite with a memes table (id, parent_id, lineage_id, term_hash, born_night, died_night) — a real queryable phylogeny — networkx or d3 for rendering it, Qwen3-4B for chronicle phrasing, python-escpos driving a USB thermal printer, shared PWA + ntfy + Tailscale stack.

Verified

DeepMind's cultural transmission line: 'Learning few-shot imitation as cultural transmission' (Nature Communications 2023) and 'Learning Robust Real-Time Cultural Transmission without Human Data' (arXiv 2203.00715) — the ablations identify the minimal sufficient starter kit (memory, expert dropout, attentional bias toward the expert, automatic domain randomization), which is exactly what to implement. 'Artificial Generational Intelligence: Cultural Accumulation in Reinforcement Learning' (arXiv 2406.00392) makes the key point for this design: accumulation appears only when social learning and independent learning are BALANCED, and existing generational algorithms fail to capture the open-ended nature of it — that gap is the room this project lives in. Stitch (POPL 2023) if you take the merge-with-KINDLING option. Craftax/JaxMARL confirm the multi-agent-in-JAX scaling; JaxMARL (NeurIPS 2024 D&B) reports up to 12,500x speedups over CPU MARL pipelines and is worth reading for its vmapped multi-agent patterns even if you write your own env.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The invention engine is the weakest of the six and it collides with the transmission channel. Random subtree mutation over a behaviour DSL, filtered only by survival, is genetic programming with a sparse, noisy, credit-free fitness signal: it finds programs of a few nodes and stops. Meanwhile transmission is lossy BY DESIGN. For a program of L nodes copied at per-node fidelity f, retention goes as f^L, so the technologies most worth accumulating are precisely the ones transmission cannot hold. That is a built-in complexity cap, and it is low: expect single-digit-node programs, maybe 5-15 durable memes per lineage rather than the claimed 20-40, with invention plateauing around month 2. Note this is a ceiling on TECHNOLOGY only — the history keeps generating indefinitely, and that is a genuinely different and defensible product from the one the brief asked for.

Is the discovery real?

Low on the sim side — lineage-original programs are real discoveries, extinction and rediscovery are real dynamics, and the sea-level/contact machinery is honest non-stationarity. The authorship creeps in at the chronicler: a Qwen pass turning event rows into 'the last woman who knew how to bank a fire died on Ashen Isle' will confabulate significance for statistically trivial events, and the chronicle is the headline artifact. Gate it to templated facts with the model only choosing phrasing, or the project's most shareable output becomes its least trustworthy one. Separate design incoherence worth fixing before week 1: memes are simultaneously DSL terms with a term_hash in a phylogeny table AND transferred by behavioural cloning into a neural policy. BC yields weights, not terms — after one update the learner has no term to hash, so parent_id becomes an inference from probe-trace clustering rather than a fact, and your 'real queryable phylogeny' is a fuzzy clustering wearing a schema.

Compute honesty

Yes, the most honest of the RL designs — vmapped 64x64 islands, a few BC gradient steps per teaching event, no training run in the loop. The uncosted risk is not compute but bootstrap: DeepMind's cultural-transmission results depended on a scripted expert bot to imitate, plus ADR and large-scale training. Here generation 0 has nothing worth copying, so the transmission machinery has no signal until innovation has already succeeded, and innovation is blind mutation. Two weak mechanisms mutually bootstrapping is the real schedule risk, not GPU hours.

Nonsense risk

Low. Memes are executable and filtered by survival, so nothing meaningless persists. The risk is the opposite: the surviving memes will be so simple that naming them technologies overstates them, and the drama (loss, drift, rediscovery) will be doing all the narrative work.

Claims to re-check

The citations are accurate: 'Learning few-shot imitation as cultural transmission' (Nat Comms 2023), arXiv 2203.00715, arXiv 2406.00392, JaxMARL's up-to-12,500x figure. The interpretation is where it slips. 2406.00392's actual finding — accumulation appears only when social and independent learning are balanced, and existing generational algorithms fail to be open-ended — is cited as 'the room this project lives in'; it reads at least as well as evidence the effect is fragile and narrow, and the pitch takes the optimistic reading without argument. The DeepMind 'starter kit' is presented as a recipe to implement, but its ablations were run with a privileged expert present by construction, which this design does not have.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Give innovation a search engine. Replace blind subtree mutation with a within-lifetime hill-climb — an agent tries small variants of a program it knows against a hunger/energy proxy over a few hundred ticks and keeps the better one — so that what enters the transmission channel is worth transmitting. That raises the complexity of the thing being copied, which is the only quantity that moves this design's ceiling; fidelity tuning cannot, because it only decides how fast simple things are lost.

▸

BYTE REEF

A spatial soup where every agent IS a short program executing on a tiny VM, eating energy and rewriting itself and its neighbours — technology is whatever code motif evolution keeps, found by mining the genome corpus for reused subroutines and proving function by ablation. No fitness function, no tech tree, no authored anything.

Not real depth
The world

A 256x256 lattice; each site holds a 64-byte tape and a program counter. The instruction set is a Brainfuck derivative extended with six world verbs — SENSE_ENERGY, MOVE, EAT, EMIT(byte), READ_NEIGHBOR, WRITE_NEIGHBOR. That extension is the crucial modification to BFF: the programs are EMBODIED. They have a position, a metabolism, and can act on a shared spatial resource field, rather than only self-copying in a formless soup. Energy diffuses and regrows spatially, every executed instruction costs energy, running out is death, and accumulating enough triggers fission.

How invention actually happens

Nothing but selection and self-modification, which is the entire point — all the engineering goes into the DETECTOR. Nightly: mine the corpus of living genomes for frequent subsequences (generalized suffix automaton, or SPADE-style sequential pattern mining, over the population). Then test each candidate motif for FUNCTION by ablation — splice it out of 200 living genomes and measure survival delta in a short sandboxed rerun. A motif with a significant negative ablation delta is a TECHNOLOGY: a discovered subroutine the organism needs. Then build the dependency graph over motifs and run the RAF algorithm on it to find autocatalytic clusters — motif sets that collectively sustain each other. Those clusters are 'civilizations' in the only sense this substrate can offer, and they are detected, not authored.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The substrate is Turing-complete and genome length is unbounded if you allow growth, so the space of discoverable programs is literally infinite with no authored ceiling of any kind. This is the only idea here with no designed tech tree even implicitly. The honest counterweight is that unbounded does not mean interesting: BFF gets self-replicators in ~40% of runs within 16k epochs and Avida reaches logic operations, but nobody has ever gotten a legible civilization out of a code soup and you probably will not either. What you WILL reliably get is genuine unfaked open-endedness at the motif level plus parasites, hyperparasites and immunity — the classic Tierra arc — which is real emergent arms racing.

The ladder

Week 1: the VM, the soup, energy; replicators appear. This is a known-reproducible result, so week 1 has a near-guaranteed payoff. Week 2: spatial embodiment via MOVE/EAT — now replicators must also forage, and you get motility. Weeks 3-4: motif mining plus ablation testing; the first named technology, usually a compact copy loop. Month 2: parasites (genomes exploiting a neighbour's copy loop), then resistance. Month 3: RAF clusters — motif groups that only function together. Wildest plausible: spatial territoriality, a signalling motif via EMIT that coordinates neighbours, and multicellular-ish clumps of specialized genomes.

What kills you

Energy, and nothing else. No reward function anywhere in the codebase. Spatial energy regrowth creates patches, patches create competition, competition creates everything else. Death is running the tape with an empty battery.

How the world changes

Mutation rate as a slow oscillation; periodic cosmic-ray bursts; instruction-set drift as an epoch event (retire an opcode, add another); and merging two independently-evolved soups, which is the single most interesting experiment this substrate offers — a real contact-between-worlds event with a measurable, gambleable outcome.

How it knows something is new

Four signals. (a) Selective sweep detection: a motif whose population frequency derivative crosses a threshold — something just won. (b) First-ever motif by canonical hash WITH a significant ablation delta. (c) RAF closure events: the first detection of a new autocatalytic set. (d) A complexity proxy: gzip size of the population's genome corpus, normalized — a step change means the population just got structurally richer. Report at most one per morning, ranked.

On your phone

The soup itself as a 256x256 image, hue = genome hash, brightness = energy. It is genuinely beautiful and different every night, so the 30-second check-in is just 'look at the reef'. One headline: the night's top motif, its ablation delta, and a hex dump. Sonification is the killer feature here and this is the substrate that most deserves it — map EMIT bytes plus birth/death events to a generative Web Audio stream (or an icecast stream off the laptop) and you have a soup radio you leave running. Most passive check-in possible.

Your hardware

Cheapest of the six by far. A 65k-cell soup of 64-byte tapes is 4MB; VM steps vectorize beautifully in JAX (numba or plain numpy also works). No neural network at all, so 8GB is barely touched and you could run 20 soups concurrently. The 64GB RAM is what you actually want, for the genome corpus archive and the motif mining. Best hardware fit of the six and the worst legibility — those two facts are related.

What you have to do

Fully autonomous. The interesting nudges are experiments rather than maintenance: merge two soups, retire an opcode, spike the mutation rate. Each is a one-line config change with a genuinely unpredictable outcome.

First weekend

Saturday: the BF VM, a random soup, the copy-and-mutate loop. You will likely see replicators emerge by Saturday night — an unusually strong first-weekend payoff because the result is already reproducible in the literature. Sunday: energy, space and EAT, plus the soup-image renderer and a cron that pushes the nightly image and starts the radio stream.

Six months in

A large motif library with ablation-verified function, documented parasite/host arms races, detected RAF clusters, and a year of soup images that make a gorgeous time-lapse. Whether any of it looks like CIVILIZATION is genuinely uncertain — that is the bet you would be making.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Highest-variance idea in the set. The realistic ceiling is ECOLOGY, not civilization: replication, parasitism, resistance, motility, maybe signalling. Two, perhaps three capability tiers, and they are biological rather than technological. If what you want is cavemen discovering fire, this will not give it to you, and I would rather you know that now than discover it in month 3. Build it if the appeal is genuine unfaked open-endedness plus beautiful images and a radio station; skip it if the appeal is watching a civilization.

Stack

JAX or numba for the VM, SQLite for genome snapshots and motif tables, a suffix-automaton motif miner (pure Python is fine at this scale), a short RAF implementation, a Web Audio PWA for the radio, ntfy, Tailscale.

Verified

'Computational Life: How Well-formed, Self-replicating Programs Emerge from Simple Interaction' (arXiv 2406.19108, Agüera y Arcas et al., 2024): random Brainfuck programs interacting with no fitness function, no selection pressure and no designer produce self-replicators in roughly 40% of runs within 16k epochs, mostly via self-modification, with or without background mutation; the same holds on real Z80/8080 instruction sets with ecosystem-like dynamics. Follow-up 'BFF: Simple explanations for complex phenomena' (arXiv 2607.01483) and 'Self-replication and Computational Universality' (arXiv 2510.08342). 'Non-Spatial Hash Chemistry as a Minimalistic Open-Ended Evolutionary System' (arXiv 2404.18027) for the minimal-substrate framing. RAF theory: 'A Concise and Formal Definition of RAF Sets and the RAF Algorithm' (arXiv 2303.01809) gives a short implementable spec; Hordijk & Steel's detection algorithms (Algorithms for Molecular Biology 2015) for the analysis side. Sakana's ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799, github.com/SakanaAI/asal, MIT Press Artificial Life 31(3)) is the modern precedent for automated open-endedness detection across Boids, Particle Life, Game of Life, Lenia and NCA — its temporal-open-endedness score is directly borrowable if you would rather score novelty in a foundation-model representation space than hand-roll the gzip proxy.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

The most damaging fact in the whole document, and this pitch does not mention it: Avida's rise in complexity is DRIVEN BY AUTHORED REWARDS — explicit energy bonuses for computing NAND, AND, and the rest of the logic ladder. Strip the reward ladder and Avida populations do not climb; they optimise replication speed and their genomes SHRINK. Tierra likewise settles into a parasite/host ecology within days. 'Energy and nothing else' is exactly the regime that has never produced a complexity ratchet in thirty years of the literature. Worse, the energy-per-instruction metabolism actively selects against the unbounded genome growth the pitch relies on: longer programs cost strictly more per replication cycle. Expect replicators week 1 (genuinely reproducible), genome shrinkage weeks 2-4, parasites and resistance month 2, and then a stable ecology forever. Two tiers, biological, permanently.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six — effectively zero. There is no authored content anywhere and the pitch is admirably direct that no one has ever gotten a legible civilisation out of a code soup. The honesty is real and should be credited. The problem is that the brief asked for cavemen discovering fire and this substrate has no channel for it, which the pitch also says.

Compute honesty

Fully honest and the cheapest here by a wide margin. A 65k-cell soup of 64-byte tapes is ~4MB, VM steps vectorise, no neural network anywhere, 20 concurrent soups is realistic, and the 64GB of RAM is correctly identified as the resource that matters for corpus mining. Nothing in the hardware section needs correcting.

Nonsense risk

Highest of the six, though not in the usual way — nothing generated is meaningless, but nothing is interpretable either. A hex-dump motif called a technology is a naming decision, not a finding, and the RAF clusters as specified would be beautiful artefacts of an invented graph. The sonified soup radio and the nightly image are genuinely excellent and are the actual product; the technology layer is decoration on top of an ecology.

Claims to re-check

(1) The 40%-of-runs-within-16k-epochs figure is real but cherry-picked: it holds at one specific background mutation rate (~0.024%), and the same paper reports state transitions as rare as 3 in 1000 under a different condition. Quoting it as the headline reproducibility number oversells the week-1 payoff. arXiv 2607.01483 (BFF: Simple explanations for complex phenomena) does exist, as does 2406.19108. (2) The Avida claim — 'Avida reaches logic operations' — is stated as though it supports a no-fitness-function design when it is the strongest single piece of evidence against it. (3) Methodologically, three of the four detectors are broken as specified: ablation by excision from a BF-like genome shifts every downstream offset and unbalances brackets, so nearly every ablation is lethal and the detector will label essentially all motifs technologies — you need a length-preserving in-place scramble control and a matched null distribution. RAF over a 'motif dependency graph' is a category error: RAF is defined on a catalytic reaction network with a food set and explicit catalysis relations; motifs do not catalyse one another's production, so you would be inventing the relation and then discovering properties of your own invention. And gzip over the corpus mostly tracks clonality and diversity, so it DROPS during a selective sweep even when the winner is structurally richer.

The one change that raises the ceiling

If you want a complexity ratchet you must make length pay. Make energy release conditional on non-trivial computation: spatially varying 'locks' that pay out only to a genome whose EMIT byte is a specified non-trivial function of a byte it sensed, with the function class drifting on a slow clock. That is Avida's logic-task reward reintroduced as a physical resource rather than a score, it is the only lever known to produce a complexity ratchet in code soups, and it costs you authoring a function class rather than a tech tree. Be clear-eyed that it is a partial retreat from the purity that is this idea's main selling point.

▸

LOAM

A world where the technology IS the landscape — tribes survive by irrigating, burning, terracing and above all breeding plants and animals whose genomes are themselves evolving, so domestication is a real evolutionary process the tribes drive rather than a recipe they unlock. The tech tree is written into the terrain and you can read it from orbit.

Ceilinged7.3
The world

256x256 raster in JAX. Layers: elevation (fixed), water (shallow-water cellular flow), soil nutrients, soil moisture, fire fuel load, biota. Each plant or animal carries a 16-gene vector — growth rate, water need, nutrient need, seed dispersal distance, toxicity, edible yield, palatability, and for animals flee distance, aggression, herd affinity, reproduction rate. These genomes mutate and are selected by the environment continuously: a real evolving ecosystem that runs whether or not agents exist. Tribes are PPO policies with bodies living on top of it.

How invention actually happens

Niche construction as a co-evolutionary loop between two coupled searches. AGENT SIDE: tribes have a small verb set that writes permanently into the raster — DIG (moves elevation and therefore water), BURN, PLANT(seed_in_hand), CULL(target), HERD, DAM, FENCE, CARRY_WATER. There is no 'irrigation' technology anywhere in the code; there is only digging, and irrigation is a PATTERN of digs a policy discovers because it raises yield. BIOTA SIDE: when a tribe selectively harvests the palatable plants and replants genomes it carried, it applies directional selection to the local plant genome distribution. Over hundreds of generations the plants in its territory BECOME domesticated — higher yield, lower toxicity, lower seed dispersal — which is precisely the archaeological signature of domestication, arising because the tribe's behaviour is the selection pressure. Nothing in the code says 'domestication'. The technology is therefore jointly a learned spatial pattern of world-modification (mined as recurring spatial motifs) and a genome cluster in biota space. Both are searched, both compound, and each reshapes the other's fitness landscape.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The invention space is the cross product of raster configurations (astronomically large, and unlike a tech tree it has no discrete states) with the biota genome space (16 continuous genes under agent-driven selection, an open evolutionary search against a moving target). Co-evolution is the unboundedness argument: the agents change the biota's selective environment, the biota changes the agents' resource landscape, and neither has a fixed optimum to converge to. It bottoms out at the resolution of the verb set — with eight verbs you get roughly the neolithic package and no further, because there is no channel for materials or mechanisms. This idea is deep in exactly one direction (ecology, landscape, domestication) and shallow in every other, and that is the trade.

The ladder

Week 1: raster, water flow, plants with genomes, no agents — watch an ecosystem run and watch plant genomes drift to fit the terrain. Already hypnotic. Week 2: tribes plus forage PPO; they overharvest and crash, which is the first real story. Weeks 3-4: PLANT and BURN enabled; the first fire regime appears, because burning raises nutrients after a delay so a burn-then-plant policy beats one that does not. That is slash-and-burn, discovered. Month 2: DIG and water — the first irrigation channel shows up as a visible line on the map and the 'look at the map' check-in becomes addictive. Month 3: measurable domestication — plot mean yield-gene inside versus outside tribal territory and watch the curves separate. That chart is the single strongest evidence-of-depth artifact in this whole document. Month 4+: herding and animal domestication (flee-distance gene falling inside herded populations — tameness, evolved), terracing on slopes, erosion as a consequence of over-digging, and a tribe that collapses from its own success. Wildest plausible: two tribes with incompatible landscape strategies — one irrigator, one pastoralist — in resource conflict, and a landscape a stranger can read as 'someone lives here'.

What kills you

Nutrient depletion, erosion, drought cycles, and the biota's own evolution. That last one gives you a free emergent failure mode: over-selecting for yield drives down the toxicity gene that was protecting the plant from a co-evolving pest, and the crop collapses. Real agricultural pathology, no authoring required. Tribes compete for watershed position, and an upstream tribe damming a river is a direct act of war.

How the world changes

A slow climate oscillation (500-night period) in rainfall and temperature, plus rare catastrophes — volcano, flood, pest bloom — drawn from a table whose TIMING is voted on by web visitors. And the world genuinely never resets: this is the one idea where the world's own accumulated history is the content, so run one long-lived world plus a couple of control shards rather than reseeding weekly.

How it knows something is new

Learning-progress surprise, done properly. Train a tiny conv net (a few hundred k params) to predict tomorrow's raster from today's, continuously fit on the world's own history. Push when its prediction error SPIKES — a Schmidhuber-style compression-progress signal that fires exactly when the world does something structurally new: the first channel, the first burn scar, the first terrace, the first collapse. Supplement with two explicit statistics: a domestication index (t-statistic on mean gene value inside versus outside territory) and spatial-motif mining that clusters recurring 8x8 patterns of agent-caused change and reports a new cluster.

On your phone

One image: the map in false colour with a diff overlay showing what changed last night in a hot colour. That is the entire 30-second check-in and it is genuinely great, because you see the SHAPE of what they built. Below it the domestication chart and one headline. The physical artifact that fits perfectly here is an e-ink frame on the wall showing the map, refreshed each morning — a slowly changing landscape is exactly what e-ink is good at, and the daily glance becomes ritual.

Your hardware

Raster sims are the cheapest thing a GPU does. 256x256 by ~10 float layers plus a few thousand biota entities in JAX is a few hundred MB; PPO on small conv policies fits easily; you can run 8 world shards in parallel inside 8GB. The surprise model is tiny. This will never be VRAM-bound — it is bound by how fast you want simulated years to pass, which makes 24/7 operation the actual feature rather than a workaround.

What you have to do

Autonomous. Nudges: add a verb — the highest-leverage knob in the whole design, since each new verb opens a technological direction — trigger a catastrophe, or fork the world to run a counterfactual.

First weekend

Saturday: raster layers, the water-flow stencil, plants with genomes reproducing and mutating; render a PNG every 100 ticks and stitch a time-lapse. That alone is a satisfying screensaver by Saturday night. Sunday: one tribe with FORAGE and PLANT, plus the nightly map-diff push. The unauthored moment lands immediately — plant genomes adapting to the terrain's moisture gradient without a line of code about adaptation.

Six months in

A landscape with centuries of legible history — old channels, abandoned terraces, forest that is now grassland — plus quantified domestication of two or three species, and a time-lapse of a year of world history that is the best possible thing to show another human being.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The neolithic, and that is it. Fire regime, irrigation, terracing, plant and animal domestication, maybe rudimentary land tenure and conflict over water. There is no path from here to metallurgy or machines without bolting on a different substrate entirely. Roughly four tiers and the last arrives around month 4. The other honest risk is balance: the ecology needs careful tuning, an unbalanced biota either goes extinct in week 1 or fills everything by week 2, and getting the parameters into a regime that stays interesting for months is real fiddly work no paper will do for you.

Stack

JAX raster sim (shallow-water flow is a ~30-line stencil), PPO, a small Flax conv surprise-model, SQLite for the event and genome-stats DB, canvas or matplotlib for the map render, a Raspberry Pi Zero driving an e-ink panel (or just a pinned phone page), shared PWA + ntfy + Tailscale stack.

Verified

Niche construction theory (Odling-Smee, Laland, Feldman) is the framing — organisms modifying their own selective environment is the mechanism this whole idea instantiates. Speed budget verified via Craftax (arXiv 2402.16801: 1B steps under an hour on one GPU) and Craftax-MA (arXiv 2511.04904: 250M multi-agent steps in 57 min on an L40S) — a raster sim is cheaper than either. purejaxrl for the PPO skeleton; JaxMARL (NeurIPS 2024 D&B, blog.foersterlab.com/jaxmarl) for vmapped multi-agent rollout patterns. Schmidhuber's compression-progress / learning-progress formulation is the basis for the surprise detector; Sakana's ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799, github.com/SakanaAI/asal, code public) is the modern precedent for automated novelty scoring over ALife renders and its temporal-open-endedness objective is directly borrowable if you would rather run a small CLIP over map images than train your own predictor. OMNI-EPIC (arXiv 2405.15568, jennyzhangzt.com/omni-epic) is the reference for the archive-plus-model-of-interestingness pattern if you later want an LLM proposing catastrophes and challenges rather than a fixed table.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Genuine mechanism, but the ladder splits in two and only the bottom half is safe. The domestication half will work: directional selection on a gene distribution driven by agent harvesting preference needs no clever policy, only a preference, and it produces a measurable archaeological signature. That is the most honest discovery mechanism in the entire document. The landscape-engineering half is the part most likely never to arrive. Burn -> delayed nutrient -> plant, or dig a 20-cell channel -> water arrives -> yield rises 300 ticks later, is a sparse-reward, long-horizon, spatially extended construction problem where a HALF-BUILT channel pays strictly negative (stamina spent, no water yet). Vanilla PPO is famously defeated by exactly this shape — Craftax exists to demonstrate it, and Craftax-MA's own conclusion is that IPPO/MAPPO/PQN all fail its long-horizon credit assignment. So expect week 2 (overharvest and crash) and month 3 (measurable domestication) to land, and months 2-4 (irrigation, terracing, the map a stranger can read) to be at serious risk of never landing. Even if they do, the verb set caps you at 6-10 recurring motifs and the neolithic, as stated.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six. There is no authored tech tree even implicitly; irrigation is a pattern of DIG and domestication is a shift in a gene distribution, and neither word appears in the code. The only authoring is the verb set itself, which the pitch correctly identifies as the highest-leverage knob and the honest bound. The catastrophe table is authored but is weather, not technology.

Compute honesty

Honest and, if anything, over-modest — except for one backwards claim. 'A raster sim is cheaper than Craftax' is wrong per tick (655k cells x ~10 layers of stencil work vs Craftax's small per-agent grid ops), though both are trivial on a GPU and 8 shards in 8GB is right. The uncosted item is not FLOPs, it is numerics: explicit shallow-water flow on a 256^2 raster is CFL-limited and 'a ~30-line stencil' will go unstable or silently lose mass, and mass non-conservation quietly corrupts the moisture field that the domestication signal is measured against. Budget a week there, and add a mass-conservation assert on every tick.

Nonsense risk

Lowest in the set, essentially nil. Every technology here is a physical pattern in a raster with a measurable consequence on yield, water or erosion. Nothing can be drivel, and the domestication chart (mean yield-gene inside vs outside territory) is the single strongest evidence-of-depth artifact proposed anywhere in these six ideas.

Claims to re-check

Citations are sound (Craftax 2402.16801, Craftax-MA 2511.04904 confirmed real with 250M interactions in under an hour — the L40S/57-minute precision is unverified paraphrase; ASAL 2412.17799 and OMNI-EPIC 2405.15568 both real; niche-construction framing is used correctly). The dubious piece is the novelty detector, not a citation: a continuously-refit predictor of tomorrow's raster on a world with stochastic weather and a 500-night climate cycle is a noisy-TV machine. Prediction ERROR will spike on rain fronts, fire stochasticity and its own optimiser's non-stationarity far more often than on the first channel, and you will be paged every morning. Schmidhuber's actual quantity is compression PROGRESS — the derivative of error measured on a held-out replay of past rasters — and the design as written uses the wrong statistic.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Fix credit assignment so landscape engineering is reachable: give agents an option layer — a small library of parameterised multi-tick construction routines (dig-a-line-of-length-n-toward-lower-elevation, burn-a-patch-then-return) searched by quality-diversity over spatial-signature descriptors rather than by gradients, so a channel is one decision instead of four hundred. That is the difference between shipping an ecology simulator and shipping a map on which a stranger can see that someone lives there, and it is the same missing layer that limits KINDLING.

Changing worlds

The world as antagonist and curriculum; many worlds in parallel

▸

Twelve Anvils

Twelve planets with different physics constants each get a tribe that survives by writing programs in a survival DSL; every night the system compresses its successful programs into new named primitives — those primitives ARE the technologies, and they differ per planet. The product is the comparative atlas: which worlds invented fire, which never could, and what they invented instead.

Ceilinged
The world

Twelve seeded planets, each a hex-globe (icosphere, ~8k cells) with its own PhysicsVector: gravity, day length, atmospheric O2 partial pressure, ignition threshold, water phase boundaries, thermal conductivity of a per-world material table, ore abundance curves, growing-season length, predator pressure. Agents are tribes (population count, kcal store, held materials, known library). Resources are cell-local material stacks with property vectors (hardness, density, melt point, calorific value, toxicity) drawn from the world's material table — so 'flint' on world 3 may be soft, and world 7 has no free O2 so open fire is physically unreachable.

How invention actually happens

DreamCoder wake/sleep, stripped to what one person can build. Data structures: (a) Program = typed lambda term over ~25 base primitives (gather, hold, strike(a,b), heat(x,T), quench, wait(d), plant, weave, carry, if-then over sensed state); (b) Library = list of {name, typed term, MDL cost, usage count, world_id}; (c) TaskSet = survival tasks auto-derived from the world state ('produce >=1800 kcal/person for 14 days at 260K given only cells reachable in 2 days'). WAKE: type-directed bottom-up enumeration with a unigram/bigram prior over library entries, budget measured in NODE EXPANSIONS not wallclock (thermal throttling must never change fitness), plus a local Qwen3.5-4B proposing candidate skeletons that get typechecked and executed against the deterministic world simulator — traceback means retry with the error string, Homunculus-style. SLEEP-ABSTRACTION: greedy MDL compression over the corpus of solutions (simplified Stitch: anti-unify subtrees, score by corpus-bits saved, accept the top candidate, repeat 5x/night). Accepted abstraction gets a name from the local model ('heat(clay,900K)->quench' becomes 'firing') and enters the library permanently. SLEEP-DREAM: sample programs from the library, run them in the world, keep any that produce a novel useful material or surplus — those become new tasks. A civilization's tech = its library. Losing the tribe deletes it.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The search space is the space of typed programs — infinite. What makes it compound rather than merely infinite is that each accepted abstraction rewrites the search distribution: a 12-node program becomes a 3-node program, so the same node budget now reaches depth-40 behaviours it could never enumerate before. That is the same ratchet DreamCoder demonstrated across domains (PLDI 2021), and the abstraction set is not authored by me — I authored only the 25 base primitives and the type system. Twelve different PhysicsVectors mean twelve different compression optima: the libraries provably diverge because the corpus of successful programs differs. Where it actually bottoms out: the abstraction wall. Once the shortest unsolved task needs ~8 more nodes than the budget can enumerate even with the best library, progress stops until the task generator produces an intermediate rung. That is why the changing climate matters — it manufactures intermediate rungs for free.

The ladder

Week 1: 25 primitives, one world, tasks are 'get 1200 kcal'; the library learns 'gather-then-eat' and 'strike(stone,stone)->sharp' and you watch a two-entry library form. Week 2-3: MDL compression starts naming things you did not author — a 'cordage' abstraction that is really weave(strip(bark),twist) appearing on 3 of 12 worlds independently. Month 2: hierarchical entries — 'firing' built on 'sustained-heat' built on 'fuel-stack'; the atlas shows world 7 (no O2) routing around fire entirely via a 'pressure-forge' abstraction using volcanic vents. Month 4: libraries reach depth 6-8 and inter-world comparison gets genuinely interesting — convergence rates per abstraction, 'inventions unique to one world'. Wildest plausible endpoint at a year: a world where an abstraction is discovered, lost in a famine (tribe dies, library dies), and re-derived 4000 sim-years later in a structurally different form, and the novelty detector flags the re-derivation as a near-duplicate at the MDL level but not at the syntax level. That is a real result about convergent evolution of technology that nobody authored.

What kills you

Calories, warmth, injury, and childbirth. Each tick a tribe must cover kcal demand (scaled by population and temperature) and survive hazard rolls whose probability the library reduces (a hardness-4 tool cuts butchery time; a shelter cuts cold deaths). Population grows on surplus and crashes on deficit. Below ~40 people, complex library entries start failing transmission rolls and are DELETED — Henrich's Tasmania effect, so technology is genuinely lossy. Between worlds there is no combat (they never meet), so competition is against the planet and against your own past best: each world's fitness is tracked as max sustained population and library MDL depth.

How the world changes

Two layers. Slow: each world runs a Milankovitch-ish climate oscillator (three sine terms with per-world periods and amplitudes) driving temperature bands and sea level, plus a tectonic drift that migrates ore seams and opens/closes mountain passes on a 50k-year cycle. Fast: stochastic catastrophes with per-world rates (bolide, supervolcano, plague, decade drought). The critical design point is that climate change INVALIDATES LIBRARY ENTRIES — an abstraction whose preconditions include 'liquid water in cell' silently stops solving tasks when the cell freezes, which fires a re-search and usually produces a different abstraction. The world is the task generator.

How it knows something is new

Three signals, combined into one surprise score.
