(1) FIRST-EVER: every event is emitted with a structured signature (event_type, argument type-hashes); a global seen_signatures table across all twelve worlds means 'first time in the multiverse' is a one-line query. (2) MDL DELTA: when a new abstraction reduces total corpus description length by more than 6%, that is objectively a big idea, not a tweak — this is the best novelty measure in the whole set of ideas because it is a number the compressor already computes. (3) DISTRIBUTION SHOCK: nightly z-score of per-world summary stats (population, library depth, kcal margin) against a 30-night rolling baseline. Push to ntfy only the top 3 by score per day, hard-capped; everything else goes in the chronicle for bingeing.

On your phone

Twelve tiles in a 3x4 grid, one per world, each tile: a 40x40 climate sparkline, population number, library depth as a stack of bars, and a one-line 'newest technology: FIRING (day 8,214)'. Red border = a world is dying. Tap a tile for that world's library as a scrollable tree with each entry's discovery date, current usage count, and the exact program text; tap an entry for the three-frame animation of the tribe executing it on the hex map. The daily push is one sentence: 'World 7 compressed a new primitive (-11% corpus MDL): PRESSURE-FORGE. No other world has it.'

Your hardware

This one barely needs the GPU, which is why it is the safest first build. The simulator and the program search are pure Python/NumPy and CPU-bound: 12 worlds = 12 worker processes on the laptop's cores, each with its own SQLite file in WAL mode; 64GB RAM is plenty since the biggest object is the program corpus (tens of MB). The 5070's 8GB runs llama.cpp with Qwen3.5-4B-Instruct at Q4_K_M (~3GB weights, tens of tok/s on a laptop Blackwell) for skeleton proposals and naming — called maybe 2k times a night, entirely optional; kill it and the system still works, just searches blinder. Nothing here needs PyTorch, so the sm_120 toolchain risk is zero. Benchmark hygiene: all budgets in node expansions and all costs in simulated days, never seconds.

What you have to do

Zero chores. Weekly nudge, 10 minutes: a 'primitive proposals' page shows the 3 abstractions the compressor most wants to accept and lets you veto (rarely needed, but it is the good kind of tinkering); plus a slider to add a 13th world with a physics vector you pick by hand, which is the most fun button in the project.

First weekend

Saturday: hex world, 8 materials with property vectors, one tribe, kcal accounting, and the typed DSL with 25 primitives plus an interpreter. Sunday morning: bottom-up enumerative search that solves 'feed the tribe' and logs solutions. Sunday afternoon: the anti-unification compressor — 60 lines, find the most-compressive shared subtree, name it 'skill_001', add to library, re-run search. The delightful moment is watching skill_001's definition print and realising you would have called it 'a cutting tool' and you never typed those words. Sunday night: kick off 12 worlds, wake up to twelve different libraries.

Six months in

Libraries 200-600 entries deep per world with 6-9 levels of nesting; a cross-world convergence matrix showing which abstractions are universal and which are physics-contingent; a graveyard page of technologies lost in population crashes with the exact famine that killed them. You will have rewritten the search prior at least twice (unigram to bigram to a small learned recognition model) and added primitives when the abstraction wall bit. The surprise at month 6 is mostly comparative: not 'they invented X' but 'seven worlds needed X to get to Y and world 4 didn't'.

Where it stops (self-reported)

It plateaus when enumeration cannot reach the next rung even with a good library — realistically around library depth 8-10, or 3-5 months in, unless you keep feeding it harder tasks. Two honest weaknesses: the base primitive set is mine, so the vocabulary of possible thought is authored even though the abstractions are not (world 7 can only 'route around fire' in ways expressible in 25 primitives); and MDL compression rewards frequent patterns, which is not the same as rewarding important ones — you will get elegant abstractions for boring things. Mitigations exist (weight compression by task value, mint new primitives from LLM-written+tested functions), but be clear that this is a compounding ratchet with friction, not perpetual motion.

Stack

Python 3.12, NumPy, SQLite (one DB per world + multiverse.db for cross-world signatures), msgpack event log, uvicorn+FastAPI serving a vanilla-JS canvas PWA, Tailscale serve for phone access, nightly rsync of a static snapshot to GitHub Pages so it works when the laptop sleeps, ntfy.sh for push. llama.cpp server with GBNF grammar for constrained JSON. Optional: python-escpos to print the daily 'first-ever' events on a thermal receipt printer.

Verified

Verified via search: DreamCoder wake-sleep library learning is real and published (PLDI 2021, dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454080; NSF PAR 10320121) — abstraction sleep uses refactoring/E-graph matching to extract common sub-components and accepts them by MDL. Stitch formulates library induction as greedy MDL optimisation over a program corpus and is much simpler to reimplement (arXiv 2211.16605, 'Top-Down Synthesis for Library Learning'); LILO (arXiv 2310.19791) shows LLM-assisted variants that also auto-document abstractions, which is where the naming step comes from. TAP / adjacent-possible formalism for combinatorial invention: arXiv 2204.14115 and arXiv 2307.15083. Local model for 8GB: Aug-2026 guides converge on Qwen3.5-4B (or Gemma 3 4B) at Q4_K_M as the standard fit for 8GB VRAM (canitrun.dev/guides/best-llms-for-8gb-vram, atomic.chat/blog/guides/best-local-llm-8gb). Planet-gen priors worth stealing from: github.com/MightyBOBcnc/nixis (Python, icosphere, tectonics+climate+biomes) and github.com/FreezeDriedMangos/realistic-planet-generation-and-simulation.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Real ratchet, but it stalls far earlier than claimed. The binding constraint is not the enumeration wall they name — it is corpus homogeneity. MDL compression only finds subtrees that RECUR, and every task here is one scalar objective ('get N kcal, don't freeze'). A single-objective task family produces a near-homogeneous solution corpus, so after the first ~10-25 abstractions the marginal corpus-bits-saved collapses and their own 6% MDL-delta trigger stops firing. Expect a library of 15-40 entries at nesting depth 3-4, flat by week 3-6 — not '200-600 entries at depth 6-9 by month 6'. Second wall behind it: each accepted abstraction buys a constant-factor reduction on the exact pattern compressed, not an exponential; type-directed bottom-up enumeration on ~1 core/world reaches maybe 10^6-10^7 nodes/night, i.e. effective program size ~12-16 nodes. Climate invalidation manufactures re-search, but re-search over the same primitive set and the same kcal objective mostly re-derives cousins of existing entries — which their own novelty detector will correctly flag as MDL near-duplicates, and that is a ceiling symptom being reported as a headline result.

Is the discovery real?

The abstractions are genuinely not authored — this is the least fake discovery in the set. But the SEMANTICS are fully authored: the 25 primitives, the type system, and above all the world simulator's material-transformation table. 'Fire' is discoverable only because someone wrote combustion into heat(); world 7's 'pressure-forge' is discoverable only because someone wrote vent chemistry. So library depth is bounded by the depth of the authored state space, and the depth ladder's late rungs (writing, trade, institutions) have no representation at all — nothing in the DSL can express them, so 'eventually something wild' is unreachable by construction, not by search budget. The honest framing is: the abstractions are found, the vocabulary of possible thought and the set of reachable world-states are both hand-written.

Compute honesty

Mostly honest, one significant understatement. Hashing-free pure-Python/NumPy sim and SQLite are fine, and putting budgets in node expansions rather than wallclock is the right call. But 'barely needs the GPU / it is the safest first build' hides that DreamCoder's cost is CPU enumeration, and the published experiments used tens of cores per domain for hours-to-days per wake cycle. Twelve worlds on one laptop means roughly one core per world — order 1-2% of a single published DreamCoder domain run, and Python-interpreted enumeration is another 10-50x behind their OCaml enumerator. The wake phase will be starved, which is exactly the regime where the ratchet does not start. The LLM side is genuinely optional and cheap (~2k Qwen-class calls/night at Q4_K_M fits 8GB easily).

Nonsense risk

Low-moderate, and the lowest in the set. Every abstraction executes against a deterministic simulator, so entries mean something mechanically. Two leaks: the 4B model's names are flattering fiction over terms like (lambda (strike x x)) and will make a shallow library read as deep — log the term next to the name always; and their own admission that MDL rewards frequency not importance is correct and severe (you will get an elegant abstraction for 'walk two cells then pick up').

Claims to re-check

Sourcing is unusually good; the problems are misuse, not fabrication. (a) 'the abstraction set is not authored by me' is true but oversold — see above. (b) Stitch is described as 'greedy MDL optimisation'; it is a top-down branch-and-bound/beam search over abstraction space, and the 60-line anti-unification weekend version will be materially weaker than either Stitch or DreamCoder's version-space refactoring. (c) The Henrich/Tasmania 'complex entries fail transmission below ~40 people' mechanism is presented as settled; the population-size-drives-complexity result is contested (Vaesen et al. 2016 PNAS; Read's critiques) and here it is a designer-set knob that directly caps depth. (d) DreamCoder is cited as evidence of a compounding ratchet 'across domains' — true, but always with a human-authored, difficulty-ordered task curriculum, which is precisely the ingredient this design replaces with a calorie threshold. That substitution is the whole risk and the notes do not acknowledge it.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Replace the calorie-threshold task generator with an autotelic goal generator over the world's state space — goals as unreached regions of the material-property / settlement / population-structure space, with each accepted abstraction seeding new goals that use it as a precondition. Corpus heterogeneity, not search budget, is what keeps MDL delta above zero; this is the single change that converts a 1-D curriculum into an open one and it is where the whole ceiling lives.

▸

Ashfall Chemistries

Every planet is seeded with its own deterministic pseudo-random chemistry — a hash function that says what happens when you combine any two substances under any conditions — and tribes discover it by experiment, building recipe DAGs that are their technology. No LLM in the loop and no authored recipe table, so a hundred thousand substances per world costs nothing but CPU.

Not real depth
The world

Six to twelve planets, each a 256x128 equirect grid with elevation, temperature, precipitation, and a crust composed of ~40 primitive substances whose abundance map is generated per world (volcanic worlds are metal-rich and sulfur-heavy; a water world hides everything under ocean). A substance is an integer id plus a 16-dim property vector: hardness, density, melt point, boil point, calorific value, conductivity, toxicity, reactivity, opacity, tensile strength, elasticity, solubility, magnetism, radioactivity, nutrition, decay rate. Tribes are villages with population, stores, a kiln/hearth with a max attainable temperature, and a personal recipe DAG. Everything a civilization can physically do is: bring substances together at some temperature, pressure, and duration, with or without a catalyst.

How invention actually happens

A keyed artificial chemistry plus an experiment scheduler. react(world_seed, sorted(a,b), temp_bucket, pressure_bucket, catalyst) = BLAKE2b hash -> a deterministic branch: 65% no reaction, 20% mixture (property vector = mass-weighted blend), 10% new compound (property vector = a NONLINEAR mix — element-wise blend plus a hash-seeded rotation matrix and a hash-seeded 'emergent bonus' on 1-2 dims), 5% decomposition into fragments. Because it is a hash, the chemistry is infinite, deterministic, reproducible from a 64-bit seed, requires zero storage until discovered, and is DIFFERENT ON EVERY PLANET. Discovery: each village runs an experiment scheduler — a UCB bandit over untried (pair, condition) cells, scored by expected information gain and by proximity to an unmet need ('we need hardness>6', 'we need something that burns above 900K'). Successful reactions are appended to a recipe DAG in SQLite. A technology is a named sub-DAG that reliably produces a substance crossing a need threshold. Two special detectors run nightly: AUTOCATALYTIC SET DETECTION (find cycles in the DAG where a product is a catalyst for its own precursors — this is the moment a craft becomes an industry, and it is a graph query, not a vibe) and PROPERTY FRONTIER tracking (a pyribs MAP-Elites archive whose behaviour dims are hardness x melt point x calorific value; a substance that lands in an empty cell is genuinely new capability).

Why it doesn't bottom out

The reachable substance graph branches at roughly (discovered substances choose 2) x condition buckets per generation, so the frontier grows super-exponentially in the TAP sense — Kauffman's adjacent possible with an efficiency factor. Crucially the space is not authored: I write the mixing function, not the products, and the seed makes each world's chemistry a different draw from the same generative law. Where it honestly bottoms out: hash chemistry is unbounded in COUNT but flat in MEANING — substance #91,000 is not more interesting than #900 unless deep combinations are systematically better. The design fix, and you must build it deliberately: emergent bonuses are only granted to products whose precursor DAG is deeper than the current median, and need thresholds escalate with population and cold, so the civilization is always chasing a property value it cannot reach with shallow recipes. With that, depth is selected for. Without it, you get a very large pile of grey sludge, and I'd rather say that up front.

The ladder

Week 1: 40 substances, one village, random pairwise experiments; you watch the first compound get discovered and named by property ('a hard, dense, high-melt solid'). Week 2: needs and thresholds — the village that finds hardness>5 gets better tools and outgrows its neighbour. Month 1: multi-step recipes and the first kiln temperature ceiling, so an entire branch of chemistry is gated behind discovering a better fuel — the first real tech-gate the system generated itself. Month 2: autocatalytic cycle detected; a substance whose production catalyses its own precursor means the village can mass-produce, population doubles, and the event is worth a push notification with a graph picture. Month 3-4: cross-planet comparison — 'metallurgy' (a cluster in property space) is reached on 5 of 8 worlds by wildly different DAGs, and on the sulfur world the equivalent cluster is reached through what is functionally biochemistry. Wildest plausible endpoint: a world discovers a substance with high calorific value AND low decay, i.e. storable fuel, which decouples them from the growing season entirely and produces a visible hockey-stick in their recipe count — self-generated industrial revolution from a hash function.

What kills you

Villages compete for grid cells with the substances they need. Fitness is population supported, which depends on tools (hardness), warmth (calorific value + shelter), food preservation (decay rate), and defence. Villages fight or trade on contact; a village with better material properties wins raids and absorbs the loser's recipe DAG (partial — 30-60% of entries transmit, weighted by how many people knew them). Starvation deletes recipes: any recipe not executed within N years and known by fewer than K people is forgotten. Between planets there is no contact; the competition there is your own dashboard leaderboard.

How the world changes

Geology gates chemistry, and geology moves. Ice ages (a global temperature oscillator with per-world period and amplitude) change what temperature a hearth can reach ambient-relative and freeze water-mediated reactions entirely; sea level change exposes or drowns mineral-rich shelf cells; volcanism spawns high-temperature, high-pressure vent cells that unlock a condition bucket no hearth can reach, making volcano-adjacent villages temporarily the most advanced on the planet until the eruption kills them. Tectonic collision raises mountains that expose new crust layers with substances absent from the surface for 10,000 years. Migration matters concretely: the same chemistry hash is global to the planet, but ABUNDANCE is local, so moving to a new continent means a different reachable subgraph.

How it knows something is new

MAP-Elites archive occupancy is the primary signal and it is beautifully cheap: a new substance that fills a previously empty cell of the hardness x melt-point x calorific-value grid is genuinely new capability, and 'cells filled' is a single number per world you can plot forever. Secondary: first-ever autocatalytic cycle, first-ever recipe of depth N, first cross-planet convergence (two worlds independently reach the same archive cell), and a substance whose property vector is Pareto-non-dominated by everything discovered anywhere. Nightly ranking pushes the top 2. Everything else is chronicle.

On your phone

A 2x4 grid of planets; each tile is that planet's MAP-Elites archive rendered as a tiny heatmap — literally a picture of how much of the possible-materials space that civilization has illuminated, filling up over months. Under it: population, recipe count, deepest recipe. Tap through to a scrollable substance codex sorted by discovery date with property bars and the DAG path that made it, plus a 'reactions tried / reactions that did something' ratio which is the civilization's science efficiency. Push text: 'Ashfall-3 filled a new frontier cell: hardness 8.2, melts at 1740K, made from 6 steps starting with river silt.'

Your hardware

Pure CPU, and that is a feature: 8-12 worlds as separate Python processes with NumPy for the grid layers, hash chemistry via hashlib/BLAKE2b (fast enough at millions of calls per night), pyribs for archives, SQLite per world. 64GB RAM lets you keep every discovered substance and every experiment result in memory. The GPU is free for other projects, or optionally runs a 4B local model purely to write flavour names and codex prose for discovered substances (cached forever, keyed by substance id) — pure garnish, removable.

What you have to do

Effectively none. Monthly, if you feel like it: mint a new planet by choosing an abundance profile and a chemistry seed, or hand-place a 'need' (declare that this world's winters now demand insulation) and watch the experiment scheduler re-aim. There is also an irresistible optional toy: a web page where visitors pick two substances from a world's codex and the result of their experiment is injected as a wandering-stranger event.

First weekend

Saturday: grid world, 40 substances with property vectors, the hash reaction function, and a village with needs. Sunday: the bandit experiment scheduler, the recipe DAG in SQLite, and a codex page. The moment: you set hardness threshold 5 for good tools, walk away for an hour, come back and the village has a four-step recipe involving heating a mineral you never thought about with a plant ash you added as a decoration, and it works because the hash said so and the property blend crossed the line.

Six months in

Eight planets with 20k-200k discovered substances each, archives visibly different in shape, several autocatalytic industries, at least one civilization that industrialised and one that got stuck for 50,000 years because its crust lacked a high-calorific fuel and never broke the kiln temperature ceiling. The comparative page — 'which property frontiers are universally reachable and which are planet-locked' — is the artifact you show people.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Two real limits. First, meaningfulness saturates: without the depth-gated emergent bonus and escalating needs, discovery count keeps rising while nothing important happens, and you will feel that around week 3 if you skip the fix. Second, there is no agency in the invention itself — the bandit is a good searcher but nobody is REASONING about chemistry, so you never get the satisfying 'they had a theory' moment. You can bolt on a theory layer (villages fit a linear model predicting product properties from precursors and use it to aim experiments; a village with a good model outcompetes one without, and you can literally watch science emerge as predictive accuracy) — that is the single best extension and it is maybe two weekends. Even so, this plateaus as a materials-discovery sim, not a full civilization.

Stack

Python 3.12, NumPy, hashlib.blake2b, pyribs for MAP-Elites, SQLite WAL, networkx (or hand-rolled) for cycle detection in the recipe DAG, FastAPI + vanilla-JS canvas PWA, Tailscale, GitHub Pages static mirror, ntfy.sh. Sonification option: map each planet's archive occupancy to a drone chord and each discovery to a plucked note via sounddevice — leave it playing in the room; you hear industrialisation as the note density rises.

Verified

Verified: TAP / adjacent-possible combinatorial innovation math (arXiv 2204.14115, 'The TAP equation: evaluating combinatorial innovation'; arXiv 2307.15083 'Reaction Diffusion TAP') — these give the actual growth equations for 'new things made from combinations of old things with an efficiency factor', including the hockey-stick regime. Autocatalytic-set framing traces to Kauffman and is well covered in the ALife literature; Flow-Lenia (MIT Press Artificial Life 31(2), 2025, Plantec/Hamon/Etcheverry/Chan/Oudeyer/Moulin-Frier) is the current state of the art for intrinsic open-ended evolution in continuous CA and is worth reading for how they force mass conservation to keep the sim honest — the same discipline (conserve mass across reactions) makes this idea's economy real. MAP-Elites/QD tooling: pyribs (bare-bones Python QD library) is the right choice on CPU; QDax (JMLR v25, 23-1027) is the JAX one if you later go GPU. Infinite Craft's caching trick (store every combination result so the same pair always yields the same output) is documented on its Wikipedia page and reimplemented in github.com/bufferhead-code/opencraft and github.com/sshh12/llm_alchemy — this idea replaces the LLM with a hash and keeps the determinism.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Count is unbounded; meaning stops in week 2-4 and the design already knows it. The fatal detail is that the author's own fix is the disease: 'emergent bonuses are only granted to products whose precursor DAG is deeper than the current median' means every expansion of the property frontier is produced by a designer-written depth reward. Strip that clause and the frontier saturates almost immediately, because the stated composition rule (mass-weighted blend + hash-seeded rotation) is a contraction toward the corpus mean with isotropic noise — repeated blending regresses properties inward, so deep chains are on average LESS extreme than shallow ones. So: with the bonus, the tech tree is a hand-authored difficulty gradient wearing a hash costume; without it, the MAP-Elites archive fills its reachable envelope in days and then nothing happens for six months. Concretely: hardness/melt/calorific archive occupancy plateaus within the first week or two of real running, and substance count keeps climbing into the hundreds of thousands as pure decoration.

Is the discovery real?

Inverted from the usual failure. The products are not authored, but they are also not meaningful, so what carries the appearance of a tech tree is the authored part: need thresholds, the escalation schedule, the depth-gated bonus, the kiln-temperature gate. Those four hand-tuned curves are the tech tree. 'Metallurgy reached on 5 of 8 worlds' is a statement about a labelled region of a property grid, not about a technology — nothing in the sim distinguishes a bronze age from a substance whose hardness float crossed 6.

Compute honesty

Fully honest — the best of the six on this axis. BLAKE2b at millions of calls/night is trivial, pyribs archives are cheap, 8-12 CPU processes and SQLite are well within one laptop, and the GPU is genuinely unused. No hidden datacenter, no API bill. The compute plan is the only part of this idea that does exactly what it says.

Nonsense risk

Highest in the set. A 16-dim vector blended through a random rotation has no compositional structure, so there is no fact of the matter about what any substance IS. The DAG is real, the properties are real numbers, and none of it composes into a technology; substance #91,000 differs from #900 only in bonus tag. The autocatalytic-cycle detector is a graph query that will fire on hash coincidences and be narrated as 'a craft became an industry'.

Claims to re-check

(a) TAP is misused. The TAP equation counts combinations of existing things; its hockey stick is a statement about cardinality, not about usefulness or depth. Citing it as the unboundedness argument is exactly the count-vs-meaning conflation the idea then admits to. (b) Flow-Lenia's mass conservation is invoked to make 'the economy real' — Flow-Lenia conserves mass in a continuous CA to prevent pattern blow-up; it is not evidence that a hash chemistry with conserved mass produces meaningful technology, and Flow-Lenia itself demonstrates open-ended pattern evolution, not open-ended invention. (c) Infinite Craft is cited for the caching trick, correctly, but its actual observed behaviour — rapid convergence to absurd noun soup — is evidence against this design's semantics and goes unmentioned. (d) The proposed best extension ('villages fit a linear model predicting product properties from precursors, and you watch science emerge') is close to provably weak in this design: the hash-seeded rotation is engineered to destroy exactly the precursor→product mutual information a linear model would need. You have built an unlearnable universe and then proposed learning as the headline upgrade.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Delete the hash and give the chemistry compositional structure: a few dozen typed element tokens, conserved stoichiometry, and a small randomly-seeded rule algebra so a product's property vector is a deterministic FUNCTION of its composition and conditions. Then products are predictable in principle (theory formation becomes possible and can be selected for), depth means structural distance rather than a bonus tag, and you can drop the authored depth reward entirely — which is the only way this stops being an authored gradient.

▸

Winterforge

The planet is an adversary with a learning algorithm: an ACCEL-style level editor mutates geology and climate specifically to maximise the tribe's regret, while the tribe is a neural policy trained across thousands of GPU-parallel worlds. Technology here is the set of open-ended crafting recipes a lineage reliably reproduces and teaches, and the world's whole job is to break them.

Not real depth
The world

A JAX gridworld in the Craftax lineage: 64x64 tiles, biomes, seasons, day/night, resources, hazards, and a tribe of 4-16 embodied agents with hunger, warmth, health, and an inventory. The world is defined by a level vector (elevation seeds, biome bands, resource density per type, glacier extent, monsoon phase, predator density, catastrophe schedule) that is fully mutable — that vector is what the adversary edits. Thousands of these worlds run simultaneously on the GPU as a batched tensor; a 'civilization' is a lineage of policy weights plus a recipe book that survives across level mutations.

How invention actually happens

Two layers, deliberately hybrid because pure RL is bad at long invention chains and I will not pretend otherwise. (1) OPEN CRAFTING: there is no recipe table. craft(item_a, item_b, verb, station, temperature) returns an item whose 8-dim attribute vector is a deterministic procedural composition (hash-seeded matrix blend, with the station and verb selecting the blend operator) plus a validity test — most combinations return junk. So the recipe space is combinatorial and untabulated exactly like Ashfall's chemistry, but embodied. (2) DISCOVERY: a symbolic experiment layer sitting above the RL policy — each tribe holds a small MCTS/bandit over craft attempts using items it currently possesses, weighted by unmet need; discovered recipes go in the lineage's recipe book, and the RL policy is conditioned on the book (recipes as an embedding set), so learning to USE technology is RL's job and FINDING it is search's job. That split is the design's whole trick. (3) THE ADVERSARY: an ACCEL/PLR-style level buffer scored by positive value loss as a regret proxy; the top-regret levels get mutated by geological edit operators (raise range, shift monsoon 30 degrees, advance glacier, delete an ore type, schedule a bolide at step 4000) and the mutants go back in the buffer. The teacher therefore learns which catastrophes actually hurt this civilization, which is the most viscerally 'the world is the antagonist' mechanic available.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two separate unbounded-ish spaces multiplied together: the craft-composition space (combinatorial in items x verbs x stations, and items are themselves craftable, so recipe DEPTH is unbounded) and the level space (continuous level vector under unlimited mutation, which is the standard UED premise that a teacher can generate an infinite curriculum). The honest part: RL policies in 8GB are small, and small policies plateau at a competence ceiling regardless of how open the environment is. The invention space is vast; the AGENT'S reach into it is the binding constraint, and it is the reason the symbolic experiment layer exists. Also honest: procedural attribute composition is combinatorially vast but semantically shallow — an item is 8 floats, and there are only so many ways 8 floats matter. Depth comes from chains and gating, not from the attribute space itself.

The ladder

Week 1: Craftax-style env running batched, one tribe, hand-picked levels, PPO learning to eat and not freeze. Week 2: open crafting swapped in for the recipe table plus the bandit experiment layer; the tribe finds a two-item recipe you never wrote. Month 1: PLR level buffer with regret scoring — you see the curriculum auto-order itself from easy to brutal, which is the first genuinely uncanny moment. Month 2: ACCEL mutations, so the world starts targeting the tribe's dependencies: they lean on a fuel type, the editor deletes that ore, and the recipe book has to route around it. Month 3-4: lineage persistence and teaching — recipe books transmit imperfectly between tribes, and you get technology loss under population stress. Wildest plausible endpoint: a documented arms race where the editor's mutation distribution visibly co-evolves with the recipe book — a plot of 'which world edit was most damaging' over time that shows the planet learning to attack whatever the civilization most recently invented.

What kills you

Direct survival: freeze, starve, get eaten, or persist. Regret (value-loss) selects LEVELS, not agents. Agents are selected by episodic return and lineage survival; recipe entries are selected by usage — an entry unused for K episodes and known by <2 tribes is dropped. Across the batch, lineages compete for a fixed number of persistent slots, so a lineage that solves harder levels displaces one that only handles easy worlds.

How the world changes

This idea's non-stationarity is the point and it operates at three timescales: within-episode seasons and weather; across-episode level mutation from the ACCEL editor (the slow geological pressure); and a scheduled 'era shift' every N million steps where the base generator's priors change wholesale (an ice age era where all levels are colder, a greenhouse era, a volcanic era). Additionally I would run 4 divergent BRANCHES from the same lineage checkpoint under different era schedules — that is the multiple-worlds product here: same starting civilization, four climate futures, compare their technology books at the end. That is a real comparative experiment and it is exactly what UED tooling makes cheap.

How it knows something is new

First-ever recipe signatures (hash of the item-attribute-cluster produced, so functionally-equivalent rediscoveries collapse); recipes of record depth; the regret ranking itself ('the world found a new way to hurt them' — a mutation operator that jumps to the top of the regret buffer for the first time is a great notification); and behaviour-descriptor novelty via a QD archive over (calorie strategy, mobility, aggression, tech depth) so a lineage occupying a new cell is flagged. Rate-limited to the top 2 per night.

On your phone

A live 'regret weather map': four branch worlds side by side, each showing the current top-regret level as a tiny rendered map thumbnail, the tribe's survival rate as a big number, tech-book depth, and what the editor changed most recently ('advanced the glacier 12 tiles'). Tap for a 6-second replay GIF of the most surprising episode of the night, pre-rendered at 4am so the phone never renders anything. Push: 'Branch C lost fire: the editor deleted surface coal and survival fell 41% in one generation. They have not recovered.'

Your hardware

This is the GPU-heaviest idea and the one where 8GB actually bites. Realistic plan: JAX with a small CNN policy (<2M params), 1024-4096 parallel envs, batched PPO — Craftax reports enormous throughput for exactly this shape of workload, and the bottleneck at 8GB is activation memory for the rollout buffer, so keep rollout length modest (128) and observations small (symbolic local view, not pixels). Expect single-digit-hours per meaningful training run, which fits the overnight rhythm perfectly. Real risk to check on day one: jaxlib CUDA wheels on Blackwell sm_120 — PyTorch got stable sm_120 wheels from 2.7 with CUDA 12.8, and JAX CUDA12 wheels generally work on recent drivers, but VERIFY with a two-line matmul before building anything, and have the fallback ready (drop to a smaller CPU-side env count, or port the env to PyTorch which definitely supports sm_120).

What you have to do

Weekly: glance at the regret buffer and optionally hand-author one nasty level to inject (this is fun and takes two minutes). Monthly: fork a new branch with a different era schedule. Otherwise nothing; runs continuously.

First weekend

Saturday: the JAX env — grid, resources, hunger, temperature, batched step function, and a random policy running 1024 worlds at once (seeing that number scroll is its own reward). Sunday: PPO from PureJaxRL patterns, plus the open-craft composition function. The first delightful moment is smaller than in the other ideas and arrives later — realistically end of week 2 when the level buffer starts ordering the curriculum by itself. Be honest with yourself that this is the slowest ramp of the six.

Six months in

Four branch civilizations with divergent recipe books shaped by their climate era, a regret history showing the editor's evolving attack strategy, and a set of held-out 'exam' levels the lineages are periodically tested on so capability growth is measurable rather than vibes. You will also have a real RL debugging skillset, which is either a bonus or a warning depending on your mood.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The hard ceiling is agent capability: a 2M-param policy on 8GB will learn foraging, shelter, seasonal migration, and short tool chains, and will not learn 12-step technological reasoning no matter how open the crafting space is — that gap is why the symbolic layer carries the invention load, and it means the 'civilization' is thinner than in the symbolic ideas. Expect the interesting curve to flatten around month 3 without a redesign. Also: RL is the least debuggable thing here; a bug and a plateau look identical for days. Pick this one only if you actively want to learn JAX/RL — as a pure product bet, ideas 1, 2, or 5 dominate it.

Stack

Python, JAX + Flax + Optax, PureJaxRL-style PPO, env written from scratch in the Craftax mould (do not fork it — the brief wants a from-scratch world, and writing a JAX gridworld is a genuinely good learning arc), level buffer with prioritised replay by positive value loss, SQLite for lineage/recipe/event history, offline GIF rendering with Pillow, FastAPI + PWA, Tailscale, ntfy.sh. Benchmark hygiene: all comparisons in environment steps and gradient steps, never wallclock; log GPU clocks and temperature alongside runs so a thermally throttled night is visible in the chronicle rather than silently polluting a comparison.

Verified

Verified: Craftax (arXiv 2402.16801, craftaxenv.github.io) is a JAX open-ended RL benchmark reporting 169-257x speedups over CPU Crafter with an end-to-end GPU pipeline via PureJaxRL — that establishes the throughput claim. Multi-Agent Craftax exists too (arXiv 2511.04904) if tribes-as-multiple-agents appeals. Kinetix (arXiv 2410.23208) is the open-ended physics-task-space cousin and its level-mutation machinery is the closest published template for a geological editor. UED background: PLR/ACCEL regret-based curricula, plus current work — 'Efficient Unsupervised Environment Design through Hierarchical Policy Representation Learning' (arXiv 2602.09813), 'Improving Regret Approximation for Unsupervised Dynamic Environment Generation' (arXiv 2601.14957), and 'Imagined Autocurricula' (arXiv 2509.13341) which induces UED curricula over world-model-generated worlds. JaxLife (arXiv 2409.00853, github.com/luchris429/JaxLife) is the single most relevant precedent for this exact fantasy: JAX-native embodied agents evolving with programmable Turing-complete 'robots', reported to show rudimentary communication, agriculture, and tool use. Hardware: PyTorch stable sm_120/CUDA 12.8 wheels from 2.7 onward (pytorch/pytorch issue #164342); Unsloth documents Blackwell/RTX 50 fine-tuning with TORCH_CUDA_ARCH_LIST=12.0.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Two ceilings arrive before the interesting part. (1) The invention carrier is the symbolic bandit over hash-composed crafts — 8 floats, procedural blend, validity test — which is Ashfall's shallow space with less room, so the same meaning-saturation applies within weeks. (2) The RL side plateaus where Craftax says it plateaus: the paper's own headline is that PPO baselines reach only a small fraction of achievements after ~1e9 steps on the full benchmark, and long-horizon credit assignment is the named open problem. Their honest_ceiling says 'flattens around month 3'; the more likely story is that a 2M-param policy on a from-scratch env plateaus on forage/shelter/short-chain within the first few overnight runs, and everything after is debugging. There is also a third, non-stationarity ceiling nobody flags: PPO against an ACCEL-mutated level distribution AND a growing recipe-book conditioning set AND scheduled era shifts is three simultaneous distribution shifts; catastrophic forgetting will make capability curves non-monotone and indistinguishable from bugs.

Is the discovery real?

Moderate on invention (craft composition and validity are procedural, not tabulated) but high on narrative. 'The planet learns to attack whatever you invented' is a misreading of the mechanism: positive value loss is a learnability/regret PROXY that scores levels where the critic is wrong — it does not identify dependencies. Worse, an edit operator like 'delete an ore type' can make levels unsolvable, and PVL happily ranks unsolvable levels high, which is the classic regret-proxy collapse mode. The evocative plot 'which world edit was most damaging over time' will largely be a plot of where the value function was undertrained. The story is authored even though the levels are not.

Compute honesty

The least honest of the six, in a specific and checkable way. Craftax's 169-257x figure comes from an end-to-end jitted pipeline where the ENTIRE env step is a pure tensor op. This design bolts a per-tribe MCTS/bandit over craft attempts, a growing per-lineage recipe book, lineage bookkeeping and SQLite writes on top. None of that is jittable as described; if any of it runs host-side per-env-per-step across 1024-4096 envs, throughput drops by two to three orders of magnitude and 'single-digit hours per meaningful run' becomes weeks. 8GB is survivable for a small CNN at rollout 128 with symbolic obs, but XLA fragmentation plus a growing recipe-embedding set will bite. Also unaddressed: the batch is the parallelism, so 'four divergent branches' quarters an already-marginal step budget. Day-one sm_120 verification is the right instinct but it is the smallest risk here.

Nonsense risk

High. An item is 8 floats; a station and verb pick a blend operator; 'most combinations return junk'. Nothing about a depth-9 item is qualitatively different from a depth-2 item, so recipe DEPTH is bookkeeping, not capability. Embodiment adds physical plausibility to USING items, which is real, but it does not make the item space mean anything.

Claims to re-check

(a) Craftax cited for throughput while its capability result — which directly contradicts the depth pitch — is omitted. (b) JaxLife is cited as showing 'rudimentary communication, agriculture, and tool use'; those are small-scale qualitative observations in a preprint, generously labelled, and are not evidence that this shape of system produces technology chains. (c) UED references check out (2410.23208 Kinetix, 2402.16801 Craftax, 2511.04904 Multi-Agent Craftax, 2602.09813 all real), but ACCEL's published wins are on navigation/physics task spaces with solvable-by-construction mutations, not on 'delete a resource type from an economy'. (d) 'the recipe book as an embedding set conditions the policy' is asserted, not argued; a 2M-param CNN will functionally ignore a set input that grows into the hundreds.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Cut PPO entirely. Keep the JAX env and the ACCEL editor, and make the tribe a search/planning agent (MCTS over craft+act against the simulator) rather than a policy network. You immediately get a capability you can measure, deepen and debug; the adversary then attacks a real dependency structure instead of a critic's error; and you stop paying the throughput tax that the symbolic layer imposes on the jitted pipeline. If you want to learn JAX/RL, that is a legitimate reason to build this — but say that out loud, because as a depth bet it is dominated by ideas 1 and 5.

▸

The Ninefold Constitution

Nine worlds, each with its own procedurally written laws of physics, and a local 4B model adjudicates every novel invention against that world's constitution plus its own case law of prior rulings. Identical ideas resolve differently on different worlds — fire burns violet and eats salt on world 4 — and every ruling is cached forever, so the unbounded space costs almost nothing.

Not real depth
The world

Nine planets. Each has (a) a CONSTITUTION: ~300 tokens of procedurally composed physical law drawn from a generator over clause templates with randomized constants ('liquid water boils at 340K here', 'metals are brittle below 300K', 'no free oxygen; combustion requires the sulfur cycle', 'the long night lasts 90 days'), and (b) a machine-readable shadow of that constitution as typed constants the engine actually simulates. Terrain, climate, and resources are generated consistently with those constants. Inhabitants are tribes with population, needs (kcal, warmth, safety, water), a settlement, and an inventory of INVENTIONS — each invention being a named thing with a typed effect record the engine executes every tick.

How invention actually happens

LLM-as-physics, but fenced so the engine never loses coherence. A tribe proposes an experiment: ingredients (things it has), a method verb, a station, conditions. The adjudicator prompt = world constitution + the k=6 nearest PRECEDENTS retrieved by embedding similarity from that world's ruling table (case law: the model sees how it ruled on similar attempts before, which is what actually keeps a small model consistent) + the proposal. Output is constrained by a GBNF/JSON schema to a typed EFFECT RECORD: {name, description, requires:[typed preconditions], produces:[resource deltas], modifies:[need_multipliers], durability, failure_modes}. The engine typechecks it, sanity-bounds the numbers, and only then admits it. Every ruling is cached in SQLite keyed by (constitution_hash, sorted ingredients, method, station, condition bucket) so it is computed exactly once ever and is deterministic thereafter — the Infinite Craft trick. The escape hatch from 'effects can only be what my grammar expresses': a tribe that reaches sufficient sophistication can trigger a MECHANISM proposal where the model writes an actual small Python function (sandboxed, fixed signature, must pass generated property tests: conserves mass, terminates, monotone where declared) that becomes a new engine-level effect primitive — Homunculus's tested-skill loop, but the skill is a law of nature and the tribe that funded it owns it.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The proposal space is natural language over an ever-growing set of held things, which is combinatorially open in the strict sense — this is the Infinite Craft argument, and caching makes it affordable. Two things keep it from being noise: the constitution makes rulings world-specific (so the same combination is nine different technologies across the multiverse), and the effect grammar makes every ruling mechanically executable, so an invention either helps the tribe survive or it does not, and the engine decides that, not the model. The grammar is the real ceiling — mechanical novelty is capped by what the effect language can express — which is exactly why the mechanism-proposal escape hatch matters: it lets the model extend the effect language itself, with tests, which is a genuine unbounded ratchet on top of a bounded one. Honest failure mode: LLM drift toward generic fantasy nouns. Precedent retrieval plus the mass/energy sanity checker fights it; it does not fully win.

The ladder

Weekend 1: one world, hardcoded constitution, 12 starting resources, local model adjudicating combinations into effect JSON, tribe survives or starves. Week 2: nine constitutions generated, nine caches, and the first delicious divergence — 'clay+fire' rules differently on the sulfur world. Month 1: precedent retrieval kills the consistency problem and you can read a world's ruling log as a coherent legal history. Month 2: needs escalate with climate, so inventions must chain (the model starts requiring a tool from a prior ruling as a precondition, unprompted, because the precedents taught it to). Month 3: mechanism proposals — the model writes and passes tests on a new effect primitive, e.g. a preservation mechanic nobody's grammar had. Month 4+: the constitutions AMEND (see changing worlds) and whole invention branches are invalidated. Wildest plausible endpoint: nine complete divergent technological histories readable as nine books, with a cross-world index showing which inventions are constitution-independent (universal) and which are contingent — plus a handful of engine primitives that were written by the system, tested, and are now part of your simulator's physics.

What kills you

Needs vs supply, per tick, per tribe. An invention only spreads if executing its effect record improves the tribe's survival margin; useless inventions are recorded in the codex as curiosities and never used again (and the codex of failed inventions is one of the funniest pages). Tribes on the same world compete for territory and trade inventions; a conquered tribe's codex is partially absorbed. Population collapse deletes inventions not written down — and 'writing down' is itself an invention the model can rule into existence, which is a delightful bootstrap.

How the world changes

Constitutions AMEND on a slow schedule: every few sim-millennia one clause mutates (the sun dims by 8%, the ocean acidifies, the long night lengthens) and every cached ruling whose preconditions touch the changed clause is invalidated and re-adjudicated lazily. Technologies stop working, and the tribes must re-derive. Layered on top: normal climate cycles, migration, and island isolation (a tribe cut off by sea level develops a divergent codex from the same world's constitution — allopatric technological divergence within a world, which is a nice second-order effect). Public perturbation: the web page lets anonymous visitors leave one line in a guestbook; nightly, the highest-voted line is injected into one world as an OMEN, prepended to that world's adjudication prompts for a day ('the sky spoke of iron'), which measurably tilts what gets invented and slowly accretes into that world's scripture. Cheap non-stationarity and the best reason for the artifact to be public.

How it knows something is new

Cache-miss rate is the baseline heartbeat (a world whose miss rate spikes is doing something new). Ranked novelty combines: embedding distance of a new invention from every prior invention on that world and across all nine; whether its effect record is Pareto-non-dominated on the need axes; first-ever precondition depth; and any accepted mechanism proposal (always pushed — a new law of nature is always news). Contradiction detection runs weekly: a cheap hosted model audits sampled ruling pairs for inconsistency, and contradictions become an in-world event ('the laws were revised') rather than a bug report, which is a lovely way to turn model error into content.

On your phone

Nine cards, each showing the world's one-line constitutional summary, tribe count, and the single newest invention with its emoji and effect summary ('SALT-SMOKE RACK — preserves meat 40 days, requires hardness-3 blade + sustained low heat'). A 'divergence bar' shows how far this world's codex has drifted from the multiverse median. Tap into a world for the codex (chronological, searchable), the ruling log (its case law), the amendment history, and the scripture page of accreted visitor omens. Push: one line, at most three a day, always naming the world.

Your hardware

Comfortable fit. llama.cpp serving Qwen3.5-4B-Instruct at Q4_K_M (~3GB) with a GBNF grammar for the effect schema leaves headroom on 8GB for a small embedding model (bge-small/gte-small, ~130MB) used for precedent retrieval — both resident simultaneously. Nine world simulators are CPU processes and near-free. Throughput reality: expect tens of tokens/sec, so budget adjudications rather than spamming them — a few thousand per night is plenty, and the cache means the steady-state miss rate falls fast (that decay curve is itself a plot worth watching). Hosted models are optional and cheap: with the Aug-2026 price floor around $0.10-0.20 per million input tokens for small models, the weekly contradiction audit and the occasional high-stakes ruling run to single-digit dollars a month; you can also run zero-cost by keeping everything local.

What you have to do

None required. Optional weekly: read the contradiction report and hit 'ratify' or 'strike' on a disputed ruling — you are the supreme court, which takes three minutes and is genuinely fun. Optional: hand-write a tenth constitution.

First weekend

Saturday: the engine — resources, needs, ticks, tribe death, plus the typed effect record and its typechecker (build this BEFORE touching the model; the grammar is the project). Sunday: llama.cpp with a JSON grammar adjudicating combinations, SQLite cache, and two worlds with different hand-written constitutions running the same tribe start. The moment lands Sunday evening when the same combination produces two different technologies on two worlds and both are mechanically real — the tribe on one world actually survives winter because of it.

Six months in

Nine codices of several thousand inventions each, readable as histories; an amendment log showing technologies going extinct when physics changed; a scripture page shaped by strangers; a handful of engine primitives written by the system itself. The cross-world index is the thing you will show people: 'the wheel-equivalent appears on 7 of 9 worlds; on the two heavy-gravity worlds nothing like it ever appears'.

Where it stops (self-reported)

A 4B model is the weak link and it will show: rulings get repetitive, descriptions get flowery, and around invention 500 on a world you will notice the model reaching for the same tropes. Precedent retrieval delays this a lot; it does not cure it. The effect grammar caps mechanical depth until mechanism proposals work, and mechanism proposals are the hardest part of the build (sandboxing plus test generation is a weekend of unglamorous work). Also be honest that the invention space's openness is linguistic, not physical — the model can rule anything into being, so the world's coherence is a maintained property, not a guaranteed one. Realistic plateau: month 4-5, unless you upgrade the adjudicator or lean harder on mechanism proposals.

Stack

Python 3.12, llama.cpp server (GBNF-constrained JSON), fastembed or llama.cpp embeddings for precedents, SQLite per world (rulings, codex, events) + multiverse.db, a sandboxed exec harness for mechanism proposals (subprocess, no imports, CPU/time limits, generated property tests), FastAPI + vanilla-JS PWA, Tailscale serve, GitHub Pages nightly static mirror, ntfy.sh push. The public guestbook is a static page posting to a tiny rate-limited endpoint.

Verified

Verified: Infinite Craft (Neal Agarwal) uses LLMs to generate new elements and caches every combination in a database so the same pair always yields the same result for everyone — that caching pattern is the affordability argument, documented on its Wikipedia page. Working open clones to read for the plumbing: github.com/bufferhead-code/opencraft, github.com/sshh12/llm_alchemy (elements+recipes+art), github.com/Fus3n/infinite-sides (local LLM via Ollama), github.com/CharlesFauman/infini-craft (local Llama-2-7B-GPTQ). Local model choice for 8GB in Aug 2026: Qwen3.5-4B or Gemma 3 4B at Q4_K_M is the consensus fit (canitrun.dev/guides/best-llms-for-8gb-vram, atomic.chat/blog/guides/best-local-llm-8gb); llama.cpp is the hobbyist-standard server with GGUF+grammar support, SGLang/vLLM are the throughput options if you outgrow it. Hosted-model economics: as of Aug 2026 the mainstream floor is roughly $0.10-0.20 per million input tokens for small models, with several cheaper (pricing trackers modelgrep.com/pricing, costgoat.com/compare/llm-api) — verify before wiring any paid call. Precedent-retrieval-for-consistency is the same idea LILO (arXiv 2310.19791) uses when it documents library abstractions so later prompts stay coherent.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

This is the purest case of authored content revealed in random order, and the author's own plateau estimate is off by roughly an order of magnitude. At tens of tok/s with ~2k-token precedent-laden prompts and 200-400 token effect records, you get maybe 2-5k rulings a night across nine worlds — 200-500 per world per NIGHT. Their admitted trope-collapse point ('around invention 500 on a world') therefore arrives in the first week, not month 4-5. After that, precedent retrieval makes it worse, not better: rulings condition future rulings, so the system locks into its own early vocabulary and the ruling log becomes a self-reinforcing mode. Structural ceiling underneath the model ceiling: every technology is an effect record of {resource deltas, need multipliers, preconditions}. That ontology makes all technology isomorphic to 'multiply a need by k, given a list' — a 12-step chain and a 1-step invention differ only in precondition bookkeeping. There is no structural depth for depth to accumulate in.

Is the discovery real?

Maximal, and disguised twice over. The author is not the builder — it is a 4B model's pretraining prior, which IS the human tech tree. Asked what happens when you fire clay, it will produce pottery; asked for sulfur-cycle combustion, it produces fire with a violet reskin and a salt flavour note. Nine constitutions do not change the nearest neighbour in the prior; they change the adjective. So 'nine divergent technological histories' will be one history in nine costumes, and the cross-world index ('the wheel-equivalent appears on 7 of 9 worlds') is measuring the model's prior, not the worlds. The second disguise: the model emits the numbers, and 'the engine sanity-bounds them' means the builder's clamp is the only thing making the economy coherent — the balance is authored, the fiction is free.

Compute honesty

Honest about hardware, quietly dishonest about which part carries the depth. Qwen-class 4B at Q4_K_M plus a bge/gte-small embedder co-resident on 8GB is a correct fit, and the caching argument genuinely makes the steady state cheap. But the ONLY unbounded mechanism in the design — mechanism proposals, where the model writes a tested Python effect primitive — is a frontier-model task. A 4B model will not reliably author novel, terminating, mass-conserving simulation primitives that pass generated property tests; you will get a very high rejection rate and the accepted ones will be trivial. So the ratchet needs a hosted frontier model, and if it runs often enough to matter it is no longer pennies; if it runs rarely enough to stay cheap, it is not a ratchet. The sandbox-plus-test-generation work is also correctly called the hardest part and is the part most likely never to ship.

Nonsense risk

Moderate on the surface, severe underneath. Individual inventions will read beautifully — the effect grammar and the clamp guarantee mechanical executability, which is genuinely better than most LLM-crafting toys. But the tech tree's SHAPE is a language model's associative neighbourhood, so you get plausible-sounding technology in an order that is neither discovered nor physical. That is the most seductive failure in the set: it is the one that will look best on the phone and mean least.

Claims to re-check

Citations largely check out (Infinite Craft caching, the clone repos, LILO, the Qwen3.5-4B/Gemma-3-4B 8GB fit — though canitrun.dev and atomic.chat are SEO-shaped sources, not authorities). The misuses: (a) Infinite Craft is offered as the affordability precedent while its most famous property — convergence to incoherent noun soup — is the exact failure mode here and goes unmentioned; (b) LILO's documentation-for-consistency is cited to justify precedent retrieval, but LILO documents abstractions that are VERIFIED PROGRAMS, whereas here precedents are unverified prior rulings, so retrieval propagates errors as well as consistency; (c) 'the model can rule anything into being, so coherence is a maintained property' is admirably honest and should be read as a concession that this is not a physics, it is a narrator; (d) the weekly hosted-model contradiction audit will find contradictions faster than you can adjudicate them — turning them into in-world 'the laws were revised' events is charming but it means model error is now unbounded content.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Move the model from adjudicator to proposer. Make it emit PROGRAMS in a typed DSL over engine primitives with conservation laws (borrow Twelve Anvils' interpreter wholesale); the engine executes them, measures the survival delta, and accepts or rejects. The constitution becomes the interpreter's constants rather than prompt text. That keeps the model's strength (proposing plausible combinations) and removes its fatal weakness (inventing outcomes and numbers), and it is the only version where a world can invent something that is not in the model's prior.

▸

Landbridge

One planet, one million years, at fifty years a tick: plate tectonics and ice ages open and close land bridges while technology — stored as a genome of executable recipes — mutates, recombines, transmits imperfectly, and goes extinct. The product is a technology phylogeny you can scroll: allopatric divergence of tools on isolated islands, and hybridisation spikes when the glaciers drop the sea and the continents touch.

Ceilinged
The world

An icosphere planet (~10k cells) with real-ish deep-time machinery: 8-14 tectonic plates on Voronoi regions drifting with per-plate velocity vectors, collision raising mountains and subduction opening volcanic arcs, elevation feeding a cheap climate model (latitude + altitude + a simple orographic rainfall pass + ocean-distance), and global ice volume driven by a three-term Milankovitch-style oscillator that sets sea level. Cells flip between land, shelf, and ocean as sea level moves by up to 120m. Agents are CULTURES: a population, a territory of cells, a subsistence mode, and a tech genome. Time: 1 tick = 50 years, 20,000 ticks = a million years, and a full run takes a night.

How invention actually happens

Genetic programming over a survival DSL, with per-culture MDL library extraction, plus lossy cultural transmission. Data structures: (a) Recipe = typed program over resource/action primitives (same shape as Twelve Anvils' DSL but simpler), with a complexity score = node count; (b) TechGenome = a set of recipes plus a per-culture library of extracted abstractions; (c) a PhyloDB recording every recipe's parents, birth tick, birth culture, and death. Mechanisms: MUTATION (point-edit a recipe: change a constant, swap a primitive, graft a subtree) and RECOMBINATION (crossover between two recipes, including recipes acquired from a neighbouring culture — this is where contact makes technology explode). Every candidate is mechanically verified by executing it against the culture's actual local resources and climate: does it terminate, is it feasible here, does it yield surplus kcal / reduce mortality? Only survivors enter the genome. Every 200 ticks, each culture runs MDL compression over its genome to extract shared abstractions (its 'craft traditions'). TRANSMISSION is the cruel part and the best part: copying a recipe of complexity C to a neighbour succeeds with probability that falls with C and rises with the number of people who know it and contact frequency — so complex technology cannot survive in small isolated populations and is genuinely LOST, with the loss event recorded in the phylogeny as an extinction.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Program space is infinite and recombination is the engine, so the reachable set grows with the diversity of contacted genomes — the space is not merely large, it is a function of the population structure, which the geology keeps rearranging. Isolation creates divergence (two islands explore different regions of program space), contact creates hybridisation (crossover between distant lineages produces recipes neither could reach alone), which is textbook allopatric-then-secondary-contact dynamics applied to technology rather than to genes. That is a structurally different unboundedness argument from every other idea here and it is the one most tightly coupled to a changing world: the tech tree's shape is literally the planet's connectivity graph over time. Honest bottoming-out: GP bloat (programs get big without getting better) and fitness saturation (once a culture covers its needs, selection goes flat). Countermeasures that actually work: MDL/parsimony pressure in the fitness, and climate that keeps moving the needs so the fitness landscape never stops sliding.

The ladder

Week 1: icosphere, plates drifting, sea level oscillating, and a map that animates a million years in 30 seconds — this alone is worth the weekend and it is a gorgeous phone view. Week 2: cultures, kcal, migration to adjacent habitable cells, population dynamics. Week 3: the recipe DSL, mutation, verification, and the first invention. Month 2: transmission with complexity-dependent loss, plus the phylogeny DB — you get your first technology extinction and it will hurt. Month 3: a glacial maximum drops sea level, a land bridge opens between two continents that have been separate for 300,000 years, and the recombination rate spikes; you watch a hybridisation burst in the phylogeny. Month 4-6: MDL library extraction per culture gives named traditions; a supercontinent assembles and the whole planet's tech pool merges. Wildest plausible endpoint: a run where an isolated island culture holds the only surviving copy of a deep abstraction lost everywhere else during a bottleneck, the bridge opens 80,000 years later, and it reseeds the mainland — and your novelty detector flags it because that recipe's signature had been marked extinct.

What kills you

Carrying capacity. Each cell yields calories as a function of climate, biome, and the culture's applicable recipes; population grows toward capacity and crashes past it. Mortality comes from cold, drought, disease (population-density dependent), and conflict at borders. Cultures expand into empty or weaker-held cells; a culture that loses its territory is dead and its genome dies with it unless a neighbour already copied entries. Small populations lose complex tech automatically via the transmission rule, so 'being cut off on a small island' is a slow technological death sentence — which makes migration decisions genuinely load-bearing.

How the world changes

This is the deep-time engine, so change is constant and multi-scale: tectonic drift over hundreds of thousands of years reshaping continents; Milankovitch cycles at ~20k/40k/100k years driving ice volume and sea level, hence connectivity; volcanic arcs and flood basalts as punctuated catastrophes; bolide impacts on a Poisson schedule with a decade-scale impact winter. The connectivity graph over cells is recomputed each tick and it is the single most important derived quantity in the sim because it decides who can teach whom. For multiple worlds: run 4-6 planets with different orbital eccentricity, axial tilt, land-to-ocean ratio, and plate count — a high-tilt world has violent seasons and (hypothesis) faster storage technology; an ocean world with tiny islands should produce maximal divergence and maximal loss. That comparison is the science.

How it knows something is new

The phylogeny gives objectively good signals: first-ever recipe motif (structural hash, so cosmetically different rediscoveries collapse), record-depth abstraction, an extinction of a recipe that had survived >100k years, a resurrection (extinct signature reappears), a hybridisation burst (crossover-origin recipes exceeding a rolling z-score after a bridge opens), and a culture crossing a carrying-capacity regime it had never reached. Plus the MDL-delta measure from library extraction. Nightly the top 3 by surprise go to the phone; the rest fill the chronicle.

On your phone

The hero view is a single animated globe thumbnail showing 'last night's million years' with ice sheets pulsing and coastlines breathing, and beneath it three numbers: cultures alive, deepest tech, technologies lost. Second tab is the phylogeny — a vertical time-scrolling tree where each branch is a recipe lineage, colour-coded by culture, with extinctions as terminated lines; scrolling it for ten minutes with coffee is the binge experience. Third tab: the atlas of 4-6 planets with their divergence statistics. Push: 'Landbridge opened at tick 14,220 (sea level -108m). Two isolated tech pools met after 340,000 years; 61 hybrid recipes in 3,000 years — highest recombination burst ever recorded.'

Your hardware

CPU and RAM, which suits this perfectly: the globe is ~10k cells x 20k ticks, all NumPy, and a full million-year run is minutes to an hour per planet. Run 6 planets as 6 processes and still have cores spare; 64GB RAM comfortably holds the full event log and phylogeny in memory before it is flushed to SQLite. The GPU is entirely optional — a local 4B model writing the chronicle prose for each night's headline events (cached, ~100 calls a night) is the only use, and pure garnish. No PyTorch, no sm_120 risk, and no timing-based fitness anywhere so thermals cannot corrupt results.

What you have to do

Zero. Optional monthly: mint a new planet by choosing tilt/eccentricity/land ratio/plate count, or drop a hand-placed catastrophe at a chosen tick to see counterfactuals (the sim is deterministic from a seed, so you can re-run the same million years with and without a meteor — a counterfactual button is trivially available and enormously fun).

First weekend

Saturday: icosphere, plates, drift, collision, elevation, sea level oscillator, and a canvas globe that animates deep time. Sunday: cultures, calories, migration, and the simplest possible recipe genome with mutation and verification. The moment: run a million years, then look at the map of who ended up where and notice that the cultures on the two sides of a strait that closed 400k years ago have different recipe sets — and you did not write a single line of divergence logic. Geology did it.

Six months in

Six planets, each with a complete phylogeny of thousands of recipe lineages; a comparative study of how orbital parameters shape technological trajectories; a library of counterfactual runs (same seed, one meteor changed); and an ambient audio archive. The phylogeny is the artifact — a picture of invented history that nobody authored, scrollable on a phone.

Where it stops (self-reported)

GP over programs bloats and stalls; expect the fitness curve to flatten within each run unless climate keeps sliding the needs, and expect to spend real time on parsimony pressure and typing. The cultures have no minds — there is no reasoning, only variation and selection, so you never get 'they figured it out', you get 'it was selected for', which is a different and colder pleasure. Also, deep-time realism is a rabbit hole with no bottom; the climate model will always be embarrassingly crude next to the geology, and you must decide early that it is a toy physics you keep cheap. Plateau: the invention engine flattens around month 4, but the comparative-planet layer keeps generating new questions long after — this is the idea with the longest tail per unit of effort, and the least raw invention depth.

Stack

Python 3.12, NumPy, a hand-rolled icosphere + spherical Voronoi (scipy), SQLite for cultures/recipes/phylogeny/events, msgpack event stream, offline frame rendering to a small mp4/webp per night with Pillow or matplotlib, FastAPI + vanilla-JS canvas PWA (the globe view is a canvas orthographic projection — right in your wheelhouse), Tailscale, GitHub Pages mirror, ntfy.sh. Sonification is exceptionally natural here: map global ice volume to a drone pitch, population to density, and each invention to a note — one hour of audio per simulated million years, left playing in the background as ambient radio.

Verified

Verified: solid open-source priors for the planet layer — github.com/MightyBOBcnc/nixis (Python, icosphere planet-scale maps, plate tectonics + hotspots, precipitation, climate/biome derivation), github.com/FreezeDriedMangos/realistic-planet-generation-and-simulation (tectonics + weather + ocean currents, with a live web demo), PyPlatec (used by WorldMachina, github.com/SAED2906/WorldMachina) for tectonic heightfields, and github.com/weigert/SimpleTectonics for the convection-based approach. Cultural-loss-in-small-populations (the Tasmania effect) is the Henrich demographic model of cumulative culture and is the mechanism that makes technology genuinely lossy here. Library extraction from a corpus by MDL: Stitch (arXiv 2211.16605) and DreamCoder (PLDI 2021) as above. For quality-diversity framing of divergence across islands, pyribs on CPU or QDax (JMLR v25) on GPU. Note on 'the wheel': the comparative claim only works because the sim is deterministic per seed — same seed, same million years, so counterfactuals are exact.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The most intellectually honest idea here, and the one whose ceiling is provable rather than empirical — because the designer built it deliberately and called it realism. The transmission rule (copy success falls with recipe complexity C, rises with number of knowers) is Henrich's model, and Henrich's model has a closed-form maximum sustainable complexity as a function of effective population. So recipe depth is not open-ended; it converges to f(N), and N is bounded by cell carrying capacity. Second, self-inflicted cap: they add MDL/parsimony pressure to fitness to fight GP bloat, which is the same pressure that punishes depth — you cannot use parsimony as the anti-bloat mechanism and expect deep programs. Third, the fitness function is scalar carrying capacity with saturating returns, so once local calories are covered selection goes flat and everything after is neutral drift. Realistic stop: typed recipes of depth 3-6, per-culture libraries of a handful of 'traditions', flat by month 1-2. The phylogeny, the counterfactual button and the comparative-planet layer keep generating interest long after invention stops — which is the correct read of 'longest tail, least raw depth'.

Is the discovery real?

Low on divergence, which is the real achievement: nobody writes the divergence logic, geology does, and 'two sides of a strait closed 400k years ago have different recipe sets' is a genuine emergent result. Authored: the primitive set, the resource/climate model, and — decisively — the shape of the fitness landscape. The hybridisation-burst headline is the weakest claim: crossover between two typed programs from lineages that diverged 300k years ago mostly yields verification failures, so '61 hybrid recipes in 3,000 years' will largely be a burst of neutral variants caused by having more parent material in the pool, not a qualitative capability jump. Report accepted-and-USED hybrids with a fitness delta, or the flagship moment is an artifact of the counter.

Compute honesty

Honest about the planet layer, silently wrong about the invention layer. 10k cells x 20k ticks of NumPy in minutes-to-an-hour is right. But that estimate omits the GP inner loop: tens of cultures x 20k ticks x candidate programs mutated, crossed over and EXECUTED against local resources is easily 10^7-10^9 interpreted program executions per planet-run. In Python that is not 'a night for six planets', it is days per planet unless you cut candidate counts per tick to single digits — at which point the mutation-selection process is too weak to climb anything. Either amortise invention (run GP every Nth tick, on a subsample of cultures) and say so, or write the interpreter in something fast. Zero GPU need and no timing-based fitness are both correct and admirable.

Nonsense risk

Low-moderate. Recipes execute against real local resources and climate, so every entry means something concrete. The catch is that everything they can mean is 'acquire, process, eat, shelter' — you get a gorgeous, scrollable phylogeny of calorie routines. The structural-hash novelty signals (extinction, resurrection, record depth) are objectively computed and genuinely trustworthy, which is rare in this set.

Claims to re-check

(a) Tasmania/Henrich presented as the mechanism that makes technology 'genuinely lossy' — the empirical claim that population size drives cultural complexity is actively contested (Vaesen et al. 2016 PNAS; Read), and here it functions as an analytic depth cap rather than realism. (b) 'Program space is infinite and recombination is the engine, so the reachable set grows with contacted diversity' — true of the reachable set, false of the SELECTED set, which is bounded by a saturating scalar fitness. (c) Planet-layer repo citations (nixis, PyPlatec, SimpleTectonics) are plausible and appropriate; the DreamCoder/Stitch citations are being used for a much weaker per-culture MDL pass than those systems do, so do not expect their compression quality. (d) 50-year ticks mean there is no within-lifetime learning anywhere — the notes never say this, but it forecloses 'they figured it out' at the level of the clock, not just the algorithm.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Replace the scalar carrying-capacity fitness with a quality-diversity objective — MAP-Elites over subsistence behaviour descriptors (diet breadth, mobility, storage horizon, processing depth, biome tolerance) maintained per culture — and demote parsimony from the fitness function to a tie-break. Scalar saturating fitness is the thing that flattens GP; illuminating a behaviour space keeps selection rewarding NEW KINDS of solutions after calories are covered, and it composes perfectly with the phylogeny you are already recording.

▸

Bureau of Comparative Worlds

An agent-scientist runs your multiverse as a controlled experiment: it forms hypotheses about what makes civilizations invent, designs the night's batch of worlds with paired seeds and ablations, runs them while you sleep, and publishes a paper with figures to your phone by morning. Its predictions are pre-registered and scored, so you wake up to a finding and a calibration curve, not a feed.

Not real depth
The world

A parameterised world generator is the substrate — use Twelve Anvils' library-learning worlds or Ashfall's chemistry worlds as the engine (this idea is the meta-layer and deliberately reuses one of them, which is also the 'one shared substrate across projects' play). What matters here is the WORLD PARAMETER SPACE as the independent variable: ~20 knobs (gravity, ore abundance and clustering, climate variability amplitude, catastrophe rate, island fragmentation, growing-season length, predator pressure, initial population, contact frequency between tribes). A 'world' is a point in that space plus a seed; a civilization is whatever the underlying engine grows there.

How invention actually happens

Two engines stacked. The object-level invention engine is inherited (library learning by MDL compression, or hash-chemistry discovery — pick one and do not build both). The meta-level engine is the interesting part and it is the actual product: a HYPOTHESIS REGISTRY in SQLite with columns {hypothesis text, formal form (parameter -> outcome metric), pre-registered predicted direction and effect size, planned N, seed list, status, result, replication status}. The nightly loop, driven by a Claude Code agent with a fixed toolset: (1) read yesterday's results and the current registry; (2) propose 1-3 new hypotheses, each with a pre-registered numeric prediction; (3) DESIGN the batch — paired seeds with common random numbers so the only difference between world A and A' is the one knob under test, plus one-factor-at-a-time ablations and a couple of random exploration worlds; (4) launch 12-16 worlds across CPU workers; (5) at dawn compute bootstrap CIs, effect sizes, and a Brier/calibration update for its own predictions; (6) write the paper — abstract, one claim, two figures, methods, limitations — and mark any finding as UNREPLICATED until it survives a fresh-seed rerun scheduled automatically.
