The agent cannot mark a finding confirmed without that replication; that rule is enforced in code, not in the prompt.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The object-level unboundedness is inherited from whichever engine you pick (program-space compression or combinatorial chemistry — both argued above). The meta-level adds a different kind of open-endedness: the space of QUESTIONS about a 20-dimensional world-parameter space with interaction effects is astronomically larger than the space of worlds, and each answered question suggests new ones (mechanism questions follow correlational ones; 'why' follows 'whether'). This is the only idea here where the thing that keeps growing is understanding rather than artifacts, which is also its honest weakness: understanding of a simulator you wrote is bounded by the simulator's own richness. If the underlying engine is shallow, the Bureau publishes true findings about a boring world, forever. Pick a deep engine or this is elegant machinery around nothing.

The ladder

Week 1: the batch runner — 12 worlds on 12 processes, a results table, and a single hand-written hypothesis tested with a paired-seed design. Week 2: the agent writes the nightly report from the results table (Claude Code on a cron, or a local model for the prose with the stats computed in Python — always compute stats in Python, never let a model do arithmetic). Week 3: pre-registration and the Brier scoreboard; the first time it predicts an effect direction and gets it wrong is the moment the project becomes addictive. Month 2: automatic replication scheduling and a 'findings' page with confirmed/unreplicated/refuted status. Month 3: interaction hunting — the agent starts testing 2-factor designs because main effects are exhausted. Month 4-6: a mechanism layer, where a finding must come with a proposed causal story that is then tested by an intervention (knock out the mediating variable and see the effect vanish). Wildest plausible endpoint: a genuinely non-obvious, replicated, mechanistically explained law of your multiverse — something like 'invention rate peaks at intermediate climate variability, and the mechanism is that low variability never invalidates library entries while high variability kills the population before compression can run' — that you did not know and could not have guessed, with the figures to prove it.

What kills you

Two selection pressures, which is what makes this fun. In-world: whatever the underlying engine uses (calories, cold, competition). Meta-world: HYPOTHESES compete — a hypothesis that fails replication is retired to a graveyard page, and the agent's calibration score (Brier on its own pre-registered predictions) is a public, permanent number on the dashboard. There is no way to fake it, because pre-registration happens before the runs and is timestamped in SQLite. A hypothesis-generation strategy that keeps producing refuted predictions gets down-weighted in the proposer.

How the world changes

Worlds change within runs (the inherited engine's climate cycles and catastrophes) and BETWEEN runs by design — the batch designer deliberately samples non-stationary schedules as an experimental factor: constant climate vs. cyclic vs. punctuated, mild vs. severe. 'Does catastrophe accelerate or destroy technology?' is the flagship question and it is exactly the sort of thing that needs many worlds run in parallel with paired seeds to answer honestly. Long-running 'cohort' worlds persist across nights so the Bureau also has longitudinal subjects, not just fresh batches — and a cohort world getting wiped out is a real loss, which gives the meta-layer stakes it would otherwise lack.

How it knows something is new

Novelty here is statistical by construction, which is the cleanest version in this set: an effect is newsworthy if its bootstrap CI excludes zero AND the pre-registered prediction was wrong (surprise = the agent's own miscalibration, which is the correct definition of learning something), or if an interaction term is large. Object-level first-evers from the inherited engine feed in as a second channel. Anti-noise discipline is enforced structurally: preregistration, replication before promotion, a cap of one push per night carrying the single best finding, and a visible count of tests run so p-hacking is legible on the dashboard rather than hidden.

On your phone

One card, and this is the tightest 30-second check-in of all six: today's headline finding in a sentence, a single small figure (paired dot plot or effect-size bar with CI), a status chip (CONFIRMED / UNREPLICATED / REFUTED), and the agent's running calibration number. Swipe for the findings archive, the graveyard of refuted hypotheses, and the multiverse map (a 2D projection of world-parameter space with dots coloured by outcome, which after months becomes a genuinely informative picture). The physical artifact fits perfectly here: a receipt printer on the desk prints the abstract every morning — a one-paragraph paper on thermal paper next to the coffee, which is the best version of the ritual you described.

Your hardware

CPU-dominant and ideal for 24/7: 12-16 world processes overnight, each a few hundred MB, well inside 64GB. Stats in NumPy/SciPy. The GPU is used only if the inherited engine wants a local 4B model, or optionally to run the report prose locally instead of via a hosted agent. Cost honesty: if the nightly scientist is a hosted agent, one report a night at a few thousand tokens of context is pennies-to-low-dollars per month at Aug-2026 small-model prices; if you use a frontier model for the reasoning it is still dollars, not tens of dollars, because it runs once a night. Benchmark hygiene is critical here since this idea is all about comparisons: every metric must be in simulated units (ticks, node expansions, kcal) and never wallclock, and the runner should log CPU temperature and clock throttling per batch so a hot night is visible as metadata rather than silently biasing a comparison.

What you have to do

The lowest of all six by design: read a paragraph with coffee. Weekly, optionally, add a hypothesis of your own to the registry and race the agent on calibration — a human-vs-agent Brier scoreboard is a delightful and nearly free feature. Monthly: retire a parameter or add a new one to the generator, which reopens the whole question space.

First weekend

Saturday: take the simplest possible object-level engine you have (even Twelve Anvils' weekend version), wrap it in a batch runner with a manifest per run, and get 12 worlds finishing overnight into one results table. Sunday: paired-seed design for one knob, bootstrap CI, a matplotlib figure, and a templated one-paragraph report pushed to your phone at 6am. The moment arrives Monday morning: you read a sentence about your own worlds that you did not know was true and had not thought to check.

Six months in

A hundred-plus findings, maybe fifteen confirmed and replicated, a calibration curve showing the agent got better at predicting its own multiverse, a parameter-space map with visible regimes, and a small body of knowledge about an invented universe. If you rotate the object-level engine (chemistry worlds this quarter, program-library worlds next), the Bureau layer transfers wholesale — this is the shared substrate you wanted, and it is the one component worth designing for reuse from day one.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Statistical power on one laptop is the hard wall: 12-16 worlds a night with noisy outcomes means you can detect large effects and will never resolve subtle ones, so the findings skew obvious early ('more ore, more tools'). Second wall: the garden of forking paths is real even with preregistration, because the agent chooses what to test after seeing yesterday's data — the honest fix is the enforced fresh-seed replication plus reporting the total number of tests, and you should assume some fraction of 'confirmed' findings are still noise. Third and most important: this layer cannot be more interesting than the engine underneath it, so do not build it first. Build engine 1 or 2 for a month, then add the Bureau — at which point it is maybe two weekends and it doubles the value of everything already running.

Stack

Python 3.12, NumPy/SciPy, SQLite (registry, results, cohort worlds, calibration history), a batch runner over multiprocessing with per-run manifests capturing the exact seed and code git-hash (reproducibility is the whole point), matplotlib rendering figures to PNG at dawn, a Claude Code cron job as the scientist (or a local 4B for prose with all statistics precomputed), FastAPI + PWA, Tailscale, GitHub Pages mirror of the findings, ntfy.sh push, python-escpos for the morning receipt.

Verified

Verified: UED framing (a teacher generating environments as a curriculum) is the closest published analogue to 'the world-parameter sampler as experimenter' — see 'Efficient Unsupervised Environment Design through Hierarchical Policy Representation Learning' (arXiv 2602.09813), 'Improving Regret Approximation for Unsupervised Dynamic Environment Generation' (arXiv 2601.14957), and 'Imagined Autocurricula' (arXiv 2509.13341); the Bureau differs by optimising for INFORMATION about worlds rather than agent robustness, which is a legitimately under-explored direction and a decent reason to build it. Project Sid (arXiv 2411.00114, github.com/altera-al/project-sid, Altera) is the best precedent for reporting emergent civilizational phenomena — specialisation, currency, religion spread — from many-agent runs, and worth reading precisely for how hard it is to state such results rigorously; their PIANO architecture and their metrics are the cautionary tale that motivates preregistration here. QD/illumination tooling for mapping parameter space: pyribs (CPU) and QDax (arXiv/JMLR v25 23-1027, JAX). Reproducibility practice — pin the git hash and the seed in every run manifest — is the one non-negotiable piece of engineering, since every claim the Bureau makes is a comparison between runs.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Not a depth mechanism at all, and it says so: 'this layer cannot be more interesting than the engine underneath it'. It contributes exactly zero invention depth — it is instrumentation, narration and experimental hygiene wrapped around whichever engine you pick, so its ceiling is that engine's ceiling plus a calibration curve. Its own independent wall is statistical power: 12-16 worlds/night with 8 per arm against outcomes like library depth or recipe count, which are heavy-tailed with CV near 100%, means you can only resolve effects around one SD or larger. Those are the effects you could have guessed ('more ore, more tools'), and they are usually restatements of code the builder wrote — a unit test with a confidence interval. Interaction effects, which is where the genuinely non-obvious findings live, are permanently out of reach at that n. Expect the findings feed to be interesting for 3-6 weeks and then become a well-calibrated stream of tautologies.

Is the discovery real?

Different failure mode: not authored content revealed as discovery, but implementation details revealed as laws of nature. Every dependent variable is something the builder chose to log about a simulator the builder wrote, so 'a law of my multiverse' is at best a characterisation of one's own code. That is a real and pleasant thing to have; it is not the cavemen-to-something-wild demand. Also under-defended: the agent both PROPOSES hypotheses and is scored on predicting them, so the dominant strategy is to propose safe hypotheses — calibration improves while informativeness collapses, and there is no code-enforced fix in the design (the enforced replication rule is enforced in code, which is genuinely good; the proposal-incentive problem is not).

Compute honesty

The most honest costing in the set, with one caveat. 12-16 CPU world processes overnight, SciPy bootstraps, matplotlib at dawn, one hosted agent call per night — pennies to low dollars a month is right, and the run-manifest-with-git-hash-and-seed discipline is the single best piece of engineering in all six proposals. Caveat: if the inherited engine is Twelve Anvils, giving each of 16 worlds ~half a core for 8 hours makes every world a starved DreamCoder run, so the Bureau will be doing rigorous statistics on the noise floor of an under-resourced search. Fewer, longer, better-fed worlds beat more arms here — power comes from effect size, and starved engines have none.

Nonsense risk

Low, and this is the layer's genuine value: statistics computed in Python and prose written by a model, with an explicit rule never to let the model do arithmetic, is the correct architecture and the only one in the set that structurally resists narrative inflation. The remaining risk is prose that dresses a 1.2-sigma difference as a law; cap it by printing the effect size and n in the push text itself.

Claims to re-check

(a) UED papers are cited as the closest analogue to the world-parameter sampler; the citations are real (2602.09813 verified, 2509.13341, 2601.14957), but UED optimises a teacher for agent robustness with a regret signal — repurposing that framing for information-about-worlds is a genuinely different objective and the analogy buys less than claimed. (b) Project Sid is cited well, as the cautionary tale, but note it ran many hosted-LLM agents at a cost structure nothing like a laptop, and its emergent-civilization claims remain largely unreplicated — a good reason for the Bureau to exist, and also a warning that 'reporting emergent phenomena rigorously' may find there is nothing to report. (c) The preregistration defence is oversold: preregistering a prediction chosen AFTER seeing yesterday's data is not preregistration, it is sequential inference. The enforced fresh-seed replication does most of the real work (roughly squaring the per-test error rate) and deserves the credit instead. (d) 'the object-level unboundedness is inherited' is doing enormous load-bearing work for an idea that ships none of it.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Close the loop: make the Bureau OPTIMISE world-parameter space rather than only publish about it — a POET/illumination-style outer loop searching for parameter regions that maximise sustained novelty rate or time-to-plateau in the object engine, with hypothesis-testing as a readout of that search. Then the meta-layer feeds depth back into the object level instead of describing its plateau eloquently, and the answer to 'which worlds keep inventing' becomes the thing you run rather than the thing you write up. Build it second, never first — after a month of engine 1 or 5, it is two weekends and it doubles the value of everything already running.

Chronicles

Deep history you read rather than watch

▸

Palimpsest

A civilization's technology IS a library of small programs it wrote to survive, and each night a corpus-compression pass finds the shared structure across everything every tribe did and mints it as a new named primitive — that minting event is a real invention, not a tech-tree unlock. You read it as an encyclopedia with etymologies, inventor biographies, and a citation graph you can walk backwards from 'reinforced haft-lashing' to 'strike two rocks together'.

Ceilinged
The world

One planet, hex map ~256x256, but the substrate that matters is a procedurally generated MATERIALS TABLE, regenerated per world: 60-200 substances, each an 8-12 dim property vector (hardness, toughness, density, melt point, fibrousness, caloric density, toxicity, reactivity class, workability) plus a generated reaction table of the form `A + B @ (temp, duration, presence-of-C) -> D`, where D's property vector comes from a fixed-seed nonlinear interaction map. You never author 'bronze'; you author 'two metals heated above both melt points interpolate nonlinearly', and world #7's specific useful alloy is a discovery. Agents are BANDS (20-200 people), not individuals: each band has a population pyramid, health, a stock vector, a territory, a lexicon, and a LIBRARY of programs. Resources are kcal, fuel joules, labour-hours, materials, water, salt. Seasons plus a stochastic hazard calendar (winter severity, flood, blight, raid, murrain).

How invention actually happens

DreamCoder wake/sleep with Stitch as the compressor, wrapped around a deterministic materials simulator that acts as the mechanical verifier — zero LLM in the causal loop. DATA STRUCTURES: (1) `library` table: id, band_id, canonical_body (alpha-normalized lambda term, hashed), type signature, arity, name, gloss, invented_tick, parent_ids (the library entries appearing in its body — this is the citation DAG). (2) `solution` corpus: every program that ever passed a goal predicate, with band, task, tick. (3) `task` rows auto-generated from world state, never hand-written: a generator emits (goal_predicate, initial_stock, environment) from actual pressure — `survive(40 people, -20C, 90 days)`, `cross(river, 30m, in flood)`, `store(3000 kcal, 90 days, humid)`, `kill(aurochs, at range)`. WAKE: for each band, bottom-up type-directed enumerative synthesis over the band's current library, with observational-equivalence deduplication (Bustle/Probe style) and a bigram prior over the band's own historical library usage. Budget is measured in NODES EXPANDED, never seconds (thermal hygiene: a hot laptop must not make a tribe stupid). Critically, the node budget is proportional to the band's SURPLUS LABOUR-HOURS — thinking is a luxury good paid for by food. Every candidate is executed against the materials sim; only programs whose output satisfies the goal predicate given the band's real stock survive. SLEEP: nightly, run `stitch` (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, POPL 2023, Rust with Python bindings, 3-4 orders of magnitude faster than DreamCoder's own compression) over the solution corpus. Stitch proposes abstractions maximizing corpus compression under MDL. Each accepted abstraction becomes a new library primitive. THAT IS THE INVENTION. It permanently reshapes the search space: what cost 100k nodes last week now costs 3. NAMING (LILO-style, arXiv 2310.19791): local Qwen3-4B, GBNF-constrained to `{name, gloss, etymology_parents[]}`, given the abstraction body, 3-5 call sites, and the band's phoneme inventory + existing lexicon. Sound-change rules applied per generation keep the lexicon internally consistent, so words visibly drift across centuries. DIFFUSION: trade, marriage, and conquest copy library entries with fidelity < 1. A copied abstraction can lose an argument, producing a degenerate variant that occasionally outperforms the original. ARCHAEOLOGY: a dead band's library is written to a `ruin` row with a damage mask (holes punched by preservation rules). A later band excavating that site recovers a holey program and runs sketch-completion synthesis to fill the holes. The encyclopedia records 'reconstructed from the Tablets of Ashen Ford; three arguments conjectural.'

Why it doesn't bottom out

The invention space is the set of well-typed lambda terms over a signature that GROWS every night, and every new abstraction can appear inside the next one. That is a countably infinite space whose measure under the learned prior keeps shifting, not a finite list. Two independent compounding axes: the term space (unbounded, self-referential) and the materials chemistry (procedurally generated per world, so the semantics are also not authored). MDL compression is the selection pressure that keeps growth in useful directions instead of random ones — an abstraction only enters the library if it pays for itself in bits across the whole corpus, which means it only becomes a 'technology' if multiple independent survival problems happened to share that structure. That is a shockingly good operational definition of a technology.

The ladder

WEEK 1: 20 materials, 15 primitives, one band, one task (survive winter). Bottom-up enumeration finds `(heat (grind acorn) fire 2h)`; stitch names it; you watch that named thing appear inside three later solutions. First delight. WEEK 3: 5 bands, diffusion on. Two bands independently invent something with the same canonical hash — the encyclopedia gets its first 'independently discovered at X and Y, 40 years apart' entry. MONTH 2: citation DAG hits depth 5. Techniques exist that no human wrote, made of techniques no human wrote. Bands with better tools have more surplus, therefore bigger search budgets, therefore runaway — checked by disease scaling with density and by fuel depletion (deforestation raises the cost of every heat-based abstraction, which retroactively devalues an entire branch of the library). MONTH 4: first cataclysm. A band dies with a 60-entry library. 200 years later someone digs it up half-legible. ENDPOINT (plausible, not promised): a world where the encyclopedia has an entry whose etymology chain is 12 deep, whose parent techniques come from three different extinct cultures, and where the reconstruction from ruins produced a variant that is measurably better than the original because a lost argument was replaced by a constant.

What kills you

Starvation, mechanically. Winter kcal deficit kills people, dead people are lost labour-hours, lost labour-hours shrink the synthesis node budget, a smaller budget means fewer solutions, fewer solutions means weaker nightly compression, which means the library stops growing — a death spiral you can watch in the numbers. Competition: bands raid for stock AND for libraries (a successful raid copies 1-3 random library entries). Niche construction bites: deforestation, soil exhaustion, and game depletion are tracked per-tile and are caused by the very techniques that made the band successful, so success is self-undermining on a 100-200 year lag. Disease pressure scales superlinearly with settlement density, so the sedentary route is not a free win.

How the world changes

A SHELF OF WORLDS: 3-5 worlds run concurrently under one scheduler, each with its own generated materials table and hazard climate, sharing nothing but your reading list. The encyclopedia is cross-world: an entry says 'analogous to the Bitterment of world Kesh, though there fibre came from a mollusc'. Within a world, long climate cycles (10k-tick Milankovitch-ish sine stack) plus a Poisson cataclysm (bolide, plague, volcanic winter, ~1 per 3000 ticks) that wipes 60-95% of population and creates a hard archaeological layer. Post-cataclysm bands inherit ruins, not libraries. The cross-world comparison is where the biggest reading pleasure lives: 'in 4 of 5 worlds, someone eventually invented the arch; in Kesh, nobody ever did, and here is what they built instead.'

How it knows something is new

Four channels, all cheap, ANDed against a hard push budget of 2 notifications/day. (1) FIRST-EVER: an abstraction whose alpha-normalized canonical hash has never appeared in any world's library table. Trivially exact, zero false positives. (2) COMPRESSION SHOCK: nightly MDL delta (bits saved by the new abstractions) exceeding 3 sigma of the trailing 30-night distribution — this fires exactly when a genuinely load-bearing idea lands. (3) DEPTH RECORD: a new abstraction whose depth in the citation DAG exceeds the all-time max. Technology built on technology built on technology. (4) ASAL-style historical novelty (arXiv 2412.17799): embed each night's world-state summary with bge-small-en-v1.5, keep the running archive, score = mean cosine distance to the k=20 nearest previous nights, push only if in the top 1% of the trailing distribution. This catches things you did not think to instrument, like a demographic pattern nobody has ever seen. Everything else goes into the encyclopedia silently and is there when you want to binge.

On your phone

Lock-screen ntfy line: 'Kesh, year 4,182 — 3 new words.' Open the PWA: a single scrolling column. Top card is tonight's headline invention — its name in the band's own orthography, a one-line gloss, the inventor band's sigil, and its parent techniques as tappable chips. Below it, a 30-second strip: population sparkline per band, a 'words known' counter, and any deaths of note. That is the whole 30-second view. Tap the headline and you fall into the encyclopedia: full entry, etymology tree rendered as an SVG DAG, list of every surviving program that calls it, the biography of the band that coined it, and 'see also' links that keep you there for an hour. FTS5 search across every entry ever written, in every world.

Your hardware

CPU-dominant and honest about it. The synthesis search is the hot loop and it is pure CPU — 64GB RAM is the real resource here, holding the observational-equivalence tables and the full solution corpus in memory. Run 8-12 band-workers as separate processes; a 24/7 run at a few hundred thousand nodes per band-tick is comfortable. Stitch is Rust, single-digit seconds on corpora of 10^4-10^5 programs. The GPU does almost nothing: Qwen3-4B-Instruct at Q4_K_M is ~2.5GB, fully offloaded on the 5070's 8GB with room to spare, used only for naming (a few hundred calls a night at ~200 tokens each) and for the nightly prose pass. bge-small-en-v1.5 embeddings are 90MB and could run on CPU. You will never touch the 8GB ceiling in this project, which is exactly why it is a good first build for this hardware.

What you have to do

Zero daily chores. Read with coffee. Two genuinely god-tier nudges are available and worth doing about monthly: (a) add a primitive verb to the DSL — one line, and it opens an entire semantic continent (adding `mark(surface, symbol)` is how writing becomes discoverable); (b) hand-seed a new world with a deliberately weird materials table (no metals; one absurdly abundant fibre) and watch the encyclopedia diverge. Both are 20-minute acts with month-long consequences.

First weekend

Saturday: materials table generator, 20 substances, the property-vector interaction map, and the stock simulator. 15 DSL primitives, a typed bottom-up enumerator with observational-equivalence dedup, one task (`survive winter`). Get a program that passes. Saturday night: pip install stitch, run it over your ~200 passing programs, print the abstractions. Sunday: wire Qwen3 for naming, add a second band and copy-with-error diffusion, write the encyclopedia entries to SQLite, and put a dumb HTML list on localhost. The delightful moment is Saturday night, before any UI exists: stitch hands you an abstraction you did not write, and you realise it is the thing every winter solution had in common.

Six months in

Five worlds, an encyclopedia of 400-900 entries, a citation DAG 10-14 deep, and a lexicon with visible sound change (the word for 'parch' in year 200 and year 4000 are cognates you can hear). You have a favourite dead civilization. You have caught yourself checking whether the arch got invented again. The public mirror has strangers reading the etymology of a word for a technique that only exists on your laptop.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The ~40 primitive verbs are authored, and no amount of compression invents a concept the primitives cannot express. If nothing in your DSL can carry information, writing will never be discovered no matter how long it runs. Realistically this generates five to eight distinct 'eras' of genuine depth and then settles into recombination inside a fixed physics — I would put the plateau at month 5-8. Two honest mitigations, neither a cure: seed a handful of high-leverage semantically-open primitives (`mark`, `teach`, `store_program_in_artifact`) so writing, schooling and libraries are discoverable rather than authored; and accept that extending the physics is a manual act you perform every few months, which is itself the fun part for a builder who likes the whole arc. Second real limitation: enumerative synthesis scales badly with library size — past ~150 primitives the branching factor hurts, and you will need to prune the library (a 'forgetting' mechanic, which conveniently is also good fiction). Third: stitch's abstractions are sometimes ugly, semantically arbitrary fragments; expect maybe 1 in 4 to be a satisfying 'technology' and the rest to be plumbing. Do not oversell the hit rate to yourself.

Stack

Python 3.12 sim (numpy for stock/property vectors, no framework). `stitch` via its Python bindings for nightly compression. SQLite in WAL mode as the single event store, with FTS5 over chronicle text — one file, greppable, backed up by copying. llama.cpp server with Qwen3-4B-Instruct Q4_K_M and GBNF grammars for every structured call (naming, glossing, prose claims). sentence-transformers bge-small-en-v1.5 for the novelty archive. Nightly build step emits a static JSON bundle; PWA is vanilla JS + a service worker so it works on the train. Served over Tailscale from the laptop for the private full-fidelity version; a pruned public mirror rsynced to GitHub Pages. ntfy.sh topic for push.

Verified

Verified: DreamCoder (Ellis et al., PLDI 2021, dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454080) — wake phase searches for programs solving tasks under the current library, sleep phase extracts common sub-expressions and admits them if they reduce total MDL. Stitch (Bowers, Olausson, Wong, Grand, Tenenbaum, Ellis, Solar-Lezama; 'Top-Down Synthesis for Library Learning', POPL 2023, dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3571234; code at github.com/mlb2251/stitch) is corpus-guided top-down abstraction synthesis, reported 3-4 orders of magnitude faster than DreamCoder's compression, with Python bindings documented as the easiest access path — this is what makes a nightly compression pass on a laptop trivially affordable, and it is the single most important dependency in this idea. LILO (arXiv 2310.19791, 'Learning Interpretable Libraries by Compressing and Documenting Code') pairs Stitch-style compression with an LLM auto-documentation step — exactly the naming layer proposed here, and evidence that LLM-naming of compressed abstractions is a working published technique rather than my invention. ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799, github.com/SakanaAI/asal, published in Artificial Life 31(3), 2025) defines open-endedness operationally as high historical novelty in a representation space at each timestep — I am reusing that formulation with cheap text embeddings instead of CLIP. llama.cpp supports guaranteed structured output via --grammar-file / GBNF and JSON-schema response_format, confirmed current in 2026 tooling guides, which is what makes LLM naming safe to automate unattended. Qwen3-4B at Q4_K_M is ~0.5-0.6GB per billion params by the standard GGUF rule of thumb, so ~2.5GB — trivially inside 8GB. Precedent for reading rather than watching: Dwarf Fortress legends are browsed like a wiki by clicking links (dwarffortresswiki.org/index.php/World_generation), and Lynn Cherny's Dwarf2Text work is the closest existing attempt at data-to-text over DF legends XML.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Real discovery, but it plateaus earlier than claimed — month 3-5, not 5-8, and for a reason the doc misses. DreamCoder only works because of a graded task curriculum; the wake phase needs tasks that are just barely out of reach. Here tasks are auto-generated from world pressure (`survive(40, -20C, 90d)`, `cross(river,30m)`), which produces a bimodal difficulty distribution: nearly all trivially satisfiable at depth 1-2, a few unreachable at any budget, almost nothing in the productive band. Enumerative synthesis is exponential in depth, so the 'surplus labour buys node budget' coupling — the design's proudest loop — buys depth logarithmically: a 10x richer band searches maybe one or two levels deeper, not ten. Meanwhile Stitch saturates: MDL gain per night is a decreasing function of corpus homogeneity, and a corpus drawn from ~6 task templates gets most of its compression out in the first 20-40 abstractions. Concretely: expect ~40-80 library entries by week 8, then nightly MDL delta collapsing to noise, at which point channel (2) of the novelty detector stops firing permanently. The citation DAG will still deepen — but depth in a DAG of MDL fragments is free and means nothing. Second, unflagged ceiling: the branching-factor problem arrives sooner than 150 primitives because bottom-up enumeration with observational-equivalence dedup is bounded by the OE table, and OE dedup only fires when two programs agree on the *test inputs* — with a rich materials sim, distinct programs almost never collide, so dedup buys much less than it does on DreamCoder's list/string domains where it was measured.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six but not zero, and the doc mislocates it. The honest_ceiling blames the ~40 primitive verbs; the real authored surface is the TASK GENERATOR. Discoverable technologies are exactly the well-typed terms that satisfy predicates the generator emits, so the generator, not the DSL, defines the space of things that can count as inventions. The `mark`/`teach` mitigation is a bluff: adding a `mark(surface,symbol)` primitive does not make writing discoverable unless the sim independently models bounded knowledge transmission, decay, and a fitness advantage to externalised memory — i.e. unless you author the entire subsystem that makes writing pay. Adding the verb without that just adds an unused primitive that inflates the branching factor. Everything below the category level is genuinely unauthored: generated materials table, MDL-mined abstractions, un-anticipated recipes. Verdict: recipes and abstractions are real discoveries; the KINDS of technology are authored, and the encyclopedia format will disguise that because Qwen3-4B will supply confident etymologies for whatever it is handed.

Compute honesty

Honest — the most honest of the six alongside Apocrypha. Pure CPU hot loop, GPU used for ~a few hundred short constrained generations a night, no API bill, nothing near the 8GB ceiling. Two caveats. (1) 'a few hundred thousand nodes per band-tick' in Python: each candidate must be *executed* against the materials sim, so throughput is roughly 10^4-10^5 candidates/sec optimistically and realistically closer to 10^4 with a Python interpreter loop — that is seconds-to-a-minute per band-tick, fine overnight, but it means the effective search depth is much shallower than the node count suggests. Budget in *solved tasks per night*, not nodes. (2) The OE table and full solution corpus in RAM is fine at 10^5 programs and starts hurting at 10^7; the corpus grows monotonically and Stitch cost is superlinear in corpus size, so you will need corpus windowing by month 3 (Stitch's headline speed is measured on fixed corpora, not on an ever-growing one).

Nonsense risk

Moderate-high, and the doc's own '1 in 4 satisfying' estimate is optimistic. Stitch maximises compression, not meaning; typical abstractions are partial applications with hole-filled arguments that correspond to no coherent concept. The naming layer makes this worse rather than better: a constrained 4B given a body, 3 call sites and a lexicon will always produce a plausible name and etymology, so the encyclopedia's readability is uncorrelated with whether an entry is a technology or plumbing. You will not be able to tell them apart by reading, which is the failure mode that matters for a project whose product IS the reading. Mitigation the doc lacks: gate library admission on a semantic test as well as an MDL test — e.g. require the abstraction to be invoked by ≥2 bands on ≥2 distinct task templates before it earns a name; leave the rest unnamed as 'unnamed fragment #331'.

Claims to re-check

(a) 'pip install stitch' — the PyPI package is `stitch_core`; `stitch` is something else. Small but it will eat a Saturday. (b) Stitch's '3-4 orders of magnitude faster' is about the COMPRESSION step versus DreamCoder's compressor; it says nothing about the enumerative search, which is your actual bottleneck. The doc calls Stitch 'the single most important dependency' — it is not; the wake-phase search is. (c) ASAL 'published in Artificial Life 31(3), 2025' — arXiv 2412.17799 and the SakanaAI repo are real, but I cannot verify that journal placement; treat as unverified. (d) DreamCoder's published results used substantial multi-core compute over days per domain with hand-designed curricula; citing PLDI 2021 without citing that compute/curriculum context oversells laptop feasibility. (e) The claim that MDL compression 'is a shockingly good operational definition of a technology' is the doc's most seductive and least supported line — MDL identifies shared syntax, which correlates with shared technique only when the corpus is semantically homogeneous, i.e. exactly when the corpus is least interesting.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Co-evolve the task generator with the library instead of deriving tasks from world state. Add a minimal-criterion/POET-style loop: generated goal predicates are kept only if they are currently unsolved at the standard budget but solvable at ~3x budget, and retired once cheap. This converts depth-in-time from logarithmic to roughly linear, keeps Stitch's corpus continuously heterogeneous (so nightly MDL delta never collapses), and it is maybe 150 lines on top of what is already specified. Without it, everything else in the design is correct and the project still plateaus in three months.

▸

Reliquary

A world of island workshops that invent materials by experiment and objects by design, and the artifact you check is a MUSEUM CATALOGUE: accession numbers, object labels, provenance chains, conservation notes, and a gallery of things that have never existed. Every morning the receipt printer spits out one object label.

Ceilinged
The world

An archipelago of 30-80 islands, each holding a different random subset of the world's ~120 procedurally generated substances — the isolation is the point, because divergent material cultures plus trade is what produces hybrid artifacts worth cataloguing. Each substance has an 8-dim property vector and participates in a generated reaction network of rules `k*A + m*B @ (temperature, pressure, catalyst) -> C (+ waste D)`, where C's properties are a fixed-seed nonlinear function of the inputs. Some reactions are CATALYTIC and a few are AUTOCATALYTIC — a product that catalyses its own production, which is what gives you a bronze age that arrives suddenly rather than as a ramp. Agents are GUILDS (workshops with a master, apprentices, a stock, a reputation, and a private recipe book), plus polities that commission work and fight wars. Objects are physical: a specific axe is a real row with a material stack, a form, a maker, a decoration, a wear state, and a location.

How invention actually happens

Two coupled engines, both mechanical, LLM confined to the label copy. ENGINE 1 — MATERIALS, as a bandit over the adjacent possible. Maintain per-guild a FRONTIER set: every pair/triple reachable from substances the guild knows, crossed with processes it knows, crossed with the conditions it can achieve (its kiln's max temperature is a real number that improves as a technology). Experiments cost materials, fuel, labour, and carry risk (toxic, explosive, ruins the crucible). Selection of what to try next is UCB, but the exploitation term is DEMAND-PULLED: the sim emits NEEDS as target regions of property space ('something harder than obsidian that does not shatter when struck', 'a pigment that survives three summers of sun', 'a binder that sets underwater'), derived from actual failures — a boat sank, a roof fell, a dye faded on a king's robe. The guild scores frontier candidates by predicted property-vector distance to an open need, using a per-guild learned linear model over past results. Results are written to a global `reaction_cache` (Infinite Craft's exact trick: a pair is adjudicated once, ever) so the chemistry is deterministic, reproducible, and free after first evaluation. ENGINE 2 — OBJECTS, as quality-diversity search. An object's FORM is a small tree-grammar program: haft + head + binding + haft-treatment, each node parameterised (length, taper, angle, bind-turns). Performance is computed from material properties and geometry by a deterministic physics-lite model. Each guild runs local search over form space, but globally you maintain a MAP-Elites archive per object class using pyribs, with behaviour descriptors like (mass, edge retention) or (draw weight, cost). The archive IS an exhibit: the museum literally has a gallery that is a grid of all extant blade forms, one per cell, with the guild and year that filled it. A newly filled cell is a first-of-its-kind object. ARTIFACT IDENTITY: every object gets an accession number, a provenance chain (made by guild G in year Y, gifted to K, buried with K, looted in the sack of S, held by the collection of T), and a conservation state. Named artifacts emerge when an object accumulates a provenance chain above a threshold of eventfulness — fame is computed, not authored.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The reachable set of a generated reaction network grows superexponentially: 120 base substances, pairs and triples, several condition axes, catalysis, and products that feed back in as inputs. Nobody enumerates it; guilds crawl it locally under a need gradient, so the crawl path is different in every world and every guild. On top of that the object design space is continuous (a tree grammar with real-valued parameters), so 'all possible axes' is an infinite set of which the MAP-Elites archive holds a diverse finite sample that keeps improving. Honest structure of the unboundedness: it is BREADTH-unbounded, not DEPTH-unbounded — see the ceiling field.

The ladder

WEEK 1: 30 substances, one island, one guild, one need ('harder cutting edge'). The bandit finds a two-step recipe you did not author and the printer prints its label. Delight. WEEK 2: MAP-Elites over axe forms; the archive fills; you can see a Pareto front of 'light and fragile' versus 'heavy and enduring' emerge as distinct local traditions. MONTH 1: trade between islands. A guild that has metal meets a guild that has resin; the first composite hafting appears and spreads. First LOST TECHNIQUE — a master dies in a raid with no apprentice, and the museum now holds an object it cannot explain. MONTH 2: an autocatalytic loop ignites on one island. Production of a substance explodes over 40 years, its price collapses, and everything downstream of it becomes cheap at once. The catalogue's 'Second Trade Age' gallery is created automatically because the material census shifted past a threshold. MONTH 4: a forgery. A guild reproduces the FORM of a famous lost artifact with the wrong material stack; the object enters the catalogue with an authenticity note; centuries later an excavation resolves it. ENDPOINT: a museum with thousands of objects, dozens of galleries organised by material era, a handful of genuinely famous named artifacts with 8-link provenance chains crossing four polities and two extinctions, and at least one technique that was lost, rediscovered independently, and named differently the second time.

What kills you

Guilds compete for commissions and die from bankruptcy: materials cost real stock, failed experiments burn it, and a guild with no commissions dissolves and its recipe book is lost unless apprentices carried it. Recipe knowledge is per-guild and transmitted with fidelity proportional to apprentice-years and to whether the culture has writing (a discoverable technology here too), so knowledge loss is a first-class mechanic rather than a scripted dark age. Polities compete militarily and objects win battles — a polity whose guilds found a better alloy actually wins wars, which redistributes commissions and accelerates the winner. Counter-pressures: resource depletion per island (the good clay bed runs out), and trade dependency (an island that specialised is ruined when its partner falls).

How the world changes

Each archipelago is a world; run 3-4 concurrently, each with an independently generated chemistry, so 'the alloys of Vess' and 'the alloys of Tarn' are genuinely different sciences. The catalogue is a single museum with wings for each world, which makes comparative material culture a browsable pleasure: the same need ('binder that sets underwater') solved four different ways by four chemistries. Within a world, sea-level change over long cycles submerges and reconnects islands, which reshuffles who trades with whom — non-stationary trade topology is the cheapest source of ongoing novelty I know. Sunken workshops become underwater sites with excellent organic preservation, which is an in-fiction reason the catalogue suddenly gains a whole category of wooden objects it never had.

How it knows something is new

(1) UNPRECEDENTED MATERIAL: a substance whose property vector's distance to its k=10 nearest catalogued neighbours exceeds the 99th percentile of the historical distribution — genuinely outside known material space, not just a new name. (2) DEEP SYNTHESIS: a substance whose production requires >=3 previously-invented intermediates. This is the compounding signal, and it is exactly the one that distinguishes real technological depth from a wide flat crafting tree. (3) ARCHIVE ILLUMINATION: a MAP-Elites cell filled for the first time, weighted by how isolated the cell is. pyribs gives you this for free as archive coverage delta. (4) PROVENANCE EVENT: an object crossing a fame threshold, or a LOST TECHNIQUE event (last holder of a recipe dies). Push budget two per day, ranked by a single scalar that is the max of the four normalised scores. Everything else accrues silently into the catalogue.

On your phone

One object card. A procedurally drawn SVG silhouette of the actual artifact (the form program renders directly to SVG — this is nearly free and it is what makes the museum feel real), its accession number, a three-line label in museum voice ('Hafted adze, Fifth Kiln period. Boar-tusk lamina over cured resin. Vess, workshop of Adda-of-the-Tin. Recovered from the flooded quarter.'), and a gallery breadcrumb. Below: today's acquisitions count and a single sparkline of the material census. Tap through for provenance chain, the recipe (if known to anyone alive), conservation notes, and 'objects like this'. Binge mode is walking a gallery, then walking a provenance chain into a war, then into an obituary.

Your hardware

This is the CPU-only idea, and that is a feature. The reaction network, the bandit, the physics-lite object model and pyribs all run in numpy on the CPU; 64GB RAM comfortably holds a multi-million-object catalogue in memory with the archive. GPU use is optional and small: Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M (~2.5GB, easy inside 8GB) writes object labels and conservation notes for the top few dozen objects a night, maybe 20k tokens total, a couple of minutes. If you ever wanted the GPU busy you could put a tiny surrogate model on it to predict reaction outcomes and prune the frontier, but you do not need to. The honest statement: this project would run on a Raspberry Pi if you dropped the prose layer, and the 5070 is idle 95% of the time.

What you have to do

None required. The one worthwhile monthly nudge is to hand-write a NEED into the world ('the king wants something that glows') and watch guilds crawl toward it — you are commissioning, not designing, and the solution is still theirs. Quarterly: generate a new archipelago with a deliberately hostile chemistry (no high-melting-point substances at all) and see what a civilization does without fire-based metallurgy.

First weekend

Saturday morning: substance generator and the reaction network, 30 substances, the property interaction map. Saturday afternoon: a single guild, the UCB frontier bandit, one hard-coded need. Print discovered substances to a terminal — this already feels alive because the second-order products are surprising. Saturday night: the form grammar and the SVG renderer, so you can look at an object. Sunday: pyribs archive over one object class, SQLite catalogue, a static HTML gallery. If you own a cheap thermal printer, Sunday evening is python-escpos printing your first object label, and that is the moment the project becomes a physical ritual rather than a program.

Six months in

A museum of several thousand catalogued objects across four worlds, twenty-odd galleries created by threshold events rather than by you, a dozen artifacts famous enough to have their own pages, three lost-and-rediscovered techniques, and a shelf of printed receipts by your coffee machine that reads like a real museum's daily accessions. You will have started caring about a particular workshop lineage.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Chemistry does not compound in ABSTRACTION the way a program library does. You get a colossally wide space of substances but the conceptual depth is capped by the number of PROCESS types you authored (heat, grind, soak, alloy, bind, ferment, precipitate — maybe ten). Once all ten are in play in all combinations, roughly month 3-5, new discoveries are new points in a space whose shape you already understand, and the catalogue starts to feel like a very good stamp collection rather than a history of ideas. Two honest patches: make process types themselves discoverable objects (a kiln is an artifact whose max-temperature parameter is under design search, so 'we can now reach temperatures nobody could reach' is a real event that opens previously-unreachable reactions); and let the object grammar be extensible by composition, so 'a thing with a haft' can become a component of 'a thing with a haft and a lever'. Even so, I would call this the widest of these six ideas and not the deepest. The second real risk: property vectors drifting into meaninglessness — nonlinear interaction maps happily produce a substance with hardness 400 and the catalogue becomes numerology. Clamp aggressively, use log-space, and sanity-check that the distribution of properties stays roughly log-normal after 10k reactions.

Stack

Python + numpy sim. pyribs (docs.pyribs.org, v0.11.x) for the MAP-Elites archives. SQLite WAL with FTS5 for the catalogue; the `object`, `provenance`, `recipe`, `reaction_cache` tables are the whole data model and they are worth designing carefully on day one because they are also the shared substrate you will reuse across projects. Form-program-to-SVG renderer in ~200 lines of Python, output inlined into the PWA (no external assets, works offline). llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B with GBNF for labels. python-escpos + the escpos-printer-db device profile database for the morning receipt. ntfy.sh push, Tailscale-served PWA, GitHub Pages public mirror of a pruned catalogue.

Verified

Verified: Infinite Craft's caching architecture — the backend checks whether a pair has ever been combined, serves cached responses, sends only genuinely new pairings to the model, and flags never-before-seen results with a 'First Discovery' indicator (quuxplusone.github.io/blog/2024/02/08/infinite-craft, and independent write-ups at blog.jverkamp.com/2024/03/14/infinite-craft-bot). I am reusing the cache-and-first-discovery pattern but replacing the LLM adjudicator with a deterministic generated chemistry, which makes it free and reproducible. pyribs (pyribs.org, docs.pyribs.org v0.11.0; GECCO 2023 paper arXiv 2303.00191) is the maintained bare-bones QD library implementing the RIBS framework with Archive/Emitter/Scheduler components and CMA-ME, CMA-MAE and variants — the archive-as-exhibit idea maps directly onto its API with no custom evolutionary code. On generated chemistries: 'Maximizing the Adjacent Possible in Automata Chemistries' (Artificial Life, MIT Press, 22(1):49) is the live research thread on designing artificial chemistries so the reachable set keeps expanding, and the autocatalytic-set literature (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocatalytic_set; PMC9695903 'Autocatalytic Sets Arising in a Combinatorial Model of Chemical Evolution'; PMC6161236 on autocatalytic sets in compartmentalised spatial worlds) supplies the mechanism for sudden regime shifts rather than smooth ramps. Physical artifact precedents that are real and maintained: python-escpos, the escpos-printer-db device database (github.com/receipt-print-hq/escpos-printer-db), chr15m/print-weather as a minimal daily-print example, and mashedkeyboard/dashday for daily summary printing on any ESC/POS device.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Month 2-4 for anything that reads as depth; breadth then continues forever and will be mistaken for progress. Two specific mechanisms, one of which the doc misses entirely. (1) The MISSED one: the UCB exploitation term is demand-pulled toward property-space targets, which is a greedy hill-climb — and greedy hill-climbing in property space is an ANTI-DEPTH mechanism. If a 1-step reaction lands near the need region, the 4-step chain that lands nearer is never explored, because the bandit's regret is computed against reachable value, not against depth. Your 'DEEP SYNTHESIS (>=3 intermediates)' detector therefore fires a handful of times early (when short paths happen not to exist) and then approximately never. Depth only appears if the reaction map has hard bottlenecks that short paths cannot cross. (2) The one the doc half-sees: conceptual depth is capped by ~10 authored process types crossed with a few condition axes; once that grid is populated, everything new is a new point in a space whose shape you already know. Also note the reaction network's own ambiguity, which the doc never resolves: if rules are a generated TABLE keyed on substance identity, the reachable closure is finite and a UCB crawl exhausts the interesting part in weeks; if rules are a FUNCTION over property vectors, the closure is infinite but structureless and you get the numerology failure the doc itself fears. There is no third option specified, and the two horns have opposite failure modes, so this must be decided before day one.

Is the discovery real?

Split verdict. Engine 1 (materials) is genuine discovery — generated chemistry, crawled locally, un-enumerable by the designer in advance. Engine 2 (objects) is authored content in disguise and the doc does not admit it: the form grammar is `haft + head + binding + haft-treatment`, so MAP-Elites is doing continuous parameter search inside a fixed four-slot vocabulary you wrote. Every 'first-of-its-kind object' is a numerically distinct instance of a shape you already specified. Worse, the archive-illumination novelty channel measures GRID RESOLUTION, not world events: a 50x50 archive has exactly 2500 possible 'first-fill' notifications available for the lifetime of the project, most of them consumed in month 1, and the rate of firing thereafter is a property of your descriptor binning, not of the civilization. The autocatalysis knob is likewise an authored 'bronze age arrives suddenly' switch dressed as chemistry.

Compute honesty

Completely honest, and the doc's self-assessment ('would run on a Raspberry Pi') is correct. numpy chemistry, pyribs archives, physics-lite object scoring — all trivial. GPU used for a few dozen label generations a night. The reaction_cache makes chemistry free after first evaluation. No hidden datacenter, no API bill. This is the cheapest-to-run idea after Apocrypha, and its costs are all in the developer's time, not the machine's.

Nonsense risk

High, and self-flagged. Nonlinear interaction maps over 8-dim property vectors drift into meaninglessness fast; 'hardness 400' is the visible symptom, but the invisible one is worse — property vectors that stay in range while ceasing to correspond to any coherent material. The clamping/log-space/log-normal advice is right but insufficient, because a distribution can stay log-normal while the *correlational structure* between properties dissolves (dense-and-fibrous-and-low-melting-and-hard), at which point the museum is cataloguing noise with good labels. Add a structural constraint, not just a marginal one: derive products by interpolation *within* the convex hull of input properties plus a bounded process-specific perturbation, so materials inherit family resemblance. The SVG-silhouette-plus-museum-voice presentation is exactly the kind of surface that hides semantic decay from its own author.

Claims to re-check

(a) 'pyribs v0.11.x' — the version number looks wrong/invented; pyribs was in the 0.7-0.8 range at last verification. The GECCO 2023 paper (arXiv 2303.00191) and the Archive/Emitter/Scheduler API description are correct. (b) Infinite Craft caching and First Discovery flag: accurate. (c) 'Maximizing the Adjacent Possible in Automata Chemistries', Artificial Life 22(1) — plausible and I believe real (Hickinbotham/Stepney lineage), but note that literature is about automata chemistries where the *rules* are themselves mutable programs; your chemistry has fixed rules, so the citation supports a stronger claim than your design makes. That gap is precisely your ceiling. (d) 'the reachable set grows superexponentially' — unsupported as stated. Superexponential growth requires products to be reliably reactive; generated property vectors will mostly produce inert or duplicate products, and the effective branching factor after dedup is likely small. Measure it in week 1 before building on it.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Delete the boundary between Engine 1 and Engine 2: make every PROCESS an artifact under design search. A kiln is an object whose max temperature is an emergent property of its form and materials; a bellows, press, mold, mill, and crucible likewise. Then the process vocabulary is no longer authored — it is whatever the object archive has produced — and material progress requires instrumental progress requires material progress. That single change converts the design's only monotone ladder (achievable conditions) into the spine of the tech tree, turns the '>=3 intermediates' detector back on permanently, and is the difference between a stamp collection and a history.

▸

The Ninefold Gazette

Nine small worlds with nine different generated physics, where a local 4B model acts as the adjudicator of what novel combinations produce — but only proposes, while arithmetic conservation laws clamp it and actual starvation decides whether the invention was real. You read a newspaper of the age each morning, front page chosen by a surprise metric, with foreign dispatches from the other eight worlds.

Not real depth
The world

Nine worlds, each 64x64 tiles with 5-8 tribes, each initialised with a generated PHYSICS CARD: a short structured description (element inventory with real numeric properties, which processes exist, what is abundant, what is deadly, what the sky does) that is injected into every adjudication prompt for that world. World 3 has no fire but has a mineral that liberates heat when wetted. World 7's atmosphere corrodes metal in a decade. Tribes have population, stores, territory, tools, beliefs, and a market with actual prices. The nine worlds run at different speeds; a slow world is a control.

How invention actually happens

LLM-as-physics, budgeted, cached, and — the crucial fix to Infinite Craft's emptiness — SUBORDINATE TO A SURVIVAL VERIFIER. STEP 1, PROPOSAL: when a tribe combines known things (A, B, process P) in a way never seen in this world, ask local Qwen3-4B, GBNF-constrained to a strict JSON schema: `{result_name, tags[3..6], props:{mass_per_unit, durability, energy_yield, labour_cost, toxicity, insulation, edge, storage_life}, requires_process, plausibility}`. Every numeric field is bounded by the grammar itself. Temperature 0.8, sampled THREE times; fields where 2-of-3 agree within tolerance survive; where they disagree the item is marked UNSTABLE, which is both an honest representation of model uncertainty and a great in-world category (unstable things behave inconsistently and are feared). STEP 2, CLAMP: a conservation engine adjusts the proposal before it enters the world. Total utility of the product is capped at (sum of input utilities x process efficiency) + a small novelty bonus; mass is conserved against inputs; energy_yield cannot exceed the chemical budget of its inputs; labour_cost has a floor derived from the process. This is ~80 lines of arithmetic and it is the entire difference between a world and a wish-granting machine. STEP 3, CACHE: write to `adjudication` keyed by (world_id, sorted inputs, process). Never asked twice. Cache hit rate exceeds 95% within a week of a world's life, which is what makes this affordable. STEP 4, SELECTION AS THE PHYSICS-CHECKER OF LAST RESORT: the item now exists with those numbers, and the deterministic survival sim runs. Tribes that adopt a bad item (because the model hallucinated a great-sounding but numerically mediocre thing) lose stores and die. Tribes that adopt a good one outcompete. Nobody adjudicates truth; starvation does. This is the honest answer to 'how do you stop the LLM lying': you do not, you make lies costly to the believer. STEP 5, INVENTION PRESSURE: tribes combine under demand (a need registry like Reliquary's) plus a small random exploration rate scaled by surplus. Adoption spreads by imitation weighted by observed prosperity, so a genuinely good invention diffuses and a bad one is abandoned — and the newspaper can report both, including a great running feature: RETRACTIONS, where a celebrated invention turns out to have been useless.

Why it doesn't bottom out

The naming and semantic space is the whole prior of a 4B language model, conditioned differently in each of nine worlds by its physics card — that is effectively unbounded and, more importantly, it is unbounded in KINDS of things rather than only in parameter values. Combinations feed back as inputs, so the reachable set is the transitive closure of a relation the model keeps extending. Nothing here bottoms out in a list. What it bottoms out in instead is described honestly below: not size, but VARIETY.

The ladder

WEEK 1: one world, six starting elements, tribes combining under need. You watch the model invent something and then watch the tribe that adopted it die because the clamp gave it a labour cost they could not pay. That failure is the moment you know the design works. WEEK 2: the market. Prices for every item, published as a table. A commodity price series is unreasonably fun to read and costs nothing. MONTH 1: nine worlds live. Divergence is visible — world 3's whole material culture is built on wetted mineral heat, and its vocabulary has no word for flame. MONTH 2: retractions and revivals; a technology abandoned in year 300 is rediscovered in year 900 under a different name because the cache keyed the same combination in a different world. MONTH 3: visitor omens start steering worlds; a guestbook line becomes a prophecy a tribe canonises. ENDPOINT: a newspaper archive of 200+ editions, a cross-world 'comparative technology' supplement, an obituary column you actually read, and at least one world that collapsed for a reason you can trace on the front page of an edition from forty issues earlier.

What kills you

Standard but tight: kcal, stores, winter, raids, disease. The distinguishing pressure here is ADOPTION RISK. Adopting a new item costs retooling (labour, stored materials, and a period of reduced output), so a tribe that adopts everything the adjudicator dreams up goes bankrupt on churn, and a tribe that adopts nothing is out-competed. The optimal adoption rate is an emergent property of the hazard climate, and different tribes land on different cultural attitudes to novelty — conservative and radical tribes arise mechanically. That is a genuinely interesting thing to read about.

How the world changes

Nine is the point. Different physics cards, different speeds, different visitor exposure (three worlds public and perturbable, three private, three frozen controls), and worlds occasionally ENDING — when a world's last tribe dies, its newspaper prints a final edition and a fresh world takes its slot with a physics card generated partly from the dead world's most successful concepts, so there is inheritance across worlds. The Gazette's masthead tracks which worlds are live. Cross-world comparison is the reading pleasure: the same section header, nine different answers.

How it knows something is new

(1) CROSS-WORLD FIRST: an item name (normalised) that has never appeared in ANY of the nine worlds — checked against a global name table, exact and cheap. (2) CONSEQUENCE MAGNITUDE: an item whose adoption changed a tribe's 20-year survival probability by more than X, measured by counterfactual replay (rerun 20 years with the item unavailable, seed-locked — this is expensive but you only do it for the ten most-adopted items per night, and it converts 'a thing was invented' into 'a thing MATTERED', which is the actual news). (3) CASCADE: an item that becomes an input to three or more further inventions within 100 years. (4) SURPRISE-VS-MODEL: keep a cheap n-gram/embedding model of what item names this world produces; flag items in the tail. This is the one that catches genuine weirdness. Seeded counterfactual replay is the single best idea in this whole set, because it is the only novelty detector here that measures IMPORTANCE rather than mere newness — and importance is what makes a front page.

On your phone

A newspaper front page, mobile-first: masthead with world name and date, one headline, one paragraph of real prose, and a strip of six one-line briefs from the other worlds. Under the fold: the market table (5 rows), obituaries (2 lines each), and the almanac. Thirty seconds gets you the headline and the briefs. An hour gets you the archive: every past edition, searchable, cross-linked by item and by tribe, plus the RETRACTIONS index which is the funniest page in the paper.

Your hardware

This is the one that actually uses the GPU, and here is the honest arithmetic. Qwen3-4B-Instruct Q4_K_M is ~2.5GB, fully offloaded on the 5070's 8GB with a large KV cache; expect roughly 40-80 tok/s generation on a laptop Blackwell part. Each adjudication is a ~250-token prompt and ~150-token grammar-constrained completion, x3 samples, so about 4-8 seconds wall-clock per novel combination. An 8-hour overnight run therefore supports roughly 4,000-7,000 novel adjudications across all nine worlds, which sounds small until you notice that after the first week the cache absorbs >95% of combination attempts and nightly novel volume settles to a few hundred. So: the GPU is the bottleneck for the first week and nearly idle after. Do not try to run a 14B here — it fits at Q4 but halves your throughput and the extra quality does not survive the clamp anyway. Keep the prose layer separate: 3-5 Claude Haiku 4.5 calls a night for the front page ($1/MTok in, $5/MTok out, with 90% off cached prefixes) is well under a dollar a month and the writing quality difference on the ONE piece you actually read is worth it.

What you have to do

Nothing daily. Monthly: write a new physics card by hand for a slot that has opened (30 minutes, enormously high leverage — this is the most fun creative act in the whole project). Weekly-ish if you feel like it: read the retractions page and tighten a clamp constant if the model is inflating a particular property.

First weekend

Saturday: llama.cpp server up with Qwen3-4B and a GBNF grammar for the adjudication schema; get 3-sample majority voting working; write the conservation clamp. Test by hand-combining absurd things and watching the clamp refuse to grant free energy. Saturday night: SQLite cache, a 32x32 world, three tribes, a kcal loop. Sunday: the need registry and adoption dynamics, then a hideous single-column HTML newspaper with one headline. The delightful moment is Saturday night when a tribe adopts a model-invented item and the deterministic sim kills them for it — that is when it stops being a chatbot toy.

Six months in

Roughly 180 editions, nine world histories with genuinely different technological vocabularies, an adjudication cache of tens of thousands of entries that IS your physics and could be shared as a dataset, a retractions index that is a comedy, and public worlds where strangers' omens are visible in the historical record. You will be able to point at a front page from month 2 and say 'that is where world 6 was doomed'.

Where it stops (self-reported)

LLM-as-physics converges to genre. A 4B model asked for the result of combining things reaches for 'crystal', 'rune', 'essence', 'shard' across every world within weeks, and the nine worlds will start to rhyme by month 3 — this is the real failure mode, not hallucinated numbers. Partial mitigations, all of which I would actually build: maintain a cross-world token frequency table and apply logit bias / grammar exclusions against any token appearing in more than 2% of item names; condition each world on a GENERATED phonology and a generated material vocabulary so the model is naming in a constructed language rather than in fantasy English; and periodically rotate the adjudicator model (a different 4B has a different cliché attractor). Even with all of that, expect flavour convergence to be noticeable by month 4. Second honest limit: a 4B model's proposals are shallow — it will not give you a mechanism, only a name and some numbers, so the 'why' of a technology is always thin compared to Palimpsest, where the why is literally the program. Third: the counterfactual-replay novelty detector is O(20 years of sim) per candidate; budget it hard or it eats your night. Fourth: this design is only as good as its clamp, and clamps are fiddly — expect two weeks of tuning where the world is either static or exploding.

Stack

Python sim, llama.cpp server with Qwen3-4B-Instruct Q4_K_M and per-call GBNF grammars (llama.cpp's --grammar-file / response_format JSON-schema support is what makes unattended structured adjudication safe). SQLite WAL for everything, with the `adjudication` cache as its own table so it survives sim rewrites — that cache is the world's physics and it is the most precious file you own. Claude Haiku 4.5 via the Anthropic API for the daily front-page prose only, with prompt caching on the style guide and the world bible. Newspaper rendered as static HTML per edition (a real archive of files, which is both cheap and pleasing), PWA wrapper with a service worker, Tailscale for the private version, GitHub Pages for the three public worlds. ntfy.sh for the one daily push.

Verified

Verified: Infinite Craft's architecture — every pair checked against a cache, only novel pairings hit the model (originally Llama 2), results cached forever so the same pairing is stable for every player, with a 'First Discovery' flag on never-before-seen results (quuxplusone.github.io/blog/2024/02/08/infinite-craft; blog.freestyle.dev/posts/multiplayer-infinite-craft for a full-stack reimplementation). This is the proven affordability trick and my design depends on it. llama.cpp guarantees structured output via --grammar-file GBNF or server response_format with a JSON schema, and this works offline with any model — confirmed in current (2026) llama.cpp documentation and guides; this is non-negotiable for unattended overnight adjudication. Qwen3-4B at Q4_K_M is ~2.5GB by the ~0.5-0.6GB-per-billion GGUF rule of thumb, comfortably inside 8GB with a large KV cache; smeltcore.com's RTX 5070 recipes cover Qwen3-8B and 14B Q4_K_M on exactly this class of card if you want to trade throughput for quality. Claude Haiku 4.5 is $1.00/MTok input and $5.00/MTok output as of August 2026, with prompt caching cutting cached input to 10% of standard and the Batch API cutting both by 50% — so a nightly front-page prose pass is cents per month. On LLM-driven civilizations that actually exist: CivAgent (github.com/fuxiAIlab/CivAgent) runs LLM agents as digital players in Unciv, and Altera's Project Sid demonstrated emergent role specialisation and cultural norm propagation with large agent populations in Minecraft — both are evidence the genre works and neither delivers a readable chronicle, which is the gap this fills.

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

Depth never starts. Item count grows forever and will be misread as depth for about six weeks. The structural reason: every invention in this world is a NAME plus an 8-field numeric vector. There is no representation in which one technology is MADE OF another — only a parent pointer in a provenance table. So 'compounding' is a graph annotation, not a mechanism; nothing about invention #400 is cheaper, better, or differently-shaped because inventions #1-399 exist. Compare Palimpsest, where a new abstraction literally reshapes the search space. Empirically, Infinite Craft is the control experiment and it already ran: deep combination chains degenerate into modifier stacking and self-reference rather than into structure, because the model does not track what an item IS across compositions. Your clamp constrains arithmetic, which is the failure mode you can see, and does nothing about semantic decoherence, which is the one that kills you. Expect nine worlds that rhyme by month 2-3 (the doc says month 4; it is optimistic), and a front page that is entertaining prose over a flat namespace indefinitely.

Is the discovery real?

Maximal, and disguised better than in any other idea here. The invention space is a 4B model's pretraining prior — that is the single largest body of authored content on this list, revealed in an order determined by which tribe combined what. The physics card conditions the surface vocabulary; it does not condition the model's combinational semantics, which are learned from human text and will keep reaching for the same attractors. The doc treats this as a flavour problem to be patched with logit bias and generated phonologies; it is a substance problem, and token-level bans against 'crystal/rune/shard' will produce differently-named versions of the same three concepts. The honest description of this design is: an LLM is asked to reveal, one pair at a time, what human culture already believes results from combining things, with numbers clamped so the reveal cannot break the economy. That is the fatal flaw the brief warns about, in its purest form.

Compute honesty

Honest on the per-call arithmetic, wrong on the aggregate, and the error is load-bearing. 40-80 tok/s for Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M on a laptop 5070 is a fair-to-conservative estimate; ~4-8s per 3-sample adjudication is right; 4,000-7,000 adjudications per overnight is right. But the claim that novel volume 'settles to a few hundred a night after the first week' is backwards. The number of *possible* combinations is O(n^2) in the item count and n grows monotonically; with need-pulled exploration plus a random exploration rate scaled by surplus, novel-pair encounters grow rather than shrink. Cache hit rate is high only if tribes keep retrying the SAME pairs, which is exactly what a need-driven explorer avoids. You will be GPU-bound permanently, not for a week, and the practical consequence is that you must hard-cap combination attempts per tribe-year — which caps invention rate — or accept a growing adjudication backlog. Separately, counterfactual replay at 20 sim-years per candidate for ten candidates a night across nine worlds is the other budget hole the doc flags but does not cost out. Claude Haiku for the front page really is cents/month; that part is fine.

Nonsense risk

Highest of the six by a wide margin. The output is a name and eight numbers, with no mechanism, no recipe, and no executable content — so 'meaningful' can only mean 'the name sounds plausible given the inputs', which is precisely what a language model optimises and precisely what carries no information about the world. The three-sample UNSTABLE category is a genuinely clever touch and the only honest signal in the pipeline, but at temperature 0.8 on a 4B, disagreement will be dominated by numeric sampling noise rather than by real model uncertainty, so 'unstable' will mostly tag boring items. Selection-as-verifier does not rescue this: starvation can kill tribes that adopt numerically bad items, but it cannot make a semantically incoherent item coherent, and it never runs on items nobody adopts — which is most of them.

Claims to re-check

(a) '>95% cache hit within a week, nightly novel volume settles to a few hundred' — arithmetically unsupported, see above; this is the assumption the whole affordability story rests on. (b) 'smeltcore.com's RTX 5070 recipes' — I cannot verify this source; treat as fabricated until checked. (c) Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per MTok with 10% cached input and 50% Batch API discount — consistent with what I can verify. (d) CivAgent (fuxiAIlab) and Altera's Project Sid are real and correctly characterised, but neither is evidence that LLM adjudication produces *technological depth*; Sid demonstrated role specialisation and norm propagation, which is a different claim than the one it is cited for here. (e) Infinite Craft's original adjudicator was Llama 2 — correct. (f) 'the naming and semantic space is the whole prior of a 4B model... effectively unbounded' — unbounded in string space, extremely bounded in concept space, and the doc conflates the two.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Invert the LLM's role: stop asking it for RESULTS and start asking it for RECIPES. Constrain it to emit a program in a small typed process DSL over substances the world already has, and let the deterministic sim compute what that program produces. The model becomes a proposal distribution over structured actions — which is what LLMs are genuinely good at and where their prior is an asset rather than a contaminant — and the world's physics becomes real, compositional, and yours. This converges the design toward Palimpsest with an LLM proposer instead of enumerative search, which is a legitimately good architecture and arguably the best hybrid available on this list. Keep the clamp, the cache, the retractions page, and the nine physics cards; they all survive the change.

▸

Strata

The simulation is deliberately hidden from you: civilizations rise, invent, and fall in deep time, and every night an autonomous expedition digs three trenches and publishes an excavation report built only from what physically survived. The chronicle stays fresh for months not because new things keep happening but because your understanding of what happened keeps being revised — including by a famous forgery you will not spot at first.

Ceilinged
The world

One planet, 12 regions, 200,000 in-world years at a coarse tick with fine-grained focus periods where interesting things happen. Civilizations arise, build, invent, exhaust their environment, and collapse; several rise on the ruins of others. The load-bearing subsystem is not the civilization sim but the DEPOSITION MODEL: every object, structure, burial, and text has a material class with a preservation function over (soil pH, waterlogging, temperature cycling, later construction, erosion, looting). Bone dissolves in acid soil. Wood survives only waterlogged or charred. Ceramics survive essentially forever but travel. Metal is recycled by later people, which means the more valuable a thing was, the less likely it survives — a beautiful and real inversion. Every site accumulates a proper Harris-matrix stratigraphy: numbered contexts, cut-and-fill relationships, intrusions, residual finds.

How invention actually happens

Grammar-based genetic programming over a CRAFT GRAMMAR, coupled to niche construction with long lags. A craft is a derivation in a context-free grammar whose terminals are actions over materials and whose fitness is its contribution to survival in the population that holds it.
