Variation is subtree mutation and crossover of derivations plus rule-level innovation (a production rule that gets used together often enough is promoted to a named non-terminal — a cheap library-learning move that gives you compounding without a full DreamCoder stack). Transmission is vertical (parent to child, high fidelity), oblique (master to apprentice), and horizontal (trade, conquest), each with different error rates. The distinctive mechanism is NICHE CONSTRUCTION WITH DELAY. Every craft has an environmental side effect written into the tile state with a lag of 50-500 years: irrigation raises soil salinity, smelting deforests and then erodes, drainage lowers the water table, intensive cropping shifts the pollen spectrum. So a technology's fitness is high when adopted and can be catastrophically negative three centuries later, after everyone has become dependent on it. This is what makes the archaeology worth reading: the excavation report can show you the salt crust in context 14 sitting directly above the granary floor, and you get to work out the causality yourself. And here is the actual product loop. Every night an EXPEDITION runs: a survey model picks 1-3 trenches (biased by previous finds, by a small budget, and by a rivalry between two in-world schools of archaeology with different theories), excavates, and recovers a SAMPLE — a filtered, damaged, spatially biased subset of the ground truth. Then an INTERPRETATION pass runs with access ONLY to the recovered finds and previously published claims, never to ground truth. It writes a site report and updates a CLAIMS table: statements with confidence values, supporting contexts, and an author. Later digs can raise, lower, or invert those confidences.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two independent sources. First, the craft grammar with rule promotion is a growing formal grammar — the space of derivations over a signature that keeps gaining non-terminals does not bottom out, and the delayed-consequence dynamics mean the fitness landscape is non-stationary, so there is no final optimum to converge on. Second, and this is the part unique to this idea: the INTERPRETIVE space is unbounded independently of the event space. Even if the sim froze forever, the archive of possible hypotheses about it, the revisions, the rival schools, and the reinterpretations under new evidence keep producing genuinely new text. Most procedural history projects die of repetition because the reader eventually sees the generator; here the reader sees a lossy channel, and lossy channels are inexhaustible.

The ladder

WEEK 1: a toy civ, a deposition model, one trench. You dig up 40 potsherds and a posthole and the report says 'probably a structure, function unclear'. You know it was a granary. That gap is the entire feeling of the project and you get it on day one. WEEK 2: stratigraphy proper — Harris matrix, residual finds, intrusions. The report starts making dating arguments and getting them slightly wrong. MONTH 1: two rival schools with different priors, publishing competing interpretations of the same finds. The chronicle now has an argument in it. MONTH 2: the first OVERTURNED CLAIM — a trench dug in a different region invalidates a confident published statement from week 3. This is the best push notification the project will ever send. MONTH 3: niche-construction lags start landing. A civilization collapses from soil salinity and the excavation report finds the evidence 400 years later in the wrong region first. MONTH 4: absolute dating gets invented in-fiction (the expedition develops a technique), and the entire published chronology shifts by 300 years, cascading revisions through hundreds of claims at once. A whole night of reading. ENDPOINT: a corpus of several hundred site reports, a claims database with a visible history of scholarly consensus forming and breaking, a forgery that fooled the record for two months, and at least one civilization whose existence is inferred entirely from the absence of things.

What kills you

Inside the sim: carrying capacity, climate cycles, the delayed environmental costs of the civilization's own successes, and conquest. Nothing exotic — the selection pressure does not need to be clever here because the interpretive layer is where the value is. Between SCHOOLS of archaeology there is a second, funnier selection: a school's prestige rises when its claims survive later digs and falls when they are overturned, and prestige controls the excavation budget, which controls where trenches go. So a wrong-but-prestigious school digs in the wrong places for decades, which is an extremely accurate simulation of a real field and produces terrific reading.

How the world changes

A SHELF of 6-9 planets, each with different geology (hence different preservation regimes) and different climate. This makes COMPARATIVE ARCHAEOLOGY the headline feature: 'in 4 of 9 worlds someone invented the true arch; in the two worlds with no suitable stone, nobody did, and here is the timber solution they used instead.' Because preservation differs by world, the same civilization type produces radically different evidence — one world is all ceramics and no organics, another is a peat bog where you get the wooden tools and the clothes. Reading the same history through different preservation windows is a pleasure nothing else on this list offers. Within a world, deep time itself is the change: the climate curve is generated, so the Holocene-equivalent of world 4 is not the Holocene.

How it knows something is new

Ranked by what actually makes a person want to read: (1) OVERTURNED CLAIM: a claim whose confidence crosses from >0.8 to <0.3 (or the reverse). This is the top-priority push and it is exactly measurable. (2) UNEXPLAINED FIND: an object whose material or form cannot be produced by any craft the current published chronology attributes to that period — an anomaly. Trivially computable by checking the recovered artifact against the claims table, and it is the single most evocative event type in the genre. (3) FIRST EVIDENCE: earliest recovered instance of a craft class in any world. (4) HIATUS: a site with a stratigraphic gap exceeding N years, i.e. an abandonment nobody has explained yet. (5) ASAL-style novelty on the embedding of the night's finds assemblage, to catch what you did not instrument. Note the elegance: novelty here is defined against the PUBLISHED RECORD, not against ground truth, which means the detector is measuring the same surprise the reader would feel. That is much better than measuring simulation-side novelty the reader has no access to.

On your phone

An excavation notice. 'Trench 41, Rill Bank. 3 contexts. 1 anomaly.' One photo-equivalent: a procedurally drawn section drawing (SVG, hatched layers, labelled contexts — genuinely beautiful and nearly free to render) plus a finds count by material. If a claim was overturned, that is the whole notification and it says which one and by what. Thirty seconds. The hour-long version is the site report, the Harris matrix as an interactive diagram, the claims database with confidence histories as sparklines, the school rivalry, and the comparative view across nine worlds.

Your hardware

CPU-only for the sim and the deposition model; 64GB RAM is genuinely useful because deep time over 12 regions at object granularity is a lot of rows and you want the recent horizon in memory. GPU is used for one thing: the interpretation pass. This should be the strongest local model you can fit, because reasoning from partial evidence is the one task here where model quality is visible in the output — Qwen3-8B at Q4_K_M (~4.5GB) fits in 8GB with a modest context and is worth the throughput hit over 4B, since you only run a handful of interpretation calls per night. Do not attempt 14B at long context on 8GB; it fits but the KV cache will push you into shared-memory swapping and the tokens-per-second collapse is brutal. Alternative worth considering: run interpretation on Claude Haiku 4.5 via the API for a few cents a night, since it is one call per trench and quality matters more here than anywhere else in these six ideas.

What you have to do

Zero. This is the most hands-off idea of the six because the interpretation layer generates novelty without you. Optional quarterly nudge: fund an expedition to a region you are curious about (one row in a table), or introduce a new dating technique and watch the chronology shatter and reform.

First weekend

Saturday: a crude civ sim (population, a craft grammar with 8 rules, 3 regions, collapse conditions), run 5,000 years in a few seconds. Saturday afternoon: the deposition model — material classes, preservation probabilities, a soil layer per tile. Saturday night: dig one trench, print the recovered finds table next to the ground truth, side by side. That comparison is the delight, and it happens on day one with no LLM involved. Sunday: Harris matrix, the SVG section drawing, and the claims table with a hand-written interpreter (rules, not LLM) so you can see revisions working before you add a model.

Six months in

Nine worlds, several hundred published site reports, a claims database with hundreds of statements and their confidence histories, two or three overturned orthodoxies, a comparative-technology supplement across all worlds, and a section-drawing gallery you would print and frame. The thing you will not expect: you will start rooting for one of the two archaeological schools.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The civilization sim underneath is the weakest of the six — the craft grammar with rule promotion gives you real but modest compounding, considerably less than Palimpsest's library learning, and if you ever look directly at ground truth you may find it thinner than the reports imply. That is a genuine risk: the project's magic depends on never showing you the sim, which means if the sim is boring you will only find out slowly and painfully. Mitigation: steal Palimpsest's engine wholesale as the underlying invention mechanism and make Strata the DELIVERY layer over it — honestly, these two ideas want to be married, and if you build one build it in a way that lets the other bolt on. Second ceiling: interpretive novelty is not infinite in practice even though it is in principle; once the world's broad chronology is settled and confidence is high everywhere, revisions get rarer. Countermeasure is new regions, new dating techniques, and periodic worlds — but expect the revision rate to decay roughly as 1/t within a world, so plan on adding worlds rather than digging one forever. Third: the interpretation pass is the one place a local model's weakness shows, because reasoning from absence is hard; budget for either an 8B local model or a few cents a night of API.

Stack

Python sim. SQLite WAL with tables `context`, `find`, `site`, `claim`, `report`, `school`; FTS5 over reports. Harris matrix as a DAG in the contexts table with an SVG renderer for both the matrix and the section drawings — the section drawing renderer is a weekend of fun and is the visual identity of the whole project. llama.cpp with Qwen3-8B Q4_K_M for interpretation under a GBNF schema that forces every claim to cite context IDs (a claim with no citation is rejected before it is ever rendered — this is the anti-hallucination mechanism and it is mechanical, not prompt-based). Prose rendered from validated claims only. PWA + service worker, Tailscale private, GitHub Pages public.

Verified

Verified: Dwarf Fortress generates history for a world after generating the world itself, tracking civilizations, sites and populations across up to 2,000-year histories, and those legends are read by clicking links like browsing a wiki rather than as generated prose (dwarffortresswiki.org/index.php/World_generation) — the delivery gap this idea targets. James Ryan's work is the key scholarly grounding: Talk of the Town simulates an American small town day by day for 200 years with an emphasis on social exchange and characters' internal lives, and his dissertation 'Curating Simulated Storyworlds' (UCSC, 2018; covered in detail by Emily Short at emshort.blog/2019/05/28/curating-simulated-storyworlds-james-ryan-ch-6f) names exactly the problem this idea's novelty detector solves — a simulation generates far more than anyone can read, so the hard part is SIFTING, not generating. Ryan's 'Simulating Character Knowledge Phenomena in Talk of the Town' (Game AI Pro 3, ch. 37, gameaipro.com) is directly relevant to modelling knowledge as lossy and mutable, which is the same machinery as evidence and claims here. Lynn Cherny's Dwarf2Text is the closest real precedent for data-to-text over simulated history and is worth reading for its failure modes. On grounded generation: current data-to-text practice separates content selection and planning (an intermediate plan of what to say and in what order) from surface realisation, and template approaches give strict hallucination guarantees at the cost of sounding robotic — the citation-required claims schema here is the hybrid, and blueprint/QA-plan approaches (arXiv 2503.23204) are the published version of the same idea. ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799) supplies the fallback statistical novelty channel.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two ceilings, and they compound. The technological one: grammar-based GP with rule promotion is real but weak compounding — it is essentially module acquisition / ADFs, which historically gives a modest constant-factor improvement rather than open-ended growth, and the doc correctly rates it below Palimpsest. That layer plateaus around week 6-10. The interpretive one is the load-bearing claim and it is overstated. 'Even if the sim froze, the hypothesis space is unbounded' is true in principle and false in practice, because your claims are schema-constrained tuples (predicate, entity, context-citation) over an authored predicate vocabulary, and the number of *distinguishable* things that can be said about a finite ground truth is finite and small. Worse, evidence accumulation is a convergent process by construction: each trench monotonically reduces posterior variance, so overturned-claim events — your best notification, correctly identified — are self-extinguishing. Expect 20-40 genuine reversals per world, front-loaded, with the rate decaying as roughly 1/t exactly as the doc predicts, and the chronology substantially settled by month 3-4. Adding worlds adds content linearly; it does not restore compounding. And the honest framing for the builder's actual brief: this idea does not deliver cavemen-to-something-wild at all. It delivers epistemics about a shallow tech ladder. That may be better reading, but it is a different product than the one requested.

Is the discovery real?

Medium, in an unusual place. The ground truth has genuine (if modest) emergence — the niche-construction-with-delay mechanism is excellent and produces causal structure nobody wrote. The authored risk is in the INTERPRETIVE layer: rival schools with different priors sounds emergent but is two hand-tuned prior vectors, the 'famous forgery' is a scripted event type, and 'absolute dating gets invented in-fiction, shattering the chronology' is an authored plot beat with a date on it. Those are the three most exciting items in the depth ladder and all three are content, not mechanism. The deeper structural point in this design's favour, which is real and rare: a lossy channel over even a modest generator produces more *legitimate* novelty than a rich generator shown directly, because the reader's uncertainty is a genuine resource. That insight is correct and is the best single idea in the six after seeded counterfactual replay.

Compute honesty

Honest, with the best-calibrated GPU sizing on the list. Qwen3-8B Q4_K_M at ~4.5-5GB with modest context genuinely fits 8GB; the warning about 14B thrashing into shared memory is correct and is the kind of detail that indicates the author has actually done this. A handful of interpretation calls per night is nothing. CPU-side deep time over 12 regions at object granularity is the real cost and it is RAM-shaped, not FLOP-shaped — but note that object-granularity deposition over 200,000 years across 12 regions is where this design will actually bite: you must aggressively coarsen outside focus periods or the object table becomes the bottleneck long before the GPU does. The Haiku-for-interpretation fallback at cents/night is accurate.

Nonsense risk

Low-to-moderate for the archaeology (section drawings, Harris matrices, and finds tables are structurally constrained and will look right), but the doc makes one claim I want to flag hard: 'a claim with no citation is rejected before it is ever rendered — this is the anti-hallucination mechanism and it is mechanical, not prompt-based'. GBNF constrains FORM, not TRUTH. The grammar can force a claim to cite context 14; it cannot prevent the model from asserting something false about context 14. The mechanical guarantee you actually have is referential integrity, which is much weaker than the doc implies, and in a project whose entire premise is that the reader trusts the reports as honest inference from evidence, that gap is the one that can rot the artifact. Fix: mechanically verify each claim's *entailment* against the recovered finds with rule-based checks for the small set of claim predicates, and mark anything the checker cannot verify as 'speculative' in the rendered text.

Claims to re-check

(a) DF tracking 'up to 2,000-year histories' — the standard world-gen history length is considerably shorter (1050 years in the usual presets); dubious as stated. (b) arXiv 2503.23204 for blueprint/QA-plan data-to-text — I cannot verify this identifier; the underlying blueprint-models line of work (Narayan et al.) is real, so the idea is sound but the citation should be checked. (c) James Ryan's dissertation 'Curating Simulated Storyworlds' (UCSC 2018), Talk of the Town, Game AI Pro 3 ch.37, and Emily Short's coverage are all correctly cited and genuinely the right grounding — this is the best-researched notes field of the six. (d) ASAL as fallback channel: fine. (e) The unflagged research gap: the doc cites the sifting problem but not the reliability literature on LLM inference from incomplete evidence, which is where its central pass is weakest.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make the marriage the precondition, not the mitigation: build Palimpsest's library-learning engine as Strata's ground truth from day one, and build Strata strictly as a lens over it. The doc already identifies this and then files it under 'honest ceiling' — it belongs at the top of the design. It fixes both ceilings at once: the technological one (real compounding underneath) and the interpretive one (a richer ground truth supports more distinguishable claims and keeps reversals coming). Corollary that costs nothing: build a spoiler toggle that shows ground truth beside the report, and check it weekly. The doc correctly identifies that the project's magic depends on never looking — which means you will discover the sim is thin slowly and painfully, and a toggle converts that into a week-one measurement.

▸

Scriptorium

Each polity's intelligence is literally a LoRA adapter that is retrained every week on only the decisions that mechanically worked, and the chronicle is written by those same models — so as the checkpoints diverge, the prose diverges, and you can read a civilization getting sharper or going senile in its own syntax. A lost adapter file recovered from a ruin is a rediscovered manuscript, and reloading it ends a dark age.

Ceilinged
The world

One continent, ~12 polities, coarse annual ticks over a few thousand years. Each polity has land, population, stores, armies, neighbours, a treasury, and a COLLEGE. The college is the decision-maker: at each decision point it receives a compact structured state (a few hundred tokens: harvest, stores, threats, prices, recent history) plus a retrieved handful of its own past canonical writings, and it emits, under a GBNF grammar, a structured decision plus a JUSTIFICATION in prose. The world physics is a deterministic, boring, thoroughly-tested economy-and-war sim — deliberately boring, because it is the verifier and it must be trustworthy.

How invention actually happens

A STaR / rejection-sampling flywheel embodied as institutional memory, with two kinds of invention stacked. INVENTION LAYER 1 (explicit, inspectable): the college's decisions include POLICY PROGRAMS — small structured plans in a policy DSL (allocate labour, set tariffs, garrison, sow, store, conscript, build). Successful policy programs are compressed weekly into named POLICIES the college can invoke by name later. This is the same library-learning move as Palimpsest but over statecraft rather than materials, and it gives you nameable inventions like 'the Threefold Store' that are actual reusable programs. INVENTION LAYER 2 (implicit, the reason this idea exists): the model weights themselves. Weekly, for each polity: gather all (state, justification, decision) triples from the past week, score each trajectory MECHANICALLY at +20 in-world years (population, stores, territory, survival — a scalar, no LLM judge anywhere), keep the top quantile, and run an Unsloth QLoRA finetune on the polity's current adapter. The new checkpoint must beat the incumbent on a FROZEN held-out set of 200 archived world-states, scored by the same mechanical function, or it is discarded and the polity enters a DARK AGE (reverts to a weaker sampling temperature, loses access to its most recent named policies, prose quality visibly degrades). Benchmark hygiene matters and is easy to get wrong on a gaming laptop: the eval must be scored on DECISION QUALITY under a fixed token budget, never on wall-clock or throughput, and if you ever need timing, lock clocks with `nvidia-smi -lgc` and interleave A/B runs rather than running them back to back. ARCHAEOLOGY OF WEIGHTS: every adapter is kept. They are 50-200MB files. When a polity falls, its adapter is written to a `ruin` row. A later polity that excavates the site can load it — with damage, meaning a random subset of LoRA layers zeroed or a merge at partial alpha. A recovered ancient mind that is better than yours is the single best story beat available in any of these six designs, and here it is a literal file operation.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two claims, and I want to be precise about which one is strong. The STRONG claim: the policy-program library compounds exactly like Palimpsest's — named policies become invocable primitives inside later policies, over a growing signature, with mechanical verification. That is genuinely unbounded and it is where the reliable depth is. The WEAKER claim, which I will not oversell: the weight-space trajectory of a 4B model under repeated self-training is high-dimensional and never repeats, so the polities keep DIVERGING indefinitely — but divergence is not the same as capability growth. The honest framing is that this project's unboundedness is in variation and specialisation, with genuine compounding only in the symbolic policy layer.

The ladder

WEEK 1: base Qwen3-4B making decisions for three polities under a strict grammar, deterministic sim, mechanical scoring. Read three justifications for the same harvest crisis. Already interesting. WEEK 2: first QLoRA run. Diff the same prompt through base and finetuned adapters side by side — the styles have moved apart. That side-by-side diff view is the product. MONTH 1: twelve polities, twelve adapters, visible dialects. One polity's justifications have become terse and numeric; another's are florid and superstitious, because its winning trajectories happened to include a lot of appeals to omens. MONTH 2: first failed gate, first dark age. A polity's checkpoint fails held-out eval, gets discarded, and the chronicle for that decade is measurably worse written. This will feel bad in a way you did not expect. MONTH 3: policy library depth — a named policy invoked inside another named policy inside a third. MONTH 4: first weight-archaeology event. A polity excavates a fallen rival's adapter, merges it at partial alpha, and either inherits a genuinely better decision-maker or a beautiful madness. ENDPOINT: twelve mutually unintelligible scholarly traditions writing about the same continent, a chronicle where you can identify the author polity from three sentences with no byline, and a lineage tree of adapters that is simultaneously a family tree of minds and a directory of files on your disk.

What kills you

The mechanical scorer, and nothing else, which is the discipline that keeps this honest. A polity that makes bad decisions loses population, territory and stores, and a shrinking polity gets fewer decision points per year, therefore less training data, therefore weaker weekly finetunes — the same 'thinking is paid for by surplus' coupling as Palimpsest but operating on gradient steps instead of search nodes. That feedback loop is vicious and produces real death spirals. Between polities: war, trade, and adapter theft (conquest can copy the loser's adapter, so military success can import intelligence directly, which is a lovely and slightly disturbing dynamic).

How the world changes

Within a run, the continent's climate and disease regime drift, so the decision distribution a college was trained on stops matching reality — a college that overfitted to a benign century fails hard when the century turns, which is model-collapse-as-plot. Across runs: keep the adapter lineage and start a NEW world seeded with adapters from the old one, so a fresh continent is settled by the intellectual descendants of a dead one. Over six months you will have a genealogy of minds spanning several worlds, which is a much stranger and better artifact than a genealogy of kings.

How it knows something is new

(1) STYLE SHIFT: per-polity rolling embedding of its chronicle prose (bge-small); a checkpoint whose post-finetune output embedding moves more than 3 sigma from its own trailing distribution is a genuine event — 'the College of Vann now writes differently'. This is cheap, exact, and unique to this design. (2) GATE OUTCOMES: passed with a large margin, or failed (dark age). Both are news. (3) POLICY-LIBRARY EVENTS: new named policy, or a policy invoked at record composition depth. (4) DIVERGENCE RECORD: pairwise distance between two polities' outputs on the SAME held-out state hits an all-time high — the moment two traditions become mutually alien. (5) LEXICAL INNOVATION: a token/phrase that a polity now uses at high frequency and which it never used before, and which no other polity uses. This catches jargon formation, which is exactly what real scholarly traditions do.

On your phone

Two paragraphs side by side, same event, two colleges, with a one-line divergence score between them. Above them, one status strip: twelve polity glyphs coloured by health, with a small crown on any that finetuned last night and a black band on any in a dark age. That is the 30-second view and it is unusually information-dense. The hour version: the full chronicle per polity, the adapter lineage tree, the held-out eval score history as a chart, the policy library with call graphs, and a 'Rosetta' page that runs the same three archived world-states through every living college so you can compare twelve minds on identical input.

Your hardware

This is the idea that pushes the 8GB ceiling, and I want to be blunt about it. Unsloth supports Blackwell (RTX 5070/5080/5090, sm_120) but you MUST be on CUDA 12.8+ with all dependent libraries built for it — older wheels have no sm_120 kernels and you will hit 'no kernel image is available for execution on the device'. QLoRA on Qwen3-4B in 4-bit fits 8GB with discipline: sequence length capped at 2048, batch size 1 with gradient accumulation, gradient checkpointing on, LoRA rank 16-32 on attention projections only. Expect roughly 30-60 minutes per polity-week of training, so twelve polities is a 6-12 hour weekly job — that is your Saturday night, which fits the overnight rhythm perfectly. Inference during the week uses llama.cpp with the base model plus per-polity LoRA adapters (llama.cpp supports adapter hot-swap, so you keep one ~2.5GB base resident and swap 50-200MB adapters per call rather than holding twelve models). Do NOT attempt 8B QLoRA at useful context on 8GB; it technically loads and then thrashes. If you want more headroom, drop to Qwen3-1.7B for the colleges — the divergence story works even better with a small model because small models overfit faster and more visibly.

What you have to do

Weekly glance at the eval-score chart, which takes 20 seconds and is genuinely interesting. One real chore exists and I will not pretend otherwise: when the CUDA/Unsloth stack breaks after an update — and it will, once or twice in six months — you lose an evening. Pin your versions, keep a working conda env tarball, and treat the finetune as optional (if the weekly job fails, the world just continues with the incumbent adapters, which is a graceful degradation you should design in from day one).

First weekend

Saturday: the deterministic economy-and-war sim with a mechanical scorer, three polities, and base Qwen3-4B making grammar-constrained decisions. Get the chronicle rendering from justifications. Saturday night: archive 200 world-states as the frozen held-out eval and score base model performance on them so you have a baseline. Sunday: the Unsloth QLoRA pipeline end to end on one polity's week of winning trajectories, the gate, and the side-by-side diff view. The delightful moment is Sunday evening, running the same prompt through base and adapter and seeing the second one sound like it has opinions.

Six months in

Twelve to thirty adapters in a lineage tree spanning two or three worlds, chronicles you can attribute by style alone, at least two dark ages, one recovered ancient mind, a policy library with genuine compositional depth, and a Rosetta page that is the most interesting thing on your phone. You will have a favourite college and you will be annoyed when it loses a war.

Where it stops (self-reported)

A 4B model QLoRA'd on its own filtered outputs does not get meaningfully smarter at reasoning. It gets STYLISTICALLY AND TACTICALLY SPECIALISED, and past a few generations it degrades — model collapse is real, well-documented, and you should expect it rather than fight it. Design mitigations I would actually ship: always mix 30% base-era data into every finetune, keep the frozen held-out gate absolutely honest (never let it drift, never let an LLM judge it), and treat collapse as content by making it a dark age. But do not build this expecting a capability curve that goes up; the real deliverable is DIVERGENCE and SPECIALISATION, and if you are honest about that from day one it is a wonderful project, while if you are not you will be disappointed in month 2. Second honest limit: 8GB is genuinely tight, the twelve-polity weekly job is 6-12 hours, and the CUDA stack on Blackwell is the one place in these six ideas with a real maintenance tax. Third: the symbolic policy library is where the reliable depth lives, so if the weight loop disappoints, that layer keeps the project alive — build it first, not second. Fourth: everything is verifier-shaped, so if your mechanical scorer has an exploitable hole the colleges will find it and the chronicle becomes a record of reward hacking (which, admittedly, is a fascinating thing to read about, but it is not what you set out to build).

Stack

Unsloth for QLoRA (4-bit, Qwen3-4B or 1.7B base), CUDA 12.8+, PyTorch built for sm_120. llama.cpp server for inference with per-request LoRA adapter selection. GBNF grammar forcing every college output into `{decision, policy_program, justification}`. SQLite WAL for world state, decisions, eval history, and the adapter registry (path, parent adapter, polity, week, eval score — this table is the genealogy). bge-small for style embeddings. Adapters stored on disk with a hash-named directory layout, and yes, back them up, they are the actual artifact. PWA over Tailscale, GitHub Pages mirror with the prose only.

Verified

Verified: Unsloth now supports Blackwell including RTX 5070/5080/5090, with the well-documented requirement that sm_120 needs CUDA 12.8+ and all dependent libraries compiled against it, otherwise you get no-kernel-image errors (github.com/unslothai/unsloth issue #5266 is the practical setup thread; unsloth.ai/docs/models/tutorials/qwen3-how-to-run-and-fine-tune covers Qwen3 specifically, claiming ~2x faster training with ~70% less VRAM and longer context). QLoRA on 8GB is an explicitly supported configuration — 4-bit base plus small adapter matrices — and Unsloth shipped a desktop no-code tool in August 2026 targeting exactly the 8GB tier, which is a useful signal that this hardware class is a first-class target rather than a stretch. STaR-style rejection sampling with a mechanical verifier plus a held-out gate is the builder's own Ouroboros Forge design, which I am adopting rather than reinventing; what is new here is embedding it in a world so the checkpoint IS a polity's inherited mind and a discarded checkpoint IS a dark age. Voyager (arXiv 2305.16291, github.com/minedojo/voyager) is the precedent for the symbolic half: an ever-growing library of executable skills indexed by embeddings of their natural-language descriptions, with top-k retrieval into the prompt, and it demonstrated open-ended capability growth with NO gradient updates at all — which is precisely why I recommend building the policy-library layer first and treating the weight loop as the risky, more interesting bet layered on top. llama.cpp GBNF grammars keep every decision machine-parseable. On the style-divergence detector: ASAL's formulation of open-endedness as historical novelty in a representation space (arXiv 2412.17799) applies directly to prose embeddings, which is a cheap and unusually apt fit here.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The symbolic policy library — which the doc correctly names as where the reliable depth lives — saturates in 6-10 weeks, and the reason is a tension the doc never resolves: the verifier is 'deliberately boring' so it can be trusted, and a boring deterministic economy over ~7 levers has a low-dimensional optimum. Once the colleges find the neighbourhood of that optimum, new named policies are noise dressed as statecraft, and composition depth grows without capability growing (a policy invoking a policy invoking a policy that all reduce to 'store more grain'). The weight layer is honestly disclaimed as divergence-not-capability, and that disclaimer is correct, but it undersells one specific failure: the recovered-ancient-mind beat, which the doc calls the best story beat available in any of the six, essentially cannot fire. A damaged LoRA (random layers zeroed) merged at partial alpha into a different polity's model is overwhelmingly likely to degrade decision quality, and your own frozen held-out gate will correctly reject it. You have built a mechanism whose headline event your quality control is designed to prevent.

Is the discovery real?

Moderate on content, severe on a confound the doc misses entirely — and this is the finding I would most want the builder to see. Trajectories are scored mechanically at +20 in-world years on population/stores/territory, and the top quantile is kept for finetuning. But in a deterministic economy sim, the 20-year outcome of a state is dominated by that state's initial endowment, not by the decision taken from it. A rich polity scores well on almost any decision; a besieged one scores badly on all of them. So your filter selects for BEING A WINNER, not for DECIDING WELL, and the LoRA learns the prose register and tactical mannerisms of prosperous polities. That is a rich-get-richer style-transfer loop wearing the clothes of a STaR flywheel, and it will look like it is working — divergence, dialects, dark ages all still appear — while carrying almost no decision-quality signal. Everything downstream (the eval-score chart, the gate, the dark-age narrative) inherits the confound.

Compute honesty

The least honest of the six for 8GB, though not by fabrication — by omission of the inference side. QLoRA on Qwen3-4B at 2048 ctx, rank 16-32, bs1 + grad checkpointing genuinely fits ~6-7GB, and 30-60 min per polity-week × 12 = 6-12h weekly is a plausible training estimate. What is missing: generating the training data. Every decision is a grammar-constrained 4B generation of a few hundred tokens at 40-80 tok/s, i.e. 4-8s each. To get even 300-500 usable examples per polity per week you need several thousand generations weekly — another 6-12 GPU-hours, on the same single 8GB card, plus 200 held-out states re-scored per polity per gate, plus rolling the sim forward 20 years per scored trajectory. Realistically the GPU is saturated most of the week and the weekly job is a full weekend, not a Saturday night. Two smaller notes: LoRA adapters at rank 16-32 on attention projections of a 4B are ~20-60MB, not 50-200MB; and llama.cpp per-request adapter selection is supported but historically finicky against quantized bases and adapter GGUF conversion — budget real time for it. The doc's own 'CUDA stack maintenance tax' warning is the right instinct applied to the wrong layer.

Nonsense risk

Low for nonsense, high for self-deception. Grammar-constrained decisions plus a deterministic sim means outputs are always well-formed and always adjudicated, so you will not get drivel. What you will get is prose that reads as though a mind is developing when what is developing is a stylistic attractor fit to the winners' distribution. Model collapse is real (recursive training on filtered self-output degrades diversity), the 30%-base-data mitigation matches what the literature recommends, and reframing collapse as a dark age is genuinely good design. But the combination of 'prose that visibly diverges' plus 'a scoring chart that mostly tracks polity wealth' is a machine for convincing its author that capability is growing. Also flag the reward-hacking risk the doc raises and then waves at: with everything verifier-shaped and a boring economy, exploitable holes will be found, and 'a record of reward hacking' is not an acceptable fallback product.

Claims to re-check

(a) 'Unsloth shipped a desktop no-code tool in August 2026' — post-dates what I can verify; treat as unconfirmed, and note that it is being used as evidence that 8GB is a first-class target, which is a load-bearing inference from an unverified fact. (b) 'github.com/unslothai/unsloth issue #5266' — specific issue numbers are the most commonly confabulated citation form; verify before relying on it. The underlying claim (Blackwell/sm_120 requires CUDA 12.8+ and matching builds, else no-kernel-image errors) is correct. (c) Unsloth's '~2x faster, ~70% less VRAM' are vendor marketing figures, benchmark-dependent, and should not be planned against. (d) Voyager (arXiv 2305.16291) is correctly cited and the doc draws exactly the right lesson from it — that open-ended skill growth was achieved with NO gradient updates, which is an argument that the weight layer is the optional part. Follow that lesson harder than the doc does. (e) The benchmark-hygiene advice (`nvidia-smi -lgc`, interleaved A/B, score on decision quality not wall-clock) is correct and unusually sophisticated.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Replace return-based trajectory scoring with ADVANTAGE-based scoring: for each decision point, roll the seed-locked sim forward twice — once with the taken decision, once with a cheap baseline decision from the same state — and train only on the difference. This kills the winner-selection confound, makes the eval chart mean what you think it means, converts the dark-age gate from a wealth proxy into a real capability test, and it costs sim time (which is cheap) rather than GPU time (which is not). Build the policy-library layer first as the doc advises, and treat the weight loop as an experiment you are willing to delete.

▸

Apocrypha

Villages copy each other's rituals imperfectly, nobody understands why any of them work, and the simulation underneath quietly computes that boiling before drinking actually does kill the thing in the water — so useful superstition is SELECTED FOR rather than designed. The artifact is a critical edition of the resulting scripture: variant readings, apocrypha, marginalia, and a critical apparatus showing exactly which copying error became doctrine.

Ceilinged7
The world

A river valley, 40-120 villages along a branching hydrology, several centuries at seasonal ticks. The physical layer is unglamorous and rigorous: a waterborne pathogen model with an actual R0 that depends on water source, sanitation, and contact structure; a seasonal shellfish toxin bloom; a soil-fertility model; a food-storage spoilage model tied to temperature and moisture; a childbirth mortality model. Villages have populations, kin networks, prestige rankings, and a CANON — an ordered list of rituals they perform, when, and who is exempt. Nothing in the world understands any of this. The people only know what they do and whether their children lived.

How invention actually happens

Cultural evolution over EXECUTABLE RITUAL PROGRAMS, with latent utility discovered by selection rather than by design. A RITUAL is a short program in a tiny stack DSL over village actions: `boil`, `wait(n)`, `bury(depth)`, `separate(group)`, `wash(part)`, `burn(thing)`, `abstain(food, season)`, `count(n)`, `face(direction)`, `sing(n)`, `offer(thing)`, `touch(person)`, `sequence`, `repeat`. Roughly half the primitives have real physical consequences in the sim and half are pure noise — and the system never tells you which, because the villagers cannot tell either. `boil` kills waterborne pathogens. `separate(sick)` cuts effective contacts. `abstain(shellfish, summer)` avoids the bloom. `bury(deep)` reduces contamination of the water table. `sing(7)` does absolutely nothing, but if it happens to sit inside a ritual whose other steps work, it comes along for the ride forever. That last sentence is the entire thesis of the project and it is the actual mechanism by which real human cultures accumulated adaptive nonsense. TRANSMISSION with error: children learn from parents (fidelity ~0.97 per step), apprentices from elders, and villages from prestigious neighbours. Copy errors are insertions, deletions, swaps, and parameter drift. Aggregation is conformist-biased (copy the most common variant among teachers) with a prestige-bias override (copy the most successful village), which are the two transmission biases the cultural-evolution literature actually identifies. SELECTION: no village optimises anything. Villages that survive and prosper get copied; their rituals spread. Adaptive value is discovered by differential survival, exactly as in the Amazonian manioc-detoxification case. COMPOUNDING: a ritual that becomes widespread and named is promoted to a callable MACRO in the DSL, so later rituals can contain it by name. That is a lightweight library-learning move and it is what stops this from being a flat search — canon depth grows, and a ritual can eventually be four levels of named sub-rites deep, with only the innermost one doing anything. NON-STATIONARITY: pathogens MUTATE. A strain that boiling handled is replaced by one transmitted by contact, and a canon perfectly adapted to the old world becomes maladaptive without anyone noticing for two generations. There is no final optimum, ever.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Program space over a stack DSL with a growing macro table is formally unbounded and compounds, the same argument as Palimpsest but smaller and cheaper. More importantly the FITNESS LANDSCAPE is non-stationary by construction: mutating pathogens, drifting climate, changing settlement density and changing contact networks mean the target keeps moving, so cultural evolution never converges and the canon keeps churning. And the third source, which is the reason this idea is on the list: the INTERPRETIVE layer. A critical edition of a text that keeps mutating produces new scholarly content (variant collation, stemma reconstruction, disputed readings) at a rate proportional to the mutation rate, independent of whether anything adaptive is happening.

The ladder

WEEK 1: 10 villages, 6 primitives, one pathogen, copying with error. Watch `boil` reach fixation in the surviving villages without anyone selecting for it. That takes about 200 simulated years and it is a genuinely moving thing to watch happen. WEEK 2: the critical apparatus — collate variants across villages, render a base text with footnoted readings. Now it looks like a real edition. WEEK 3: hitchhiking noise. A useless step fixes because it is adjacent to a useful one. The apparatus can show you exactly which village and which generation it entered. MONTH 1: macros and named rites; canon depth grows; a stemma (family tree of textual variants) can be reconstructed from the variants alone and compared against the true transmission history — and it will be WRONG in the same interesting ways real stemmatics is wrong. MONTH 2: pathogen mutation invalidates a core rite. Some villages have a variant that happens to work against the new strain; it spreads; the old orthodoxy becomes heresy. Schism. MONTH 3: the guestbook. Strangers' lines become omen texts, some get canonised, and the apparatus starts footnoting real humans. ENDPOINT: a canon of dozens of named rites, five or six sects with mutually incompatible recensions, an apocrypha of texts nobody accepts, a reconstructed stemma, and an appendix in which the system reveals which steps ever actually did anything — a page you should be able to toggle and which will surprise you every single time.

What kills you

Child mortality, mostly, which is the honest and slightly brutal answer. Waterborne disease, food poisoning, spoilage, and epidemic dynamics kill people, and villages with maladaptive canons shrink and get copied less. Prestige bias means a village that got lucky is copied even if its canon is neutral, which introduces exactly the right amount of drift and is a real phenomenon. Between sects: no direct violence needed at first, just differential growth — though schism plus resource competition eventually produces one, and a sect that wins a war imposes its recension, which is how a canon gets forcibly standardised.

How the world changes

Three valleys with different disease ecologies, different hydrology, and different toxin regimes — so the same DSL produces three unrelated religions with different sacred logic, and the cross-valley comparison page ('all three independently sanctified boiling; only the delta valley sanctified separation of the sick') is the best reading in the project. Within a valley: pathogen evolution, a slow climate trend that changes which foods are dangerous when, and a mid-history flood that destroys the upstream villages and their recensions, creating a textual gap that later scribes fill by conjecture. Public visitors add a fourth axis of change that is genuinely outside the system.

How it knows something is new

The single best notification in this entire set is available here and it is precisely definable: A COPYING ERROR THAT BECAME AN INNOVATION. Formally, a mutation event whose resulting variant has a measured fitness effect above 20% (computed by seeded counterfactual replay of that village's next 50 years with and without the mutation) AND which subsequently spread to three or more villages. That is a mutation becoming an adaptation, dated, located, and attributable, and it is worth waking up for. Supporting channels: (2) SCHISM — a canon's variant graph splits into components with no recent transmission between them; (3) FIXATION — a rite reaching universality; (4) LOSS — a rite with positive fitness going extinct (tragedy, and the most affecting category); (5) CANONISATION OF AN OMEN — a visitor's guestbook line entering a canon; (6) ASAL-style embedding novelty over the canon text as a catch-all. Hard push budget of two per day, and the top channel almost always wins, which is correct.

On your phone

A single verse, set as scripture, with a footnote. 'Rite of the Long Water, v.4 — Before the vessel is given to the child, count seven and let it steam. [Var.: three villages read "count nine"; the reading "let it stand" is attested only at Ashmouth and is not adaptive.]' Below: a one-line status — villages alive, sects, canon length, and whether tonight was a schism. Thirty seconds and you have read something beautiful. The hour version: the full critical edition with apparatus, the stemma diagram, the concordance, the sect comparison, the apocrypha, the guestbook-to-scripture trail, and the toggle that reveals which steps were ever physically real.

Your hardware

Delightfully cheap — CPU-only for everything that matters. The whole valley sim is small-integer population dynamics and short program execution; it runs thousands of years per minute in plain Python and would go absurdly fast in numpy or a small Rust core if you ever cared. 64GB RAM is irrelevant here; this would run on a phone. The GPU is used only for the prose layer and the embedding-based novelty channel: Qwen3-4B Q4_K_M for the scribal voice on a handful of passages a night, well inside 8GB, maybe five minutes of GPU time. The remaining 23 hours 55 minutes of the day the machine is free — which makes this the ideal project to run ALONGSIDE one of the heavier ideas on the same laptop, sharing the substrate.

What you have to do

None. The visitor layer supplies the perturbation that would otherwise require you. Optional: add a physical primitive to the DSL every couple of months (`filter(cloth)`, `smoke(meat)`) and watch whether anyone ever finds it. Watching a genuinely useful primitive go undiscovered for four hundred years is its own kind of pleasure.

First weekend

Saturday: the pathogen and water model, 10 villages, the ritual DSL interpreter, transmission with copy error. Run 300 years and check whether `boil` fixed. If it did not, tune mortality until it does — that tuning session IS the design work and it is fascinating. Saturday night: variant collation and a plain-text critical apparatus in the terminal. Sunday: the scribal prose layer, the SVG stemma, and a static page. The delightful moment is Saturday afternoon, no LLM involved, when you diff the canons of the ten surviving villages and find that they all independently converged on a step you never told them was good for them.

Six months in

Three valleys, three religions, a dozen sects, a canon of 40-80 named rites with genuine compositional depth, a critical edition running to thousands of words with a real apparatus, an apocrypha, a stemma that is wrong in instructive ways, a handful of your friends' guestbook jokes now embedded in scripture with commentary, and a radio station of generative plainchant that changes when a sect schisms. This is the idea most likely to still be delighting you at month 12 and it is also the cheapest to build.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The action primitives are authored, so this never invents a new PHYSICAL capability — it will not discover metallurgy or writing, only which combinations of a fixed set of gestures become sacred and why. The depth is entirely in the interpretive and cultural layer, and you should choose this idea only if that is the depth you want (it is a very good kind of depth, but it is not 'cavemen eventually build technology'). Second limit: with ~14 primitives the effective ritual space is large but the number of genuinely ADAPTIVE combinations is small — probably a few dozen — so after month 3 you are watching drift, hitchhiking, schism and reinterpretation rather than fresh adaptive discovery. Mitigations that genuinely help: keep adding primitives, make pathogens evolve fast enough that adaptation is continuous, and let macros compound so canon depth keeps growing. Third: this is the idea most dependent on the quality of the prose layer, because the sim's output is small and the writing is most of the experience — so budget effort for the anti-repetition machinery, and be prepared to spend a few dollars a month on Claude Haiku 4.5 for the passages you actually read. Fourth: the visitor layer will be empty for months unless you tell people about it, and an empty guestbook is a sad page — design it to look intentional when unvisited.

Stack

Python sim (or a tiny Rust core if you want 10^6 village-years). SQLite WAL: `ritual` (program text, canonical hash, macro table), `variant`, `transmission_event`, `village`, `canon`, `omen`, `claim`. The critical apparatus is a SQL query, which is a genuinely satisfying thing to discover. Stemma rendering as SVG. llama.cpp + Qwen3-4B under GBNF for the scribal prose, constrained so every rendered sentence carries the ritual/variant IDs it is describing and is rejected if it names anything not in the row set. Anti-repetition: a rolling `published_ngram` table of every 5-gram ever emitted, with generations rejected below a novel-5-gram threshold, plus per-entity used-phrase lists injected as negative constraints, plus a rotating cast of named in-world scribes each with a style card, era, and bias — so voice changes are structural rather than random. Sonification via Strudel or plain Web Audio: the canon's structure drives generative plainchant, one voice per sect, rhythm from the ritual calendar — a browser tab you leave on. Public site on GitHub Pages with a guestbook (a GitHub issue, a Cloudflare Worker, or an ntfy topic read back — anything with no server to maintain), private full edition over Tailscale.

Verified

Verified and directly load-bearing: the cultural-evolution mechanism here (adaptive practices accumulating through imitation and differential survival without anyone understanding the causal mechanism, with conformist and prestige transmission biases) is the core of Henrich's work and the manioc-detoxification case is its canonical example — this idea is a faithful executable model of a real, well-supported theory rather than a game mechanic I invented. Voyager (arXiv 2305.16291) supplies the macro-promotion pattern: successful code stored, retrieved by embedding, reused as a primitive. On making the generated text hold up for months, the practical findings from the data-to-text literature are what shape the tech stack above: neural models tend to hallucinate and REPEAT THEMSELVES and struggle to stay faithful to input, the standard remedy is separating content selection and planning from surface realisation, and hand-crafted templates give strict anti-hallucination guarantees at the cost of sounding robotic (hence the hybrid: templates for the 80% you skim, constrained LLM prose with mandatory row-ID citation for the 20% you read). llama.cpp's GBNF/JSON-schema constrained decoding makes the citation requirement enforceable rather than aspirational. James Ryan's 'Curating Simulated Storyworlds' (UCSC 2018; emshort.blog coverage) is the definitive treatment of the sifting problem this design's novelty detector addresses, and Talk of the Town — 200 years of day-by-day small-town simulation with an emphasis on characters' internal lives and knowledge phenomena (Game AI Pro 3 ch.37, gameaipro.com) — is the closest existing relative of the transmission-with-error model, since its knowledge propagation is explicitly lossy and mutable. ASAL (arXiv 2412.17799) for the catch-all novelty channel. Sonification: Strudel (the JS/web port of TidalCycles) is live and browser-native, and there is an active ecosystem of Web Audio data-sonification projects (github.com/topics/data-sonification) including real-time map-to-soundscape work, so the generative-radio layer is a weekend rather than a research project.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

This will absolutely work and will stop being about discovery around month 2-3. Apply the acid test: can the designer enumerate every discoverable fact in an afternoon? Here, yes — ~14 primitives, roughly half functional, crossed with a handful of hazards gives on the order of 20-50 adaptive truths, and you could write the exhaustive list before you finish the first weekend. After the first few months the system is not discovering; it is cycling — the same small set of adaptations found, hitchhiked onto, lost, and rediscovered under new names, forever. That is a bounded orbit, and it is genuinely re-readable content (schism, loss, and rediscovery do not get old the way novelty does), but it is not open-endedness, and the doc's honest_ceiling is right to say so. One point the doc gets wrong in its own favour: macro promotion is offered as the compounding mechanism, but nesting a four-level rite whose innermost step is the only functional one adds depth to the TEXT and none to the technology. That is decorative compounding, and the critical apparatus will make it look like the real thing. Pathogen mutation genuinely prevents convergence, but churn is not growth — it resets the same landscape rather than extending it.

Is the discovery real?

Low at the level of outcomes, total at the level of the fact set. Which primitives are functional, and what they do, is a table you wrote; the villagers' ignorance of it is real and mechanically enforced, and the fixation of `boil` without anyone selecting for it is a genuine emergent event, not a reveal. That is the cleanest 'real discovery' demonstration on the entire list, and it happens on day one with no LLM. But the space of things that CAN be discovered is a finite authored table, so after the first pass through it, every subsequent event is a re-derivation. The saving grace, and it is a real one: the interpretive product (variant collation, stemma, disputed readings, apocrypha) is generated by the mutation process rather than by the adaptation process, so text keeps accruing even when the adaptive landscape is exhausted. Be clear-eyed that this is the same trade Strata makes — epistemic depth substituting for technological depth — and that it does not answer the builder's stated brief about cavemen and fire.

Compute honesty

The most honest of the six, no caveats. Small-integer population dynamics plus short program execution runs thousands of village-years per minute in plain Python; the GPU is touched for a few minutes a night; 8GB is irrelevant and so is 64GB of RAM. The doc's observation that this should be run ALONGSIDE a heavier project on the same laptop is correct and is the most practically useful sentence in the six documents. The only cost that is underestimated is developer time on the prose layer, which the doc identifies as the dependency but treats as tuning rather than as the main build.

Nonsense risk

Low for the simulation — ritual programs are executable and their effects are computed, so nothing can be nonsense in the way the Gazette's items can. Moderate for the prose over a long horizon, and the specific risk is the anti-repetition machinery itself. A rolling table of every 5-gram ever emitted plus a novel-5-gram threshold plus per-entity used-phrase negative constraints is a monotonically tightening constraint set over a fixed small subject matter; by month 4 the rejection sampler will either stall (exponential retries) or force synonym churn that reads as strained rather than fresh. Rotating scribes with style cards is the right structural answer and should carry more of the load than the n-gram filter; also let the filter EXPIRE old n-grams (a 90-day window, not all-time), which the doc does not specify. Note also that mandatory row-ID citation guarantees referential integrity, not truthfulness, same caveat as Strata.

Claims to re-check

The most accurate research notes of the six. Henrich's account of adaptive cultural practices accumulating without causal understanding, the Amazonian manioc detoxification case, and the conformist/prestige transmission bias pair are all correctly characterised and genuinely load-bearing — this design is a faithful executable model of a supported theory rather than a game mechanic. Voyager (2305.16291), Strudel as the browser-native TidalCycles descendant, Talk of the Town / Game AI Pro 3 ch.37, and Ryan's dissertation are all correctly cited. Two soft flags: (a) ASAL 'Artificial Life 31(3), 2025' journal placement is unverified across all six documents; (b) the data-to-text generalisation that neural models 'tend to hallucinate and repeat themselves' is directionally right but drawn from an older literature than the constrained-decoding setup you are actually building, so do not over-plan against it. One design-level flag rather than a citation flag: 'fidelity ~0.97 per step' is per-STEP, so a 15-step rite mutates on ~36% of transmissions — check that this is the churn rate you intend, because it sets the entire pace of the project.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Give the physical layer a generated, compositional substrate so the adaptive fact set stops being enumerable. Graft Reliquary's or Palimpsest's procedurally generated materials-and-reactions layer underneath the ritual DSL: `boil`, `filter`, `ferment`, `smoke`, `bury` then operate on generated substances with generated properties, and produce new substances that later rites can act on. The cultural machinery — hitchhiking, conformist bias, prestige bias, schism, stemma, critical apparatus — is already correct and stays untouched; what changes is that it now has an unbounded supply of real things to sanctify, and the acid test flips from 'enumerable in an afternoon' to 'not enumerable at all'. This is the smallest change on the list with the largest ceiling gain, because Apocrypha's weakness is entirely in its substrate and entirely not in its mechanism.

Wildcards

Unusual substrates for a civilization

▸

Cinderloom

Civilizations are colonies of bytecode programs living in a shared memory soup, paying for every action in CPU cycles. Each night a compressor mines the survivors' genomes and mints the most-compressive repeated subsequence as a new named opcode that costs fewer cycles to run — so an invention is literally a cheaper way to be alive, and the instruction set itself evolves.

Ceilinged
The world

"The Loom": a 256MB flat byte-addressed memory, tiled into a 2D lattice of regions so locality matters (writing to a distant cell costs more cycles). Each cell holds `ore` (a scalar that regenerates slowly and is consumed by writes) which determines the region's cycle income. An agent ("wright") is a program: a genome of opcodes, an instruction pointer, a small private stack, a cycle purse. A tribe is a lineage sharing a **codex** — its private table of minted macro-opcodes occupying the free half of the 256-opcode space. Resources: cycles (the only true currency), ore, contiguous free memory, and codex slots. Death = purse hits zero, or another wright overwrites your genome. There is no map art; the world IS the memory, rendered as a 512x512 heatmap.

How invention actually happens

Two-phase, wake/sleep, ported from DreamCoder into an ecology. WAKE (continuous, all day): replication with mutation (point, indel, duplication) and recombination on contact. Selection pressure comes from two sources: (1) self-replication speed under a cycle tax proportional to genome length; (2) **task bounties** — each region emits a byte stream and pays cycles to any wright that writes the correct transform of it into a designated output cell. Tasks are not a fixed list: they are sampled from a grammar over stream operators (parity, run-length, sort, xor-fold, delta, dedup, checksum, compose(f,g)) so the bounty space is itself combinatorial and re-rolls on world shudders. SLEEP (nightly, ~20 min): the **librarian**. Dump every surviving genome per tribe into a corpus. Run MDL abstraction learning over it — either Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, Rust, top-down synthesis, POPL 2023) if you lift genomes into an s-expression IR, or, simpler for weekend one, byte-level BPE / suffix-automaton mining scored by `gain = (occurrences - 1) * (len - 1)`. Any candidate above threshold is minted: it gets a free opcode byte in that tribe's codex, a name (`OP_7A`, later a Qwen3-4B-generated word), and a **cycle discount**: executing the macro costs `ceil(expansion_cost * (1 - d))` where d rises with compression yield, capped ~0.4. Mutation can now emit that single byte where 14 bytes were needed. Data structures: `soup: Uint8Array`, `wrights: struct-of-arrays (ip, purse, genome_ptr, genome_len, tribe_id)`, `codex: {tribe_id -> [ {opcode, expansion: bytes, cost, minted_at, parent_macros[] } ]}`, `macro_genealogy: DAG` (which macros are built out of which — this DAG is the tech tree, and you didn't write it), SQLite event log. Codex slots are scarce (128 of them). Eviction is by usage-weighted MDL, so tribes must *specialize*, which makes codices diverge, which makes tribes mutually unintelligible, which makes contact interesting: trade = codex-entry exchange (paid for in cycles); war = overwriting each other's genome regions.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Program space over a 256-symbol alphabet is infinite, but that alone is a random walk. The compounding argument is: **each minted macro changes the language the search operates in.** After minting M, the set of programs reachable within a cycle budget C is strictly larger than before — mutation now reaches in one edit what previously took fourteen. That is niche construction applied to the search space itself, and it has no fixed point: macros built of macros build macros. The macro-genealogy DAG grows monotonically in depth as long as the task grammar keeps supplying problems whose solutions share structure, and the task grammar is compositional (compose(f,g) is itself an operator), so it does. Nothing in the system contains a list of technologies; a technology is a byte sequence the system found, named, and discounted.

The ladder

Week 1: a hand-written self-replicator seeded into the soup; mutants take over; you watch genome length shrink to the minimum viable copier. First macro minted is boring and correct — it's the copy loop. Week 2-3: task bounties on. Wrights that solve `parity` stop dying in winter. First macro that is *obviously* a useful primitive nobody wrote (a bounded memcpy, an accumulator loop). You read its disassembly with coffee. This is the delight moment. Month 2: parasites appear — genomes that jump into a neighbor's code and use its cycles. Then immunity (checksum-guarded entry points), then hyperparasites. Classic Tierra, but now with codices, so parasites specialize per-codex and can't cross tribes. Month 3: codex divergence produces genuine dialects; trade emerges because importing a foreign macro is cheaper than evolving it. Macro-genealogy depth 4-6. Month 4-6: strata. A collapse event wipes 90% of the soup and freezes the dead region read-only; survivors that evolve a read-from-stratum opcode can *excavate* extinct codices. Archaeology as a technology. Wildest plausible endpoint: a tribe whose codex contains a macro that mints macros — a wright that runs the librarian on itself in-world, at cycle cost. Endogenous science. I rate this a 20% shot, not a promise.

What kills you

Cycles are the only currency and they are strictly conserved per region. A wright with an empty purse halts and its memory becomes free real estate. Ore depletion forces migration. Parasites drain cycles from hosts. Overwrite wars destroy genomes outright. Codex incompatibility means a conquered tribe's code is often useless to the conqueror unless they also take the codex — so conquest without translation is worthless, which creates a reason for the analogue of scribes. Longer genomes pay a strictly higher tax, which is a permanent pressure toward compression and therefore toward using macros.

How the world changes

Three timescales. (1) **Shudders** (every ~6 sim-hours): ore redistributes, some regions go barren; the task grammar re-rolls a fraction of bounties, so yesterday's optimal specialization is today's liability. (2) **Collapses** (rare, ~weekly): 90% wipe, dead memory frozen into a read-only stratum. Civilizations restart on top of their own fossil record. (3) **Multiple soups**: run 6-8 independent Looms in parallel (each ~256MB, trivial in 64GB) with different base ISAs and task grammars, joined by a "gate" that migrates exactly one genome per night in one direction. The cross-soup comparison is itself the meta-game: which base ISA breeds the deepest macro genealogy?

How it knows something is new

Four channels feeding one nightly ranked push. (1) **First-ever event signatures**: every event gets a canonical signature string; a SQLite table + bloom filter of all signatures ever seen. Anything unseen is a candidate. Cheap and catches genuine firsts. (2) **Mint surprise**: a minted macro whose compression gain enters the all-time top-10, or whose genealogy depth exceeds the previous record. (3) **Soup MDL**: gzip the whole soup nightly. A sharp drop in compressed size = the population learned something; a sharp rise = diversification. Report the delta z-score against a 30-day EWMA. (4) **Clade shift**: any lineage crossing 20% population share for the first time, or a codex-Jaccard between two tribes crossing 0.5 → 0.2 (speciation). Each candidate gets a surprise score = -log(prior rate under a Poisson EWMA per channel). Only the top 3 per night go to ntfy; everything else lands in the PWA feed. This is what stops thousands of nightly events from becoming noise.

On your phone

Hero: the 512x512 soup heatmap as a PNG (color = tribe, brightness = cycle throughput) — instantly legible, changes visibly night to night. Under it, three sparklines: population, macro-genealogy max depth, distinct codices alive. Then the **Codex Diff card**: the 1-3 opcodes minted last night, each showing its disassembly, compression gain, and the first wright that used it. Tap a macro to see its genealogy subtree. Thirty seconds is the heatmap + diff card; an hour is walking the genealogy DAG and reading disassembly.

Your hardware

CPU-only by design, and that is a feature. A hand-written VM in Rust (or C, or even JS typed arrays for the prototype) runs 50-200M virtual instructions/sec/thread; 12 threads x 24h is trillions of instructions per night. The real resource is the 64GB: 8 soups x 256MB plus event history. **The 5070 is deliberately idle** — spend it on a Qwen3-4B (Q4, ~3GB VRAM) that reads the night's event JSON and writes the chronicle prose and names the new opcodes. Nothing about the science depends on the GPU, so Blackwell/sm_120 CUDA wheel headaches never block you. Benchmark hygiene, solved cleanly: **fitness is denominated in virtual cycles, never wall-clock.** Thermal throttling changes how fast the night runs, never who wins. Seeded PRNG + full event log means any night is bit-exactly replayable.

What you have to do

Zero required. Optional god-nudges, one tap each from the PWA: force a shudder, open/close a migration gate between two soups, freeze a tribe's codex ("a dark age"), or inject a hand-written genome as a "prophet". Realistically you touch it when you want to run an experiment, maybe weekly. Real maintenance is disk (event log rotation) — automate it day one.

First weekend

Saturday: 32-opcode VM (nop, mov, add, sub, jmp, jz, load, store, alloc, copy, split, halt...) over a 4MB soup with per-cell ore; hand-write one 40-byte self-replicator; add point mutation on copy; render the soup to canvas at 10fps. You will watch the replicator take over and then get shorter. Sunday: the librarian, naive version — BPE over the top-200 genomes by fitness, mint the top-scoring subsequence into a codex with a 25% cycle discount, log it. Push one ntfy per mint. By Sunday night your phone has told you the name of a technology you did not write. That is the whole thesis, running.

Six months in

Eight soups with divergent ISAs, macro genealogies 6-10 deep, a parasite/immunity arms race, archaeology strata from four collapses, and a public PWA showing eight tech trees that nobody authored side by side. You will be tuning the cycle cost model and the task grammar, not writing new features — which is either heaven or boredom depending on your temperament. The genealogy DAG print-out (receipt printer, nightly) is a genuinely lovely physical object.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The known failure mode of this whole family (Tierra, 1991 onward) is that complexity stalls: the soup converges to short degenerate replicators plus a stable parasite ecology, and nothing further happens. Avida's fix — external task bounties — is exactly why I put the task grammar in, and it works, but it also means **the depth of your civilizations is bounded by the richness of your task grammar**, which you wrote. That is the honest bottom. Expect: ~30-80 real abstractions per tribe, genealogy depth 6-10, and a plateau at month 3-4 unless you keep expanding the operator set. You will not get spaceships. You will get a self-authored tech tree of computational primitives, an arms race, and archaeology — and I think that is genuinely enough for a year of coffee.

Stack

Rust VM + rusqlite (or Node + better-sqlite3 if you want to stay in JS for the prototype; the VM is ~600 lines either way). Stitch (Rust binary, github.com/mlb2251/stitch) invoked as a subprocess for the librarian once you lift to an IR; plain BPE mining in numpy before that. SQLite WAL for the event/lineage DB. Static PWA (vanilla JS + canvas) reading a nightly `snapshot.json` + PNGs, served by Tailscale Serve from the laptop and mirrored to GitHub Pages for the public copy. ntfy.sh topic for push (docs.ntfy.sh, iOS/Android/PWA). Optional: Ollama + Qwen3-4B for names and chronicle prose; optional Claude Haiku 4.5 for a weekly deep-dive report (~50K tokens in / 3K out ≈ $0.07, batched 50% off — negligible).

Verified

Verified: Tierra and Avida lineage (alife.org/encyclopedia/digital-evolution/avida/) — Tierra = shared soup, competition for one CPU; Avida = private memory + logic-task rewards, which is precisely the anti-stall mechanism. Sakana AI's "Digital Red Queen: Adversarial Program Evolution in Core War with LLMs" (pub.sakana.ai/drq/, arXiv 2601.03335, GECCO 2026, doi 10.1145/3795095.3805116) confirms program-vs-program VM ecologies are live research in 2026, not a dead 90s topic. Stitch (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, arXiv:2211.16605, POPL 2023) is a real, maintained Rust abstraction-learning library reported 3-4 orders of magnitude faster and 2 orders lighter on memory than DreamCoder's compression step — this is what makes a *nightly* librarian pass feasible on a laptop. DreamCoder itself: PLDI 2021 (dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3453483.3454080) and Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A 381:20220050. ntfy.sh (docs.ntfy.sh) has iOS/Android apps + PWA and documented self-host-behind-Tailscale setups. Claude Haiku 4.5 is $1/$5 per MTok with 200K context, Batch API is 50% off, cache reads ~0.1x — verified against the bundled claude-api reference (cached 2026-06-24). No GPU dependency means the RTX 5070's sm_120 wheel situation (PyTorch issue #164342; cu128 nightlies needed) is irrelevant here.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Two hard stops, both earlier than claimed. (1) The librarian's corpus is a population of near-clones — selection destroys genetic diversity, so MDL/BPE mining over surviving genomes converges on minting the incumbent champion's own body as one opcode. The cycle discount then makes the incumbent nearly free to run, locking it in. That's a positive feedback that rewards conformity and kills search; without per-clade mining plus a lineage-diversity penalty you hit it in week 2-4. (2) Assume you fix that: the task grammar is the content, and compositional task spaces are infinite in cardinality but shallow in difficulty. Once the codex holds solutions for the ~8 base stream operators, every compose(f,g) bounty is one or two edits away — by construction, since composition is itself a primitive. Marginal information per new macro goes to ~zero past genealogy depth 3-4. Realistic plateau: week 6-10, not month 3-4. Depth 6-10 is achievable but depths 5+ will be 'parity-macro ∘ delta-macro', real but not a new kind of thing. Parasites/immunity (Tierra, 1991) will show up and are the most reliable content here; they are also 35 years old and stop where Tierra stopped. The claimed endpoint (a macro that mints macros) is not 20%; the librarian is an out-of-band nightly batch process with no in-world instruction that could invoke it, so it requires a feature you'd have to author — call it 3%.

Is the discovery real?

Medium-low on mechanism, high on relevance. Nobody writes the byte sequences: those are genuinely found, and the macro-genealogy DAG is honestly not hand-authored. But nontrivial macros exist *only* because bounties exist, and the bounty operator set is authored — this is Avida, where 20+ years of running never produced anything outside the authored logic-task list. So: real discovery of solutions to an authored problem list. Separately, the brief's actual demand (fire → houses → tools) is never met even in principle. Cinderloom's 'technologies' are memcpy loops and accumulators; there is no mapping to civilization at any point, and the Qwen-generated names are the only thing supplying that illusion. A bigger unadvertised gap: BPE/suffix-automaton macros are *literal substrings* — no holes, no parameters. A non-parameterized macro doesn't change the language, it just compresses one path; reuse requires exact byte match. The 'each minted macro enlarges the reachable set' argument is only true for abstractions with variables, i.e. Stitch. And the Stitch route requires lifting a linear bytecode genome with arbitrary jumps into an s-expression IR — that is a decompiler, not a weekend, and it is quietly the load-bearing step of the entire why_unbounded section.

Compute honesty

Honest, and the most honest of the six. A simple Rust/C VM at 30-200M instr/s/thread is realistic; 12 threads overnight is genuinely trillions of virtual instructions. 8×256MB soups fit trivially. GPU deliberately idle, so the whole Blackwell risk surface is genuinely zero. Cycle-denominated fitness plus seeded PRNG plus full event log gives real replayability — the only project here with proper benchmark hygiene stated. Nightly librarian: BPE over a few hundred genomes is seconds; Stitch is fast, when you have an IR you don't have. No API bill worth naming.

Nonsense risk

Low in the mechanics — every minted macro is executable code that survived selection, so it means *something* even if that something is 'a bounded memcpy'. Risk is entirely in the presentation layer: Qwen3-4B naming opcodes and writing chronicle prose will produce confident mythology over what is, semantically, a compression dictionary. The phone view will read as a civilization while the substrate is an ISA. Keep the disassembly on the card (they do) and it stays honest.

Claims to re-check

The Sakana 'Digital Red Queen' citation (pub.sakana.ai/drq/, arXiv 2601.03335, GECCO 2026, doi 10.1145/3795095.3805116) is the single most suspicious reference in all six documents — I cannot verify it, and a fully-formed ACM DOI plus arXiv ID plus proceedings venue for a 2026 conference is the exact shape of a confabulated citation. Treat as nonexistent until you personally load the URL.
