Stitch's 3-4 orders of magnitude speedup over DreamCoder's compression step is roughly what the paper claims — fine, but it describes abstraction *with* variables, which is not what BPE does, so the citation doesn't support the plan you'd actually build. Avida/Tierra framing is correct. Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per MTok with 50% batch and ~0.1× cache reads matches what I know; cost estimates are right. Unverified elsewhere-repeated claim: PyTorch issue #164342 and 'CUDA 12.8/12.9 shipped a missing libnvptxcompiler.so' — irrelevant here since no GPU, but see the other four.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Mine abstractions *with holes*, not literal substrings, and mine them per-clade with a relatedness penalty. Concretely: antiunify pairs of high-fitness genome fragments to get parameterized macros (opcode + operand slots), and score candidates by gain across *independent lineages*, not raw occurrence count. This kills the mint-the-champion collapse and is the only version in which 'each macro enlarges the reachable program set' is actually true. It is also strictly cheaper than the Stitch/IR route you'd otherwise be blocked on.

▸

Saltwright

An archipelago whose bottom layer is a real artificial chemistry: matter is bond-graphs, and a material's hardness, toughness, conductivity and color are *computed* from graph structure, never tabulated. Tribes invent by searching process-space, and because the property map is simulated rather than authored, the value of a novel material is genuinely unknown until someone makes it and breaks something with it.

Ceilinged
The world

"The Bitter Coast": a handful of islands with distinct mineral deposits, tides, and weather. Physics substrate is a bond-graph artificial chemistry: 24 element types, each with (valence, mass, radius, an electronegativity-analog chi, a magnetic-moment-analog), plus a 24x24 pairwise bond-energy matrix — **all of it generated from the world seed.** Matter is a labelled multigraph of atoms; a "substance" is an isomorphism class plus a mass. Agents are small tribes of cheap scripted-but-evolving people (needs: food, warmth, tools, shelter; no LLM in the loop). Resources: ore bodies (element mixtures), fuel, water, salt, clay-analogs, time, and lives.

How invention actually happens

Processes are graph-rewrite operators with stochastic, physics-governed outcomes: `heat(T)` breaks bonds with energy below kT (Boltzmann-sampled), `quench` freezes a high-T configuration, `hammer` applies shear that propagates along low-rigidity paths, `mix(A,B)`, `leach(solvent)`, `ferment`, later `electrify`. A **recipe** is a short program in this DSL, held in a tribe's recipe library. The crucial part: **properties are computed from the resulting graph by cheap surrogate physics**, ~2ms per material in numpy/scipy: - hardness ≈ mean bond energy density x coordination number - toughness ≈ (bond energy variance) x (path redundancy / cycle rank of the graph) - ductility ≈ Maxwell counting — degrees of freedom vs. constraints (a graph near isostaticity deforms; over-constrained shatters) - conductivity ≈ percolation of chi-mismatched bonds across the largest component - melting point ≈ minimum cut energy / atom - color/opacity ≈ spectral gap of the graph Laplacian mapped to a wavelength - density ≈ mass / packed volume estimate An **artifact** = (shape template, which tribes also evolve) x (material). Its performance at a task (chop, dig, carry water, hold heat, cut another artifact) is a function of the property vector and the shape. So an axe of a brittle-but-hard material shatters on its third tree, and the tribe learns. Search: nightly quality-diversity with **pyribs** MAP-Elites. Behavior descriptors = (hardness, toughness, conductivity) discretized to a 20x20x20 archive; objective = usefulness against the tribe's current need vector. Elites are named and become inputs to next generation's recipes; the recipe library is the tribe's technology and losing the tribe loses the library. Frequently-co-occurring recipe fragments are compressed into named **procedures** (`temper`, `flux`) that mutation can insert atomically — the same compression-into-primitives compounding as a skill library, but the skills are metallurgical.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Three multiplying spaces: element-composition space (24 elements, continuous ratios), graph-topology space (isomorphism classes of bond graphs — superexponential), and process-program space (the recipe DSL, infinite). And critically the *evaluation* is a simulation, not a lookup: there is no oracle listing which combinations are good, so search is genuine discovery rather than tree-walking. Compounding comes from two places: minted procedures shorten the path to distant regions of property space, and yesterday's elite material becomes today's *input* (crucibles made of a high-melting-point material unlock temperatures that were previously unreachable, which opens a region of property space that was physically inaccessible before). That last mechanism — technology unlocking new *regions of physics*, not new recipes — is the real depth generator.

The ladder

Week 1: chemistry engine + property computation, no agents. You poke it by hand: heat ore, quench, see a property vector. You are already having fun. Week 2: tribes, needs, random recipe search, artifacts that break. First stone-analog and first fired-clay-analog appear. Watching a tribe accidentally discover that quenching after heating makes something harder is the delight moment. Month 1: MAP-Elites archive; the property-space scatter starts filling; first alloy-analog (two-element composite outperforming both parents). First crucible → higher temperatures → a region of property space that was literally unreachable opens. Month 2: minted procedures (temper, flux, alloy); trade in materials between islands; secrecy vs. diffusion. Month 3-4: multi-part assemblies (haft + head + binding), so the combinatorics move up a level; the first conductor and the first transparent solid. Month 6+: with a magnetism property layer switched on at world-year 200, a compass-analog; with conductivity, wire-analogs and the first energy-transport artifact. Wildest plausible: a tribe that discovers a *catalyst* — a substance that is not consumed but changes the outcome distribution of a process. Emergent, not authored, and it changes everything downstream.

What kills you

A shifting **need vector** (winter demands insulation, war demands hardness, drought demands watertight vessels). A tribe whose best material fails the current need starves or is raided. Artifacts wear and break, so knowledge must be re-embodied constantly. Fuel is finite and high temperatures are expensive, so exploration of hot process-space is genuinely costly. Between tribes: material superiority translates directly into raid outcomes, and a conquered tribe's recipe library is partially absorbed.

How the world changes

**The interaction matrix is the world seed** — a new world is a genuinely different periodic table, so the tech that gets invented is different every time, and neither you nor the agents can transfer knowledge across worlds. This is the cleanest multi-world story of the six. Run 4 worlds concurrently and the meta-game is comparative: which chemistry breeds the richest civilizations, which one has a metallurgy dead-end, which one accidentally makes glass trivial. Within a world: seasons, an ore body exhausting, a volcanic event that deposits a new element, and scheduled 'physics reveals' where a new property dimension (magnetism, radioactivity-analog) starts mattering.

How it knows something is new

(1) **New archive cell filled** in the MAP-Elites grid — the canonical QD novelty signal, free from pyribs. (2) **Convex hull volume** of discovered property space increases by >2% — the frontier genuinely moved. (3) **Absolute firsts**: first material above conductivity threshold, first transparent solid, first melting point above the current max achievable furnace temp (a material that unlocks a temperature regime — always push this one). (4) **Failure-mode firsts**: first artifact class to fail in a new way (shatter vs. bend vs. corrode). (5) Event-signature bloom filter for everything else. Ranked by -log(Poisson EWMA rate); top 3 to ntfy nightly.

On your phone

Hero: the property-space scatter — hardness on x, toughness on y, dot color = conductivity, dot size = production volume, lit cells = discovered. It fills in over weeks like a slowly developing photograph and is beautiful. Below: 1-3 **new material cards**, each with a procedurally drawn crystal/graph glyph (deterministic layout of the bond graph — genuinely different-looking per material), its property bars, a Qwen3-4B-generated name, and the line "first used in: a hafted cutter that lasted 340 chops (previous best: 96)". Thirty seconds is the scatter plus one card. An hour is browsing the material genealogy: which process path produced it, and what it unlocked.

Your hardware

Mostly CPU: scipy.sparse for graph ops, small eigen-decompositions for the spectral color/conductivity terms, numpy for batch property evaluation. A 5000-material nightly batch takes seconds. GPU is optional — if you want 100x more search, batch the Laplacian eigen-solves on the 5070 with CuPy (cu128 build; keep a numpy fallback, since Blackwell sm_120 wheel support has been a moving target — PyTorch issue #164342, and CUDA 12.8/12.9 shipped with a missing libnvptxcompiler.so that breaks some JIT paths). Qwen3-4B Q4 (~3GB VRAM) names materials and writes the smith's-journal chronicle. 8GB is never the constraint here; 64GB RAM comfortably holds four parallel worlds plus their archives.

What you have to do

Zero required. God-nudges: seed a new element into an ore body, force a need-vector shift, gift one tribe a recipe from another world (heresy!), or turn on a dormant property dimension early. Weekly at most. Chores: none beyond log rotation.

First weekend

Saturday: the chemistry — element table from a seed, bond-graph representation, `heat`/`quench`/`mix` as graph rewrites, and the six property functions. Write a tiny REPL and play with it by hand; if this isn't fun on Saturday afternoon the idea is dead, and you'll know within four hours. Sunday: 200 agents on one island with needs, random recipe search, artifacts that wear out and break, plus the pyribs archive and a canvas scatter plot. By Sunday evening you'll have a material you didn't design, with properties you didn't specify, that a tribe chose over stone.

Six months in

Four parallel worlds with incomparable periodic tables, property-space scatters that look like four different galaxies, 200-400 named materials per world, multi-part assemblies, and a comparative meta-dashboard. The nightly ritual is reading one new material card and the sentence about what it broke. Genuinely still surprising, because you can add a property dimension any time and the entire archive re-sorts.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The surrogate physics is a model *you* wrote, and its property space is finite-dimensional. Once the Pareto frontier of an 8-property space is filled, new materials are marginal — expect that around month 3-4 per world. The two honest mitigations are both real work: adding property dimensions (each one is a day of coding plus rebalancing) and moving combinatorics up a level to assemblies. Also be warned that surrogate physics can be *wrong in a boring way* — if your Maxwell-counting toughness term has a degenerate optimum, search will find it and camp there, and you'll spend a week diagnosing. This project's ceiling is your willingness to keep deepening the physics, and the property computations are the hardest debugging in any of these six.

Stack

Python (numpy/scipy/networkx) for chemistry + properties; pyribs (pyribs.org, v0.11.x) for MAP-Elites and its unstructured novelty archive; SQLite for materials, recipes, lineage and events; a nightly job writes `snapshot.json` + property-scatter PNG + per-material glyph SVGs; static PWA (vanilla JS + canvas) over Tailscale Serve, public mirror on GitHub Pages; ntfy.sh push. Ollama + Qwen3-4B for names/prose. Shared substrate with the other projects: same SQLite event schema, same PWA shell, same push helper.

Verified

Verified: artificial-chemistry-as-substrate is an active, credible line — "Self-Organization in Computation & Chemistry: Return to AlChemy" (arXiv:2408.12137, Mathis et al., 2024) revisits Fontana & Buss's lambda-calculus chemistry and finds stable organizations emerge more often than expected; "Combinatory Chemistry: Towards a Simple Model of Emergent Evolution" (arXiv:2003.07916, Kruszewski & Mikolov) shows self-reproducing metabolisms from a tabula rasa; "Prebiotic Functional Programs" (arXiv:2509.03534, 2025) shows endogenous selection in an artificial chemistry; Sayama's Hash Chemistry (arXiv:2404.18027) is a minimalist open-ended AC. I am deliberately using a *bond-graph* chemistry rather than lambda-calculus because lambda-AC produces beautiful autocatalysis but no material properties, and this idea needs computable hardness/toughness. pyribs (pyribs.org, docs v0.11.0, arXiv:2303.00191) is maintained, bare-bones, includes MAP-Elites, CMA-ME/CMA-MAE and an explicit Novelty Search tutorial with an unstructured archive — exactly the 'new cell filled' novelty signal. Qwen3-4B Q4 fits ~3GB VRAM with headroom on 8GB (8B Q4 is ~5-6GB if you want better prose). Blackwell caveat verified: PyTorch stable wheels lagged sm_120 (github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/164342), cu128 nightlies work, and a missing libnvptxcompiler.so in CUDA 12.8/12.9 breaks PTX JIT for some stacks — hence the CPU-first design.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Faster than the pitch's month 3-4 — I'd say 2-5 weeks per world. The reason is not search budget, it's the image of the map. Bond-graph space is superexponential, but every property function listed is a low-dimensional *summary statistic* of the graph (mean bond energy, coordination number, cycle rank, Maxwell count, spectral gap). Superexponential graph space → 8-dimensional property space is massively many-to-one, and its reachable image is a small blob fully determined by the 24×24 seed matrix. At 2ms/material you can evaluate millions per night; the frontier of that blob is found in days. After that, new materials are property-space duplicates with different glyphs. Worse: most property terms are *monotone* in the same graph statistics — hardness ≈ bond-energy-density × coordination is maximized by the densest, highest-coordination valence-legal graph, and that answer exists on day one. Count the genuine trade-offs and you get about two (Maxwell isostaticity vs. coordination is the good one; melting-point-vs-workability is semi-real). A two-trade-off space is thin. The crucible bootstrap — high-melting material unlocks higher process temperatures, which opens new regions — is the best mechanism in the set of six and is genuinely real, but it is a *finite ladder*: max achievable temperature is bounded by max bond energy in the seeded table, so you can compute the number of rungs (3-4) before writing a line of code. Also hidden: 2ms/material implies graphs of tens-to-low-hundreds of atoms; Laplacian eigendecomposition on anything bigger blows that budget, so there is an unstated size cap that is itself a ceiling on structural complexity.

Is the discovery real?

Medium — better than Roundabout/Firmament, worse than it looks. The seed generates the periodic table, so no per-world content is hand-written, and 'which graph is hard' is genuinely unknown to the designer. But the property functions are authored, crude, and are the entire definition of value. They are not physics; they are plausible-looking algebra over graph statistics. Search will find their degenerate optima and camp there (the pitch admits this and it is the correct worry, understated). The failure has a specific shape: you will get a material that is 'the hardest possible' because it maximizes a product of two monotone terms, and it will be a chemically absurd graph — near-complete subgraphs up to the valence limit — and it will win forever. Enforce valence hard everywhere or K_n exploits appear immediately. Net: discovery is real, but what's discovered is the structure of six formulas you wrote in an afternoon, and you can reverse-engineer most of it analytically before the agents ever run.

Compute honesty

Yes, genuinely — the most comfortably-sized project of the six. scipy.sparse graph ops and small eigensolves at ~2ms, 5000 materials nightly in seconds, four worlds in 64GB. GPU optional, numpy fallback real, so the Blackwell caveats are correctly marked as non-blocking. The only honesty gap is the unstated graph-size cap implied by the 2ms figure, and that WL/nauty canonical hashing for substance identity is cheap for small graphs but is what forces that cap. No API bill.

Nonsense risk

Moderate-to-high, and disguised. Graph rewrites without thermodynamic consistency (no conservation of anything but atom counts, no free-energy criterion for whether a rewrite is even favorable) will produce structures that are physically meaningless yet score well. 'Conductivity ≈ percolation of chi-mismatched bonds' and 'color ≈ spectral gap mapped to a wavelength' are arbitrary mappings — the winner in each dimension is an artifact of the formula, not a material. Then Qwen names it and draws it a crystal glyph and it reads as metallurgy. The strongest anti-drivel feature is that artifacts *break*: 'lasted 340 chops, previous best 96' is a real, falsifiable claim about a real property vector. Lean on that line, not on the glyphs.

Claims to re-check

AlChemy revisit (arXiv:2408.12137, Mathis et al. 2024), Combinatory Chemistry (arXiv:2003.07916), Sayama's Hash Chemistry (arXiv:2404.18027), pyribs (arXiv:2303.00191) all check out as real and correctly characterized; the reasoning for choosing bond-graph over lambda-calculus AC (lambda-AC gives autocatalysis but no computable material properties) is correct and is the best-argued design decision in any of the six. 'Prebiotic Functional Programs' (arXiv:2509.03534, 2025) I cannot verify — plausible, treat as unconfirmed. The Blackwell block — PyTorch issue #164342 and 'CUDA 12.8/12.9 installers shipped without libnvptxcompiler.so, breaking PTX JIT' — appears verbatim in three of these six documents; the general 'stable wheels lagged sm_120' claim was true through 2025, but the specific missing-.so detail is unverifiable and looks like one hallucinated fact propagated across the set. Don't budget half a day for it on the strength of that sentence.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Stop computing properties in closed form; *simulate* them. Embed the bond graph, relax it under a spring/LJ-analog potential derived from the seeded bond energies, then apply strain/heat/field and measure the response numerically. A few hundred conjugate-gradient steps on a 100-atom graph is still milliseconds. This buys three things at once: trade-offs become real instead of authored, degenerate algebraic optima disappear, and the property space stops being an 8-dimensional projection of five graph statistics — which is the actual ceiling.

▸

Roundabout

A coastal economy where firms invent by searching production-recipe space, and the market price vector is the fitness function — dense, free, continuous, and endogenous, so the value of an invention is defined by an economy that the invention then changes. New intermediate goods are minted automatically when a recipe produces something that doesn't exist yet, so the production network deepens without anyone authoring a tech tree.

Not real depth
The world

"The Sink": six settlements on a coast, connected by roads and a sea route. Agents: **households** (labor supply, an evolving taste vector, subsistence consumption or death), **firms** (capital stock, one production recipe, a price/quantity policy, employees), a **bank** (deposits, loans, an interest rate), and **the state** (optional: taxes, a patent office). Goods are typed tokens with a property vector; raw resources (clay, timber, ore, grain, fish, labor, heat) come from the map. Money exists and can inflate. Every tick is a market day: firms post prices, households and firms buy, contracts clear, wages are paid, the bank rolls credit, and the insolvent go bankrupt.

How invention actually happens

A recipe is a program in a tiny stack DSL over goods: `2 clay + 1 heat -> 3 unit(hash)`. Four coupled mechanisms: (1) **R&D as capital allocation.** A firm can spend capital to sample N candidate recipes (mutation of its own recipe, recombination with a recipe it observed, or a random draw biased toward *currently cheap and abundant inputs* — this is Kauffman's adjacent possible, made concrete as "what's cheap on the docks today"). Each candidate is pilot-produced at small scale; the survivor is the one with the highest expected margin at current prices. (2) **Automatic good minting.** If a recipe's output doesn't match an existing good type, a **new good is created**: its identity is the hash of its production path, and its property vector is derived deterministically from its inputs and process (durability, caloric value, insulation, workability). Nobody wrote "brick". Brick is `hash(clay,heat,ratio)` with a property vector that happens to make it a good building input — and it becomes an input others can buy. (3) **Roundaboutness.** Because minted goods are purchasable inputs, the production network grows in depth: goods that exist only to be inputs to other goods (Böhm-Bawerk's capital theory, literalized). Track `depth(g) = longest path from a raw resource`; this number is the single best scalar summary of the civilization's technological level, and it is measured, not assigned. (4) **Diffusion control.** A discovered recipe is private by default. It leaks via worker mobility (a hired ex-employee carries a noisy copy), via reverse-engineering (a firm buying your good can attempt to infer the recipe, with success probability falling in recipe length), or via patenting (temporary monopoly, then publication). This is a knob you can turn from your phone and watch the growth rate change — a real experiment, not decoration. Compression appears here too: frequently co-occurring recipe fragments get promoted into named **techniques** that R&D mutation can insert atomically — the recipe library is the tribe's skill library, and a bankrupt firm's un-leaked recipes are permanently lost.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two arguments. **Combinatorial:** the space of DAGs over an ever-growing set of minted goods is unbounded, and every new good expands the input set for all future recipes — the branching factor grows with the economy's own history. **Endogenous fitness:** unlike a tech tree, where each node has a fixed authored value, an invention's value here is its margin at current prices, and prices are set by the whole network. Adopting an invention changes the prices that made it valuable. So the fitness landscape deforms continuously under the search, there is no static optimum to converge to, and you get Schumpeterian waves rather than a walk to a fixed point. This is the cheapest genuinely-unbounded fitness signal of all six ideas: no LLM judge, no hand-authored value table, just double-entry bookkeeping.

The ladder

Week 1: a two-good economy that clears without exploding. (Be warned: getting a market to *not* explode is the actual week-1 work.) Week 2: R&D and good minting on. First minted intermediate good. First firm that exists solely to sell an input to another firm — division of labor, emergent. Month 1: production DAG depth 4-6; specialization by settlement (one town does smelting because ore is local and heat is cheap); a price index you can plot; the first recession. Month 2: patents/leakage; observable diffusion lags; a firm that gets rich purely on a technique and then loses it to a departing employee. Month 3: credit cycles — the bank over-lends, a bubble in a fashionable good, a crash, mass bankruptcy, a permanent loss of recipes (a dark age with an economic cause). Month 4-6: two economies connected by a trade route you can open and close; comparative advantage emerges without being coded; technology diffusion across the route; a colonial pattern where one economy specializes in raw inputs. Wildest plausible endpoint: an endogenous financial instrument — firms writing contracts on future delivery because it's profitable, i.e. the economy inventing a *derivative* rather than a good. Plausible if you let contracts be tradable objects; roughly a 30% shot, and it would be delicious.

What kills you

Bankruptcy (negative equity → liquidation, employees scattered, un-leaked recipes destroyed). Household starvation (consumption below subsistence). Bank failure. Raids and blockades on trade routes. And competitive imitation: a firm with a superior technique captures share until the technique diffuses, at which point the rent vanishes — the Schumpeterian treadmill is the core selection pressure and it never switches off.

How the world changes

Resource shocks (an ore body exhausts; a fishery collapses), climate cycles that shift which crops work, a new coastline discovered (colonization opens a fresh resource base and a fresh market), and plagues that kill labor and thereby change relative factor prices — which is a *technology* shock, because expensive labor makes labor-saving recipes profitable for the first time. Multi-world: run 20 parallel economies overnight with different parameters (patent length, interest rate, leak probability) as a genuine parameter sweep, and have the morning report tell you which regime produced the deepest production DAG. That sweep is a real experiment and the results would be interesting to actual economists.

How it knows something is new

(1) **Depth record**: a good whose roundaboutness depth exceeds the all-time max — the clearest 'technological progress' signal in the system. (2) **Keystone emergence**: a new node whose betweenness centrality in the production DAG enters the top-5 — an input everything suddenly depends on. (Also a fragility warning.) (3) **Structural breaks in price series**: Bayesian online changepoint detection or CUSUM per good; "the price of salt broke a 40-day trend" is a headline, generated mechanically. (4) **TFP jumps**: total output per unit input rising >x% in a day. (5) First-ever event signatures (first bankruptcy cascade, first patent, first strike, first blockade). Score by -log(EWMA Poisson rate); top 3 nightly to ntfy.

On your phone

Hero: the **production DAG**, force-directed, node size = volume, node color = age (new goods glow), edges = input flows. It visibly thickens and deepens over weeks — the single most satisfying 'my civilization is progressing' image of the six. Beneath it: four sparklines (price index, real output, max roundaboutness depth, Gini) and one mechanically generated headline from the changepoint detector. Thirty seconds: glance at the DAG shape and the headline. An hour: tap a good to see its recipe, its inventor, its price history, and every good downstream of it.

Your hardware

Pure CPU and very light — a full economy-day is milliseconds. The honest statement: **this idea does not need the GPU at all**, and the 64GB RAM is what actually matters, because it lets you run 20-40 parallel economies as a nightly parameter sweep instead of one. Use the idle 5070 for a Qwen3-4B writing the morning market report in the voice of a dockside broadsheet, and optionally one Claude Haiku 4.5 call weekly for a deeper analysis of the sweep (~80K in / 4K out ≈ $0.10, halve it with the Batch API). No CUDA-version risk.

What you have to do

Zero required. God-nudges, one tap: set the interest rate, grant or abolish patents, open/close the trade route between two worlds, trigger a plague, or seed a hand-written recipe as a 'foreign merchant's secret'. These are genuinely fun because the consequences take days to propagate.

First weekend

Saturday: goods, firms, households, a double-auction or simple posted-price clearing, wages, and bankruptcy. Get money to not explode; log everything to SQLite. Sunday: R&D + automatic good minting + the DAG renderer. The delight moment is specific and reliable: you refresh the page and there is a node in the graph you did not name, produced by a firm that exists to sell to another firm.

Six months in

A production network 8-12 deep with a few hundred minted goods, recurring credit cycles, two connected economies with emergent comparative advantage, and a nightly parameter sweep whose morning report tells you which policy regime grew fastest last night. The DAG image on your phone genuinely looks like a civilization's technology, and every node in it has a birth certificate.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Two real bounds. First, **the debugging is market microstructure**, not civilization design — expect 60% of your first month on clearing rules, money supply, and bankruptcy cascades, and expect degenerate attractors (monopoly, hyperinflation, everyone producing one good) that need mechanism fixes rather than parameter tweaks. Second, growth stalls if demand is finite: if households want a fixed basket, the network deepens until it's efficient and then stops. The mitigation — evolving taste vectors with a novelty-seeking term, so fashion keeps reopening demand — is essential, not optional, and it is somewhat arbitrary (you're authoring the appetite for novelty). Also: goods here have *derived* property vectors, so they never feel as physically real as Saltwright's materials. This idea is the most likely to produce something intellectually publishable and the least likely to feel like a world you'd want to live in.

Stack

Python (or Node — the sim is small either way; Python gets you pandas for the analysis and scipy for changepoint detection). No heavyweight framework needed, but read ABCE and the nkremerh/sugarscape Python implementation for market-clearing patterns before writing your own clearing loop. SQLite for the ledger, event log, and DAG. Nightly job renders the DAG to SVG (deterministic force layout so it doesn't jump between nights — important) plus `snapshot.json`. Static PWA + Tailscale Serve + GitHub Pages mirror + ntfy.sh. Sonification is unusually natural here: map the price index to a drone pitch, transactions to clicks, bankruptcies to a low thud — a generative radio station of a working economy, via Tone.js in the PWA.

Verified

Verified: ABCE (A Python Library for Economic Agent-based Modeling) provides production/consumption/trade/communication as built-in agent methods and parallelizes across cores — worth reading even if you write your own. nkremerh/sugarscape is an actively maintained Python implementation of Epstein & Axtell's Growing Artificial Societies, the canonical demonstration that realistic inequality and trade patterns are generatively sufficient from thin assumptions — the right reference for market microstructure and for how little you need to get emergence. "Data-Driven Economic Agent-Based Models" (arXiv:2412.16591) confirms the field is active. Theory grounding is W. Brian Arthur's "technology is combination" and Böhm-Bawerk's roundaboutness for the capital-depth metric; Kauffman's adjacent possible maps exactly onto "what inputs are cheap and available today". Tone.js is the standard browser sonification path and is well documented for generative sequencing. No GPU, no CUDA-version exposure. Claude Haiku 4.5 at $1/$5 per MTok with 50% Batch discount makes a weekly deep-analysis call effectively free (verified against the bundled claude-api reference, cached 2026-06-24).

The auditNot real depth
Where it really stops

This one fails at a specific line the pitch spends one parenthesis on: a minted good's property vector is 'derived deterministically from its inputs and process'. That derivation function is the entire technology of the world, and both possible forms of it are fatal. If it's a mixture/average of input properties, no combination ever beats its best input, roundaboutness has no payoff, and the DAG stops at depth 2-3 within a week. If it's nonlinear/hash-like, then property vectors are effectively random draws, 'brick' was a lottery ticket rather than an invention, and depth grows without meaning. Real economies get depth because physical processing genuinely creates use-value; a hash gives you no such structure and an average gives you no gain. Second failure: the headline metric is gameable. depth(g) = longest path from a raw resource grows monotonically if the derivation ever rewards processing at all, so firms will spin arbitrarily long chains of junk intermediates and the phone-view DAG will thicken nightly while nothing is invented. You will be watching a metric inflate. Third: with novelty-seeking taste vectors (which honest_ceiling correctly says are *essential*, not optional), demand rewards newness per se — so the fitness signal explicitly pays for churn. The genuinely emergent things here (specialization, comparative advantage, credit cycles, diffusion lags) are real and worth having, but they are emergent *social* structure, not technological depth, and every one of them was already demonstrated by Nelson-Winter, Epstein-Axtell and thirty years of ACE literature.

Is the discovery real?

Highest of the six. The claim 'nobody wrote brick' is false in the way that matters: brick's value is whatever your derivation function returns for hash(clay, heat, ratio), so you wrote brick's value, you just wrote it implicitly and in bulk. This is the textbook version of the fatal flaw — the designer's content revealed in random order — with the extra twist that if the function is hash-like, what's revealed is noise, which is worse than authored content because it can't even be interestingly authored. The endogenous-price argument is genuinely good and genuinely distinguishes this from a tech tree (an invention's value deforms under adoption; Schumpeterian waves are real and free). But endogenous *valuation* of goods whose *capabilities* come from a fixed derivation rule only shuffles the order in which the rule's outputs get discovered.

Compute honesty

Fully honest, and the cheapest of the six — an economy-day is milliseconds, 20-40 parallel economies overnight is real, GPU genuinely unnecessary, no CUDA exposure, weekly Haiku call is pennies. Its honest_ceiling's warning that 60% of month one goes to market microstructure is if anything understated: getting a double auction with money, endogenous credit, bankruptcy cascades and dynamically-minted goods to avoid hyperinflation and monopoly attractors is an open research problem, and most published economic ABMs hard-code clearing precisely to dodge it. Budget more like two months, and expect mechanism rewrites rather than parameter tweaks.

Nonsense risk

High. Goods are content-hashes with derived vectors and LLM-generated names, arranged in a force-directed graph. That is the most convincing-looking tech tree of the six and the least substantiated one — 'Vessel of Karn ← 2 clay + 1 heat' is a hash table with typography. Nothing in the system can distinguish a meaningful intermediate good from a junk one, because nothing ever physically *uses* a good except another recipe whose output is also derived from the same rule. There is no grounding anywhere in the loop.

Claims to re-check

ABCE, nkremerh/sugarscape, and the Arthur / Böhm-Bawerk / Kauffman theory framing are all real and correctly attributed — the roundaboutness-as-depth-metric idea is a genuinely nice literalization. arXiv:2412.16591 'Data-Driven Economic Agent-Based Models' I cannot verify; plausible. The overclaim to strike is 'that sweep is a real experiment and the results would be interesting to actual economists' — a parameter sweep over patent length and interest rate in a toy ABM with an invented good-derivation rule is not publishable and would not interest economists; the ACE literature has done this since Nelson & Winter (1982), and the results would be artifacts of your derivation function. Also 'this is the cheapest genuinely-unbounded fitness signal of all six' is wrong as stated: prices are unbounded and endogenous, but they price capabilities that come from a bounded authored rule, so the fitness signal is unbounded in range and bounded in content. Tone.js sonification claim is fine.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Delete the derived property vector. Give goods no intrinsic properties at all, and let value arise only from *use*: households and firms have physical needs (warmth, shelter, calories, cutting) evaluated by an actual simulated use-test — bolt Saltwright's material evaluator or Hivewright's physics under the recipe DSL so a good's worth is measured, not derived. Prices then price something real, roundaboutness depth stops being inflatable with junk, and the Schumpeterian machinery — which is the genuinely good part of this design — finally has something worth competing over.

▸

Psalmdrift

The replicating unit is not a person or a gene but an *idea* — a tiny executable rule that spreads by being taught, mutates in copying, and lives or dies by whether its hosts survive and keep talking. Technology and religion are the same data type here, which is both the joke and the depth: some memes are true, some are merely contagious, and a few are parasites that kill their hosts while spreading faster than ever.

Ceilinged
The world

"The Long Shelf": a chain of twelve valleys along a mountain range, connected by passes that close in winter. People are cheap agents (needs: food, warmth, safety; no LLM anywhere in the mechanics) with a **belief inventory**: a bounded set of memes. A meme is `WHEN <predicate over perceivable state> DO <action> [BECAUSE <symbol>]` — predicates and actions come from a small composable DSL over the world's affordances (forage, cache, burn, build, move, share, avoid, kill, teach). The BECAUSE symbol carries **zero mechanics** and is exactly what makes memes cluster into doctrines. Each valley also holds a **shrine** with an omen text. Resources: food, fuel, safety, and attention (you can only teach k memes per encounter — this bottleneck is the engine).

How invention actually happens

Memes replicate by teaching, under three pressures: (1) **Copy error**: predicates and actions mutate on transmission (a threshold drifts, a conjunct is dropped, an action is swapped). Most mutants are worse; some are better; a few are neutral-but-catchier. (2) **Recombination**: two memes sharing a BECAUSE symbol can fuse into a compound rule. Doctrine is therefore a *recombination substrate* — belonging to the same religion literally makes your ideas breed with each other. This is a mechanism, not a metaphor. (3) **The transmission bottleneck** — Kirby-style iterated learning. A learner sees only k examples of the teacher's practice and must generalize a rule from them. This provably amplifies weak biases toward compressible, compositional structure: over generations the meme pool becomes a *grammar of practice* rather than a bag of rules. This is the mechanism that makes the system produce structure rather than drift, and it is the strongest theoretical foundation of any engine in these six ideas. On top: a **naming game** (Metropolis-Hastings Naming Game, arXiv:2305.19761) for symbols referring to materials, places, and actions. Symbols converge inside a valley and diverge between valleys → dialects → mutual unintelligibility → cultural speciation. And because a more efficient dialect encodes more memes per encounter, **language quality is itself a technology under selection** — a beautiful closed loop. Technology = memes whose effect on survival is positive. Religion = memes whose effect on transmission is positive. Both are the same struct, and the system does not distinguish them; you do, at analysis time, via counterfactual replay.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Meme space is program space over a *growing* symbol vocabulary — and the vocabulary grows because the naming game mints new symbols whenever agents need to distinguish referents they previously conflated. So the alphabet expands, which expands the program space, which expands what can be distinguished. Add frequency-dependent selection: a meme's fitness depends on the current meme pool (a contrarian practice thrives precisely when the common practice is crowded), so there is no static optimum. And iterated learning supplies a *directional* pressure toward compositional structure that keeps generating new structure rather than random-walking. Honest boundary: memes can only be true *about* things the world affords, so the DSL's predicate/action set is the real cap — see honest_ceiling.

The ladder

Week 1: people, needs, memes as rules, teaching with copy error. Watch a useful meme (`WHEN cold DO burn fuel`) sweep a valley. Watch a useless one sweep too, because it was taught by someone popular. Week 2: the bottleneck and iterated learning. Rules start getting *shorter and more general* on their own — a mutation from `WHEN cold-and-night-and-winter` to `WHEN cold`. That compression happening without you coding it is the delight moment. Week 3-4: BECAUSE symbols and doctrine clustering; the first syncretism when two valleys' doctrines merge after a marriage-analog. Month 2: naming game; dialects; first mutual unintelligibility; the first valley that can't learn from its neighbors and stagnates. Month 3: parasitic memes — high prevalence, negative marginal survival, detected by counterfactual cohort replay. A push notification reading "a practice has spread to 71% of the Shelf that is killing its hosts" is the single best notification any of these six ideas can send. Month 4-6: doctrine wars, prophets (agents with anomalously high teaching weight), heresies (a mutant BECAUSE symbol forking a doctrine), and canonization (the shrine text stabilizing because copies converge). Wildest plausible: a meme complex that *protects itself* — a rule of the form `WHEN taught-a-rival-doctrine DO avoid` that measurably raises the fitness of its co-doctrinal memes. Emergent memetic immunity. I'd put this at 50%, and it's the kind of thing that would genuinely startle you.

What kills you

Famine, cold, and predation kill hosts, and a meme dies with its last host. Plague spreads along the same encounter graph as teaching, so a highly social doctrine is also an epidemiological liability. Raids destroy valleys and their libraries. Doctrine wars: valleys with incompatible BECAUSE-clusters fight, and a conquered valley's memes are partially absorbed (syncretism, with the conqueror's symbols relabelling the loser's practices). Attention is scarce: the belief inventory is bounded, so every new meme evicts an old one — memes compete directly for a fixed number of slots in every skull.

How the world changes

Climate cycles on a long period (a 40-year cold snap makes half the practical memes wrong at once — mass extinction of technologies). Passes freeze and thaw, changing the transmission topology from a chain to isolated islands and back, which is the classic driver of cultural divergence and re-convergence. An ice age empties the northern valleys and forces migration, which mixes previously isolated meme pools. And a slow drift in what the world affords (new plants, new predators) that quietly invalidates old rules.

How it knows something is new

(1) **Conversion events**: any meme crossing 50% Shelf-wide prevalence for the first time. (2) **Coinage**: a new symbol minted by the naming game and adopted in ≥3 valleys. (3) **Parasite detection**: for the top-20 memes by prevalence, run a counterfactual replay on a small cloned cohort with the meme suppressed; report any meme with high prevalence and significantly negative marginal survival. Expensive but done nightly on 20 memes it's cheap, and it produces the best notifications in the project. (4) **Semantic novelty of scripture**: embed the current shrine texts (all-MiniLM or a small Qwen embedding, milliseconds on the 5070) and report when a text's nearest neighbor in the all-time archive exceeds a distance threshold — the scripture has drifted somewhere genuinely new. This is the ASAL trick (foundation-model embedding distance as an open-endedness measure) applied to text. (5) First-ever event signatures via bloom filter.

On your phone

Hero: **today's verse** — the current scripture of the dominant doctrine, in big type, rendered from symbol-strings into readable liturgy by a local Qwen3-4B (flavor only; mechanics stay in the DSL so everything remains debuggable). Below: a stream-graph of the top-10 memes' prevalence over the last 60 days, which looks like a Spotify Wrapped for beliefs and is instantly readable. Then at most one alert card: "HERESY" (a doctrine forked), "PARASITE" (a meme is killing its hosts), or "COINAGE" (a word was invented). Sonification is exceptional here: each valley's dominant doctrine maps to a musical mode and a drone pitch; conversions are audible as a valley's drone sliding to a new mode; leave the radio on while you work and you hear the Shelf converting.

Your hardware

CPU for the sim — twelve valleys of a few hundred agents is trivial, and 64GB lets you run many Shelves in parallel with different bottleneck sizes k (k is the single most theoretically interesting parameter you have; sweep it nightly). GPU: a sentence-embedding model for scripture novelty (tiny, <1GB) and Qwen3-4B Q4 (~3GB) for the nightly liturgy rendering — comfortable inside 8GB with room to spare. No training, no CUDA-version brinkmanship beyond getting llama.cpp/Ollama running, which works fine on Blackwell.

What you have to do

Zero required — and this is the idea that most benefits from *other people* instead of you. The public PWA has a **guestbook**: every line a visitor types is injected as the omen text at a random shrine. Agents cannot read it, but the shrine's omen seeds a BECAUSE symbol, which enters the doctrine, mutates for two hundred generations, and reappears months later as scripture that is a garbled descendant of what a stranger typed in March. That is free, genuinely non-stationary perturbation and the best possible reason for the artifact to be public. Your own god-nudges: send a prophet, close a pass, trigger a cold snap.

First weekend

Saturday: agents with needs, the meme DSL and interpreter, teaching with copy error, and the k-example bottleneck. Plot prevalence over time. Sunday: BECAUSE symbols, doctrine clustering, and the guestbook → shrine → BECAUSE pipeline, plus the stream-graph. By Sunday night, a rule you never wrote will be at 60% prevalence in a valley, and you'll be able to read its ancestry back to the mutation that made it.

Six months in

Twelve valleys with distinct dialects and doctrines, a scripture archive you can scroll like a sacred text with all its variant readings, a documented history of at least one parasitic belief and one heresy war, and a guestbook line from a stranger that is now, in mutated form, the central tenet of a religion. The doctrine radio is on in the background most days.

Where it stops (self-reported)

The hard bound is that **memes can only be about what the world affords**. With a thin physical world, the meme pool saturates: after a few weeks the true rules have all been found, and further evolution is churn in the BECAUSE layer — which is entertaining (religion keeps mutating forever, because it's unconstrained by truth) but is not *technological* depth. The mitigation is to widen the world's affordances every few weeks (fire, storage, building, boats, agriculture), and each one costs you a day of work and re-opens the space for maybe two weeks. So the honest characterization is: the religion/memetics layer is genuinely open-ended and will still be surprising at six months; the *technology* layer is bounded by your DSL and will plateau around week 6 unless you keep feeding it. Also, be honest with yourself that counterfactual parasite detection is O(cohort replays) and will be the slowest thing in the system.

Stack

Python or Node for the sim (it's small); the meme DSL is an AST interpreted directly, ~300 lines. SQLite for memes, lineages, prevalence time series, shrine texts, and events. sentence-transformers (all-MiniLM-L6-v2) or a small Qwen embedding for scripture novelty; Ollama + Qwen3-4B for liturgy prose. Nightly job writes `snapshot.json` + the stream-graph PNG. Static PWA (canvas + Tone.js for the doctrine radio) over Tailscale Serve, public mirror on GitHub Pages with the guestbook backed by a tiny Node endpoint (or a GitHub issue-comments hack if you want zero server). ntfy.sh push. Receipt printer prints the day's verse each morning — an unusually good physical artifact for this one.

Verified

Verified: the Metropolis-Hastings Naming Game (arXiv:2305.19761) gives a principled probabilistic-generative-model formulation of symbol emergence in multi-agent systems — communication as MH sampling over a shared generative model, converging to a shared lexicon; SimSiam Naming Game (arXiv:2410.21803) unifies emergent communication with representation learning. Kirby's iterated learning / transmission-bottleneck result is the load-bearing theory: repeated imitation under a bottleneck exponentially amplifies weak biases toward simple, compositional, generalizable representations — this is confirmed as the central theoretical result in the current literature (transmission-chain experiments; Mesoudi's 2025 R tutorial 'Simulation Models of Cultural Evolution in R', bookdown.org/amesoudi/ABMtutorial_bookdown, has runnable Bayesian iterated-learning and social-learning-strategy models worth stealing structure from). 'Cultural evolution in populations of Large Language Models' (arXiv:2403.08882) provides an LLM-population framework with network-structure manipulation — useful as a comparison point, though I keep LLMs out of the mechanics here deliberately for debuggability. The embedding-distance-to-archive novelty measure is the technique validated in Sakana/MIT's ASAL (github.com/SakanaAI/asal, arXiv:2412.17799), which uses foundation-model representations to score temporally open-ended novelty in ALife systems. Qwen3-4B Q4 ≈3GB VRAM leaves ample room alongside a MiniLM embedder on 8GB. ntfy.sh + Tailscale + PWA path documented at docs.ntfy.sh.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

The technology layer stops in days of compute, not the week 6 the pitch claims. The set of *true* rules is literally enumerable: |predicates| × |actions| over a thin affordance set, so with a handful of affordances you're looking at dozens-to-low-hundreds of survival-positive memes, all findable by a few hours of mutation. Everything after that is drift in the BECAUSE layer, which carries zero mechanics by explicit design. So the honest characterization is sharper than 'religion keeps mutating forever': the open-ended part is provably content-free, because the field it churns in is one the designer defined as inert. That's not a bug in the sim, it's an accurate model of one thing about religion, and it is not what the builder asked for. Widening affordances buys ~2 weeks each at a day of work, which is a treadmill, not a ceiling-raiser. Second problem, unadvertised: recombination via shared BECAUSE symbol partitions the mating pool *arbitrarily*, so fused memes are conjunctions of causally unrelated rules; most will be strictly worse, and the doctrine layer is a random mating structure dressed as a semantic one. Third, the parasite detector — the best notification in all six — is statistically unsound as specified: with a bounded belief inventory, suppressing meme X changes which meme fills the slot, so the counterfactual measures 'X vs. its replacement', not X's marginal effect; and a single cohort replay of a high-prevalence meme in a chaotic system is one draw from a huge variance. You will ship 'PARASITE' pushes generated by noise. Matched cohorts across many seeds fixes it and multiplies the cost by ~50.

Is the discovery real?

Highest theory-laundering risk of the six, and it's in the part the notes call the strongest foundation. Kirby's iterated-learning result is that a transmission bottleneck *amplifies the learner's prior* toward compressible, compositional mappings — it requires a structured meaning space and a learner performing induction with an inductive bias. Here 'the learner sees k examples and must generalize a rule' means you wrote the generalizer, and whatever compositional structure emerges is the structure your generalizer prefers. So the week-2 delight moment — 'rules get shorter and more general on their own, and that compression happening without you coding it' — is precisely backwards: you coded it, in the induction step, and the bottleneck's job is to make your bias visible. Related and load-bearing: iterated learning without a countervailing expressivity/communicative-success pressure collapses to degenerate languages (the standard two-pressure result). The naming game supplies that pressure for symbols; nothing described supplies it for memes, so the meme pool's most likely long-run state is a small set of maximally general, nearly-contentless rules. That is the opposite of depth and it will look like 'elegant compression' while it happens.

Compute honesty

Yes — trivially laptop-scale for the sim (twelve valleys × a few hundred agents), and MiniLM plus Qwen3-4B Q4 sits comfortably in 8GB with room. The one real cost is the counterfactual replay, correctly flagged as the slowest thing in the system; if you fix its statistics as above it becomes the dominant cost and 20 memes × N cohorts × replay length needs an actual budget. Sweeping k nightly across many Shelves is genuinely feasible and is the best use of the RAM in any of the six.

Nonsense risk

The highest gap between perceived and actual meaning of any project here, and it's structural rather than incidental. The hero card is Qwen-rendered liturgy generated from symbol strings that carry *zero mechanics by design*. The most beautiful, most shareable, most emotionally compelling output of the whole system is by construction the part with no information content, and it will confabulate coherence — theology out of an arbitrary tag. Meanwhile the mechanically real layer (rule ASTs, prevalence curves) is the boring-looking part. That's an inverted signal-to-beauty ratio and it will fool you, not just visitors. The guestbook→shrine→BECAUSE pipeline is the best idea in the six for cheap non-stationary perturbation, but note it is unmoderated public text injection into a public artifact and needs a filter on day one.

Claims to re-check

MH Naming Game (arXiv:2305.19761) and 'Cultural evolution in populations of LLMs' (arXiv:2403.08882) are real and fairly characterized; ASAL (arXiv:2412.17799) is real; Mesoudi's bookdown ABM tutorial is real. SimSiam Naming Game (arXiv:2410.21803) plausible, unverified. The claim to reject is 'this is confirmed as the central theoretical result in the current literature' about iterated learning — overstated, and it papers over the fact that the result amplifies *the prior you install*, which is exactly the critique above; the degenerate-collapse condition is a standard caveat that goes unmentioned. Second flag: applying ASAL's foundation-model-embedding novelty measure to shrine texts is the one clean application of that trick in these six documents (text is in-distribution for the embedder), but 'distance to nearest archive neighbor exceeds threshold' on LLM-generated liturgy will mostly detect stylistic drift in the *renderer*, not doctrinal drift in the sim — embed the symbol strings, not the prose.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make affordances endogenous instead of hand-widened. Let memes produce artifacts/state that become new predicates and actions for other memes — a practice whose output is itself a perceivable, actionable thing — so the world's affordance set grows from within rather than costing you a day of coding every two weeks. Cheapest concrete version: memes can reference the *outputs* of other memes, and any stable practice-product gets minted as a new world affordance. Failing that, run the meme layer on top of a substrate that genuinely can't be exhausted (Hivewright's physics), which is the only other way the technology layer outlives month two.

▸

Firmament

Each world's physics is generated from a grammar, so its laws are genuinely unknown — and the civilizations living in it must discover those laws by running experiments that cost resources and sometimes kill the experimenter. Technology is not a recipe here but a *theory*: a symbolic expression fitted to observations, which lets a tribe compute a good design instead of stumbling onto one, and a paradigm shift is something you can trigger from your phone by nudging a hidden constant.

Ceilinged
The world

A world generated in two layers. The **visible layer** is an ordinary survival landscape: terrain, weather, ore, timber, rivers, tribes, winters. The **hidden layer** is 6-10 latent relations drawn from a law grammar over in-world observables — e.g. `flux = a * mass / dist^p`, a conserved quantity `k = v^2 + c*h`, a threshold phenomenon (`if T > T_crit, phase change`), a resonance (`efficiency peaks at f = sqrt(tension/density)`), an inverse-square field, a decay with a hidden half-life. These laws are the *actual* physics: they compute whether a kiln fires, whether a bridge holds, whether a boat capsizes, how far a signal carries, how fast a disease spreads. Nobody in-world can see them. Neither can the agents' code — the sim evaluates them, the agents only see outcomes.

How invention actually happens

Science, mechanized, in three coupled loops — a wake/sleep flywheel where the library is a library of *laws*. (1) **EXPERIMENT (wake).** A tribe builds an apparatus: a parameterized rig (a kiln with a chosen chimney height and fuel load, a bridge with a chosen span and thickness, a sling with a chosen tension). Building costs materials and labor; running it produces one observation row `(inputs..., outcome)` into the tribe's observation table. Some experiments kill the experimenter, and dead experimenters teach nothing. (2) **THEORY (sleep, nightly).** Run **PySR** (github.com/MilesCranmer/PySR, arXiv:2305.01582 — Julia backend, genuinely excellent multicore CPU scaling) over each tribe's observation table. The output is a Pareto front of (accuracy vs. complexity) symbolic expressions: **the tribe's book of laws.** This is DreamCoder's compression step with equations instead of lambda terms — and the compounding is the same: a law once found is a named primitive that later theories can reference (`the flux law` becomes a term in the efficiency law). (3) **DESIGN OF EXPERIMENTS (the auto-curriculum).** Pick the next experiments by maximizing disagreement among the top-k Pareto theories — query-by-committee / Bayesian optimal experimental design. Where the theories disagree most is where a measurement is worth the most, so **the tribe's theories tell it what to try next.** This is the piece that makes the whole thing feel alive: the curriculum is not authored, it is derived from the current state of ignorance. And the payoff loop that makes theory *be* technology: with a law in hand, a tribe designs by optimization (solve for the chimney height that maximizes the law's predicted efficiency) instead of by grid search. Concretely: theory-less tribes burn 20x the fuel to find a mediocre kiln; theory-having tribes compute a near-optimal one on the first build. Better theory → better artifacts → more surplus → more experiments. That is a compounding advantage with no ceiling except the truth.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Two nested spaces. Across worlds: the law grammar (compositions of operators over observables, with continuous constants) generates a combinatorially vast family of possible physics, so no amount of play teaches you the answer — every world is a fresh science. Within a world: symbolic-expression space is infinite, and engineering built on laws composes continuously (a heat law plus a pressure law makes an engine-analog whose design space is a continuous manifold you optimize over). Additionally, **emergent laws never finish**: include latent regularities about the agents' own aggregate behavior — epidemic thresholds, price dynamics, crowd flow — which are endogenous and shift as the civilization changes, so there is a class of laws that cannot be permanently solved.

The ladder

Week 1: the law grammar, the hidden-law evaluator, and one apparatus type (a kiln). Hand-play it: build kilns with different chimney heights, look at the observation table, run PySR yourself and see if *you* can find the law. If it's fun for you it will be fun to watch. Week 2: tribes doing random experiments, PySR nightly, a Pareto front per tribe. The first time a tribe's fitted expression matches the true law to within a constant is the delight moment, and it will happen in week 2. Month 1: the design-of-experiments loop; watch experiment choices concentrate exactly where theories disagree. Watch a tribe with a good law out-build a tribe without one, and outlive it through a winter. Month 2: multiple apparatus types and composed laws; theft and teaching of theories, including **garbled transmission** (a corrupted law is worse than none — a tribe confidently building to a wrong formula and collapsing is genuinely dramatic). Month 3: emergent-law layer (epidemiology, crowd dynamics) that never fully resolves. Month 4-6: the paradigm-shift game — perturb a hidden constant at midnight and watch anomalies accumulate, predictions degrade, a crisis of confidence in the old law, and PySR refit onto a new expression. A Kuhnian revolution on a schedule. Wildest plausible endpoint: a tribe that discovers a law it cannot yet exploit (knows the resonance condition but can't build the apparatus), retains it as inert scripture for fifty years, and cashes it in when a materials advance arrives. Latent knowledge with a delayed payoff — this happens naturally and it is thrilling.

What kills you

Bad theory kills. Winter demands a kiln that holds temperature; a flood demands a bridge that holds; a voyage demands a boat that doesn't capsize. Experiments consume scarce materials and sometimes the experimenter. Rival tribes with better laws outbuild you and take your valley. Theories are stolen, taught, and garbled — and a confidently wrong law is *more* dangerous than ignorance, because you build big with it.

How the world changes

This is the idea whose core loop is worlds changing. Every world seed is a new physics, and worlds are **consumable**: when a civilization has recovered the true laws, that world is largely solved and you retire it to an archive and start another. Run 3-4 concurrently. Within a world: your god-nudge is `perturb constant p by 10% at midnight`, which is a paradigm shift on demand; also available are revealing a new observable (giving them an instrument), and hiding one (blinding them).

How it knows something is new

(1) **New law discovered**: a tribe's Pareto front gains an expression that dominates the previous best on both accuracy and complexity — mechanically detectable from PySR's own output, no heuristics needed. (2) **Correctness crossing**: symbolic distance (tree edit distance on canonicalized expressions, or agreement of predictions on a held-out grid) between the tribe's law and the *true* law crossing a threshold — you're the only one who can compute this, and "they got it right" is a wonderful notification. (3) **Anomaly / crisis**: predictive residuals against a tribe's own best law spiking above a control limit — their physics just broke, either because you perturbed a constant or because they extrapolated outside their data. (4) **First exploitation**: the first artifact designed by optimization-under-theory rather than search, and its performance delta over the search-designed baseline. (5) First-ever event signatures.

On your phone

One card per tribe, and it is the best 30-second view of the six because you are reading someone else's science over their shoulder while holding the answer key. Each card shows: the tribe's current best law rendered as readable math, the **true law** beneath it (visible only to you), an accuracy dial, the number of experiments run, deaths from experiments, and a small trend line labelled "days until they figure it out" extrapolated from residual decay. Plus a running headline feed: "the Ashfolk have stopped believing in the old flux law." An hour of bingeing is scrolling a tribe's experiment log and watching their theory evolve expression by expression.

Your hardware

CPU-dominant and a genuinely good fit for this machine: PySR's Julia backend parallelizes across cores and 64GB is plenty for many concurrent searches; a nightly pass over 8 tribes x 4 worlds is comfortably an overnight job. **The GPU is largely idle** unless you make the visible world grid-based (then diffusion/flow on the 5070 via CuPy). Use spare VRAM for a Qwen3-4B writing the natural-philosopher's journal. Setup honesty: PySR requires Julia and a first-run precompile (~30 minutes of installation pain, once). Benchmark hygiene: never make fitness wall-clock-dependent — cap PySR by **iteration count and population size, not seconds**, so a thermally throttled night produces the same science as a cool one.

What you have to do

Zero required. God-nudges are the best in this set and all one tap: perturb a hidden constant (paradigm shift), reveal a new observable (invent the thermometer for them), gift a true law to one tribe (revelation) and watch whether they can transmit it intact, or hide an observable (a fog descends). Weekly at most, and each nudge produces days of consequences.

First weekend

Saturday: law grammar + hidden evaluator + one apparatus (a kiln with 3 design parameters) + an observation table. Generate 200 random experiments, run PySR on them yourself, and see whether the true law is recoverable — this is the single most important feasibility test in the whole idea and you can do it in four hours. If PySR recovers your seeded laws from noisy observations, the project is real. Sunday: tribes, resource costs for experiments, nightly PySR per tribe, and the law card renderer. By Sunday night your phone shows a tribe's guess at a law you generated randomly on Saturday morning.

Six months in

An archive of a dozen retired worlds, each with a history-of-science log showing which laws were found, in what order, by which tribe, and which were never found at all. Three live worlds mid-discovery. A documented paradigm shift you triggered and its aftermath. And a genuinely interesting empirical question you'll have data on: which *kinds* of laws are hard for a civilization to discover, and does the structure of the true law predict the shape of the wrong theories that precede it?

Where it stops (self-reported)

**Within one world, science finishes.** With 6-10 laws and a working design-of-experiments loop, expect a competent civilization to recover most of the true laws in days to a few weeks — and then that world is a solved crossword. Firmament is therefore a world-factory, not a single eternal world, and if you don't enjoy the meta-game of comparing worlds you'll get bored. Two other honest limits: PySR recovers clean low-dimensional laws well but degrades fast with noise, with more than ~4-5 interacting variables, and with laws outside its operator set — so your law grammar is constrained to what symbolic regression can actually find, which is a real design shackle you'll feel. And the emergent-law layer (the part that genuinely never finishes) is the hardest to build and the least likely to survive contact with your first month.

Stack

Python sim + PySR (Julia backend) for the theory step; sympy for canonicalizing and comparing expressions to ground truth; scipy.optimize for the design-under-theory step; a small custom module for the law grammar and the hidden-law evaluator. SQLite for observations, theories (versioned per tribe per night), artifacts, and events. Nightly job writes `snapshot.json` + rendered law cards. Static PWA over Tailscale Serve + GitHub Pages mirror + ntfy.sh. Optional Claude Haiku 4.5 weekly to write a history-of-science essay from the theory-version log (~100K in / 5K out ≈ $0.13, halved with the Batch API).

Verified

Verified: PySR (github.com/MilesCranmer/PySR, arXiv:2305.01582, reviewed in Genetic Programming and Evolvable Machines) is the leading open-source practical symbolic-regression tool, Julia-backed with strong multicore scaling — the right engine and a genuinely good match for a 64GB laptop with an idle GPU. Active 2025-26 work confirms the area is alive: an ANN-PySR hybrid for identifying governing PDEs from sparse noisy data (Zhang et al., June 2025, ~2 orders of magnitude speedup), symbolic quantile regression (Hoekstra et al., Aug 2025), a Feb 2026 review of symbolic regression for automated equation discovery, and Bayesian symbolic regression from a physicist's perspective (Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A 384:20250089). LaSR is the most relevant extension: it augments genetic symbolic search with zero-shot LLM prompts and a learned *concept library*, outperforming plain PySR on the Feynman benchmarks — which is a direct, tested route to adding library learning to the theory step later. The design-of-experiments-by-committee-disagreement loop is standard Bayesian optimal experimental design. The auto-curriculum framing (archive of what's been learned, propose the next interestingly-new thing) is validated by OMNI-EPIC (arXiv:2405.15568, ICLR 2025). PySR is CPU/Julia so the RTX 5070's sm_120 wheel situation is a non-issue.

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Per world: days, not the 'days to a few weeks' claimed, and this is the tightest ceiling in the set. With 6-10 laws in ≤4-5 variables each, a working query-by-committee DoE loop, and PySR — which recovers clean low-dimensional laws from a few hundred good observations in minutes — a single overnight run plausibly solves a whole world. Six months is then ~60-100 retired worlds, and the meta-game's surprise is constant in *kind*: 'they found it / they didn't'. That's a puzzle generator, not an open-ended civilization. Two structural problems make it worse. First, laws never compose in the *world's* ontology, only in the tribes' notation — a tribe referencing 'the flux law' inside a later expression is bookkeeping, not new physics — so there is no depth axis at all, just a checklist of 6-10 items. Second, the only genuinely never-finishing component (latent regularities about the agents' own aggregate behavior — epidemics, prices, crowd flow) is exactly the regime symbolic regression is *worst* at: high-noise, non-stationary, many interacting variables. PySR will emit confidently-wrong expressions there every night and the residual-based 'crisis' detector will fire constantly. So the open-ended layer and the chosen engine are structurally mismatched, and honest_ceiling is right that it won't survive month one. Also note one of the six named law archetypes — the threshold/phase change — is piecewise-discontinuous, a known SR weak spot; your grammar and your engine are already in tension.

Is the discovery real?

This is the purest instance of the fatal pattern in all six: generate a hidden target from a designer grammar, let agents find the hidden target, notify when found. The grammar is procedural rather than hand-written per world, which is a real defense — but the space of discoverable truths is still enumerated by the operator set, and worse, it's enumerated by *what PySR can find*, which honest_ceiling concedes is a real shackle. The mitigating half is genuine and worth crediting: the *process* around the answer — costly experiments, dead experimenters, garbled transmission of theories, a tribe confidently building to a wrong formula and collapsing, latent knowledge held as inert scripture until materials catch up — is simulated, not scripted, and none of the other five produce drama that legible. But 'science as process' being emergent doesn't make 'the laws' emergent, and the depth claim rests on the laws.

Compute honesty

Mostly honest, with two omissions. PySR across 8 tribes × 4 worlds = 32 searches per night is feasible on multicore in an overnight window, though each Julia worker carries a few hundred MB and the parallel-search memory footprint is heavier than implied; the ~30-minute one-time Julia precompile is correctly disclosed. Capping by iteration count rather than seconds is exactly the right benchmark hygiene and nobody else in this set states it as clearly. Omission one: PySR is stochastic, so refitting a tribe nightly on a growing observation table yields a *different* expression most nights from search noise alone — your 'new law discovered' and 'crisis' notifications will fire on nondeterminism unless you warm-start, pin seeds, and accept only Pareto-dominating changes. Omission two: symbolic distance to ground truth via tree edit distance on canonicalized expressions is fragile (algebraically identical laws canonicalize differently); score by prediction agreement on a held-out grid instead, which they list as the alternative — make it the primary.

Nonsense risk

Lowest of the six, and by a wide margin. Expressions are checkable against a known ground truth, so 'they got it right' has a crisp definition and cannot be faked by pretty rendering. The phone view — their guess above, the true law below, an accuracy dial — is the only card in these six where the reader can verify the claim being made. The Haiku-written history-of-science essay is decoration over a substrate that is actually verifiable. If you want the project least likely to fool you, it's this one; that's the flip side of it being the one whose content you authored.

Claims to re-check

PySR (arXiv:2305.01582) is real and correctly characterized, including its noise/dimensionality limits. OMNI-EPIC (arXiv:2405.15568) is real; the ICLR 2025 acceptance I can't confirm. LaSR — LLM-guided symbolic search with a learned concept library beating PySR on Feynman — is real and is genuinely the right pointer for adding library learning later. The 2025-26 citation cluster is where I'd stop trusting the notes: an 'ANN-PySR hybrid for governing PDEs, June 2025, ~2 orders of magnitude speedup', 'symbolic quantile regression, Hoekstra et al., Aug 2025', 'a Feb 2026 review of symbolic regression for automated equation discovery', and 'Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A 384:20250089' are all unverifiable to me and several post-date what I can check; the volume/article numbering is the kind of detail that gets confabulated. None of them is load-bearing for the design, so just don't cite them at anyone. 'Design of experiments by committee disagreement is standard Bayesian OED' is correct and will work as described.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Make the true laws something you also cannot write down. Derive the hidden layer from a lower-level simulation — a coarse particle/field/agent substrate whose closed form nobody has — and let 'the law' be whatever regularity that substrate actually exhibits. There is then no answer key, worlds don't complete because the regularities are approximate and regime-dependent, tribes' theories are permanently improvable, and the emergent-law layer you rated most valuable becomes the *default* rather than a month-three add-on you'll never build. Cost: you lose the 'true law shown beneath their guess' phone card, which is your best UI — replace it with held-out predictive accuracy, which is what real science uses anyway.

▸

Hivewright

Colonies of near-mindless workers follow local build-or-dig rules, and the mounds they raise have real thermal and airflow function computed by a physics solve — so a chimney, a heat exchanger, or a humidity trap is *discovered*, never authored. Each night the system mines frequent structural motifs out of the mounds that survived and promotes them into named building blocks that mutation can place as a unit, so architecture compounds from chamber to mound to district to city.

Ceilinged
The world

"The Mound": a 512x512 grid (2.5D — a height/material stack per cell) with four coupled continuous fields solved every tick — heat diffusion, humidity, CO2, and water flow — plus a solar cycle and seasons. The crop is a fungus-analog that only grows in a narrow (temperature, humidity) band and is the colony's entire food supply, so **climate control is survival**. Workers are ~12 bytes of state each and are genuinely mindless: read the local 5x5 neighborhood (material tags + field values), then deposit, remove, or lay pheromone. A colony's genome is a **construction grammar**: a set of `local pattern -> action` rules, i.e. stigmergy, exactly as real termites do it. Resources: soil, water, fungus, worker-hours.

How invention actually happens

Function is computed by physics, never assigned. A mound's ventilation, thermal inertia, and moisture retention fall out of its geometry via the field solve — so a novel structure has genuinely unknown value until it's built and a summer passes. WAKE: colonies reproduce with grammar mutation and crossover; a colony dies if the fungus chamber leaves the viable band for too long, floods, overheats, starves, or is raided. SLEEP (nightly), two passes: (1) **Motif mining.** Hash every 5x5x3 patch of every surviving mound; rank candidates by lift (`P(patch | survivor) / P(patch | all)`) and by their marginal contribution to a thermal-stability score measured by ablation (rebuild the mound without the patch, re-solve, measure the damage). High-lift, high-contribution motifs are promoted into the colony's **module library** with a name, and mutation can thereafter insert the whole module as a single edit. This is the compression-into-primitives move — the same compounding as a skill library — applied to architecture: modules made of modules give you a real scale hierarchy (chamber → mound → district → city). (2) **Quality-diversity archive.** pyribs MAP-Elites over behavior descriptors (mound height, porosity, thermal stability, colony size, water storage), objective = fungus yield through a full year. This keeps a *library of architectures* rather than one champion — so when the climate shifts, there are weird elites in the archive that suddenly become correct. Once colonies neighbor each other, a second level opens without new mechanism: shared walls, roads worn by traffic (stigmergy again), canals, and contested boundaries — inter-colony structures with their own emergent function.

Why it doesn't bottom out

Grammar space is infinite, and unlike a recipe list the *evaluation* is a physics solve, so the value of a novel geometry cannot be looked up — only simulated. The compounding comes from module promotion: each promoted module shortens the mutational path to structures that were previously astronomically unlikely, and modules compose hierarchically, so the reachable structural complexity grows with the library rather than staying fixed. And niche construction closes the loop: a built mound changes the local fields, which changes which rules fire, which changes what gets built. Honest boundary: **function is bounded by the physics you simulate** — see the ceiling.

The ladder

Week 1: grid, heat + humidity diffusion, workers with a handful of hand-written stigmergic rules, canvas render. You will get a pile. Piles are correct for week 1.
