Week 2: fungus with a viability band, colony death, grammar mutation and selection. The first mound with an *interior* that holds temperature better than outside — the delight moment, and it arrives fast. Week 3-4: motif mining; the first promoted module, which will probably be a wall-with-a-gap (a vent). You didn't write it and you can see it in cross-section. Month 2: MAP-Elites archive filling with structurally distinct architectures; the first genuine chimney (a vertical shaft driving convective flow) — verifiable in the airflow field, not just visually. Month 3: multi-colony contact; walls, roads, and the first shared canal; raids. Month 4-6: climate drift makes existing architecture lethal and you watch retrofit — old chambers sealed, new vents cut into hundred-day-old structures. Then a load/collapse physics layer, and mounds get *tall* with buttresses that were never designed. Wildest plausible endpoint: a district-scale structure whose function only exists at district scale — a shared thermal mass or a canal network that no single colony's grammar encodes, arising from the interaction of several grammars. Emergent infrastructure. Maybe 40%, and it would be the single best screenshot of any of these six ideas.

What kills you

Overheating, mold (humidity too high), desiccation, flooding, starvation when the fungus band is missed, structural collapse when overbuilt, and raids from neighbor colonies. All of it is cheap to compute and brutally legible — a dead colony's mound stays on the map as ruin, and neighbors build into it.

How the world changes

Climate drift is the core: the viable (temperature, humidity) band slowly moves, so architecture that was optimal becomes lethal and colonies must retrofit or die — this is the most viscerally satisfying 'the world changed' mechanic in the set, because you can *see* the retrofit in cross-section. Plus seasonal floods, a multi-year drying that forces water infrastructure, a wet period that rewards drainage, and occasional fires. Multiple worlds: different climate regimes (arid, monsoon, temperate) run in parallel produce visibly different architecture, which is a lovely comparative gallery.

How it knows something is new

(1) **New MAP-Elites cell filled** — free from pyribs, and the canonical QD signal. (2) **Motif promotion with no prior occurrence in any world** — a genuinely new building block, checked against a global motif hash table. (3) **Functional firsts**: first mound to hold the fungus band through an entire summer; first structure whose ablation costs >30% of thermal stability (a load-bearing *function*, discovered). (4) **Visual novelty**: perceptual hash or a tiny convolutional autoencoder embedding of the mound cross-section, nearest-neighbor distance against the all-time archive above threshold — the ASAL embedding-novelty trick, applied to pictures of buildings. (5) First-ever event signatures. Top 3 nightly to ntfy, each with the cross-section image attached — ntfy supports image attachments, so the push itself is the artifact.

On your phone

Hero, and it is the strongest visual of the six: a **rendered cross-section of last night's best mound**, with airflow arrows and a temperature colormap overlaid. It is beautiful, instantly legible to anyone, and changes every day. Below it: the MAP-Elites archive as a small heatmap grid (filled cells = discovered architectures), and 1-2 **new module cards** showing the promoted 5x5 patch, where it appears in the mound, and its ablation cost. Thirty seconds is the cross-section alone. An hour is scrubbing a colony's build history as a time-lapse. The physical artifact writes itself: a receipt printer producing a dot-matrix cross-section every morning is a genuinely covetable object.

Your hardware

**This is the one that actually uses the RTX 5070.** Four coupled diffusion/flow fields on 512x512 grids are pure GPU work: a 512² float32 field is 1MB, so 8GB holds hundreds of fields — you can solve 100+ colonies' local environments in parallel. CuPy with a cu12x build is the lowest-friction path; JAX or raw CUDA also work. **Honest caveat, verified:** Blackwell sm_120 needs CUDA 12.8+ and recent wheels — stable PyTorch wheels lagged sm_120 (pytorch issue #164342; nightlies from Aug 2025 onward work with cu128), and CUDA 12.8/12.9 installers shipped without libnvptxcompiler.so, which breaks PTX JIT for some stacks. Budget half a day for the CUDA setup, and write a numpy CPU fallback path first so you are never blocked (CPU is ~30-50x slower but perfectly fine for a single colony while you develop). 64GB RAM holds the mound archive and history comfortably.

What you have to do

Zero required. God-nudges: shift the climate band, start a flood, set a fire, open a pass between two colony clusters, or transplant a module from one colony's library into another's (technology transfer). Weekly at most. The only real chore is disk: mound snapshots are images, so rotate aggressively — keep dailies for 30 days, then keep only archive elites.

First weekend

Saturday: the grid, heat + humidity diffusion (a 20-line explicit solver is fine), workers with 6 hand-written stigmergic rules, and a canvas render of the top-down view plus a cross-section. Watch a pile become a pile with a hole in it. Sunday: fungus viability band, colony death, grammar mutation, and 50 colonies competing. Render the best mound's cross-section and push it to your phone. By Sunday evening you'll have a mound with an interior microclimate that no rule you wrote specified — the physics found it.

Six months in

A gallery of architectures across three climate regimes, module libraries 40-80 entries deep with visible hierarchical composition, documented retrofits after climate shifts, multi-colony districts with shared walls and canals, and a printed stack of six months of daily cross-sections that is honestly the nicest physical artifact any of these ideas produce. Still surprising, because every physics layer you add (load, light, fire, predators) reopens the whole space.

Where it stops (self-reported)

Function is bounded by the physics you simulate, and that bound is tighter than it feels. With heat + humidity + flow you should expect roughly 15-30 genuinely distinct functional motifs before saturation — probably month 3 — after which new mounds are recombinations that look different but do the same things. The mitigations are real work: adding structural load and collapse (the best single addition, and it's a week), light, fire spread, and predators. Second honest limit: **it will look far more impressive than it is** for a while, because emergent architecture photographs beautifully even when the underlying discovery has stalled — so lean hard on the ablation-based functional metrics, not the visuals, to know whether it's still inventing. Third: multi-colony infrastructure is the exciting endgame and also the part most likely to just not emerge; do not build the roadmap assuming it will.

Stack

Python + CuPy (numpy fallback) for the field solves and the worker update (vectorized over all workers — they are 12 bytes each and update in lockstep, which is exactly the shape GPUs like); the construction grammar and motif mining in plain Python (hash-based patch mining is fast; upgrade to gSpan-style frequent subgraph mining later if you want). pyribs for the archive. SQLite for colonies, grammars, module libraries, lineage, events. Nightly job renders cross-section PNGs + `snapshot.json`. Static PWA (canvas) over Tailscale Serve, public mirror on GitHub Pages, ntfy.sh with image attachments. Shared substrate with the others: same event schema, same PWA shell, same push helper.

Verified

Verified: pyribs (pyribs.org, docs v0.11.0, arXiv:2303.00191, GECCO 2023) is the maintained bare-bones QD library — Archive/Emitter/Scheduler decomposition, MAP-Elites, CMA-ME, CMA-MAE, plus an explicit unstructured-archive Novelty Search tutorial; 'new cell filled' is the free novelty signal. Sakana/MIT's ASAL (github.com/SakanaAI/asal, arXiv:2412.17799, Artificial Life 31(3):368) is JAX-based, end-to-end jittable, and works across Lenia, Boids, Particle Life and neural CAs — both a direct precedent for GPU-accelerated ALife on modest hardware and the source of the foundation-model-embedding novelty measure I'm using for cross-section novelty; QD-AF (arXiv:2312.02231, 'Quality Diversity in the Amorphous Fortress') is a live precedent for QD over 0-player-game-like systems. DreamCoder's compression-into-named-primitives (PLDI 2021; Phil. Trans. R. Soc. A 381:20220050) and Stitch's MDL formulation (github.com/mlb2251/stitch, arXiv:2211.16605) are the theoretical basis for module promotion. Hardware verified honestly: PyTorch stable wheels lagged sm_120 (github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/164342), cu128 nightlies from Aug 2025+ detect it, community build guides exist (github.com/bajegani/pytorch-build-blackwell-sm120), and a missing libnvptxcompiler.so in the CUDA 12.8/12.9 installers breaks PTX JIT on Blackwell for some stacks — so plan a CPU fallback and prefer CuPy over exotic JIT paths. ntfy.sh supports image attachments in pushes (docs.ntfy.sh).

The auditCeilinged
Where it really stops

Functionally, ~2-4 weeks, well before the claimed month 3 — and the reason is the objective, not the search. The colony's entire fitness reduces to keeping one chamber inside a (T,H) band. A scalar objective over smooth diffusion fields admits about four physical strategies: thermal mass, convective venting, evaporative cooling, water storage. Those are the functional motifs; everything after is parameter tuning and morphological variation on four ideas. MAP-Elites will diligently fill cells with mounds that are structurally distinct and functionally identical, which is exactly the failure honest_ceiling warns about, arriving sooner than it expects. Two mechanism-level problems compound this. (1) 2.5D — a height/material stack per cell cannot represent overhangs, arches, enclosed horizontal galleries, or a chimney with a lateral duct, i.e. most of what makes real termite architecture interesting; the 'cross-section with airflow arrows' hero image will be rendering a heightfield's implied interior, which is a visualization more than a structure. (2) The claimed hierarchy does not follow from the mechanism. Promoting a 5×5×3 *literal patch* to a single mutation stamp gives you a bigger brush, not a nonterminal — a bigger patch is just a bigger patch. 'Modules made of modules → chamber → mound → district → city' requires the construction grammar to gain composable, parameterized nonterminals; patch hashing cannot produce that, and the entire why_unbounded argument rests on it. Also, lift over survivors is confounded by shared ancestry (same flaw as Cinderloom's librarian): survivors are relatives, so high-lift patches are mostly 'wall texture from the dominant clade'.

Is the discovery real?

Lowest of the six, and this is the project's real strength. Function is *computed* by a field solve, so nobody tabulates that a chimney works — thermal stability under ablation is a measurement, not an assignment, and 'first structure whose removal costs >30% of thermal stability' is a genuinely non-authored discovery event. The stigmergic rules are also the right level of abstraction: local pattern → action, exactly as real termites do it, with the mound's own fields feeding back into which rules fire. Niche construction here is real and closes properly. The authored parts are the physics you chose to simulate and the single scalar objective — and those bound *what can be discovered*, not *whether discovery is real*. That's the honest and favorable distinction.

Compute honesty

The weakest compute story in the set, and ironically it's the one claiming to be GPU-honest. Do the arithmetic the pitch skips: four coupled fields on 512² is ~1M cells × 4; explicit diffusion needs small dt, so realistically tens of substeps per tick; a 'full year' evaluation is >10³ ticks. That's roughly 10¹¹-10¹² cell-updates per colony-year. A well-fused bandwidth-bound stencil on a 5070 might sustain ~10¹⁰-10¹¹ updates/s, so one colony-year is seconds to a minute — and a QD generation needs thousands of evaluations per night. 5000 evals × 30s = 42 hours. It does not fit, and shrinking to fit (128² grid, compressed year, fewer colonies) directly shrinks the structural space that was supposed to provide the depth. Then the nightly ablation pass re-solves the mound *per candidate motif* — hundreds of extra full solves — which by itself likely exceeds the entire wake phase. Overall the compute claim is optimistic by roughly one to two orders of magnitude. The tell is in the text: 'a 512² float32 field is 1MB, so 8GB holds hundreds of fields' quotes *capacity* where *throughput* is the binding constraint. The Blackwell caveats and the numpy-fallback-first discipline are, in contrast, sensible and correctly prioritized.

Nonsense risk

Low for function, high for perception — and the document says so itself, which is to its credit. Physics validates whether a vent vents, so a promoted motif with a measured ablation cost means something real. But emergent architecture photographs beautifully whether or not discovery has stalled, and this project's whole phone experience is a gorgeous cross-section. The receipt-printed daily section is a lovely object that will keep looking like progress for months after progress stops. Second, narrower risk: the ASAL-style embedding novelty applied to mound cross-sections is a misapplication — synthetic grayscale texture images are far out of distribution for the embedder, and nearest-neighbor distance there is dominated by low-level image statistics, so channel (4) will fire on noise. Drop it and rely on ablation-based functional metrics, exactly as honest_ceiling advises.

Claims to re-check

pyribs (arXiv:2303.00191), ASAL (arXiv:2412.17799), QD-AF / Amorphous Fortress (arXiv:2312.02231), DreamCoder PLDI 2021 and Stitch (arXiv:2211.16605) are all real and reasonably characterized. Two flags. First, the Blackwell block — PyTorch issue #164342, 'cu128 nightlies from Aug 2025 onward', 'CUDA 12.8/12.9 installers shipped without libnvptxcompiler.so breaking PTX JIT', plus a named community build-guide repo — is the most specific version of a claim that appears verbatim in three of these documents; the broad 'stable wheels lagged sm_120' part was true through 2025, but the missing-.so detail and the repo name are unverifiable and read as one hallucinated fact propagated across the set. Budget the half-day if you like, but don't trust the diagnosis. Second, citing DreamCoder/Stitch as 'the theoretical basis for module promotion' is a category error given the implementation: those learn abstractions *with variables* by MDL over program structure; hash-based patch mining learns literal fixed images. The citations describe the mechanism you'd need, not the one specified.

The one change that raises the ceiling

Go to coarse true 3D voxels (128³ or even 96³) and add structural load with collapse. This is the single change that multiplies functional motif count rather than morphological variety: arches, buttresses, spans, galleries, load paths and enclosed chimneys all become real design problems, load conflicts genuinely with ventilation and thermal mass (giving you the conflicting objectives the current scalar band lacks), and 'a wall you can't remove' becomes structurally as well as thermally measurable. It also costs less than it sounds — 128³ is 2M cells, comparable to the 512² you were already solving four fields on, and dropping resolution buys back the throughput the honest arithmetic above says you don't have. If you can smuggle in a second change, make module promotion mint parameterized grammar nonterminals rather than patch stamps; without that the hierarchy in why_unbounded is not implemented anywhere.

Nine things every audit agreed on

These emerged independently across all seven lenses, which is what makes them worth trusting. Whichever design you pick, these are the parts that decide whether it is alive at month six — and the first two are the ones nobody wants to build.

01

THE AUTOTELIC GOAL GENERATOR IS THE MISSING FIRST-CLASS COMPONENT. Roughly a dozen independent audits converge on the same diagnosis — the compressor is not the bottleneck, the TASK DISTRIBUTION is — and not one design ships a curriculum engine as a primary mechanism; it appears only as remedial advice. This is well-trodden ground and cheap: OMNI-EPIC (arXiv 2405.15568, ICLR 2025, code at github.com/maxencefaldor/omni-epic) is the archive-plus-interestingness-model reference; MAGELLAN (arXiv 2502.07709) does learning-progress prediction over large goal spaces; minimal-criterion coevolution gives you the goldilocks band with no handcrafted fitness. The laptop version is ~150 lines: keep a generated goal only if it is unsolved at budget B and solved at 3B, retire it once cheap. That single loop is the difference between plateauing at depth 4 in month two and continuing to compound.

02

NOBODY BUILDS A NULL CONTROL, AND SEVERAL OF THESE DESIGNS ARE MACHINES FOR FOOLING THEIR AUTHOR. Sandwright's archive coverage keeps rising while filling with near-duplicates; Emberlisp's library keeps growing after invention stops; Kithmind's dendrogram separates on sampling noise; Hivewright photographs beautifully after discovery has stalled. The fix is one extra process and one strip on the phone card: run a matched control world nightly — same seed, same physics, invention engine disabled (or abstractions admitted at random instead of by MDL) — and render both. If your headline metric moves the same in both, the engine is not the cause. Also ship a spoiler toggle showing ground truth beside the narrated version, and check it weekly; the alternative is discovering your sim is thin slowly and painfully over three months.

03

SEEDED COUNTERFACTUAL REPLAY SHOULD BE THE UNIVERSAL NOVELTY DETECTOR, NOT ONE IDEA'S NOTIFICATION. Grist proposes it and nobody else adopts it: for the ten most-adopted inventions per night, re-run N sim-years from a locked seed with the invention suppressed and report the survival delta. That converts 'a thing was invented' into 'a thing MATTERED', which is what a front page needs and what every first-ever/MDL-shock detector fails to measure. Every deterministic design in this pool can afford it on ten candidates a night. Do it with MATCHED cohorts across several seeds, not one draw, or you will ship notifications generated by variance.

04

THE 8GB GPU IS IDLE IN ALMOST EVERY SYMBOLIC DESIGN, AND THERE IS EXACTLY ONE JOB IT SHOULD BE DOING. Every library-learning audit independently concludes that a bigram prior over a growing library stalls at depth 2-3 and that the DreamCoder recognition model is the actual plan rather than the fallback — and every one of those designs then assigns the GPU to naming things. A ~200k-parameter recogniser trained nightly on dreamed replays is the correct use of the card, and the naming pass is what should be squeezed into the leftovers. Related and unaddressed: dreaming on one small world overfits the prior to that world's tasks, so the multi-world transplant machinery should be promoted from validation to training-data generation.

05

THE SHARED SUBSTRATE IS NAMED EVERYWHERE AND SPECIFIED NOWHERE. A dozen ideas say 'same event schema, same PWA shell, same push helper' and none of them writes down the contract. It is the one deliverable that makes project #2 cost a weekend instead of a month, and it is small: a versioned SQLite schema (events with canonical signatures, a lineage/parent-edge table, a novelty_scores table with per-channel z-scores, a nightly snapshot.json), a novelty ranker that takes channel scores and emits at most 3 pushes, a static PWA shell that renders one hero card plus sparklines from snapshot.json, and an ntfy helper. Build it as its own thing in weekend one and version it, or you will rewrite it four times.

06

THE PASSIVE AND PHYSICAL CHANNELS ARE ALWAYS GARNISH, NEVER THE PRIMARY. Sonification appears in six pitches and the receipt printer in five, each time as a bullet at the end. But the brief explicitly values the most passive possible check-in, and an ambient channel has a property no phone card has: it runs while you are doing something else, so it costs zero attention and still tells you when something changed. A nine-voice drone where a voice drops a note when a tribe forgets something, or a chemistry that sounds like rain on a roof on a productive night, is a better 24/7 monitor than any dashboard. Design one channel to be primary-passive and let the phone be the drill-down.

07

THE PUBLIC-PERTURBATION LAYER IGNORES ITS TWO ACTUAL FAILURE MODES. Guestbook-as-omen shows up constantly and is genuinely the cheapest non-stationarity available, but nobody addresses (a) the empty guestbook — a hobby page linked nowhere gets zero entries a month, which makes an idea whose long-term novelty depends on strangers plateau exactly like the ones that do not, and (b) the permanent adversarial moderation obligation created by a public write endpoint feeding a corpus you publish and print in your house. The cheap fix nobody proposes: skip the anonymous internet and give six named friends a private write endpoint. It gets used, it needs no classifier, and the perturbations are funnier.

08

NOBODY PLANS FOR THE RUN DYING AT 3AM. 'Near-zero chores' requires a heartbeat, not just an absence of chores. Every one of these is a 24/7 process on a laptop that will sleep, thermally throttle, OOM, or hit a Python exception in the enumerator, and the failure mode is silent: the morning digest simply stops, and you notice on day three. A watchdog that pushes 'the sim has not ticked in 90 minutes', a per-night checkpoint you can resume from, and event-log rotation automated on day one are the difference between an unattended project and a project that needs attending. Related: several designs put nightly compression, QLoRA, and a validation sweep in the same overnight window without checking that they fit in it.

09

THE VERIFICATION LOOP EXISTS IN EXACTLY ONE PITCH AND SHOULD BE RAIDED FROM IT. Firmament is the only design where the builder can hold the answer key: hidden laws generated from a grammar, tribes discovering them by costly experiment, PySR fitting symbolic expressions nightly, and 'their guess' rendered above 'the true law' on the phone. Its own ceiling is fatal as a standalone — a world with 6-10 laws and a working design-of-experiments loop completes in days, and its own audit's fix (derive the hidden regularities from a lower-level simulation so there is no closed form and no answer key) is correct. But the LOOP is worth stealing wholesale into any of the top-ranked designs: let tribes hold explicit THEORIES about the substrate, fitted from their own observation tables, choose the next experiment by disagreement among top-k theories, and design by optimisation-under-theory rather than by search. That is the mechanism that makes a civilization feel like it figured something out rather than merely having it selected for — the coldness the symbolic designs all share.

Seven research-grounded idea generators, an adversarial depth auditor per lens, and a final judge that saw every audit before ranking. Where a card’s audit flags a claim as dubious, treat it as unverified until you check it yourself — several cite work published after the generating model’s knowledge cutoff.
